## K: K engine–Keating

### K engine
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  it is a pity you did not have a K engine. <i>(May 4, 1938,Auckland Weekly News,10</i>)

### K Gorge
 
 <i>n.</i> Karangahake Gorge [Karangahake Gorge is located south of Auckland and about 10 minutes before you reach Waihi. K Gorge has some of the North Island's best rock climbing...(from the net)]

>  With its huge boulders mid river, K' Gorge is unlike many waterways in the north, providing not only interesting whitewater, but also a playground for practising eddy turns and other stuff! <i>(1996,NZ Whitewater,pp 174</i>) [NOTES: Karangahake Gorge is located south of Auckland and about 10 minutes before you reach Waihi. K Gorge has some of the North Island's best rock climbing...(from the net)]

### K Road
 
 <i>n.</i> (informal) short for Karangahape Road in Auckland [ORIGIN: abbreviation, also as K-Road/Rd, K'Road/Rd]

>  Auckland's K Rd Friday night-life wandered into St Kevin's Arcade - the venue for <i>Polished Up!</i> - not to tarnish performance, but to add dimension. <i>(Jun 1, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),56</i>) [NOTES: also as K-Road/Rd, K'Road/Rd]

>  Alcohol - the effects thereof - is something of a theme on the K Rd Sunday walk. <i>(Jun 22, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),47</i>)

>  They had stumbled, in their droves, right out of the nightclubs of K'Rd and the city. <i>(Dec 16, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),57</i>)

>  There's a soft amber glow wafting out of Alleluya cafe, tucked away and out of sight on Auckland's seedy strip, 'K' Road. <i>(Jan 22, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),44</i>)

>  Breaking more than sleep/ it came yellow, not gold/ slim as a K Road walker's skinny legs/ equally discomforting/ to us who lie alive/ in peace. <i>(Jun 3, 1986,Campus News,20</i>) [NOTES: (Deborah Stone) (?K Road walker, a prostitute.)]

>  You can buy both bat and ball from Samoa House in K Road ... <i>(May 1987,Metro,88</i>)

>  They were different from the usual K Road people. <i>(1987,Metro Fiction,88</i>)

>  K Road's heyday was in the Victorian era when it was a major transport artery and terminus and <i>the place</i> to shop, backed by boom times and the spending power of the people in the new villa suburbs of Grey Lynn, Kingsland, Sandringham and Mount Eden. <i>(Nov 2005,North & South,108</i>)

>  i want to see an Oscar winner make a speech that involves them thanking Jesus and the blinding epiphany they had while yelling "show us your bits" on K'Rd in rush hour. <i>(Feb 17, 2002,Sunday Star Times,D2</i>)

>  i think i'm inspired by what's happening around me - by the kids in K Road who wear tapa medallions and really do their own thing. <i>(Feb 28, 1993,Sunday Times (NZ),27</i>)

>  <b>MORRiSON</b> A restaurant on 'K'Road i refuse to name. <i>(1989,Auckland City & Sea,[12]</i>)

>  Gosh, even its sexiest red [wine] has a name that most New Zealanders can relate to - K Road. <i>(Jul 3, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),44</i>)

>  So K Rd flourished as a night world, with bars, clubs and restaurants. And possibly uniquely in New Zealand, the street became so famous it is known by a single, emblematic letter. <i>(Jan 2, 2005,Sunday Star Times,D5</i>)

>  it's an upbeat little number, peopled with larger-than-life characters you would expect from its K' Rd setting, and shot through with a cheerful; warmth rarely seen in New Zealand moves. <i>(Apr 10, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),41</i>)

>  if i had carried on using it, not only would i have been unfit to work, but i swear i would have been begging on K Rd inside a week. <i>(May 15, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),60</i>)

>  <b>McGill</b> Bob consulted his phone list again and dialled The Purple Pussycat in K Road. <i>(1995,Gold in the Creek,194</i>)

>  Mr Prebble was moved to ask how "K Road" could survive. <i>(Jul 3, 1992,Dominion,5</i>)

>  K (as in Karangahape) Rd to Kingdom Come collects together this Auckland artist's work from 1995 to 2001, including pieces never shown before. The title refers to the two bookend works, beginning with <i>K Rd</i> (1995) and ending with <i>Kingdom Come</i> (2001). <i>(Nov 1, 2001,Dominion,24</i>)

>  Opposite Grafton Bridge.. is Karangahape Road, known to locals as 'K-Road. <i>(1987,insight Guides (NZ),108</i>)

>  He drove through Mt Eden, then along Symonds St and K-Rd. <i>(Mar 20, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),46</i>)

>  Walking just 50 metres down Auckland's K'Road is like spinning a radio at full-volume through dozens of raspy, blaring frequencies - reeking takeways jump-cut to red-light emporia, butted up against deadbeat thrift shops and pre-cappucino cafes. <i>(Apr 19, 1997,Listener (New Zealand Listener),44</i>)

>  The pale grey face on K'Rd at three in the afternoon is not enjoying the glorious sunshine of Queen's Birthday Monday. <i>(Jul 7, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),65</i>)

>  Because of the recession-depression, most of K Road was saved from the developer's bulldozer. <i>(Jan 1992,Metro,55</i>)

>  Rain forced the wedding party to decamp to Samoa House, in K Road. <i>(Jun 1992,More Magazine,70</i>)

>  <b>McCAULEY</b> i asked to be dropped at K Road. it was late-night shopping and the bargain tables were spread along the crowded pavements. <i>(1990,Bad Music,104</i>)

### K surface
 
 <i>n.</i> key surface, geol

>  The name 'K surface' (key surface) of Cotton (1957) has been retained for the high level, low relief, erosion surfaces that incorporate a history of faulting, westward tilting and doming, and also is accepted as a key surface to estimate the nature of subsequent deformation. <i>(Oct 1982,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,58,74 ed.</i>)

### k'noath
 
 <i>exclam.</i> [1984 14 F E143Wgton Girls C. 35C FUCKEN OATH]

>  <b>O'SULLiVAN</b> i'll have to report this, Jacko. JACKO: K'noath. We'll both report it. <i>(1985,Shuriken,58</i>)

### K-Force
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  There was no shortage of volunteers for the 1056-man Kayforce that 50 years ago tomorrow sailed from Wellington to fight alongside other units from the Commonwealth in the Korean War... After only three months of training, the 16th Field Regiment and a number of smaller, ancillary units, known collectively as Kayforce, sailed on December 10, 1950, on the troopship Ormonde, arriving in Pusan on New Year's Eve and going into action against North Korea and its Chinese ally less than four weeks later. <i>(Dec 9, 2000,Dominion,23</i>)

>  [Hdg] K Force Lines Up with Famous Unit <i>(Jan 30, 1951,Evening Post,7</i>)

>  K-Force Equipment [heading] <i>(Nov 1952,Tararua Tramper,7</i>)

>  Ever since they left New Zealand on December 10, 1950, to uphold the cause of the United Nations in repelling aggression, the members of K Force have maintained the traditions established by the N.Z. Expeditionary Forces in the First and Second World Wars ... Originally composed of the 16th. Field Regiment, R.N.Z.A., totalling 1000 men with 1st reinforcements, K Force has since been augmented by signalling, engineering, and A.S.C. units, making a combined total of 1668 New Zealanders now serving in Korea. <i>(Jan 19, 1953,Evening Post,[Leader]</i>)

>  <b>BOOTH</b> Some of Jim's K-force cobbers ... had known Dick since before Seoul. <i>(1964,Footsteps in the Sea,54</i>)

>  <b>KiNG</b> Eventually, a body of 1 100 men (`Kayforce') sailed for Korea in December 1950. it was made up of the 16 Field Regiment, a Signals Troop, a Light Aid Detachment, a Transport Platoon, an Engineers section and a Reinforcement Training Unit. <i>(1981,New Zealanders at War,277</i>)

>  Within weeks, New Zealand despatched two frigates to join other navies in Korean waters. Shortly after, a force of over 1000 men was formed. Soom to be known as Kayforce, they were quickly trained and transported to the bitter war on a cold, forbidding country. <i>(Jul 24, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),8</i>)

>  About 6000 Kiwis fought in Korea before Kayforce was withdrawn in 1957. With just 45 deaths, historian ian McGibbon points out the Korean War was the least costly of the major conflicts New Zealand has been involved with. <i>(Jul 27, 2003,Sunday Star Times,A14</i>)

>  Throughout New Zealand yesterday more than 1500 volunteers answered the call for service in the K-Force, New Zealand component of the United Nations forces in Korea. <i>(Jul 29, 1950,Dominion,9</i>)

>  <b>BARBER</b> From January 1951 through to November 1954 the 16th Field Regiment fought its way through Korea ... Those who had joined `Kay Force' soon realised that soldiering in Korea was no sinecure. <i>ibid..</i> 139 Gunner N.G. Garland, who had come to Korea with the original draft of K Force, was captured while on observation post duties with the King's Shropshire Light infantry... K Force was better equipped to face the second Korean winter, with some heated bunkers now built below ground, with warmer uniforms and better rations. <i>(1984,Red Coat to Jungle Green,135</i>)

>  in K-Force the percentage of Maoris is much higher than it would be in (say) a New Zealand Division. <i>(May 6, 1953,NZ Observer,6</i>)

>  K-Force are disgusted with the changing of the lemon-squeezer hat. The hat is solely New Zealand's own. <i>(Jan 28, 1953,NZ Truth,19</i>)

>  [Hdg] Kayforce & Jayforce. <i>(Dec 2, 1953,NZ Truth,19</i>)

>  Later that same evening, Father went down the road to have a quiet beer with an old mate from his K-force days. <i>(2003,Huia 5: Contemp. Maori Fiction,131</i>)

>  The lorries carried 64 members of K Force.. who are returning to New Zealand. <i>(Apr 15, 1952,Evening Post,4</i>)

### KA
 
 <i>abbr.</i> the largest steam locomotive used in NZ

>  We'd take a steam train into Wellington and another to Paekakariki where there was a huge KA waiting to do the trip to Gisborne. <i>(Jun 2004,North & South,83</i>)

### Ka Awatea
 
 <i>n.</i> 1991 Government plan for Maori development [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  in 1991 Winston Peters, then National's Maori Affairs Minister, released the Ka Awatea report to great fanfare... Ka Awatea listed 68 ways in which Government could intervene to lift the lot of Maori and eventually formed the basis of the Maori Affairs policy oulined in the coalition agreement between National and New Zealand First. <i>(Dec 10, 2000,Sunday Star Times,C2</i>)

>  However irritating he [sc. Winston Peters] may have been.. with his hymns of praise to himself over his Ka Awatea policy, you can't help feeling some respect for his courage and tenacity when he's been at odds with his Cabinet colleagues or big business lawyers or baleful journalists. <i>(Jun 1996,Mana,20</i>)

### ka kite/ka kite ano
 
 <i>exclam.</i> see you later [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  Until next time. Ka kite. <i>(Nov 25, 2005,Dominion Post,B9</i>)

>  'Will you write to me?' 'i can't, because we're different now. Ka kite ano, Mihi!' <i>(2003,Huia 5: Contemp. Maori Fiction,220</i>)

### Ka Mate, Ka mate
 
 <i>phr.</i> (opening of) the traditional Maori haka [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  [Hdg] Kamate calamity - haka out. The Tall Blacks will not be allowed to do their traditional pre-match haka at the Sydney Olympic Games. <i>(May 23, 2000,Evening Post,28</i>)

>  My tribe did belong to the infantry, in the halcyon days of Gallipoli, when with their comrades of the Mounted Brigade, they made the ancient slogan of "Ka Mate, Ka Mate" resound up the slopes of Bauchope's Hill and Old Number Three. <i>(Apr 16, 1917,Chronicles NZEF,ii/16 : 78</i>)

>  Before long the weird incantation of "Komate, Komate" [sic] was floating out over the plain of Flanders. <i>(Jan 2, 1918,Chronicles NZEF,iii/34 : 18</i>)

>  The speech was followed by the "kamate, kamate" haka. <i>(May 6, 1920,Auckland Weekly News,16</i>)

>  Few New Zealanders know what the Te Kamate haka is about - on the field the All Blacks were doing this haka in front of the South Africans and the rest of New Zealand was trying to get onto the field to stop the game. <i>(Dec 1981,in Touch (Wellington),9</i>)

>  <b>ORBELL</b> Ka mate, ka mate. Ka ora, ka ora. <i>(1978,Maori Poetry,80</i>)

>  Shand said the All Blacks had opted to perform Ka Mate tomorrow because the players were still getting to gripps with each haka... <i>(Jun 9, 2006,Dominion Post,C8</i>)

 <i>exclam.</i> a rare elaboration of kapai, very good

### kaeo
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Fish were hooked and netted in great quantities and shellfish were a staple food [in the Chathams], especially <i>paua, pipi,</i> and <i>kaeo</i> (sea tulips). <i>(1972,Journal of the Polynesian Society,81/354</i>)

>  <b>POWELL</b> SEA TULiP (<i>Boltenia pachydermatina</i>). A tunicate with a long stalk attachment known as Kaeo by the Maoris of the Chatham islands. <i>(1947,Native Animals of NZ</i>)

>  <b>NATUSCH</b> KAEO AND SEA POTATOES/ subclass ASCiDiACEA ... the larva swims about free ... ; but later settles on the sea floor, or a convenient wharf pile, and exchange freedom for lumpish security ... The stalked [sea] squirt <i>Pyura</i> ... is common in the south. The Maori knows it as kaeo, and eats the inner part which has a good briny taste, slightly iodiny ... But a northern Maori ... said he did not eat kaeo. `Not Taranaki food - only southland Maori food.' <i>(1967,Animals of NZ, The,187</i>)

>  <b>RiCHARDS</b> <i>Boltenia ... pachydernatina</i> ... Sea Tulip. KAEO. <i>(1952,Chatham islands,95</i>)

>  Kaeo - this may be meant for koeo, a small form of the paua or pawa with a delicately-tinted irridescent shell and an edible `sea-slug' inhabiting it, or for kaio or ngaio, the sea-nut; probably the latter. <i>(1945,Maori Place Names,62</i>) [NOTES: c1880 South island Maori Lists `Wahi Mahika Kai' in 1945 Beattie Maori Place-names of Canterbury 62]

### kaha
 
 <i>n.</i> strength

>  Wars that took two of her children - one shot in the head, the other scalped by government-Maori for the money - but not her kaha. <i>(Apr 13, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),62</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> = keha q.v.

>  <b>POLACK</b> The stones having been made red-hot, the provisions, which consisted of fish ..., after being cleansed and bound up in the leaves of the kaha [a's-acute], or wild turnip, which almost covers every spare surface of vegetable soil in the country, ... were all placed in a basket on the hot stones, ... <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/70</i>)

### kahakaha
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>WiLLiAMS`</b> [No New Zealand flowers are] remarkable for either beauty or fragrance except Kaha Kaha, Wara Wara [Kowharawhara] and the Papapa (or the Parpuppa). The Kahakaha are fragrant and oderous [sic]; it varies in colors sometimes being dark and sometimes of a light pink and yellow. <i>(1844,NZ Journal,111,1956 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HAAST</b> The Maori names the Celmisias, Kahakaha, and they used the lower side of the leaves for tinder. <i>(1848,Julius Von Haast,298</i>) [NOTES: 1862 Von Haast in 1948 Von Haast Julius Von Haast 298]

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Kaha-kaha, <i>Col[enso]. Astelia Solandri. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),765</i>)

>  Sometimes the snow-white downy fibres from the under side of the leaves of the Kowharawhara, and the Kahakaha, (<i>Astelia Cunninghamii</i>, and <i>Solandri</i>,) and the thin transparent epidermis from the leaves of the mountain Tikumu (<i>Celmisia coriacea</i>), were also used ... to ornament ... the head. <i>(1869,TrNZi,1/iii/37</i>) [NOTES: (Colenso Botany North isd)]

>  Kahakaha. Astelia Solandri, A. Cunn. <i>(1869,TrNZi,1/iii/50</i>) [NOTES: (Buchanan Botany Otago)]

>  Kahakaha, <i>Astelia solandri</i>.- The tree-flax of the settlers; abundant on lofty trees and rocks throughout the Colony. <i>(1874,TrNZi,6/55</i>)

>  ... kahakaha<i> (Astelia solandri)</i> grow[s] luxuriantly in the upper parts of the branches. <i>(1869,TrNZi,21/37</i>)

>  <b>TREGEAR</b> Sometimes a sea-side-spreading plant named <i>pingao</i> (Demoschoenus spiralis) was used for making belts, and the soft leaves of <i>kahakaha</i> (Astelia banksii) were also taken into the service of mat-makers. <i>(1904,Maori Race, The,238</i>)

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> <i>A[stelia] Solandri, A.Cunn. ... [713] Kahakaha. </i> <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,712</i>)

### kahawai
 
 <i>n.</i> an Australasian marine food fish, Arripis trutta, resembling salmon (also called (native or sea) salmon, sea trout). [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  One of the dolphins' favoured hunting stations is the tidal race off Te Titoki, and here one sometimes finds the mummified carcasses of kahawai baking on boulders above the high-water mark. <i>(Jan 2002,NZ Geographic,No. 55/20</i>)

>  New Zealanders are outdoors people and part of many Kiwis' upbringing includes going fishing, for kahawai off the beach, rocks or local wharf. Grandparents and even parents remember giant schools of kahawai churning up the sea within a stone's throw of the shore. That abundance and the fish's fighting qualities made the kahawai the "people's fish". <i>(Apr 15, 2004,Dominion Post,C3</i>)

>  <b>BEATTiE</b> Kahawai - a fish plentiful at Canterbury river mouths (but practically unknown in Murihiku). <i>(1945,Maori Place-Names of Canterbury,61</i>) [NOTES: c1880 South island Maori Lists `Wahi Mahika Kai' in 1945 Beattie Maori Place-names of Canterbury 61]

>  <b>HAY</b> in the bays and tidal rivers are the mango or sharks - the most highly-prized food-fish - the tamure or schnapper, the whapuka or rock-cod, the kahawai or mackerel, the porahi or herring, the kanae or mullet, the patiki or sole, and many others. On the shores are oysters, mussels, cockles, mutton-fish, crabs, and other shell-fish in profuse abundance. in the fresh-water creeks are eels (tuna), lampreys (pipiharau), and whitebait (inanga). <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/151</i>)

>  The fishing off the island is very good, there being abundance of hapuku, kohikohi, maumau, schnapper, kahawai, tarakihi, in addition to plenty of shell-fish such as koura or cray-fish, crabs, paua,etc. <i>(1885,TrNZi,17/419</i>) [NOTES: (Goldsmith)]

>  <b>SHERRiN</b> KAHAWAi (<i> Arripis Salar </i>). ... [51] This fish is frequently termed the native salmon, from its elegant form and lively habits, in which it resembles the true salmon. <i>(1886,Handbook Fishes of NZ,50</i>)

>  <b>MURDOCH</b> <i> STEWED KAWHAi </i> ... butter ... add ... flour ... salt ... cayenne ... two glasses of claret ... juice of a lemon. When it boils lay in the fish, cut in nice pieces, and stew ... <i>(1887,Dainties ... How to Please Our Lords and Masters,17</i>)

>  1. <i> Cape Maria van Diemen </i>. - Kahawai, snapper, moki, trevally, king-fish, haku or yellowtail, rock-cod <i>ibid.</i> 204 24. <i>Puysegur Point </i>- Hapuku, kahawai, trumpeter, moki, pig-fish, trevally, rock-cod, perch, butter-fish, red-cod, dog-fish <i>(1892,TrNZi,24/203</i>) [NOTES: (Thomson)]

>  <b>MORRiS</b> ~ Maori name for the fish <i> Arripis salar </i>, Richards.; called in Australia and New Zealand <i> Salmon </i> (q.v.). <i>(1898,Austral English,226</i>)

>  <b>DRUMMOND & HUTTON</b> The warehou, tarakihi, and gurnet are common in the north, and the moki, butterfish, and frost fish in the south, while the kahawai, hapuku (the groper of Dunedin), blue cod, barracouta, the little sea mullet, sometimes called the herring, and the flat fish are found all round the coast. <i>(1902,Nature in NZ,71</i>)

>  <b>BAUGHAN</b> <i> Kahawai (kah-ha-wy)</i>: A fish. <i>(1908,Shingle-Short,53</i>)

>  <b>HAMiLTON</b> He also gives a tale of the loss of Tapakakahu's paua-shell fish-hook, through the kahawai,... <i>ibid.</i> 64 .. with the following results: 581 eels (from 1ft. to 4ft. in length); 8 dozen flounders, of various sizes, large numbers of aua, or kataka (mullet); about 70lb. or 70lb. weight of pilchard, or mohimohi; a few snapper and kahawai; hundreds of red cod and rarii; and a number of kokopu or rawaru. <i>(1908,Fishing and Sea-Foods,32</i>)

>  <i> Arrippis Trutta </i> ... Kahawai <i>(1911,Records of the Canterbury Museum,1/219</i>) [NOTES: Waite]

>  Kahawai. <i> Arripis trutta .</i>.. Small quantities of kahawai may be seen exposed for sale during the summer months. <i>(1918,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,1/271</i>) [NOTES: (Phillipps)]

>  ARRiPiS TRUTTA (Forster). <i> Kahawai </i>. ... Seldom seen south of the Kaikoura Peninsula. <i>(Jun 1921,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,iV/115</i>) [NOTES: Phillipps Notes on the Edible Fishes]

>  Other fish such as kahawai(<i> Arripis trutta </i> (Forster)), tamure <i>(Pagrosomus auratus </i>(Forster)), snapper and moki (<i> Latridopsis ciliaris </i>(Forster)), are also caught. <i>(1926,TrNZi,58/615</i>) [NOTES: (Buck)]

>  <b>PHiLLiPPS</b> <i> Arripis trutta </i> (Forster). KAHAWAi. <i>(1927,Bibliography of NZ Fishes,34,1971 ed.</i>)

>  All the commoner deep-water species abound, schooling kahawai and kingfish being especially abundant near the Sugarloaf Rocks. <i>(1928,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,9/290</i>) [NOTES: (Sladden and Falla)]

>  <i>~ n </i>. a fish, the so-called New Zealand salmon. [Maori] <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,6</i>)

>  <b>BEST</b> The following fish were caught with hook and line: araara, maomao, moki, mango, ngoiro, pakirikiri, kahawai, tamure, tarakihi, and warehou. The kanae, kehe, kopipiro, moho, nanu and other species were taken by net. <i>(1929,Fishing Methods,43</i>)

>  <i> Arripis trutta </i>: Kahawai. <i>(1936,Handbook for New Zealand, The,72</i>)

>  <b>BUiCK</b> Next in progression came the shoals of red cod - the hoka of the Maori - chased from the [Waitaki] river-mouth by the assaults of the voracious kahawai (<i> Arripis trutta </i>). <i>(1937,Moa-Hunters of NZ,176</i>)

>  <i> Arripis trutta </i>(Forster). <i> Kahawai. </i> <i>(1938,TrRSNZ,68/.410</i>) [NOTES: Graham Fishes of Otago Harbour]

>  <i> kahawai,</i> a sea-fish (Arripis salar), sometimes called Maori salmon: vulg. ka(ra)wai, the "h" suppressed. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/149</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <i> kahawai:</i> edible fish. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,174</i>)

>  <b>BAXTER</b> The ka' wai chase the herrings in <i>(1955,Collected Poems,158</i>)

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> Kahawai <i> Arripis trutta </i> (Bloch and Schn.) (Syn. <i> Sciaena mulloides sapidissimus </i> Richardson <i> Scomber maculatus </i> Richardson, <i> Centropristes tasmanicus </i> Hombron and Jacquinot, and <i> Lepidomegas mulleri </i> Thominot) <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An identification Guide,406</i>) [NOTES: 1955 Whitley Namelist in 1956 Graham NZ Fishes 406]

>  <b>PARROTT</b> The Kahawai [<i> Arripis trutta </i> (Bloch and Schneider)] is a very active fish of graceful form, and it is probably due to its superficial resemblance to the trout that it has received the popular name in New Zealand and Australia of `Sea Trout'. <i>ibid.</i> 169 Kahawai Arripis trutta Native Salmon; Sea Trout <i>(1957,Sea Anglers' Fishes of NZ,92</i>)

>  ~ A New Zealand sea fish. it is edible, but is usually caught just for sport or for bait. -- <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),228</i>)

>  <b>ihimaera ed.</b> Just as she fins her way into the cave, a school of kahawai break water on the surface and scoot past her, as if on a rescue mission at great speed. <i>(2007,Get on the Waka,32</i>) [NOTES: (Cathie Dunsford)]

>  <b>BiDWiLL</b> Other shell-fish are also abundant, particularly Cockles - of these i have seen more than a man could carry collected by one woman during the space of a tide; Scallops are also tolerably abundant, and are most delicious eating. There are no Lobsters nor Crabs, but a great abundance of fish of all kinds; one, the Salmon of the English, or Carwai (Carwhy), is a most excellent fish, the best i have tasted in the Southern Hemisphere; ... <i>(1841,Rambles in NZ,115</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> <i> Kahawai (centropristes trutta)</i> or <i> mulloides </i>. - it is generally from fifteen to twenty inches long; is taken with a hook and a piece of the the haliotis shell: the flavor is rather sour, but its great abundance renders it very valuable as an article of food. <i>ibid.</i> 623 <i> Percoidoe </i>, eleven species are given of these; the one which is most generally known is the <i>Centopristes trutta.</i> The <i> Kahawai </i>, this is one of the most abundant, and is called the mackerel by the settlers. <i>(1855,Te ika a Maui,411</i>)

### kahawai bird
 
 <i>n.</i> a tern with forked tail and white breast, also called white fronted tern, sea swallow, tara

>  To yachtsmen and fishermen, the "Kahawai bird," or the White-fronted Tern, is a well-known companion for many a sun-drenched day on sparkling summer seas. <i>(1945,Korero (AEWS Background Bulletin),2(24) : 20</i>)

>  <b>BAUGHAN</b> <i>Kahawai (kah-ha-wy)</i>: A fish. <i>Kahawai-bird: </i>A species of tern. <i>(1908,Shingle-Short,53</i>)

>  <b>POWELL</b> WHiTE FRONTED TERN (<i>Sterna striata</i>), Tara of the Maoris, is abundant all around the New Zealand coasts and occurs in Tasmania and Eastern Australia as well. This is the graceful little `Kahawai-bird' or `Sea-swallow,' which pursues schools of small surface fish, wheeling, darting and dipping to the water, in its energetic quest of food. Since the Kahawai fish is predaceous on the small school fish also, the tern is an excellent indicator of the presence of Kahawai. <i>(1947,Native Animals of NZ,80</i>)

>  <b>RiCHARDS</b> <i>Sterna ... frontalis [? striata] </i>... it pursues schools of small fish close to the coasts of New Zealand, Tasmania and Australia, and as the kahawai fish also feed on the same class of food, the tern is a useful indicator to fishermen of their presence. Hence its name of kahawai bird. Kahawai Bird. Sea Swallow, Tom Noddy. TARA. <i>(1952,Chatham islands,82</i>)

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> WHiTE-FRONTED TERN <i>Sterna striata Other names</i>: Sea-swallow, Kahawai Bird, Tara, Blackcap, Grenadier, Swallowtail, Noddy (Chathams), Tikkitak (Stewart is.) <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,164</i>)

>  <b>SALE</b> They derive one of their popular names from the forked tail, pointed wings and agile flight which make them so like the welcome swallows of the land; but these white-fronted terns are better known by another title here. They are the kahawai birds, of the wheeling, diving flocks, which off-shore tell the trolling fisherman where to trail his line. <i>(1982,Four Seasons,105</i>)

>  <i>White-fronted Tern Sterna striata </i>(GMELiN, 1789) ... OTHER NAMES: <i>Tara, sea-swallow, kahawai bird, black cap, grenadier, swallowtail, noddy. </i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,232</i>)

>  [Caption] Kahawai birds on the nesting ground, Tiri Tiri, Auckland. <i>(Sep 14, 1938,Auckland Weekly News,51</i>)

>  Skimming the water were thousands of kahawai birds. <i>(Feb 22, 1934,Feilding Star,4</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

 <i>n.</i> (See [kahawai](k-kea.md#kahawai))

### kahika
 
 <i>n.</i> a tree

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> Ka |ika; Name of a certain tree. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,156</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> Many other trees abounded in Pamaki [a's-acute], such as the kaikatea [a2-acute], towai, rewa-rewa, totara [a's-acute], puriri, rata [a's-acute], kahika [a2-acute], tipow, tanikaha [a2-acute], rimu, and various kinds of akkas, all of which will be found described in the Appendix (Note 7). <i>ibid.</i> 2/397 The <i>Kahika</i> is a useful wood, but little known from a similar cause [as Kawaka : i.e. not much sought after for joinery, etc.]. <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/109</i>)

>  <b>SHORTLAND</b> KAHiKA, Syn. kahikatea <i>(dacrydium excelsum vel taxifolium)</i>,;a lofty tree of the order Taxaciae, acquiring sometimes a height of 200 feet. <i>(1851,Southern Districts of NZ,307</i>)

>  <b>BEATTiE</b> Where the Taieri River debouches from the hills bore the name Maka-kahikatoa, `maka' meaning a fish-hook and `kahikatoa' being a big kind of manuka. Do not confuse this name with [39] `kahikatea,' which is the North island name for the white pine, a tall tree which the southerners call `kahika.' <i>(1944,Maori Place-Names of Otago,38</i>)

### kahikatea
 
 <i>adj.</i> [attrib. <i>Dacrycarpus dacrydioides</i>]

>  We learn, for example, that a single kahikatea tree can produce 800 kg of fruit in a season, and that Maori used to climb 30 metres up the arrow-straight trunks to harvest the crop. <i>(Jul 1998,NZ Geographic,No. 39/18</i>)

>  <b>BiDWiLL</b> Scattered at intervals are small groves of tall Kaikatea trees; .. <i>(1841,Rambles in NZ,121,1952 ed.</i>)

>  <b>ANGAS</b> [These still and sombre dells] are shaded eternally from the sun by a lofty canopy of<i> kaikatia</i> foliage overhead, .. <i>(1847,Savage Life,2/14</i>)

>  <b>SHORTLAND</b> This schooner [was] ... sheathed with `kahikatea' planking, ... <i>(1851,Southern Districts of NZ,151</i>)

>  <b>EARP</b> The <i>kaikatea</i> berry is another favourite fruit with the natives; ... <i>(1853,New Zealand (various authors/titles),130</i>)

>  European readers will not be the wiser when i say it was a Kahikatia swamp - New Zealanders, who have been up to their waist in it, with their neck suspended in supplejack, and their fingers sliced by cutting grass, will appreciate our work ..<i>ibid.</i> 86 Gullies also are invariably filled with forest and swamp - the Kahikatia wood and swamp. predominating. <i>(1864,Von Tempsky Memoranda,36</i>)

>  <b>HAY</b> Kahikatea bush often occupies marshy ground, and ... has a somewhat bare and spectral aspect. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/189</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> For many miles along the low banks of the Manawatu and other tidal rivers in the North island there are what the settlers term `kahikatea swamps', extending often considerable distances inland. <i>(1888,Birds of New Zealand,2/259</i>)

>  4 Kahikatea (white pine) slab. <i>(1890,PWD Catalogue of Timbers,16</i>)



 




 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> an indigenous pine characterised by tall form and swampy habitat. [<i>Dacrycarpus dacrydioides, </i> aka white pine, kaik, kike, kahika, koroi ...]

>  <b>REES</b> The berry of the kahikatea (poacarpus excelsus), stimulating and diuretic. <i>(1851,GB Parliamentary Papers (irish Univ. series),1779/29</i>)

>  Fossil evidence shows kahikatea to have been growing on Earth in the time of the dinosaurs, making it as old as any of the living fossil trees such as Gingko or dawn redwood. <i>(Oct 2000,Growing Today,68</i>)

>  kahikatea / rimu / red pine. The kahikatea, or white pine, as the sawyers call it, is an inferior New Zealand timber, and if for a short time exposed to the weather, easily rots... The rimu is somewhat better. it is this tree which the sawyers call the red pine. <i>(1841,NZ Journal,2(29) : 51</i>)

>  The Wellington Mail inserts a large photograph of a group of kahikatea trees and labels it "kikitea" trees. <i>(Sep 24, 1904,NZ Observer,16</i>)

>  [Manunui Sawmill] was owned by Ellis and Burnand and milled native timbers and kahikatea (white pine) which was made into butter boxes. <i>(2008,NZ Memories,74/17</i>)

>  Now that all the kahikatea has been burnt, butter folk want to import deal from abroad. <i>(Jul 12, 1913,NZ Observer,7</i>)

>  <b>NiCHOLAS</b> The trees which the natives chiefly make subservient to their purposes, are [besides the pines cowrie, totarra, towha and zarida mentioned above] ... the henow, from which they extract a black dye, the towha, a tree resembling the sycamore, the <i>river river</i>, the grain of whose wood is similar to that of the beech, a species of cork-tree, called by these people vow, a large tree named eckoha, and another named kycata, a tall and beautiful tree, ... <i>(1817,Sea Anglers' Guide,2/245</i>)

>  <b>McCRAE</b> kyka kara ... Here also i saw a tree of the kind called Kyka Kara [=??: ed. does not comment: cf 16] which was at least 150 feet in height ... in appearance it was not unlike an orange tree.[kaikaro (Williams 88) is Pittosporum crassifolium = Karo, or P. tenuifolium: ?=Kaikawaka, NZ cedar; ?=Kaikomako Pennantia corymbosa waxy white fragrant flowers but 100 ft???; kahikahika M scandens rata vine???; ?kawaka or kaikawaka] [16] ... the timber which was of a different kind to that which we had seen at Whia Caddie [and which was not what they were looking for]. it ... was a species of Cypress bearing a small berry ... it is called by the natives Kaikatoria [ed. fn. Kahikatea ...], and grew in considerable quantities on both sides of the river; ... <i>(1820,Journal,14</i>)

>  <b>CRUiSE</b> ... the one [tree fit for masts] is called by the natives Kaikaterre, the other <i>Cowry</i> or Cowdy. <i>ibid.</i> Mar 1 39 They saw a profusion of kaikaterre spars, of the largest dimensions in use for naval purposes ... <i>(Feb 1820,Journal,20,1957 ed.</i>)

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> Kai ka t|ea; Name of a certain tree. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,156</i>)

>  On the Dromedary's return from Shokee Hangha to the Bay of islands, Captain Skinner purposed to take in some spars of the kikatea <i>(kahikatea)</i>(dagger sign) and afterwards to examine the River Thames or some other part of New Zealand for timber, as he was anxious if possible to get kowree <i>(kauri)</i> spars, as that wood was deemed of a much superior quality to the kikatea. <i>ibid.</i> 250 ... Captain Skinner purposed to take in some spars of the kikatea<i> (kahikatea)</i> ... he was anxious if possible to get kowree <i>(kauri)</i> spars, as that wood was deemed of a much superior quality to the kikatea. <i>(May 1820,Letters & Journals of Samuel Marsden,87,1932 ed.</i>)

>  <b>WiLLiAMS</b> The timber reported to be had, being <i>Kaikatea. </i> <i>(Nov 1, 1828,Early Journals of Henry Williams, The,149,1961 ed.</i>)

>  <b>McNAB</b> The principal trees are the ronkaterra and the cowry, which grow to an immense height without a branch, and are of such magnitude as to be fit for the masts of large ships. The ronkaterra is found in marshy grounds and on the banks of rivers; it appears to be an ever-green, and bears a red berry. <i>(1908,Historical Records,1/691</i>) [NOTES: 1829 Mar 2 Atkins's Narrative [?ronkaterra = kahikatea] in 1908 McNab Historical Records 1/691]

>  <b>CRAiK</b> ... there are two kinds of trees known in New Zealand [for masts] ...; the one ... is called by the natives <i>kaikaterre</i>, the other <i>cowry</i> or <i>cowdy. </i> <i>(1830,New Zealanders, The,176</i>)

>  <b>MARKHAM</b> Kicaitere [<i>kahikatea</i>] is another Wood but not anything wonderful and i have not seen it put to any use. <i>(1834,New Zealand (various authors/titles),33,1963 ed.</i>)

>  <b>YATE</b> The cocoa-nut tree, which abounds in the Friendly islands, appears, in the distance, much like the mangroves, and the kahikatea, or white fir, of New Zealand; ... <i>ibid</i> 39 Kahikatea (<i>Treniperus</i>, or <i>Dacrydium excelsum</i>) - This tree only flourishes in low, swampy, or alluvial soils; ... </i>ibid</i> 40 Kahikatea commonly designated white pine. <i>(1835,New Zealand (various authors/titles),9</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> Many other trees abounded in Pamaki [a's-acute], such as the kaikatea [a2-acute], towai, rewa-rewa, totara [a's-acute], puriri, rata [a's-acute], kahika [a2-acute], tipow, tanikaha [a2-acute], rimu, and various kinds of akkas, all of which will be found described in the Appendix (Note 7). <i>ibid.</i> 2/390 <i>The Kahikatea</i> [a2,e-acute] (<i>treniperus Novae Zelandicae</i>) g. coniferae, is very similar in outward appearance to the Kouri, ... The name by which it is distinguished, is that of white pine; ... <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/109</i>)

>  <b>HODGSKiN</b> The kahikatera may be ranked next to the kourie for size and beauty, ... <i>(1841,Narrative of Eight Months Sojourn in NZ, A,26</i>)

>  <b>BiDWiLL</b> in the water-courses were Totaras and Kaikateras, as the natives said; but i think they were different trees from those so called by Europeans and the natives of the coasts. <i>ibid.</i> 105 The people brought large baskets full of the berries of the Kaikatora <i>(Dacrydium excelsum)</i> for sale. <i>(1841,Rambles in NZ,80,1952 ed.</i>)

>  Kaikaterra - kahikatea <i>(1840,NZ Journal,1/ll/138</i>)

>  Bidwill kiakatea trees <i>ibid.</i> 2/32/86 kahi - katea <i>ibid.</i> 2/36/152 ...kikatea and may. <i>(1841,NZ Journal,2/30/65</i>)

>  We scarcely took a step that did not delight us; the soil good, the banks of the [Waipa] river high, interspersed with woods of kahikatea. ... Here the banks of the river were covered with wild-ducks. <i>(1842,GBPP,1842 (No.569) 98</i>) [NOTES: >1841 Jan 4 Report of George Clarke, Protector of Aborigines in GBPP 1842 (No.569) 98]

>  White Pine, or Kahikatea <i>(Podocarpus dacrydioides)</i>. This is a straight, narrow, sometimes conical tree, growing on wet flats; wood of little value. <i>ibid.</i> 49 Kahikatea. Podocarpus dacrydioides, A. Rich. <i>(1869,TrNZi,1/iii/31</i>) [NOTES: (Buchanan Botany Otago)]

>  <b>BAiNES</b> Canoes are also dubbed out of the kauri, remu, kaikatia, &c. <i>(1874,Edward Crewe,82</i>)

>  <b>LASLETT</b> The kahikatea or kakaterra-tree <i>(1875,Timber and Trees,304</i>) [NOTES: [Morris]]

>  <b>HAY</b> There is the cabbage-tree palm, with bare shank and top-knot; the nikau palm, with weird and wondrous frondage; the lancewood, ...; the fern-tree, .... There is the twisting, squirming rata; the gaunt ... kahikatea; the golden kowhai; the dark velvet-covered rimu; the feathery red tawai; the perfumy mangiao; ... <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/187</i>)

>  [Native name] Kahikatea .. [Settlers' name] White-pine .. [Family] Coniferae <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,121</i>) [NOTES: 1886 T.Kirk in 1902 Settler's Handbook of NZ 121]

>  Kahikatea (white-pine) .. Podocarpus dacrydioides .. Timber white, compact, straight in grain, and of even texture; ... <i>(1890,PWD Catalogue of Timbers,10</i>)

>  The Committee recommends the disuse of the terms `red pine', `white pine', and `black pine', in favour of rimu, kahikatea and matai, in order to prevent their being confused with timbers having similar names in Europe. <i>(Jul 17, 1896,AJHR,H-24</i>) [NOTES: 1896 (July 17) interim Report No. 3 (i) of Timber Conference. A to J's, H. 24.]

>  <b>MORRiS</b> ~ Maori name for a New Zealand tree, <i>Podocarpus dacrydioides</i>, A. Rich., N.O. <i>Coniferae.</i> Also called <i> White- Pine </i> . See <i> Pine.</i> The settlers' pronunciation is often <i> Kackatea </i> . There is a Maori word Kahika, meaning ancient. <i>(1898,Austral English,226</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> ~ Maori name for a New Zealand tree, <i>Podocarpus dacrydioides</i>, A. Rich., N.O. <i>Coniferae.</i> Also called <i> White- Pine </i> . See <i> Pine.</i> The settlers' pronunciation is often <i> Kackatea </i> . There is a Maori word Kahika, meaning ancient. <i>(1855,Te ika a Maui,439</i>)

>  <b>LAiNG& BLACKWELL</b> <i> Podocarpus dacrydioides [(The Kahikatea </i> or <i> White Pine)].</i> Native name, Kahikatea. The following, probably fanciful, derivation has been given of this name: <i> tea, whitish </i> , from the colour of the wood, and <i> kahika, an ancient chief. </i> <i>(1907,Plants of NZ,70,1940, p. 74 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BAUGHAN</b> <i>Kahikatea (kah-hee-kah-tay-ah)</i> : The white pine. <i>(1908,Shingle-Short,188</i>)

>  <i>~ n </i> . the white pine of New Zealand. [Maori] <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,6</i>)

>  The chief indigenous trees of commercial value are: Kauri (<i> Agathis Australis </i> ), Kahikatea or white pine (<i> Podocarpus dacrydioides </i> ), rimu or red pine (<i> Dacrydium cupressinum </i> ), totara (<i> Podocarpus totara </i> and <i> Podocarpus Hallii </i> ), Miro (<i> Podocarpus ferrugineus </i> ), Kaikawaka or mountain cedar (<i> Libocedrus Bidwilli </i> ), yellow pine or silver pine (<i> Dacrydium Colensoi </i> and <i> D. intermedium </i> ), red and hard beech (<i> Nothofagus fasca </i> and <i> Nothofagus truncata </i> ), silver beech (<i> Nothofagus Menziesii </i> ), black beech (<i> Nothofagus Solandri </i> ), black maire (<i> Olea Cunninghamii </i> ), northern and southern rata (<i> Metrosideros robusta </i> and <i> Metrosideros lucida </i> ). <i>(1936,Agricultural Organization in NZ,578</i>)

>  <b>ANDERSEN</b> Kahikatea is the name now used almost to the exclusion of the once-common name white-pine; the abbreviated form <i>kahika</i>, is also used, <i>`tea' </i>meaning `white'. <i>(1942,Maori Place Names,279</i>)

>  <i> kahikatea </i> , a forest-tree (Podocarpus Dacrydioides), white-pine. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/143</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <i>kahikatea </i> : white pine. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,174</i>)

>  <i>(May 21, 1954,Listener (New Zealand Listener),22</i>) [NOTES: (Sundowner) [pron and ? Southland use]]

>  ~ A large New Zealand species of pine, sometimes called white pine. <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),228</i>)

>  <b>SHADBOLT</b> (The Woman's Story) i grew to awareness rejecting as alien our scrubby backblocks farm, the thick bush of tall kauri, rimu and kahikatea ..., the mangroved tidal creek, ... the cabbage trees in the playground ... <i>(1959,New Zealanders, The,10,1986 ed.</i>)

>  <b>KEENE</b> <i> kahikatea </i> : New Zealand white pine; grows to height of 80 ft. to 120 ft. <i>(1963,O Te Raki,191</i>)

>  <b>HiLLiARD</b> Sometimes Haki looks at the stumps of the kahikateas he felled for the first house. <i>(1960,Maori Girl,11</i>)

>  <i>~ n </i> . New Zealand tree, the white pine [Maori]. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),618</i>)

>  <i>kahikatea n </i> -s [Maori] : a New Zealand evergreen tree (<i> Podocarpus dacrydioides </i> ) valued for its light soft easily worked wood, its resin, and the sweet edible aril surrounding its seed - called also <i> white pine </i> <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third international,1230</i>)

>  <b>McLiNTOCK</b> Kahikatea, white pine .. .. kahikatea .. .. <i> Podocarpus dacrydioides </i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-712</i>)

>  <i>kahikatea ... n </i> . N.Z. tree, whitepine [Maori]. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),11</i>)

>  <i>kahikatea n </i> . coniferous NZ tree, the white pine. [M] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,409</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 270]]

### kahikatea pine
 
 <i>n.</i> [<i>Dacrycarpus dacrydioides</i>]

>  <b>WiLKES</b> The Kaurie and Kaikotia pines yield spars which for large ships are not surpassed by any in the world. <i>(1852,U.S. Exploring Expedition,1/317</i>) [NOTES: 1840 Mar in 1852 Wilkes U.S. Exploring Expedition 1/317]

>  <b>HOOKER</b> ... with the exception of the groves of the Kaikatea Pine <i>(Podocarpus dacrydioides)</i> on the swampy river banks ... there is little to arrest the botanist's first glance; ... <i>(1853,Flora NZ,i Flowering Plants xxix</i>)

### kahikatoa
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Leptospermum scoparium</i>, manuka, tea-tree, katoa. (See [manuka](m-mar.md#manuka))

>  <b>McCRAE</b> ...kaikatoria... <i>(1820,Journal,16</i>)

>  [OEDS] This tree..is probably a species of Leptospermum. it is found abundantly at New Zealand,..and is named Kaetatowa, or Manuka, by the natives. <i>(Feb 18, 1832,London Medical Gazette,750/1</i>) [NOTES: G. Bennett]

>  <b>WiLLiAMS</b> The low land did not exceed a quarter of a mile, when we came to rising ground, covered with Kaikatoa - very ordinary. <i>(Nov 9, 1833,Early Journals of Henry Williams, The,342,1961 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MARKHAM</b> Kicaitore [<i>kahikatoa</i>] is Shrub with a strong Aromatic smell, a small leaf with a hard berry. <i>ibid.</i> 34 [attrib or aj] He used Gum tree from Sydney for Keels, Coudy planks, Hoc-car [aka] Timber and Kicaitore Knees, sometimes Pouriedie Keels. <i>(1834,New Zealand (various authors/titles),33,1963 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MARKHAM</b> ...kicatore ... tea Tree Brush. <i>(1834,Journal,14</i>)

>  <b>McDONNELL</b> koi katoa' [sic] <i>(1834,Extracts From a Journal</i>)

>  <b>YATE</b> The shell is covered with high fern, and a species of tree named Kahikatoa (<i>Leptospermum scoparium</i>), [6] bearing a white blossom and a hard round berry. <i>ibid.</i> 48 Kahikatoa (<i>Leptospermum scoparium</i>) - A tree of stunted growth, ... producing a hard red wood. <i>(1835,New Zealand (various authors/titles),5</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> The polypetalous <i>kahikatoa</i> [a2-acute], covered with sweet odorous flowers throughout the year, of many tints, cover the hill sides, ... <i>ibid.</i>1/105 The rourou, or plain, was covered with the roi, or fern, and the ever-flowering kaikatoa [a2-acute], which emitted a fragrant odour. <i>ibid.</i> 2/394 The <i>Kaikatoa</i>, [a2,a3-acute] (<i>Philadelphus Australis</i>,) a polyanthus, called the tea plant from its leaves possessing the same myrtiform character. <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/68</i>)

>  <b>CAMPBELL</b> ...hills covered with fern, `kikatore', and other small shrubs. <i>(1840,Present State of NZ,6</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> in the bored lobes of the ears, some white down from the breast of sea-fowl was hung pendent, and the face rubricated with a profuse quantity of red ochre (kokowai.) <i>A garland of white and pink flowers of the petalous kaikatoa</i>[o-acute], encircled the head. <i>(1840,Manners & Customs of the NZers,1/65</i>)

>  <i>(1843,Manners & Customs of the NZers</i>)

>  <i>(1843,Manners & Customs of the NZers</i>)

>  <b>DiEFFENBACH</b> At the summit of the hills we find but little wood; and the manuka [fn Leptospermum scoparium.] and kahikatoa [fn Leptospermum ericoides.], the esculent fern and euphorbium [fn Euphorbium glaucum.], and the epacris parviflora, form the chief part of the vegetation. <i>ibid.</i> 1/149-150 We stopped for the night on a low island in the Waiwakaio, called Waiwiti, grown over with kahikatoa (Leptospermum), intermixed with a junceous plant, the Hamelinia veratroides of Achille Richard (Astelia Banksii), the seeds of which form the food of the kiwi and weka (Apterix Australis and Ral-[150]lus Australis). <i>ibid.</i> /365 Kahi katoa - (<i>Leptospermum</i>) <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,1/27</i>)

>  <b>WiLLiAMS</b> The Kahikatoa, of this there are several kinds the dwarf which never reaches the size of a tree, the Manuka or fine leaf and the gigantick [sic] which grows into ordinary Size trees. <i>(1844,NZ Journal,106,1956 ed.</i>)

>  <b>COLENSO</b> [See quot at <i>Tanekaha</i>] <i>(1844,Excursion in the Northern island of NZ</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFiELD</b> 13. Kahikatoa, or Manuka, (<i>Leptospermum Scoparium.</i>) A tree of stunted growth, flourishing in clayey barren soils, and producing a hard red wood. <i>(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,143</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Leptospermum <i>scoparium</i>, Forst. ... Nat. name, `Kahi katoa' and `Manuka'. <i>(1853,Flora NZ,i Flowering Plants 69-70</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Kahikatoa, <i>Col[enso]. Leptospermum scoparium. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),765</i>)

>  MYRTLE FAMiLY. Cook's Tea tree, Scrub Manuka, or Kahikatoa (<i>Leptospermum scoparium</i>). <i>ibid.</i> i 1/iii/41 (Buchanan Botany Otago) Kahikatoa. Leptospermum scoparium, Forst. <i>(1869,TrNZi,1/iii/32</i>) [NOTES: (Buchanan Botany Otago)]

>  <b>HAY</b> The Manuka or Manukau (<i>Leptospernum scoparium et ericoides</i>) is the `ti-tree' of the settlers. in one condition it is low shrubbery, not unlike heather, called then Rawiri by the Maori. ... There are red and white varieties. The Maori particularize it as Kahikatoa, when in tree condition. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/195</i>)

>  <b>HAY</b> The Manuka or Manukau (<i>Leptospernum scoparium et ericoides</i>) is the `ti-tree' of the settlers. in one condition it is low shrubbery, not unlike heather, called then Rawiri by the Maori. ... There are red and white varieties. The Maori particularize it as Kahikatoa, when in tree condition. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/195</i>)

>  <b>HECTOR</b> <i>Leptospermum scoparium</i>, Forst. Kahikatoa, Tea-tree of Cook. --it is ornamental, and useful for fuel and fencing; generally a small shrub. <i>(1883,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),126</i>)

>  [Native name] Manuka (kahikatoa) .. [Settlers' name] Manuka .. [Family] Myrtaceae <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,122</i>) [NOTES: 1886 T.Kirk in 1902 Settler's Handbook of NZ 122]

>  <b>FEATON</b> LEPTOSPERMUM ERiCOiDES. (<i>A.Rich</i>.) ... The `KAHiKATOA'. ... it attains a height of forty to fifty feet. <i>ibid.</i> 177 Kahikitoa [sic] [Maori name] .. Tea-tree [Settlers' name] .. Leptospermum scoparium .. Shrub <i>(1889,Art Album NZ Flora,157</i>)

>  <b>MORRiS</b> ~ [kahi' katoa] Maori name for New Zealand shrub, but no longer used by the settlers. -- <i>(1898,Austral English,126</i>)

>  <b>LAiNG & BLACKWELL</b> <i>Leptospermum scoparium (The Manuka)</i>... Maori names <i>Manuka, Kahikatoa.</i> Colonists' [English] name, Tea-Tree. <i>(1907,Plants of NZ,272,1940 p. 286 ed.</i>)

>  <b>ANDERSEN</b> in the name kahikatoa the second `a' is long, seeming to call for the division, kaki-katoa- if any division at all is called for. <i>(1942,Maori Place Names,279</i>)

>  <b>BEATTiE</b> When in a hurry, or feeling orthographically tired, he [the southerner] calls the big manuka `kaikatoa.' A southern Maori once said to me:- `We did not pronounce the word manuka like some northerners who throw the accent forward and say `man-u-ka,' nor like the bushmen, who say `m'nooka,' but about halfway between these two pronouncing each part clearly as mah-nook-a.' Unless my hearing deceives me this throws the emphasis on the central syllable while leaving the first syllable clearly pronounced. it is a remarkable fact that while the manuka is so common its name is so seldom used in the composition of place-names. <i>(1944,Maori Place-Names of Otago,39</i>)

>  <i>kahika[a]toa, see </i>manuka in List 1. Whilst much used in place-names, that will hardly justify inclusion in the Dictionary. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/151</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  The Kahikatoa and the Kohekohe are both fine grained red woods; the former hard with a fragrant perfume, the latter more easily worked, like cedar, so as to adapt it for common household furniture. <i>(Jun 1840,NZ Journal,1(1) : 139</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>POLACK</b> The ladies performed their utmost ... wielding spears made of the kaikatoa-tree, and paddles of the same popular wood. ibid 1/103 ... the fallen leaves of the kaikatoa-tree [a2-acute], which had been brushed off in passing ... <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/82</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> ??black oak

>  Q. Are there many harbours there [on the west side of New Zealand]? A. Several ... All covered with wood Q. Of what sort? A. Of white and red pine and a wood called `black oak' Q. is it of the species called `kaikatua'? A. it is. Q. Did you see any of the species called `cowrie'? A. i did not. <i>(1908,Historical Records,1/559</i>) [NOTES: 1821 May Evidence of McDonald before Commissioner Bigge in 1908 McNab Historical Records 1/559]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>POWER</b> The berries of the Karaka and Kahikatoa trees are articles of food, and though, in an extensive tract, these trees may not average more than two or three to an acre, yet they give value to the whole forest. <i>(1849,Sketches in NZ,134</i>)

### Kahili ginger
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Kahili ginger, for example, is reluctant to grow in the South island because of the frosts, yet it is nationally banned. <i>(Feb 14, 2004,Dominion Post,E10</i>)

>  Amber also visited the Buller area where she was particularly impressed with the work of the local Weedbusters group which is making good progress in their assault in kahili ginger. <i>(Jul 2004,Conservation Action,33</i>)

>  Sadly, Kahili ginger has misbehaved so outrageously in New Zealand that it has been banned, but there are several cultivated varieties which can still be grown. <i>(Feb 21, 2004,Dominion Post,E10</i>)

>  Among the wicked ones are: Kahili ginger, gone now from all but secret hiding places; Passiflora mollissima, the delicious banana passionfruit; and Chilean flame flower, Tropaeolum speciosum, with its hanging masses of brilliant red, nasturtium-like flowers. <i>(Jun 5, 2004,Dominion,E16</i>)

### kahore
 
 <i>n.</i> no

### Kahoroa ash
 
 <i>n.</i> geological classification

>  The upper part of the section comprises Taupo Pumice, Kaharoa Ash and Rotomahana Mud intercalated with beds of peat. <i>(May 1975,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,39/36</i>)

>  He estimates 30,000 pitswere dug in the Rangitaiki Valley (Bay of Plenty) in the period between the Kaharoa and Tarawera Ashes (c. 1300 - 1886). <i>(Oct 1982,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,58/52</i>)

>  Long after this investigation, a 1/4 in. seam of Kaharoa Ash was found lying on the grey mud. <i>(Feb 1964,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,16/17</i>)

### kahotea
 
 <i>n.</i> a greenstone

>  <b>FiRTH</b> Among the Maori, considerations of aesthetic interest helped quite materially ... to decide the relative worth of objects made of the <i>pounamu, </i> the nephrite so much prized for pendants, <i>mere</i>, and adzes. Most prized was the <i>kahurangi</i> stone, bright green, translucent, hard and clear; the<i> inanga</i> variety perhaps comes next, with its white milky-veined appearance, [389] followed by the <i>kawakawa</i>, dark olive-green, dull and opaque. ... <i>Tangiwai</i> (`tear drop'), <i>bowenite</i>, a softer stone of a clear pale green, with the appearance of drops of water in the texture, was less esteemed, though more beautiful in the European eyes, whilst <i>kahotea</i>, green, spotted or streaked with black, was an inferior type, and was used for small chisels, and for objects of subordinate interest. <i>(1929,Primitive Economics of NZ Maori,388</i>)

### kahu
 
 <i>n.</i> a large harrier hawk of Australasia and the South Pacific [Maori kahu[a-macron]]. harrier (hawk), falcon, New Zealand.

>  The visitor was a well developed specimen of the, often much unloved, kahu. <i>(Feb 1947,Forest & Bird,10</i>)

>  Twenty to 30 birds feeding on clover lift off as a kahu (Australasian harrier) contour-flies over the miniature savannah stretching westwards from the ranger's homestead. <i>(Jan 2002,NZ Geographic,No. 55/22</i>)

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> K|ahu, s. Name of a certain bird. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,155</i>)

>  <b>YATE</b> <i>Kahu </i> - A large and powerful bird, of the hawk species. <i>(1835,New Zealand (various authors/titles),60</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> The <i> kahu </i>[a-acute] is of the hawk species; and, like the tikaka [a's-acute], are very destructive to the farm-yard; ... <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/301</i>)

>  <b>DiEFFENBACH</b> 1. <i> Falco harpe.</i> Forst. ... Kahu of natives? <i> Yate, Polack, Dieffenbach </i>. <i>ibid.</i> 2/365 Kahu - name of the hawk. <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/186</i>) [NOTES: 1842 Gray's Fauna in 1843 Dieffenbach Travels in NZ 2/186]

>  <b>WiLLiAMS</b> early (erroneous) spg or mistranscription <i> katiu </i> The katiu or common hawk is to[o] well known to need any description, ... <i>(1844,NZ Journal,113,1956 ed.</i>)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> Of the <i> Falcon </i> family, there are two species in New Zealand; one, called Kahu, is about the size of a Pigeon, and the other, named Karewarewa, is a sparrow-hawk: <i>(1859,Story of New Zealand, The,1/23</i>)

>  Owing to the misapplication of the native names, Kahu and karearea, in Mr. Gray's first list [in Dieffenbach], writers in this country have inevitably fallen into the error of considering our large brown hawk the <i> Falco harpe </i> and our `Sparrow-hawk' the <i> Falco brunnea </i>, of that author. <i>(1869,TrNZi,1/iii/6</i>) [NOTES: (Buller Ornithology)]

>  CiRCUS ASSiMiLiS, Jard. Kahu. Harrier. One of the commonest large birds met with on `the plains'. <i>(1870,TrNZi,2/52</i>) [NOTES: (Potts Birds)]

>  <b>HUTTON</b> 2. CiRCUS ASSiMiLiS. Jard. Harrier. Kahu <i> C. Gouldi,</i> Bp. <i>(1871,Birds of New Zealand,2</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> CiRCUS GOULDi. (GOULD'S HARRiER.) ... <i> Native names </i>. Kahu and Manu tahae; ... <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,11,{1888 2nd edn 1/206} ed.</i>)

>  <b>MEREDiTH</b> For instance, when comparing the two governors, Sir George Grey and Sir George Bowen, they [the Maori MPs] compared the former to a <i> kahu </i> (hawk) which flies overhead - one can always tell what it is doing; while they likened Sir George Bowen to a <i> kiori </i> (rat) which burrows underground - one never knows where it is coming up. <i>(1875,Adventuring in Maoriland in the Seventies,56,1935 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HAY</b> The Kahu (<i> Circus Gouldi </i>) is chief among several of the hawk tribe. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/220</i>)

>  <i> ka[a]hu </i>, a bird (Circus gouldi), hawk, harrier. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/151</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <i> kahu </i>: harrier. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,174</i>)

>  <b>POWELL</b> HARRiER (Circus approximans), Kahu of the Maoris, is the common bird of prey of the countryside .... it is commonly referred to as the hawk, but our true hawk or falcon is another species, <i> Falco novaeseelandiae ...</i> <i>(1947,Native Animals of NZ,82</i>)

>  <b>RiCHARDS</b> <i> Circus approximans Gouldii </i> ... Harrier Hawk. KAHU. <i>(1952,Chatham islands,77</i>)

>  <i> kahu n </i>-s [Maori] : a common harrier (<i> Circus approximans </i>) represented by several distinct races in Australasia and the East indies <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third international,1230</i>)

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> HARRiER <i> Circus approximans Other names </i>: Hawk, Swamp Harrier, Gould's Harrier, Kahu. <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,98</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> ...they could watch a hawk wheeling in the sky. Kahu the Harrier, <i> Circus Approximans </i>, his wife, who had Zoology in her degree, had told him. <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,20</i>)

>  <i>119.a. Circus approximans gouldi </i> Bonaparte, 1850 <i> Australasian Harrier (Kahu)</i> Australia, Tasmania, New Guinea and New Zealand ... <i>(1970,Annotated Checklist of Birds of NZ,39,1980 ed.</i>)

>  <i> Australasian Harrier Circus approximans </i> PEALE, 1848 ... OTHER NAMES: <i> Kahu, swamp harrier, marsh harrier.</i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,152</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> in adding the native names an unfortunate mistake occurred \{in Gray's `List of Birds of New Zealand' in 1843 Dieffenbach\}; for <i> Falco harpe </i> was stated to be the bird known to the inhabitants as `Kahu' and `Kahu-papango', whereas these are in reality the native appellations for the Harrier, ... omitted from the list. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,2</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> kingfish

>  <b>AYLiNG</b> <i>KiNGFiSH</i> (Yellowtail, kahu)<i> Seriola grandis </i> <i>(1982,Collins Guide,217,1984 ed.</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a New Zealand-bred cultivar of Timothy grass (Phleum pratense). [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  All the seed is certified, the Raunu is mother seed and the Huia is first generation. Here is the mixture he uses per acre: Ryegrass (Raunu) 4 lb .. Timothy (Kahu) 2lb. <i>(Oct 1974,NZ Journal of Agriculture,33</i>)

>  Kahu - Useful for spring-summer growth in mixed pastures in cool regions, particularly in moist, fertile sites. <i>(1995,Grasslands Range of Forage and Conservation Plants,25</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>FORSTER</b> Over this mat which the[y] call <i>Taigook[a-macron, o2-grave]</i> they wear in winter another, which is of loose texture & is on the outside quite shaggy, which they tie round their Necks & let it hang round the body, it is called (<i>Kagheeae</i>)[a1-grave]. [[ed. fn] Possibly one of <i>kahu,</i> cloak; <i>kaku</i>, a rough garment, a cloak; <i>kohiku,</i> garment, cloak or <i>koka</i>, rough cape.] <i>(Jun 8, 1773,Resolution Journal of J.R. Forster, The,2/298</i>)

### kahu huruhuru
 
 <i>n.</i> a feather cloak [kahu = area; cloak; layer. huruhuru = bristles; feather; fleece; fur; furry; hair; wool. http://translator.kedri.info/index.html]

>  Take it from me, wearing a kahu huruhuru (feather cloak) makes one feel "like a million dollars". <i>(Oct 24, 1987,Listener (New Zealand Listener),77</i>)

>  A framed Maori feather cloak (kahu huruhuru) was sold recently at Watson's Specialist Auctions in Christchurch for $45,000. <i>(Aug 14, 2004,Dominion Post,E3</i>)

### kahui ariki
 
 <i>n.</i> [ORIGIN: Maori, kahui = flock (P.M. Ryan Dict. Modern Maori (1995) 69)]

>  "He had the ability to awhi [care for] the kahui ariki of Tuwharetoa and to work closely with Waikato." Mr Moana said. "He was a great man for Kingitanga." <i>(Dec 14, 2005,NZ Herald,A15</i>)

### kahuna
 
 <i>n.</i> [Has recently crept into NZ use]

>  This being the time of the year for the Qantas Awards - the big kahuna of journalism prizes in New Zealand - the reason why journalism feels moved to applaud itself in public calls for closer examination. <i>(Jun 8, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),33</i>)

>  i want the big Bonus Bonds kahuna of .. $300,000. <i>(Sep 23, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),94</i>)

>  Kate Mitchell raises an fascinating point about the phrase "the big kahuna"... Where it gets interesting, though, is finding references to similar words beginning with t- instead of k-, the sort of consonant transliteration not uncommon in Polynesian languages... Throughout the eastern Pacific the words tahunga and tahuna crop up for people with special powers such as priests and sorcerers. it doesn't take much imagination to see a relationship with our own Maori tohunga, the word for a priest or specially skilled person. <i>(Jul 27, 2002,Dominion Post,F8</i>)

### kahurangi
 
 <i>n.</i> a rare translucent grade of greenstone

>  <b>FiRTH</b> Among the Maori, considerations of aesthetic interest helped quite materially ... to decide the relative worth of objects made of the <i>pounamu, </i> the nephrite so much prized for pendants, <i>mere</i>, and adzes. Most prized was the <i>kahurangi</i> stone, bright green, translucent, hard and clear; the<i> inanga</i> variety perhaps comes next, with its white milky-veined appearance, [389] followed by the <i>kawakawa</i>, dark olive-green, dull and opaque. ... <i>Tangiwai</i> (`tear drop'), <i>bowenite</i>, a softer stone of a clear pale green, with the appearance of drops of water in the texture, was less esteemed, though more beautiful in the European eyes, whilst <i>kahotea</i>, green, spotted or streaked with black, was an inferior type, and was used for small chisels, and for objects of subordinate interest. <i>(1929,Primitive Economics of NZ Maori,388</i>)

>  Unlike gold, pounamu comes in a great range of colours and patterns... Kahurangi was highly translucent, flawless and rare, inanga pearly white to grey-green, kawakawa the dark-green stone that is commonly used in today's jewellery. <i>(Mar 2003,NZ Geographic,62/8</i>)

>  Kahurangi is the rarest variety of pounamu. it is highly translucent and often comes in vivid shades of green. Kahurangi is particularly esteemed by Maori. in the past, it was the preferred variety of stone for the blades of toki poutangata (ceremonial adzes) owned by rangatira, (chiefs). <i>(Oct 23, 2009,Press, The,4</i>)

### kahuriruhi
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <i>80. Phalacrocorax varius varius (Gmelin, 1789) Pied Shag (Kahuriruhi) </i> <i>(1970,Annotated Checklist of Birds of NZ,32,1980 ed.</i>)

### kai
 
 <i>n.</i> food, a meal (See [also kaikai](k-kea.md#kaikai))

>  The beauty of this model is that it's not kai in the sky when you die. it is here and now... <i>(Dec 15, 2001,Dominion,12</i>)

>  ...we need some of 'em to run the local dairy and the takeaway-kai shop, if you get my meaning. <i>(Nov 26, 2002,Dominion Post,B4</i>)

>  [Caption] italian kai guy - Corrado Ronchetti with a chicken breast and mushroom sauce dish. <i>(Jul 3, 2002,Evening Post,25</i>)

>  <b>BAKER</b> 'Where is the kai? Where are the waka?... "Kaiwaka" is the sound of somebody with a chest complaint clearing their throat.' <i>(2000,Kokopu Dreams,74</i>)

>  Pitcairn is also with me working for his kai. <i>(1960,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,i/244</i>) [NOTES: 1856 quote]

>  MacRoo the 'commissariat' was, and cooked the kai by day. <i>(1904,NZ Observer,Xmas issue : 11</i>)

>  We'd all get a big hug from her, and she would cook kai in her big black pots on the coal-range... <i>(2008,Mana,84/28</i>)

>  <b>FORSTER</b> A Comparative Table of the Various Languages in the isles of the South-Sea ... [English] <i> To eat </i> ... [New-Zeeland] e kai [a-macron grave] <i>(1778,Observations Made During a Voyage,284</i>)

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> Kai,<i> s. a. v. n. - s. </i> Victuals, support, &c.<i> a.</i> Eatable; <i>v.n</i>. Eating. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,156</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFiELD</b> My boys set off ... for a native settlement, where they could get some kai. <i>(Mar 15, 1840,Diary,1839 ed.</i>)

>  ... a still better [excuse for not proceeding] might have been found in the pigs and abundance of other Kaikai steaming in the Hangis. ... Our Canoe took the lead the boys consoling each other by remarking that there was lots of Kai for te Martinga. <i>(Apr 12, 1842,Journal of Ensign Best,346,1966 ed.</i>)

>  ...cooking the `kai' <i>(1846,NZ Journal,3/171/183</i>)

>  <b>ANGAS</b> ... on the opposite bank we rested half an hour, and partook of a mouthful of <i> kai </i> (food). <i>(1847,Savage Life,2/10</i>)

>  The Maoris said that ... they often cook their potatoes and other kai, quite easily in it. <i>(1870,TrNZi,2/172</i>)

>  Kai.i (ka.i.e.;<i> colloq.</i> ka.&ibreve. and <i> k&imacr </i> .), n. [Maori.] Food. <i> [Colloq., New Zealand & Queensland]. </i> <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third international,2025</i>)

>  <b>MORRiS</b> <i> Kai </i> Maori word for <i> food </i> ; used also in the South Sea islands. <i> Kai-kai </i> is an English adaptation for feasting. <i>(1898,Austral English,226</i>)

>  <b>SAVAGE</b> <i>(1807,Some Account of NZ,75</i>)

>  The evening kai was finished but the hapu sat on. <i>(Sep 1904,NZ illustrated Magazine,449</i>)

>  <b>SCOTT</b> The spree was at the Pahi pub/ ... Te kai, kapai, waipiro plenty,/ Advice to you who bottle empty/ When full of ink, to walk unable,/ Don't hesitate but try the stable. <i>(1987,Seven Lives on Salt River,55</i>) [NOTES: 1912 poem in 1987 Scott Seven Lives on Salt River 55]

>  [frontispiece of Maori soldier] <i> Private Puripeef </i> `Plenty Kai up there!' <i>(1917,New Zealand At the Front</i>)

>  <i>kai n.</i> food.-kai'-kai n. feasting. [Maori] <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,6</i>)

>  <b>LANCASTER</b> He looked very ruddy and English among the fat Maori women ... plaiting little green-flax baskets for kai; ... <i>(1938,Promenade,115</i>)

>  <i>kai,</i> food; to eat: kainga, the place where food is eaten; kaika in South island, often corrupted to kaik. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/149</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <i> kai </i> : food. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,174</i>)

>  kai The Maori word for food, in general use in the Pacific islands. <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),228</i>)

>  <b>HiLLiARD</b> They only want you there to cook their kai and wash their clothes. <i>(1960,Maori Girl,143</i>)

>  <b>MaCALLUM</b> Eat the good kai and drink the good beer. <i>(1963,Half-Gallon Jar,35</i>)

>  <i> kai n.</i> New Zealand South Sea islands and New Guinea word for food. Sometimes duplicated. kai-kai. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),618</i>)

>  <i> kai ... n.</i> N.Z. South Sea is. and New Guinea word for food. Sometimes duplicated, <i> kai-kai. </i> <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),11</i>)

>  These are the holy objects of the town marae. The mattresses, the chairs, the sofa, the table, the kai, the cigarettes, have all been shared. <i>(1972,islands,1/27</i>) [NOTES: Baxter (Elegy for Boyle Crescent)]

>  <b>FERNANDEZ</b> The girls made quips at the two kuias who were a little late with the kai. <i>(1973,Tussock Fever,5</i>)

>  <b>FiNLAYSON</b> kai: food; earn their kai, earn a living. <i>(1973,Brown Man's Burden,140</i>)

>  <b>BAXTER</b> kai <i> food </i> <i>(1982,Selected Poems,188</i>)

>  <b>HULME</b> Kai = food <i>(1983,Bone People, The,469, 389</i>)

>  <b>HOWE</b> Kai - `food'. This generally refers to the food you eat, though in the past certain tribal areas were famous for particular foods (eg, in the Whangaparoa, moki fish, in the Tuhoe region, the kereru or wood pigeon). When these were served guests knew they were being honoured. The hosts' `mana' thus increased <i>(1985,Towards Taha Maori,19</i>)

>  Paul Temm feels essentially unchanged by his experience on the tribunal, sitting at marae around the country, listening to the elders vent their frustrations in Maori, hongi-ing with both tangata whenua and manuhiri (visitors), chatting over tea and kai. <i>(Jun 21, 1986,Evening Post</i>)

>  A letter from Sam Pikimaui ... He chides me for using in a recent column the Australian slang word for food - tucker. `if you had wanted to demonstrate that you are indeed one of the people,' writes Mr Pikimaui, `Could you not have selected the Maori word kai?' <i>(Nov 28, 1986,Contact (Wgtn),2</i>)

>  ... after the speeches were completed an invitation was made to rub noses, shake hands and have kai. <i>(Nov 12, 1988,Dominion,3</i>)

>  <b>MiTCALFE</b> What say me and the girls come round, make a proper kai for you, tomorrow night? <i>(1985,Hey Hey Hey,117\</i>)

>  And don't cook. You come down the marae after and have a kai down there you hear me ... [sic] <i>(1985,Landfall,156/453</i>) [NOTES: Grace (Waimarie)]

>  <b>FiNLAYSON</b> `This is what pays for the kai,' [Uncle Ted] says. `Look at the nice little drop [of cream] in the can already.' <i>(1948,Tidal Creek,30,1979 ed.</i>)

>  [Advt] A class act with fabulous Thai kai. <i>(Nov 7, 2002,Dominion Post,C10</i>)

>  it is horrid to contemplate a young lady coming home from church and seeing a surf-bather's bare feet as he toddled home to kai. <i>(Apr 16, 1910,NZ Truth,4</i>)

>  <b>EDWARDS</b> We would eat boiled kai too, like puku poaka. <i>(1992,Mihipeka,39</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) [of farm stock] to eat. [ORIGIN: Maori] (See [also kaikai](k-kea.md#kaikai))

>  <b>RiCHMOND</b> His grass is very low and he has sent his 52 ewes and 56 lambs to 'kai' our grass. <i>(Aug 13, 1851,Letter,102</i>) [NOTES: in Scholefield (1960) Richmond-Atkinson Papers i]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> a meal; `eats'. (Popular with NZ soldiers WW1: contrast WW2 mango.) [1953 14 M A7 Thames DHS 22B KAI [food] -->1953 16 M A31 Thames DHS 22B KAI -->1953 16 M A33 Thames DHS 22B KAI -->1953 17 M A35 Thames DHS 22B KAI ---->1960 16C F B5 Wellington Girls C. 22 KAI -->1960 17C M B7 Hamilton H.S. 22B KAI --->1984 16 F E7 Pakuranga Coll. 22B KAI -->1984 16 F E10 Pakuranga Coll. 22B KAI -->1984 16 M E71 Pakuranga Coll. 22B K(A)I -->1984 16 M E.ho;1984 Pakuranga Coll. 22B KAI]

>  When just within a few yards of the winning post, they both had a spill, <i> Bobby </i> [a racehorse] bidding us adieu for the day went home for, as we suppose, some `ki ki' <i>((12/0/1842),NZ Herald</i>) [NOTES: 1842 January 12 NZ Herald and Auckland Gazette quoted in Best Journal (1966) 417]

>  <b>VOGEL</b> (`a meal') We might have `kai' at the Maungaiti bush. <i>(1898,Maori Maid,274</i>)

>  <b>WALKER</b> They ... got in plenty of `tucker' or `kai' <i>(1902,Zealandia's Guerdon,95</i>)

>  <b>314/6/1</b> The simple defendant [a half caste Maori], acknowledged thirty-seven years of age, but said he had four or five brothers who should also help to keep the kainga of Ma over her head in Otaki and provide her with kai. <i>(Jul 1, 1911,NZ Truth</i>)

>  it is horrid to contemplate a young lady who regards the beach as sacred to the uses of the Lovers' Paradise, coming home from church and seeing a surf-bathers, bare feet as he toddled home to kai. <i>(Apr 16, 1910,NZ Truth,251/4/7</i>)

>  Our boys growled at the amount of sand they had to eat with the kai at Zeitoun. <i>(Jul 3, 1915,Hutt Valley independent,3</i>)

>  KAi. - Food, rations, pork and beans, etc. <i>(1917,New Zealand At the Front,xiii</i>)

>  <b>MiLLER</b> We felt the need of some `kai' or `skoff' as they call it [at Capetown]. <i>(Feb 23, 1917,Camps, Tramps and Trenches,26</i>)

>  Pray where is my kai, sir? <i>(May 20, 1917,Digger Transport Magazine, The</i>)

>  <b>FRASER & GiBBONS</b> <i> KAi:</i> Food. (A Maori word, used among the New Zealand troops in the War). <i>(1925,Soldier and Sailor Words,134</i>)

>  <b>LEE</b> . `Do you want the kai?' `Kai', the Maori word for food, was the [reform] school term for slices of inch-thick bread. <i>(1936,Hunted, The,22</i>)

>  Slang words of Maori origin were once common, but most of them are dead and dying [and include] ... `kai,' `kapai,' `hoot' (utu), ... <i>(Apr 2, 1938,Press, The,18</i>) [NOTES: Mcnab NZ Slang]

>  <b>BAKER</b> The colloquial use of kai for food in general, especially for a meal, was also well-established, and had already done good service before 1840. <i>(1941,NZ Slang,25</i>)

>  <b>SOLJAK</b> New Zealand colloquialisms which are of Maori origin include: ... kai : food Notes <i>(1946,New Zealand (various authors/titles),116</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> The Maoris must smile at the pakeha going all Maori when he's overseas. People on the ship to England wearing tikis and saying good kai this morning. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,224</i>)

>  <b>SYDER & HODGETTS</b> Food .in 4;`What's for kai? i'm hungry.' <i>(1974,Australian & NZ English [TS],618</i>)

>  <b>LELAND</b> <i>kai</i>: Maori word for FOOD which has come into general use. <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,57</i>)

>  <i> kai </i> <i>(Feb 1981,Avondale College Slang Words in Use</i>) [NOTES: (Goldie Brown Collection)]

>  <i>kai:</i> food (Maori) <i>(1981,Auckland Secondary Teachers College</i>) [NOTES: (Goldie Brown Collection)]

>  <i> kai n. NZ inf </i> . food. [< Maori] <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,613</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 409; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 270]]

>  <b>WiLSON</b> it was rich food, good kai. <i>(1984,South Pacific Street,6</i>)

>  <b>RiCHARDS</b> On the shearing board of Otupae,/ Shore six of the world's best,/ And with the best of kai, you bet,/ ... Starting from the end pen,/ Billy Richards rang the board./ <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,98</i>)

>  The Works closed at four, which meant at least two hours before Danny could expect them [the parents] - and the `kai' - greasies probably, and his stomach sent out signals. <i>(Dec 1986,Landfall,160/491</i>) [NOTES: (Smither)]

>  <b>McGiLL</b> <i> kai </i> food; from Maori to eat or drink anything other than water; used WWi soldiers, while Australian soldiers preferred Melanesian pidgin `kaikai', meaning food. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,65</i>)

### kai cart/kai kart
 
 <i>n.</i> pie cart or food caravan (See [also kai wagon](k-kea.md#kai-wagon))

>  <b>BAKER</b> One week in that mouldering, medieval pile of black rock, in a wing with the rapists and child molesters, eating food that made Gregory's Kai Kart menu look like haute cuisine, was enough for him. <i>(2000,Kokopu Dreams,86</i>)

>  Sitting at his unofficial office in front of Queen Street's Kai Cart takeways, after having just appeared in court for the second time on wilful damage charges, he is dismissive of such accusations. <i>ibid.</i> 33 Smith's supporters, who have also adjourned to the Kai Cart to korero, know why they're there. <i>(Jan 14, 1995,Listener (New Zealand Listener),32</i>)

>  You've got the pie-cart, known as the kai-cart, on the left. <i>(1957,Tavern in the Town,150</i>) [NOTES: 1984 edition]

>  [Caption] Joseph Hohapata (9) sells muffins from Ruatoria's Kai Kart to earn pocket money/ <i>(Jan 9, 2000,Sunday Star Times,A5</i>)

>  i am writing to salute the staff of the Ruatoria Kai Kart on the East Coast. <i>(Feb 9, 2000,Dominion,10</i>)

>  <b>McGiLL</b> <i>kaikart</i> fast food takeaway or piecart. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,65</i>)

>  in Ruatoria the kai kart opposite the pub has to compete with the spectacle of 20 horse-riding, flag-waving Rastafarians galloping through the town. <i>(Aug 16, 1986,Listener (New Zealand Listener),82</i>)

>  ..more than $500,000 was raised through the Kai Cart catering caravan, which operated for the past 22 years... <i>(Jun 18, 2005,Listener (New Zealand Listener),34</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

>  He had to go through the menu, boiled and smoked eels, fricassed [sic] shark and kai corn. <i>(Feb 13, 1913,NZ Observer,15</i>)

 <i>n.</i> a feast; see hakari [ORIGIN: Maori]

### kai moana
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The title, "Kai Moana" - Food of the Water, is appropriate. <i>(May 1963,Kai Moana,i : 1</i>)

>  Participants catch kai moana (seafood) which is cooked and served to them on the privately owned Motungarara island. <i>(Dec 18, 1992,Evening Post,40</i>)

>  i used to read Witi [sc. ihimaera] and wish i had a life by the sea, having kai moana. <i>(Oct 1999,North & South,95</i>)

>  ...washing down gear after a dive, with a dozen curious kaka perched on our shoulders, on strewn wetsuits and also on the catching bag full of kai moana, their hooked beaks tentatively touching the feelers and spiked claws of kicking red crustaceans. <i>(Jan 2002,NZ Geographic,No. 55/31</i>)

>  On the face of it, the list seemed rather disparate: bouillabaisse, muttonbird, paella, kai moana, Otago Pinot Noir, single malt Scotch, the wines of Te Mata Estate and the smoky, flaxy taste of hangi food. <i>(Jan 2001,Cuisine,133</i>)

>  <b>EDWARDS</b> Since moving away from the north, i miss the sea and the lovely fresh kaimoana. <i>(1992,Mihipeka,63</i>)

>  <b>McNAUGHTON</b> i wanted to tell my children to put their kits down on the sand and mimi on them so that we would find plenty of good kai moana to take home. <i>(1986,Countless Signs,46</i>) [NOTES: From Patricia Grace (1975) : Waiariki]

### kai Pakeha
 
 <i>n.</i> (a variety of sweet potato) [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  <b>POLACK</b> ... the potatoes, kumeras, melons, <i>kai pakeha</i>[a1-e-acute], or sweet yam, were planted in small mounds, laid out with great regularity. <i>ibid.</i> 1/289 There is a much larger variety of this esculent [sc. kumera], called <i>kai pakeha</i> [a2-acute], or white man's food. <i>ibid.</i> 2/4 Previously to his leaving the village he desired a female slave to prepare some kai pakeha [a2-acute] or large sweet potatoes against his return. <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/193</i>)

### Kai Tahu
 
 <i>n.</i> Si form of Ngai Tahu [ORIGIN: Maori]

### kai tangata
 
 <i>n.</i> a human for eating

### kai to compost
 
 <i>phr.</i> a waste-reduction programme
