### kai-oh
 
 <i>exclam.</i>

>  When his usual cheery "Kai-oh!" reached our ears the diggers rushed forward with delight to get their eating utensils. <i>(Aug 16, 1918,Chronicles NZEF,V/50 : 31</i>)

### kai-weta
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>LAiNG & BLACKWELL</b> <i>Carpodetus serratus (The Serrate Carpodetus)</i>... The native name <i>Puta-puta-weta</i> signifies full of weta holes. in the North island trees of this species are rarely cut down unperforated by longitudinal insect galleries. Another name by which it is known is <i>Puna-weta. </i>in the Urewera country it is called <i>Kai-weta</i> (i.e., weta food). [That ugly innocent the weta (<i>Deinacrida</i>) has thus been accused of perforating the tree; but as a matter of fact though often used as convenient residences [195] by the weta, the holes are made by the caterpillar of the large moth <i>Hepialus virescens...</i> ]. <i>(1907,Plants of NZ,188,1940, p. 194 ed.</i>)

### kaiaia
 
 <i>n.</i> karearea, sparrow hawk

>  <b>HUTTON</b> 1. HiERACiDEA NOVAE ZEALANDiAE. Lath. Quail Hawk. Sparrow Hawk. Kaiaia. <i>H. Brunnea</i> Gould. <i>(1871,Birds of New Zealand,1</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> HiERACiDEA \{HARPA\} NOVAE ZEALANDiAE. (QUAiL HAWK.) ... <i>Native names.</i> Karearea, Kaiaia, Kaeaea, Kakarapiti, Karewarewa, and Tawaka. : <i>ibid.</i> HiERACiDEA BRUNNEA \{HARPA FEROX\}. (BUSH-HAWK.) ... <i>Native names.</i> Karearea, Kaiaia, Kaeaea, Kakarapiti, Karewarewa, and Tawaka; but sometimes distinguished as Karewarewa-tara. `Sparrow-Hawk' of the colonists. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,{1888 2nd edn 1/213, 1/220}</i>)

>  <b>HAY</b> The Kaiaia (<i>Hieracidea Brunnea</i>) is a sparrow-hawk, smaller than the Kahu. ... By the same native name the `quail-hawk' (<i>Hieracidea N.Z.</i>) is also known. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/221</i>)

### kaiawhina
 
 <i>n.</i> assistant; helper; support worker [http://translator.kedri.info/translate.php]

The department's head office has told hospitals about the benefits traditional healers and kaiawhina (helpers) can have on the wellbeing of Maori patients. ... Hospital awhina (helpers) and traditional healers were specially trained gifted people who voluntarily visited hospitals ... <i>(Apr 1, 1987,Dominion,8</i>)

>  Kaiawhina .. Criteria: Enjoy working with mokopuna; Competent in Te Reo me nga Tikanga Maori; Good communication, planning and organisational skills . . . <i>(Aug 16, 2003,Hawke's Bay Today,C5</i>)

>  [Heading]FAMiLY START KAiAWHiNA/FAMiLY WORKER <i>(Oct 11, 2006,Whakatane Beacon,9</i>)

>  Watching the public interact with the work as it arrived, along with the kai awhina was amazing... "Sharing ideas with other kai awhina, meeting the artists,.. you learn to be creative. <i>(Mar 25, 1991,Listener (New Zealand Listener),49</i>)

>  Kai Awhina are Maori women who work alongside Plunket nurses with mothers who have special needs or want some extra support and advice. <i>(Oct 12, 1999,independent Herald (Wellington),13</i>)

>  Positions available: Full time Kaiako Kaiawhina <i>(May 12, 2010,Horowhenua Chronicle,24</i>)

### kaihautu
 
 <i>n.</i> leader, canoe steersman [ORIGIN: Maori, [Ma. <b>kaihautu</b> leader, representative, contrller, steersman] cf. P.M. Ryan Dict. Modern Maori (1995) 71.]

>  Maori Television Service's current head commissioner for programming.. has been appointed to the new position of kaihautu (leader), effective September 22. Traditionally, a kaihautu is the person in a waka who steers the boat in the right direction. <i>(Aug 12, 2003,Dominion Post,A2</i>)

>  One month into his controversial appointment as TVNZ's kaihautu - the one who steers the waka - Edwards is now in a position of power, created under the charter to ensure a significant Maori voice in programmes and programme planning... While detractors have derisively translated the title kaihautu as the "waka paddler", Edwards says: "i tell you, if this was pre-European times, a paddler i would not be." <i>(Nov 23, 2003,Sunday Star Times,C1</i>)

>  [She] asked the minister to deny that two tangata whenua had been paid out bu TVNZ because of a "cultural misunderstanding" because they thought TVNZ chief executive ian Fraser had offered them the kaihautu's job. <i>(Aug 15, 2003,Dominion Post,A2</i>)

>  Te Waru White, a <i>Kaihautu</i> (representative)of Te Papa, says that many Maori, himself included, had been isolated from the significance of Matariki. <i>(Jun 7, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),31</i>)

### kaihua
 
 <i>n.</i> (Environment) Parsonsia capsularis, <i> a climbing plant.: Parsonsia </i> spp. [ORIGIN: Maori, kaihu also kaihu] (See [kaihu](../K#kaihu))

>  Kaihua can be seen growing through the plant collections at Otari and also throughout the bush, though you are more likely to smell it than to see it. <i>(Dec 10, 2005,Dominion Post,indulgence p23</i>)

>  Kaihu <i>(1890,PWD Catalogue of Timbers,10</i>)

>  <b>LEVY</b> .. kaihu, a long narrow-leaved climber, changes its leaf form considerably in the adult; ... <i>(1951,Grasslands of NZ,9,1970 ed.</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> Maori jasmine, Parsonia heterophylla .

>  Kaihua is a vigorous, soft-wooded vine that climbs by twining stems. ... Leaf variability is a characteristic of kaihua [sic], as its species name heterophylla suggests. ... Kaihua likes a shaded root run in a free draining soil, with the leaves up in the sun .. Kaihua can be seen growing through the plant collections at Otari and also throughout the bush, though you are more likely to smell it than see it. <i>(Dec 10, 2005,Dominion Post,indulgence 23</i>)

>  The native <i> Parsonia heterophylla </i> (New Zealand jasmine, kaihua) is found in coastal to lower mountain forest. A second native species, <i> Parsonia capsularis </i>, occurs in shrubland and on forest margins. <i>(Mar 2009,Open Space,75/19</i>)

>  <b>MOORE & iRWiN</b> <i>PARSONSiA HETEROPHYLLA, kaihua.</i> A twining woody liane that climbs high on forest trees ... <i>(1978,Oxford Book of NZ Plants,120</i>)

>  <b>GiBBS</b> Craw ... found that at Linden the first brood fed mainly at flowers of the liane kaihua (<i>Parsonsia heterophylla</i>), ... . <i>(1980,NZ Butterflies,152</i>)

>  You might identify the common liane, kaihua, also known as Maori jasmine because of its small, white, scented flowers. <i>(1983,Land of the Mist,65</i>)

### kaihuka
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>MANNERiNG</b> i had made friends with a Maori family whitebait fishing there [at Lower Waiwakaiho], from them usually procuring bait for night fishing - generally smelts or `kaihuka' as they called them, a name i never heard from the Hawke's Bay Maoris. <i>(1943,Eighty Years,166</i>)

### kaik
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BOWMAN</b> <i>Omati</i>: Now usually called "the Kaik". Maori church and meeting-place erected there. <i>(1948,Port Chalmers,221</i>)

>  Over the hill is the marae, and a gravel road leading through farmland and twisting around the peninsular to First and Second Kaik, two bays housing a cluster of ramshackle cribs. <i>(Jun 19, 2005,Sunday Star Times,Escape p.9</i>)

>  <b>HULME & MORRiSON</b> For quite a while, my uncle Bill lived at the kaik' beach. <i>(1989,Homeplaces,59</i>)

>  <b>HULME & MORRiSON</b> it's mainly the place where you pick up the milk and papers, and then scuttle back to the kaik'. <i>(1989,Homeplaces,118</i>)

>  <b>BATHGATE</b> The aboriginal natives in this great province of Otago are to be numbered in hundreds, there being only about five or six villages or "kaiks" studded along the coasts. <i>(1874,Colonial Experiences,255</i>)

>  <i>Kaik</i> (k&imacr.k),<i> n. [Maori</i> kaika.] <i>See KAiKA. </i> <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third international,2025</i>)

>  The reply is expressed in that.. simplicity for which Portobello is famous all the way from Broad Bay to the Maori Kaik. <i>(Dec 28, 1920,Otago Witness,1</i>)

>  <b>FARRELL</b> Some had married into families up the kaik. <i>(1992,Skinny Louie Book,35</i>)

>  <b>McNAUGHTON</b> At each end of the kaik' bay the cliff goes / down in humps to stand blunt-nosed / against the sea. <i>(1986,Countless Signs,34</i>) [NOTES: From Keri Hulme (1982): Pa mai to reo aroha]



 
 <i>n.</i> nick name for kahikatea [<i>Dacrycarpus dacrydioides</i>]

>  <b>i.Davey (Whangarei)</b> KAiK is an abomination used in Northland by timber people for kahikatea, white pine. i have never met it elsewhere. <i>(Jul 29, 1966,Letter</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  A dreary swamp next succeeded, beside which were a few <i>kaiks</i>, the mia-mias of the Maoris, who were here for the eeling season. <i>(Sep 27, 1861,NZ Goldfields,23,1976 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1861 Sep 27 Tuapeka in NZ Goldfields 1861 (1976) 23]

### kaika
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Parsonsia albiflora</i>

>  Parsonsia albiflora Kaika <i>(1870,TrNZi,2/124</i>) [NOTES: (Armstrong Botany Canterbury)]



 
 <i>n.</i> [South Island Maori form of "kainga"]

>  A foreign violinist.. discovered by the young braves on the beach.. was deported to the kaika as treasure trove, and, being in fair condition, incontinently roasted and devoured. <i>(Aug 27, 1910,NZ Free Lance,4</i>)

>  settlement, ka|ika [kaika] <i>(1979,World of John Boultbee, The,286</i>) [NOTES: 1826-28 Boultbee Vocabulary in 1979 Begg 286]

>  During the few days we remained at the Kaika Totoe (Totoe's Settlement) we lived on fernroot, cockles, and small dried eels, - ... <i>(1835,Boultbee Journal,62,1986 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MANTELL</b> Arrived at the Kaika, Wahinei searched the wata and among a quantity of putrid fish ... found 2 kits of potatoes of which we made a hearty meal. <i>(Oct 24, 1848,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1848 Oct 24 Mantell Diary in 1947 Stephenson Maori and Pakeha 128]

>  <b>SHORTLAND</b> KAiKA, a place of eating; hence `a place of residence.' Syn. kaigna. [sic] <i>(1851,Southern Districts of NZ,307</i>)

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> The village is situated ... at the Western extremity of the Bluff Peninsula, and contains about a dozen of Maori houses. it is a `Kaika'. <i>(1940,Te Waimate,1954 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1854 Feb 11 in 1940 Studholme Te Waimate (1954)]

>  <b>ELWELL</b> Maori `Keike' ... <i>(1878,Boy Colonists,108 et pass.</i>)

>  <b>JOLLiE</b> i slept at this `kaik' once or twice. <i>(1879,Reminiscences,52</i>)

>  <i>Kai.i-ka </i>(ka.i.e.-k&adot.), <i>n</i>. [Maori.] A Maori village or community; - called also <i>kainga</i>, and, colloquially, <i>kaik. Kaika</i> and <i>kainga</i> are dialectal varieties of the same word. <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third international,2025</i>)

>  The land was consequently uninhabited, with the exception of small kaikas at Waitaki mouth, ... and Omarama. <i>ibid.,</i> 6 His usual place of residence was at the Puna-a-maru ... Maori kaike ... where a few Maoris lived in thatched whares, with very low walls built of mud. <i>(1914,History of North Otago,4,1978 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1906 W.H.S.Roberts in 1914 History of North Otago (1978) 4]

>  <b>ECCLES & REED</b> A week or two later Watkin, finding the house in the agricultural settlement too far removed from the natives, took possession of a hut nearer the Maori kaika. <i>(1949,John Jones of Otago,63</i>)

>  <b>NATUSCH</b> March 29 was spent ... on the way to the Waimakariri kaika, ... A pretty girl sat de-kutuing the head of her handsome admirer, popping the proceeds into her mouth. <i>(1978,Acheron Journal,103</i>)

>  <b>SANSOM</b> Further round from Kelly's Beach you can find the site of the ancient Maori kaik and the cemetery, its landmark a huge ngaio tree. <i>(1982,in the Grip of an island,140</i>)

>  <b>HULME</b> Kaika = Ngai Tahu dialect for home, or village <i>(1983,Bone People, The,466</i>)

>  <b>ROBERTS</b> We had to get them from the Maoris at the kaike (sic). <i>(1890,History of Oamaru & North Otago,9</i>) [NOTES: Quote from 1854]

>  <b>ROBERTS</b> When Mr Filleuil was at the kaika the bullocks had to be watched day and night. <i>(1937,History of North Otago,00</i>) [NOTES: Check page number]

>  <b>ROBERTS</b> We had to get them from the Maoris at the kaike (sic). <i>(1890,History of North Otago,9</i>) [NOTES: quote from 1854]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <i>(May 20, 1842,Piraki Log,130</i>)

>  ...Coyk [=] kaik <i>(1844,Piraki Log</i>)

>  We reached a Maori kyack, or fishing village. <i>(Jan 13, 1851,Journal of Edward Ward,109,1951 ed.</i>)

>  A few more kaiks forming a regular Maori village, are near this spot, and for the first time i saw the Maori ... in their home scenes ... <i>(Sep 27, 1861,NZ Goldfields,23,1976 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1861 Sep 27 Tuapeka in NZ Goldfields 1861 (1976)23-24]

>  There are mourays here and two kikes or villages. <i>(Apr 21, 1861,Chudleigh Diary,33,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>GOLDiE</b> ...a Maori Kyke. <i>(Aug 29, 1862,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1862 Aug 29. J. Goldie Third Diary in 1947 Beattie the Pioneers Explore Otago 134.]

>  <b>HOLLOWAY</b> Here too [in Otago] is a Maori kaik, or settlement <i>(Mar 11, 1874,Journal of a Visit</i>)

>  <b>PRATT</b> We strained our eyes ... to discover ... some faint vestige of the Maori kaik. <i>(1877,Colonial Experiences,169</i>)

>  <b>ROBERTS</b> (Sth Cby) We had to get wheat from the Maoris at the Kaik. <i>(1890,Oamaru,9</i>)

>  <b>CAMPBELL</b> We came opposite a Maori Kaik. <i>(1894,Reminiscences,44</i>)

>  <b>HOCKEN</b> At the kaik they remained two days, supplementing their old fare of salt beef and biscuit with the delicious addition of fernroot and wild turnip tops. <i>(1898,Contributions to the Early History of New Zealand,37</i>)

>  <i>kaik kaika, kainga n. </i>a Maori settlement or village. [Maori] <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,6</i>)

>  <b>AYSON</b> immediately realizing the dreadful mistake he had made, he shed to the Maori kaik which was quite near and cried out for milk. <i>(1937,Thomas,17</i>)

>  <b>BEATTiE</b> Near the kaik there was a poor water-supply ..., but when the Maoris shifted to Puketeraki they had creeks there. <i>(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,19</i>)

>  <i>kaik or kaika</i>: South island form of kainga. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,174</i>)

>  <b>SHAW & FARRANT</b> He found there that Weller's whaling station on Maturata island was almost abandoned and the natives away on a mutton-birding expedition. However, one of his party, on a mokihi (raft) constructed of korari sticks floated up the lovely gorge with the flood tide, and returned with two boats manned by natives from the Maori kaik beyond the gorge. <i>(1949,Taieri Plain,3</i>)

>  <i>kaik, kainga </i>A Maori village; `kaik' in the South island only. (Pronounce<i> kike, kah-inga|.</i>) <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),229</i>)

>  <b>HULME</b> ... They used to say, Find the kaik' road / take the kaika road, / the glimmering road of the past / into Te Ao Hou. <i>(1983,Bone People, The,97</i>)

### kaikaha
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>LiNDSAY</b> in Otago the general name of the [flax] plant, as a whole, is `Korari,' - or as metamorphosed by the settler, pronounced and spelt `Koura<i>d</i>i,' [a-macron] or `Cora<i>d</i>i' [a-macron]. The same term is applied in the North island equally to the whole plant, and to the dried flower-stem when cut for rafts or other purposes; or exclusively to the flower-stalk. in the North [83] island, the Flax-plant is also known as `Harakeke,' or `Herekiakia,' - its usual name there. ... The lower part of the flower-stem is `Kaikaha;' the outer part of the leaf `Parakoka;' [a1, o-macron] the refuse of the leaf `Hunga-hunga;' the flax, dressed or not, or scraped fibre, manufactured from the leaf, `Whitau,' or `Muka.' <i>(1868,Contributions to NZ Botany,82</i>)

### kaikai
 
 <i>n.</i> food, feasting

>  <b>SAVAGE</b> Kiki .. Food. <i>(1807,New Zealand (various authors/titles),75</i>)

>  <b>NiCHOLAS</b> Many of them had never before ... beheld an European, and to see <i>packa[h]a</i>[a3-circumflex] <i>kiki </i>(the white man eat,) was a novelty of so curious a nature, that they gazed on it with wonder and delight. <i>ibid.</i> 1/267 [Our New Zealand companions] ... were not a little surprised that we could not relish such fine <i>kiki,</i> (food,) as well as themselves. <i>ibid.</i> 2/332 ViCTUALS Kiki <i>(1817,New Zealand (various authors/titles),1/139</i>)

>  <b>WEEKES</b> We enjoyed our supper of potatoes and <i>perou</i>, a kind of eel, and they [sc. Maoris] would not let us leave without taking <i>kai-kai</i> this morning <i>(Jul 1841,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1841 July Weekes Journal in 1940 Establishment of New Plymouth 52]

>  <b>STEPHENS</b> ...kai-kai - food <i>(1843,Letters & Journals,127,1842 ed.</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFiELD</b> (also 1/265) He explained to us that everyone would cry very much, and then there would be very much kai-kai or feasting. <i>(1845,Adventure in NZ,1/29</i>)

>  <b>HUNT</b> [Rauparaha said] `... we shall have lots of kai-kai; ...' <i>(1866,Chatham islands,19</i>)

>  <b>FEATON</b> Then came the general body of Natives, women laden with kai kai and baggage bringing up the rear. <i>(1879,Waikato War,7</i>)

>  <b>GUTHRiE</b> <i>Kai-kai,</i> or feasting, was an institution with the Maoris; ... <i>(1930,NZ Memories,31</i>)

>  <b>PARTRiDGE</b> <i>Kai-kai</i>. Food; feasting: New Zealand: mid- C. 19-20. Reduplication of Maori <i>kai </i>(food), itself used by the New Zealand soldiers in the G.W. F. & Gibbons. Cf. <i>kapai. </i> <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang,449</i>)

>  <b>MiTCALFE</b> [Samoan speaks] `You know, book up the kai-kai. Didn't i give you enough?' [fn] kai-kai: big feast <i>(1985,Hey Hey Hey,122</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  James's new mansion is known as `The Palace of Art'. Harry's house is distinguished as `Kai kai Lodge' (in reference to the uniform abundance of meat & drink) whilst Henry's mansion is named `Damper Hall'. <i>(Mar 23, 1859,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/452</i>) [NOTES: (C.W. Richmond)]

>  <b>MOSER</b> Things ... were got ready at last, and served up in baskets made of flax, known as kits, calabashes, pots and kettles; and a roar of `kai-kai! haeremai', created a sudden sensation amongst the outsiders. <i>(1863,Mahoe Leaves,34</i>)

>  <b>BEATTiE</b> Mother fried some [mushrooms] and i boldly tried one, but `O kaikai!' i soon got rid of it, ... <i>(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,62</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>v.</i> to eat [also kai Early writers often use a reduplicated, esp. verbal, form kaikai; a frequentative `feast' (this may be Polynesian pidgin);] (See [also kai](k-kea.md#kai))

>  eat - kai kai [kai] <i>(1826,Boultbee Journal,113,1986 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MARKHAM</b> And some seem to think that the Missionaries pray to the Attuah [<i>atua</i>] `Spirit of God', [54] to Kiki [kaikai*] or `eat up' the people, ... [fn 1963] Strictly speaking this is not a Maori word but either a rendering of <i>kakai,</i> eat frequently, or merely an example of pidgin Maori. <i>(1834,New Zealand (various authors/titles),53,1963 ed.</i>)

>  Stopped at `Tommy's' [a Maori]. Kikied the `tiners' and the fish. Slept on a Maori mat, wrapped in a blanket: 4 in a bed-place - heads and tails. <i>(Aug 15, 1842,Piraki Log,143</i>)

>  <b>ANGAS</b> Before i took leave of Te Heuheu. he made me `kai kai', or eat with him, which i was to understand as a mark of especial favour. <i>(1847,Savage Life,2/130</i>)

>  <b>1/103</b> One cannot look at some of the ancient grey headed cannibals who tell ...[sic] with gusto of the days when they `kaikai' (eat) conquered enemies ..., who discriminate the saltness of the `pakeha' from the sweetness of the maori, and the superiority of both to `poaka' pork ... <i>(Aug 13, 1851,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1960</i>) [NOTES: (J.C. Richmond)]

>  <b>Patricia Grace</b> in other words, when people looked across the marae atea to the gate where the two were waiting to be called in, someone standing behind the kaikaranga said in a not-too-quiet whisper, 'He's brought his laptop with him.' This caused the kaikaranga to choke on the first syllable of her call, which is very bad form indeed. <i>(2001,Dogside Story,190</i>)

### kaikawaka
 
 <i>n.</i> a small subalpine conifer with red wood and pinkish bark, related to the kawaka [Maori]; also called pahautea, (mountain) cedar. 1. <i>Libocedrus plumosa</i>, kaiwaka. 2. <i>Libocedrus Bidwillii, </i>, pahautea.

>  Kai.i-ka-wai.ka (ka.i.e.-ka.-wa.i.k&adot.; colloq. ka.&ibreve.i.k&adot.-wa.@k&adot.), n. [Maori.] (Bot.) Either of two New Zealand coniferous trees of the genus <i> Libocedrus </i> ; - called also kawaka and c <i> cedar. </i> The name is more correctly applied to the species <i> L. doniana </i> ; - called also locally <i> arbor vitae. [Local, N. Z.</i> ] The correct Maori name of <i> L. Bidwilli </i> is <i> pahautea </i> , but surveyors and bushmen always call it <i> kaikawaka.</i> <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third international</i>)

>  <b>LAiNG & BLACKWELL</b> The tree <i> [Libocedrus Bidwillii]</i> is often known to bushmen as the <i> Kaikawaka,</i> [68] though Colenso states that its correct name is Pahautea. ... it and the previous species [<i> L. plumosa, Kawaka </i> ] are sometimes also called the New Zealand <i> Cedar </i> , but must not be confused with <i> Dysoxylum spectabile </i> , to which this name is often applied. <i>(1907,Plants of NZ,66</i>)

>  The chief indigenous trees of commercial value are: Kauri (<i> Agathis Australis </i> ), Kahikatea or white pine (<i> Podocarpus dacrydioides </i> ), rimu or red pine (<i> Dacrydium cupressinum </i> ), totara <i> (Podocarpus totara </i> and <i> Podocarpus Hallii </i> ), Miro (<i> Podocarpus ferrugineus </i> ), Kaikawaka or mountain cedar (<i> Libocedrus Bidwilli </i> ), yellow pine or silver pine (<i> Dacrydium Colensoi </i> and <i> D. intermedium </i> ), red and hard beech (<i> Nothofagus fasca </i> and <i> Nothofagus truncata </i> ), silver beech <i> Nothofagus Menziesii </i> ), black beech (<i> Nothofagus Solandri </i> ), black maire (<i> Olea Cunninghamii </i> ), northern and southern rata (<i> Metrosideros robusta </i> and <i> Metrosideros lucida </i> ). <i>(1936,Agricultural Organization in NZ,578</i>)

>  <i> kaikawaka n -s </i> [Maori] : either of two New Zealand evergreen trees of the genus <i> Libocedrus: a </i> : KAWAKA b : MOUNTAiN PiNE 3b <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third international,1230</i>)

>  <b>DENNiS</b> Special mention could be made here of the New Zealand cedar pahautea (<i> Librocedrus bidwillii </i> - sometimes called kaikawaka, although that name should probably be confined to <i> L. plumosa </i> which does not extend this far south.) <i>(1981,Paparoas Guide,162</i>)

>  <b>DAWSON</b> Mountain five-finger (<i> Pseudopanax colensoi </i> ) also has a wide range, but is absent north of 36[degrees]S and from Stewart island. i have observed it growing as an epiphyte on kaikawaka or mountain cedar (<i> Libocedrus bidwillii </i> ) on Mt. Taranaki ... <i>(1988,Forest Vines to Snow Tussocks,82</i>)

>  The tips of kaikawaka, or mountain cedar, which thrust above the main canopy, display a distinctive "flag form, the foliage appearing to stream out on the trees' downwind side. <i>(Jan 2004,NZ Geographic,No. 67/36</i>)

### kaikomako
 
 <i>n.</i> a small flowering native tree, Pennantia corymbosa [ORIGIN: Maori, Also known as kahikomako, cheesewood, and bridal tree.]

>  in particular there is a series of kaikomako, a prodigiously primitive species where each tree shows a development from a ground-cover that hugged the earth against the 400-km/h winds of three million years ago, through stunted bush to light-seeking trunk. <i>(Feb 6, 1993,Dominion,1</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> There are many other woods of much service to Europeans differing in quality, among others: the <i> 30 Warangai - 31 Pata -32 - Niho - 33 Tangio, - 34 Maihoi - 35 Nene </i> [e's-acute]<i> - 36 Taraiti - 37 Kohihihu 38 Waihupuku - 39 Kaikamoko - 40 Pukapuka - 41 Karanghu - 42 Utuhutu - 43 Angiani - 44 Akkas </i>of various kinds. <i> 45 Parekireki, &c </i>. Of all these woods there are abundant quantities. <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),2/400</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Pennantia <i> Corymbosa,</i> Forst. ... Nat. name, `Kaikomako', <i>Colenso. </i> <i>(1853,Flora NZ,i Flowering Plants 35</i>)

>  Kaikomako. Pennantia Corymbosa <i>(1869,TrNZi,1/iii/40</i>) [NOTES: (Buchanan Botany Otago)]

>  Of our shrubs and smaller timber trees, several are of strikingly beautiful growth ... <i>(e.g.) </i> the Houhere, <i> Hoheria populnea </i> ...; the Horopito <i> Drimys axillaris </i>; the Manuka-rau-riki, <i> Leptospermum ericoides; </i> the Kohuhu, <i> Pittosporum tenuifolium </i>; the Kowhai, especially the small-leaved mountain variety, <i> Sophora tetraptera </i> var.: <i> grandiflora </i>; the Koromiko-taranga, <i> Veronica </i>, several species; the Mairehau, <i> Phebalium nudum </i>; the Toro, <i> Persoonia Toro </i>; the Pukapuka, <i> Brachyglottis repanda </i>; the Northern Maire, <i> Santalum Cunninghamii </i>; the Tawari, <i> ixerba brexioides;</i> the Tipau, <i> Myrsine Urvillei </i> and <i> M. salicina </i>; the Tangeao, <i> Tetranthera calicaris </i>; the Ramarama, <i> Myrtus bullata;</i> the Ti, <i> Cordyline australis </i>; the Kahikomako, <i> Pennantia corymbosa </i>; the Pate,<i> Schefflera digitata </i>; the Horoeka, Panax crassifolia: and, on the sea-coast, ... the Karo, <i> Pittosporum crassifolium; </i> and the truly evergreen Ngaio, <i> Myoporum laetum ...</i> <i>ibid.</i> 1/iii/36 Fire, by friction, was obtained from several woods; the Kaikomako <i>(Pennantia corymbosa) was ... the one most prized, and also the Pate (Schefflera digitata); and a trunk stem of the Kohia </i> (Passiflora tetranddra)<i> was often sought to carry fire ..</i> <i>(1869,TrNZi,1/iii/5</i>) [NOTES: (Colenso Botany North isd)]

>  <b>HAY</b> The Kaikomako (<i> Pennantia corymbosa </i>) will be much cultivated as a garden ornament. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,ii/198</i>)

>  <b>HECTOR</b> <i> Pennantia corymbosa </i>, Forst. Kaikomako.--A small, very graceful tree, with white sweet-smelling flowers; height 20-30 feet. <i>(1883,Handbook of New Zealand,130</i>)

>  [Native name] Kaikomako .. [Settlers' name] Kaikomako .. [Family] Olacineae <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,121</i>) [NOTES: 1886 T.Kirk in 1902 Settler's Handbook of NZ 121]

>  <b>FEATON</b> PENNANTiA CORYMBOSA (<i> Forst </i>.)... The `KAiKOMAKO'.- This very graceful small tree, which, in season, produces an abundance of sweet-scented flowers, is ... most common throughout the Middle island. ... in the Middle island the kaikomako is often confounded by the settlers with the Ribbon Wood trees, (<i> Hoheria populnea,</i> ...) ... <i>ibid.</i> 177 Kaikamako [Maori name] .. Kaikamako [Settlers' name] .. Pennantia Corymbosa .. Small tree <i>(1889,Art Album NZ Flora,86</i>)

>  Kaikomako .. Pennantia corymbosa .. Timber light coloured and straight in grain, suitable for tool handles and turning <i>(1890,PWD Catalogue of Timbers,10</i>)

>  if that doesn't inspire you to feel warmer, you could try lighting your fire by vigorously rubbing some dried pate sticks with some kaikomako. <i>(Jul 7, 2007,Dominion Post indulgence,19</i>)

>  <i> kaikomako,</i> a small tree (Pennantia corymbosa), bridal-tree, Maori fire. Sometimes kahikomako. The softer of the two woods used by the Maori for producing fire by friction: he can get the blaze in under a minute and a half. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/151</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <b>LEVY</b> From the above low-forest association, or often directly from the manuka-kanuka associations, ... arise the larger trees of the forest proper, kamahi, rewarewa, hinau, totara, matai, southern beech, kaikomako, wharangi, rimu .. <i>(1951,Grasslands of NZ,87,1970 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MARTiN</b> The kaikomako, also known as CHEESEWOOD and BRiDAL TREE, commences life as a bushy shrub ... . <i>(1929,Flora NZ,178</i>)

>  <b>Crowe</b> in its slender, mature form, kaikomako makes a good specimen tree, well suited to small gardens. <i>(2009,Which Native Tree?,22</i>)

>  <b>Featon</b> PENNANTiA CORYMBOSA (Forst.)... The `KAiKOMAKO'.- This very graceful small tree, which, in season, produces an abundance of sweet-scented flowers, is ... most common throughout the Middle island. ... in the Middle island the kaikomako is often confounded by the settlers with the Ribbon Wood trees, (<i>Hoheria populnea</i>, ...) ... <i>(1889,Art Album NZ Flora,86</i>)

 <i>n.</i> Geol. classification (See [Kaikoura Orogeny](k-kea.md#kaikoura-orogeny))

### Kaikoura Orogeny
 
 <i>n.</i> Geol. classification (See [Kaikoura Orogenesis](k-kea.md#kaikoura-orogenesis))

>  That the Kaikoura Orogeny culminated during a short interval of time at the end of the middle Pleistocene (Table 12.1) and is now waning would be cheering if it could be proved, but no proof is given and it is equally likely that the culmination is yet to come, and that we have to expect as many earthquakes and uplifts in the next ten thousand years as we had in the last ten thousand. <i>(Nov 1968,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,Vol 26</i>) [NOTES: No page numbers in Newsletter 26]

### Kaikouras gecko
 
 <i>n.</i> small grey-brown gecko living east Marlborough and south as far as Kaikoura township. [<i>Hoplodactylus "Kaikouras"</i> aka Kaikoura gecko]

>  The lizard fauna of South Marlborough is diverse. Five species of the <i>Hoplodactylus maculatus</i> complex are known from the general area. Common gecko and Canterbury gecko are found in shrubland and forest, and Southern Alps gecko, Marlborough mini gecko and the threatened Kaikouras gecko (data deficient) are found in open rocky areas. The threatened black-eyed gecko (sparse) and rough gecko (gradual decline) have also been recorded. <i>(Feb 2007,internet - see Notes</i>) [NOTES: http://www.linz.govt.nz/docs/crownproperty/high-country-leases/other-crown-land/glenlee-crr.pdf]

>  The presence of the threatened (range restricted) Kaikouras gecko is particularly notable. it is an uncommon species of the "common gecko" complex and is known previously from only two sites: Patutu (peak) and inland of Ward (Whitaker and Gaze, 1999). <i>(Aug 2005,internet - see Notes</i>) [NOTES: http://www.linz.govt.nz/docs/crownproperty/high-country-leases/leaselist/middle-hill-crr.pdf]

>  <b>WiLSON</b> [kaikuu] <i>Parsonsia capsularis </i>NATiVE JASMiNE ... Akakiore ... Kaikuu ... Slender, many-branched climber. ... Flowers small, clustered, creamy, flushed reddish in bud. <i>(1982,Stewart island Plants,44</i>)

### Kaimanawa horse
 
 <i>n.</i> a horse from a wild herd running in an area of the Central North island Kaimanawa Ranges; a wild horse of the Kaimanawa region (from herds culled and sold annually by the Dept of Conservation in order to protect native plants and prevent traffic accidents on the Desert Road). (See [Kaimanawa](k-kea.md#kaimanawa))

>  However, not all the Kaimanawa horses go on to do so well. <i>(Mar 2005,NZ Geographic,72/16</i>)

>  The New Zealand Riding for the Disabled Association (RDA) has backed a move to give Kaimanawa horses a higher profile as a sustainable resource to help disabled people. <i>(Feb 16, 2006,Horowhenua Mail,45</i>) [NOTES: a 2005 entry so the year is wrong?]

>  Dominion June 12. 7 Twenty-five people have registered to buy at least 65 of the 100 Kaimanawa horses to be culled by the Conservation Department this month .. People could register their interest in buying a Kaimanawa horse up till about two days before the cull.. <i>(2001</i>)

>  HOLDEN Riding Country 62 These Kaimanawa horses were the real backbone of their team. <i>(2001</i>)

>  ibid. 69 in 1993 it was considered quite fashionable to own a Kaimanawa horse ... <i>(2001</i>)

### Kaimanawa wild horse
 
 <i>n.</i> see Kaimanawa horse [also Kaimanawa feral horse]

>  Does he have any reports on the damage caused by the Kaimanawa wild horses to the environment in the area? <i>(1993,NZ Parliamentary Debates,533/13678</i>) [NOTES: also Kaimanawa feral horse]

>  in the light of the resignation.. of the chairman of his department's animal ethics committee over the trial capture of Kaimanawa wild horses, does he intend today to call an immediate halt to the round-up programme? <i>(1993,NZ Parliamentary Debates,535/1561</i>)

>  Opponents of the plan to cull the Kaimanawa wild horses are investigating legal action to save the herd. <i>(Jun 7, 1996,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  The plan to cull the herd of Kaimanawa wild horses has hit a raw nerve among New Zealanders. <i>(Jun 7, 1996,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  This is how i see the end of the Kaimanawa wild horses : the sheer panic when the horses are rounded up and the shooting begins. <i>(Jun 6, 1996,Dominion,8</i>)

>  i write to endorse the sentiments expressed.. at the decision to slaughter1000 Kaimanawa wild horses... These "brumbies" are unique. <i>(Jun 6, 1996,Dominion,8</i>)

>  The Kaimanawa Wild Horse Preservation Society has a lawyer on the job... The Kaimanawa wild horse working party decided the herd should be culled to 800 horses. <i>(Jun 7, 1996,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  Horse (Equus), except those horses known as the Kaimanawa wild horses. <i>(1981,NZ Statutory Regulations,1981/239 : 1100</i>)

>  Once hunted to the point of extinction, the Kaimanawa wild horses now thrive in the harsh high country of the Desert Road... <i> ibid.</i> 56 Tony Batley's grandfather first saw wild horses in the Kaimanawas in March of 1876... <i>ibid.</i> 59 Their meetings were the forerunner of the Kaimanawa Wild Horse Committee <i>(Jan 1989,NZ Geographic,53</i>)

>  [Hdg] Kaimanawa Feral Horses and their Environmental impacts... Surviving population of free-ranging horses once spread over much of central North island but now restricted to the south-western Kaimanawas. <i>(1991,NZ Journal of Ecology,15(1) : 49</i>)

>  The Conservation Department has announced plans for a cull of Kaimanawa wild horses but only after details were leaked by a campaigner working to save them... Eric McLaughlin, formerly of the Kaimanawa wild horse network, said the department was being secretive and heavy-handed... <i>(Dec 27, 2003,Dominion Post,A5</i>)

>  The annual muster of Kaimanawa wild horses will take place next month and the Conservation Department wants to find homes for about 190 animals. <i>(May 13, 2003,Dominion Post,A4</i>)

### kaimoana
 
 <i>n.</i> seafood

>  Duff cites the chapter.. in which he lyrically describes an afternoon spent gathering kaimoana and hears a kind of music in the sound of pipi being pulled from the sand. <i>(Nov 16, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),20</i>)

>  <b>HiLLiARD</b> He might have had a deep-freeze for the kai moana, ... <i>(1974,Maori Woman,186</i>)

>  <b>HULME</b> `There was really only cods' eyes there ... unless you count the scallop's ... but there truly was fourteen different kai moana.' <i>ibid.</i> 466 110 Kai moana = seafood <i>(1983,Bone People, The,110</i>)

>  <b>KiNG</b> To supplement kai moana there was the garden ... <i>(1983,Whina,38</i>)

>  The kaimoana or seafood is all available from the nearest and hopefully clean coastline. Karengo (seaweed) is gathered off the rocks in August or September. Maoris used to cook it in the hangi but it can be steamed and dried or pickled in a jar with a few mussels. <i>(Jan 4, 1985,Evening Post,9</i>)

>  KAiMOANA: Maoris gather a kitful of titiko (a univalve shellfish) from the tidal flats, a traditional right reaffirmed by recent court judgement. <i>(Sep 7, 1986,Sunday Star (Auckland),A8</i>)

>  (File Mourning) The kaimoana was abundant. Mussels, paua ... <i>(Nov 20, 1989,Listener (New Zealand Listener),35</i>)

>  We all like a bit of a swim and a bit of kaimoana from time to time... <i>(Apr 2005,Tearaway,3</i>)

### kainga
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [cf. pa](p-per.md#pa))

>  <b>MARKHAM</b> ... i went up ... to a Native Village (Kangar Mourie). <i>(1834,New Zealand (various authors/titles),41,1963 ed.</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> [caption] <i>Na[a-acute] watta[a2-acute], or Platforms for holding Provisions in a New Zealand Village (Kainga maori[a-acute].) </i> <i>(1840,Manners & Customs of the NZers,1/214</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a dwelling; an unfortified place of residence; an unfortified village'; North island equivalent of "kaika" (See [also kaik(a)](k-kea.md#kaik)))

>  in Maori kaingas the natives have a sort of universal-ownership over everything. <i>(Oct 31, 1917,Hawke's Bay Herald,4</i>)

>  ... in the afternoon i visited the kahinghee (<i>kainga</i>) or place of Whittohee (Whetoi) in company with Mr. Hall. <i>(Jun 19, 1814,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1814 June 19 Kendall in Elder Marsden's Lieutenants (1934) 63]

>  <b>NiCHOLAS</b> A residence Kyhinga <i>(1817,New Zealand (various authors/titles),2/334</i>)

>  <b>McCRAE</b> Pomarre[acute] .. .. Rungatyeeda no te Kaaingha. E Wharre[acute] .. .. Whaheene[acute] no Pomarre[acute]. Poaka .. .. Tamyte[acute] no Pomarre[acute]. ... E Po .. .. Koeteedo no Pomarre Whaheene no Tenana. <i>(1820,Journal,27,1928 ed.</i>)

>  settlement - ka'ika [kainga] <i>(1835,Boultbee Journal,113</i>)

>  `Kainga. A place of residence, a home,' etc <i>(1820,Grammar and Vocabulary of Language of NZ,157</i>)

>  `A cosy-looking kainga located on the bank of a picturesque bend of the river.' -->ibid. p. 52, col. i: -->`We steamed on slowly towards Tawhitinui, a small kainga or kaik, as it is called in the South island.' <i>(Jan 23, 1896,Otago Witness,50/5</i>)

>  `The drive may be continued from Portobello to the Maori kaik.' <i>(1884,Maoriland,84</i>)

>  <i>Kai.i-nga (ka.i.e.-ng&adot.), n. [Maori.] A Maori village or community. See KAiKA. </i> <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third international,2025</i>)

>  .. to appoint from among the Maoris of any Maori kainga, village, or pa a Committee ... who shall be called the Village Committee (Komiti Marae) ... <i>(1900,NZ Statutes,64 Vict., No.48, pp. 252-60</i>) [NOTES: 1900 N.Z. Statutes 1900, 64 Vict., No.48, pp. 252-60 in 1971 Speeches and Documents 170 .]

>  <b>MARKHAM</b> All the Village (Kanger [<i>kainga</i>] Mourie) turned out to see the Horse rode, they call it (Karaddie [<i>kararehe</i>] nui nui) or Big dog ...<i>ibid.</i> 45 i have many times made a grand Meal in a Kanga Mouri or Native Village on Pippies [<i>pipi</i>], sitting round the Coppre Mouries, each helping themselves till all was done, then i would give the Natives, a dram of Rum to wash it down. <i>(1834,New Zealand (various authors/titles),30,1963 ed.</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> These animals were a disgrace to the <i>kainga</i> [a2-acute], or village, of which they formed part and parcel; ... <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/66</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> About five we reached a small kainga called Kawamomotata where i have encamped for the night. <i>(Nov 5, 1839,Journal,2/163</i>)

>  <b>MACLEAN</b> A bush road, tolerably level for a mile. Then ... on to a small kainga, on the banks of the Otawao; ... <i>(Dec 6, 1850,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1850 Dec 6 Maclean Diary in 1940 Cowan Sir Donald Maclean 59]

>  [The Waikato] runs through ... deep fern fringed with raupo (a bullrush) ...[sic] and the magnificent toi-toi, a gigantic grass whose flower stalk is from 8 to 12 feet and has an exquisite feather of two feet long playing in every air. This grass is ... with the phormium tenax the most remarkable of the open country vegetable products. ... You see many native dwellings & many canoes for they are a most locomotive people, few as ignorant of all but their own kainga as our British Hodge. There are troops of wild ducks & shags, some hawks of large size, bitterns & smaller waterfowl unknown to me. <i>(Mar 25, 1851,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/80</i>) [NOTES: (J.C. Richmond)]

>  <b>LUSH</b> At the Ferry overtook a part of Natives going to Orakei, a <i>Kainga</i> situated on the Koromarama creek. <i>(Apr 21, 1851,Auckland Journals,74,1971 ed.</i>)

>  ... the number of abandoned places of abode or `kaignas' [sic], ... the visible decline of those pahs or kaignas, which come under the traveller's observation ... <i>(1851,Southern Districts of NZ,47</i>)

>  <b>LUCETT</b> The land was cultivated ..., and we noticed many separate `kiangas'. [sic] Smith's occas. spg <i>kaanga</i>, later <i>kainga </i> <i>(1851,Rovings in The Pacific,1/75</i>)

>  <b>SMiTH</b> We continued on for about half a mile when we came in sight of a kaanga [later spg in text kainga]<i>ibid </i>364 we came to a kainga called Hokairorui. <i>(Jan 14, 1858,Notes of a Journey</i>) [NOTES: 1858 Jan 14 Smith Notes of a Journey in 1959 Taylor Early Travellers 361]

>  <b>GRACE</b> We have, more or less, followed the idea of making scholars of the Maoris, rather than in endeavouring to equip them so that they might be of service in the Kaingas (villages) and there promote the common acts of civilized life. <i>ibid </i> 132 Started early and dropped down a few miles to a small kainga [a1-macron] (village) where there are some Taupo Maoris living. <i>(Dec 20, 1858,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1858 Dec 20 T.S.Grace letter in Grace Pioneer Missionary (1928) 79]

>  <b>MANiNG</b> i would give him a piece [of land] quite close to the <i>kainga</i>, where i would always have him close to me; ... <i>ibid </i> (xiii) 206 Twenty years ago a<i> hapu</i>, in number just forty persons, removed their <i>kainga</i> from a dry healthy position, to the edge of a <i>raupo</i> swamp. <i>ibid </i> 236<i> Kainga</i> - A native town or village; their principal headquarters. <i>(1863,Old New Zealand,(i) 14</i>)

>  <b>LUSH</b> ... found that Joshua had gone to the next <i>Kaianga</i> [check] about 8 miles further north. <i>(Mar 19, 1866,Waikato Journals,74,1982 ed.</i>)

>  <b>2/284</b> She rules a large kainga, situated on the Ohinemuri creek, ... <i>(1882,Brighter Britain</i>)

>  A cosy-looking kainga located on the bank of a picturesque bend of the river. <i>(Jan 23, 1896,Otago Witness,50/5</i>) [NOTES: (Morris)]

>  <b>MORRiS</b> <i>Kainga,</i> and <i>Kaika, n. </i> now generally <i>kaik</i>, and pronounced<i> kike</i>, a Maori settlement, village. <i>Kainga</i> is used in the North, and is the original form; <i>Kaika</i> is the South island use. it is the village for dwelling; the <i>pa</i> is for fighting in. <i>(1898,Austral English,227</i>)

>  it is a much-debated question in every kainga or village, whether the European civilisation has fulfilled its expectation. <i>(Jul 1910,Kai Tiaki,103</i>)

>  The simple defendant [a half caste Maori], acknowledged thirty-seven years of age, but said he had four or five brothers who should also help to keep the kainga of Ma over her head in Otaki and provide her with kai. <i>(Jul 1, 1911,NZ Truth,314/6/1</i>)

>  <b>KNiGHT</b> [reference Porirua 1850s] There were several Pas or Kaingas in sight with their gardens where the occupants cultivated their small crops - <i>(1975,Poyntzfield,22</i>) [NOTES: c1920s Eliza McKenzie in Outlook in 1975 Knight Poyntzfield 22]

>  Warriors, Wahines, Kuri, Poaka, Tamariki, Kumeras, Karos, interpreters (1st, 2nd and 3rd grade), Poets Laureate, Poi Dancers, Guides, &c. on application to the Registrar. ...WAiPiRO - - An acursed drink ... TAiHOA - - The Police have the matter in hand POAKA - -a pig PUKEKO A bokakker out of season KORERO A long talk KAiNGA - - A native settlement <i>(1913,Auckland University Carnival Book,27</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> in the rear of the church surrounded by lines of <i>whanake</i> or cabbage-trees (these <i>whanake,</i> now grown to enormous trees, still adorn the old village-site), was the <i>kainga</i> Te Reinga, ... <i>(1922,NZ Wars, The,1/353</i>)

>  <b>FiNLAYSON</b> Only Maoris [26] were there, and one or two rough-looking Pakehas, the kind that cadge from any kainga. <i>(1938,Brown Man's Burden,25</i>) [NOTES: `New Year']

>  <b>WiLEY & MAUNSELL</b> The native [127] village at Patumahoe, he said, was by far the best-kept <i>kainga</i> he had seen. <i>(1938,Robert Maunsell,126</i>)

>  ... Maori Villages: A Tribal Committee may ... define the boundaries of any kainga, village, or pa. <i>(1971,Speeches & Documents,417</i>) [NOTES: 1945 Maori Social Welfare and Advancements Act 1945 in 1971 Speeches and Documents 417 [repeats 64 Vict. above, and states, Section 21]]

>  <i>kainga</i>, place of residence. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/149</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  kainga : unfortified native village. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,174</i>)

>  <i>kainga n</i> -s [Maori] : a Maori village usu. located on low ground - compare PA <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third international,1230</i>)

>  <b>KEENE</b> <i>kainga</i>: village. A tribe usually had a <i>pa</i> (fortified stronghold) as well as a <i>kainga</i>. Many tribes retired to their <i>pa</i> at night for safety. <i>(1963,O Te Raki,191</i>)

>  <b>BRATHWAiTE</b> KAiNGA Unfortified village or even a single dwelling place. Literally, Eating Place. -- <i>(1967,Evil Day,379</i>)

>  <i>kainga, kaika, kaik ... n</i>. N.Z., native settlement, village [Maori]. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),11</i>)

>  <b>THOMAS</b> The meeting house, situated in the centre of the kainga, was the usual type of those days with walls and roof thatched with raupo and nikau, no floor and dividing partitions. <i>(1970,Way Up North,4</i>)

>  <b>FiNLAYSON</b> kainga: village. <i>(1973,Brown Man's Burden,140</i>)

>  <b>BELiCH</b> <i>kainga</i> (unfortified) village <i>(1986,NZ Wars, The,337</i>)

>  <i>kainga n.</i> settlement or village. [M] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,409</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 270]]

>  <i>kainga n.</i> settlement or village. [M] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,409</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 270]]

>  On Christmas Day at the .. kainga, everyone was more or less a "rangatira." <i>(Mar 4, 1916,Wairarapa Daily Times,4</i>)

>  The popular song in the northern kaiangas now is "What's the matter with Father?" <i>(Mar 22, 1913,NZ Observer,7</i>)

>  Only the occasional barking of a dog in one of the kaiangas shows that approach of visitors has not passed unnoticed. <i>(Jun 8, 1938,Auckland Weekly News,7</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [Ma.] small Maori hut or collection of huts.

>  <b>WiLLiAMS</b> On the day appointed we met at this kainga in the presence of about 500 natives ... <i>(Nov 22, 1841,Journal,186</i>) [NOTES: in Porter (1974}Turanga Journals]

>  Mr Woon travelled over his district in all weathers, along the old Maori tracks, under the cliffs, across the swollen rivers and deep swamps, the discomforts of Maori kaingas, his hermit life etc. <i>(1882,Hawera & Normanby Star,Supplement 1</i>)

>  <b>ATKiNSON</b> As a Taranaki settler .. i wished to know if it was safe for us to return to our kaingas ... <i>(Dec 28, 1861,Letter,735</i>) [NOTES: in Scholefield (1960) Richmond-Atkinson Papers i]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  it is about 3 furlongs from the kainga Hursthouse <i>(Apr 15, 1851,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/90,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (J.C. Richmond)]

>  As a Taranaki settler ... i wished to known if it was safe for us to return to our kaingas, but [Gov. Grey] only evaded it. <i>(Dec 28, 1861,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/735,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (A.S. Atkinson)]

>  <b>ANGAS</b> The New Zealander has a fixed and settled habitation: he resides in his pah, which is a fortified stockade; or in <i>Kainga Maori</i>, or native settlement, which is not enclosed, where the houses are scattered about as in a village. <i>(1847,Savage Life,1/332</i>)

### kaio
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [kaeo or ngaio](../K#kaeo-or-ngaio))

### kaipakeha
 
 <i>n.</i> a variety of kumara [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  He said the mericana, or kaipakeha, a sweet potato introduced in the mid-1800s, had similar characteristics to the hutihuti and rekamaroa. <i>(Feb 1, 2007,NZ Herald,1</i>)

### kaiporker
 
 <i>n.</i> ?a wild horse, brumby (broken for riding).

>  <b>SCOTT</b> My wife mounted the Kaiporker. <i>ibid.</i> 74 a mob of half-broken <i>kaiporkers</i> we used to drive into Jock's smithy. <i>(1936,Barbara of the Backblocks,45</i>)

### kairangahau
 
 <i>n.</i> researcher [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  Garrick is a kairangahau in Te Wahanga Kaupapa Maori. <i>(2004,internet - see Notes</i>) [NOTES: www.ncer.org.nz/default.php?cPath=343]

### kairuru marble
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The turn-of-the-century Edwardian villa.. and Parliament Buildings have something in common - the same Takaka marble graces both. This link comes from one of the house's previous owners, Leonard Mills, who was a partner in.. the contracting firm that built Parliament Buildings using kairuru marble from its Tasman Bay quarry. <i>(Apr 10, 1999,Dominion,28</i>)

### Kairuruku Hui
 
 <i>n.</i> a parliamentary body

>  The Kaitohutohu/whakangungu will deputise for the Kairuruku Hui providing advice on functions and events that take place on the Parliamentary complex in their absence. <i>(Nov 19, 2005,Dominion Post,H13</i>)

>  Kairuruku Hui Functions Co-ordinator ... The Parliamentary Service and the Office of the Clerk are seeking the services of a Kiruruku Hui to provide liaison of the support services relating to the Maori component of functions and events that take place on the Parliamentary complex. ... The Kairukuru Hui will deputise for the Kaitohutohu/Kaiwhakagungu for Te Reo and Tikanga Maori programmes in their absence. <i>(Nov 23, 2005,Dominion Post,E8</i>)

### Kaiser
 
 <i>n.</i> policeman

>  A `Kaiser' heard the commotion, and came on the scene only to see Johnston doing a bit of Postle work. The `cop', who could evidently muster up a bit of pace, set sail after the kicking one,. . . <i>(Jan 13, 1912,NZ Truth,342/6/2</i>)

### kaita rongorua
 
 <i>n.</i> [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  KAiTA RONGORUA Personal Assistant .. Key qualities for the position are: *Knowledge of te reo Maori me ona tikanga ... <i>(Nov 11, 2006,Dominion Post,H10</i>)

### kaitaka
 
 <i>n.</i> a whipping top [[?Omit or get early quot from Tregear maori Race.]]

>  But always, somebody would laugh at her and play with her feeble mind as if it was a kaitaka, a top which you whipped with flax to keep spinning. <i>(1977,Listener Short Stories,91</i>) [NOTES: 1970 ihimaera `The Child' in 1977 Listener Short Stories 91]



 
 <i>n.</i> a fine cloak or cape woven from finest flax fibre, bleached, with taniko border, a Maori cloak [Maori].

>  <b>LEE& KENDALL</b> Kai t|aka; A mat so called. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,157</i>)

>  <b>MARKHAM</b> Cacahows are now getting very scarce and a Ki tuck-er <i>[kaitaka]</i> or Mat Cacahow, [with] ornamented borders, is rarely to be seen in the present day. <i>(1834,New Zealand (various authors/titles),40,1963 ed.</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> ... their dresses consisted of the kaitaka and karowai, made of silky flax, and covered with dog-skin mats, or <i>neris</i> [sic: e-acute] made of rushes, as protection against rain. <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/158</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> The flax (<i>korari</i>,) forming the dresses is of two or [176] three kinds; the most coarse, called muka, is employed for working the common garment (<i>kakaou</i>[a1-acute]<i> maori</i>[a-acute].) This flax is also made use of for laying up ropes, lines, &c. The superior dress called Kaitaka[a2-a3-acute], is made from silken flax, (<i>hunga hunga</i>[as-acute].) <i>(1840,Manners & Customs of the NZers,1/175</i>)

>  <b>DiEFFENBACH</b> Kai taka - name of a fine ornamental mat <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/364</i>)

>  <b>ANGAS</b> ... this gay [Maori] damsel ... knew how to set off her charms to advantage; for, over a European dress, she had retained her native ornaments, and wrapped herself coquettishly in a beautiful kaitaka, ... [fn] <i>E kaitaka</i> is the finest kind of dress, made of the <i>phormium tenax</i>, or New Zealand flax; it is highly valued, and is ornamented with a richly embroidered border, in vandyke patterns, from one to two feet in depth. <i>(1847,Savage Life,1/239</i>)

>  <b>MACLEAN</b> The elder men were dressed in their best dogskin and <i>kaitaka</i> mats, some of them had their heads decorated with <i>huia</i> and <i>kotuku</i> feathers, not neglecting their <i>mere</i>[es acute] <i>pounamu</i> (greenstone clubs), and every other ancient emblem of chieftainship ... <i>(May 26, 1848,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1848 May 26 Maclean Diary in 1940 Cowan Sir Donald Maclean 36]

>  <b>MOSER</b> ... in every kind of dress from the most select slop fashions ... to the normal kaitaka (bordered mat) and pihe-pihe of fig leaf simplicity. <i>(1863,Mahoe Leaves,34</i>)

>  <b>ANGAS</b> The most beautiful and valued of all these garments is the kaitaka,' or finest flax mat, wrought out of a species of <i>Phormium</i>, cultivated especially for the purpose, ... <i>(1866,Polynesia,157</i>)

>  My stock of clothes being worn out Rauparaka presented me with a new blanket, and a <i>kaitaka</i> mat. <i>(1866,Chatham islands,15</i>)

>  <b>LiNDSAY</b> The principal sorts of cloak or mantle at present made by the Maoris from their native flax are - 1. The `Korowai,' or shaggy mantle. 2. The `Kaitaka,' or fine mantle; and 3. The `Pokeha,' or rain cloak. Of these, the variety of flax plant called the `Koura' yields the best fibre for the `Korowai'. <i>(1868,Contributions to NZ Botany,78</i>)

>  <b>CAMPBELL</b> "Highly prized and beautiful kaitaka mats.' <i>(1881,Poenamo,205</i>)

>  <b>HAY</b> The kaitaka ... made from a choice variety of flax, has a gloss like silk or satin, and, though thick, is perfectly soft and flexible. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/205</i>)

>  <b>LADY MARTiN</b> Their own native mats were warm and waterproof. ... But formerly they had not only the kaitaka [i-circumflex], made of the finest flax and ornamented by a handsome border, ... <i>(1884,Our Maoris,84</i>)

>  <b>MORRiS</b> <i>Kaitaka, n.</i> Maori word for the best kind of native mat. <i>(1898,Austral English,227</i>)

>  <b>YATE</b> `Requiring from three to four months close sitting to complete one of their kaitakas - the finest sort of mat which they make. This garment has a very silky appearance.' <i>(1835,Account of NZ, An,157</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFiELD</b> Pukaro ended by flinging over my shoulders a very handsome kaitaka mat, which he had been wearing while he spoke.' <i>(1845,Account of NZ, An,i/244</i>)

>  <b>TREGEAR</b> The <i>kaitaka</i> or <i>aronui</i> was a fine white mat of flax without thrums, but with a deep border <i>taniko</i> woven in black, white, and brown [235] patterns at the bottom ... Only chiefs of good position wore the <i>kaitaka. </i> <i>(1904,Maori Race, The,234</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> He was seldom seen in any dress but the picturesque native garments of flax; and a commanding figure he was ... in a finely woven and beautifully patterned <i>parawai </i>or <i>kaitaka</i> cloak. <i>(1922,NZ Wars, The,1/134</i>)

>  <i>~ n.</i> a native mat of fine quality. [Maori] <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,6</i>)

>  <b>RiESCHEK</b> He showed me several beautiful mats, kaitaka and parawai cloaks made of flax, with plaited borders; also a valuable mat made of kiwi feathers, and a patu-punamu, or greenstone axe. <i>(1930,Yesterdays in Maoriland,69,1933 ed.</i>)

>  <b>GUTHRiE</b> Being very tall, [the Maoris] presented a dignified appearance clad only in their silky white <i>Kaitakas</i> with huia feathers in their hair. These finest of Maori mats they wore tied on the right shoulder, leaving that hand ... free to grasp the greenstone `mere'. <i>(1930,NZ Memories,37</i>)

>  Kaitaka : finest form of native cloak. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,174</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> .. she can make those <i>kaitakas</i> and feather mats she is wearing ... That silky <i>kaitaka</i> is a special kind of flax. <i>(1948,Taina,81</i>)

>  <i>kaitaka n</i> -s [Maori] : a mat of fine flax worn as a cloak by the Maoris <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third international,1230</i>)

>  <b>KEENE</b> <i>kaitaka</i>: whipping-top; sometimes called <i>potaka. </i> <i>(1963,O Te Raki,191</i>)

>  <i>~ n.</i> Maori mat used as a cloak [Maori]. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),618</i>)

>  <i>kaitaka ... n.</i> fine Maori cloak. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),11</i>)

>  <b>NATUSCH</b> The coffin, covered with a beautiful kaitaka, was carried to a whaleboat and rowed across to a Maori [143] burial ground [at Stewart island] ... then the women sat by the stream and began the low chanting ... of the Southern taki. <i>(1978,Acheron Journal,142</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The garment was a parawai or kaitaka cloak with a taniko border. <i>(Jul 30, 1933,Na To Hoa Aroha,3/103</i>) [NOTES: (Ngata)]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  i bought a kaitaka mat of E'ou's wife. <i>(Jan 16, 1850,Torlesse Papers,126,1958 ed.</i>)

>  <b>CAMPBELL</b> On this fence were hung almost the whole of the deceased's personal effects - his blankets, the highly-valued <i>kaitaka</i> mat (which has now almost ceased to be made), his musket and double-barrelled gun, cartouch-box, tomahawk, and many other things - all destined to hang there forever, tabooed to the memory of the departed. <i>(1881,Poenamo,Bk3/5/171</i>)

>  <b>COLENSO</b> Nearly in the midst stood Hakitara, a tall Native of the Rarawa Tribe, dressed in a very large and handsome silky white <i>kaitaka</i> (finest and best kind of garment, only worn by superior chiefs), ... the whole of Native (i might truly say of national) design and manufacture. <i>(1890,Treaty of Waitangi,15</i>)

### kaitiaki
 
 <i>n.</i> guardian [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  On Turia's habit of talking to her Maori kai tiaki, her spiritual guardian: "Consider the number of other New Zealanders who consult higher beings on a daily basis," says Salmond. <i>(Sep 16, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),13</i>)

>  <b>HULME & MORRiSON</b> it's the crib i'm kaitiaki for, guardian of & caretaker to: Bill and his mate. <i>(1989,Homeplaces,66</i>)

>  Rather like Gloria Rawlinson vis-a-vis Robin Hyde, the octogenarian Turner (who died last year) had become the kaitiaki of Mander's reputation, writing introductions to her books and analysing her work in far greater depth than anyone else had done. <i>(Sep 26, 1998,Listener (New Zealand Listener),45</i>)

>  Mrs Turia also questioned the ability of the psychologists to deal with Maori issues such as "mate Maori" (Maori sickness) and kai tiaki (spiritual guardians) all Maoris had. "What if i told you i have been visited a number of times by my kai tiaki and had carried out a conversation?" she asked. <i>(Aug 30, 2000,Dominion,1</i>)

>  When everyone is inside - today, perhaps 200 people are here - a kaitiaki whakamoemiti (bell-ringer, guardian of the temple) tells the people to be at pease: "Kia tau te rangimarie ete morehu." <i>(Jul 31, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),35</i>)

>  Sometimes.. the nannies are so keen to take part that the kaitiaki find them standing outside in the cold when they arrived to open up in the morning.There are four kaitiaki at Pukeatua. <i>(Jul 24, 1982,Listener (New Zealand Listener),39</i>)

>  [Title] Kai Tiaki / (The Watcher - The Guardian) / The Journal of the Nurses of New Zealand. <i>(1908,Kai Tiaki,1</i>)

>  To restore the mana of the tribes and to protect their particular interests, one set of Guardians, the Kaitiaki o Manukau, should be appointed by the Minister of Maori Affairs to seek the well-being and preservation of the traditional status of the tribes in the harbour and environs... The leaders selected as Kaitiaki would be vested with mana, and their role would be to uphold the mana of the people. <i>(1985,Report on the Manukau Claim,WAi-8 : 79</i>)

>  [index] Kaitiaki (see Fishing, guardianship) <i>(1988,Report on the Muruwhenua Claim,WAi-22 : 365</i>)

>  The affront is felt by present day Kaitiaki (guardians) not just for themselves but for their tipuna in the past. <i>(1988,Report on the Muruwhenua Claim,WAi-22 / 180</i>)

>  <b>MiNHiNNiCK</b> The appointment of kaitiaki is either determined by tribal kaumaatua, kuia, tohunga or all three over areas including all tribal waterways, fisheries, coastaland marine areas. <i>ibid. </i> 10 The function and role of kaitiaki is not only of guardianship, custodianship and protector but of accountability to tribe. 1993 NZ Statutes (4) Te Ture Whenua Maori Act / The Maori Land Act 1993 p.7. "Kai tiaki" means guardian <i>(1989,Kai Tiaki,4</i>)

>  Under the pilot scheme, an 8-bed house has been set up with a kaitiaki (house mother) to provide accommodation which is less alienating for Maori students. <i>(Mar 20, 1995,NewsVUW (Wellington),17</i>)

>  Under the pilot scheme, an 8-bed house has been set up with a kaitiaki (house mother) to provide accommodation which is less alienating for Maori students. <i>(Mar 20, 1995,NewsVUW (Wellington),17</i>)

>  There is a feeling that kai tiaki, tohunga and multiple gods are still more things that Pakeha have taken away from Maori and that have to be got back... "The trouble is, when a Maori talks about Maori spirituality, it tends to get abused ... But i'm not sure that [kai tiaki] is an awful lot different from the notion of an angel." <i>ibid.</i> 23 "When a Maori leader like Dover or Tariana says they are going back to consult their people at home, they don't necessarily just mean <i>those</i> people. They mean ancestors ... kai tiaki." <i>(Oct 7, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),22</i>)

>  i'm not rubbishing kai tiaki, because i know these things exist. There are a lot of people who use that expression now... Most people think they want to consult with some kind of spiritual figure, be it God or kai tiaki, a holy spirit. <i>(Oct 7, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),24</i>)

>  in fact, says the kaitiaki, what they often got was "much worse". <i>(May 14, 1990,Listener (New Zealand Listener),11</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> guardian, trustee (See [Kai Tiaki](k-kea.md#kai-tiaki))

>  Our supportive team at Poroporo will go out of our way to help you become a kaitiaki of the environment. <i>(Dec 13, 2005,Whakatane Beacon,4</i>)

>  The government would probably pay for the equivalent of a tribal Treaty settlement for a single wet weekend, if the kaitiaki could be tickled up by some cultural ceremony. <i>(May 10, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),14</i>)

>  it is simply not valid to make sweeping judgements that identify pakeha as rapacious exploiters of natural resources and Maori as kaitiaki committed to protect them. <i>(Apr 18, 2004,NZ Catholic,6</i>) [NOTES: Original in Michael King: Being Pakeha Now]

>  Although i am the kaitiaki, the custodian of these treasures, no one has been denied access to them. <i>(Oct 4, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),34</i>)

>  <b>NGATi POROU iN AUCKLAND</b> Your Taura Here seeks a Kaitiaki A Rohe .. *To foster relationships with Te haukaenga, tangata whenua, matawaka ... <i>(Jul 17, 2006,Dominion Post,D12</i>)

>  Matt Maihi, the kaitiaki at the marae, says the key to a good hangi is tending the fire. <i>(Jul 2004,AA Directions,Spring/28</i>)

### kaitiakitanga
 
 <i>n.</i> guardianship, stewardship [ORIGIN: Maori, misspelt or miscopied in 2003 cit.]

>  We want a clean and environmentally sustainable foreshore and seabed for everyone. Maori call this "kitiakitanga", or guardianship. We don't call this ownership. Kitiakitanga is a responsibility of hapu, iwi and us all. <i>(Sep 4, 2003,Dominion Post,B5</i>)

>  The first, on behalf of the Whakatohea iwi, is seeking rangatiratanga and kaitiakitanga - authority and guardianship - over a 50km stretch of Bay of Plenty coastline east of Whakatane. <i>(Sep 30, 2005,NZ Herald,A2</i>)

>  [Heading] <i>kaitiakitanga</i> The Native Bird Rescue Wellington Trust for caring for and rehabilitating sick, injured and orphaned native birds. <i>(Nov 2006,Footnotes,26 / 14</i>)

>  NZ Statutes Resource Management Act 69 "Katiakitanga" means the exercise of guardianship; and, in relation to a resource, includes the ethic of stewardship based on the nature of the resource itself. <i>(1991,NZ Statutes,606</i>)

>  A three year degree programme which examines all aspects of kaitiakitanga (environmental science). <i>(Dec 2, 2005,Whakatane Beacon,10</i>)

### kaiwhiria
 
 <i>n.</i> a small evergreen tree, 6 to 10 m high [Maori].

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Kaiwhiria, <i>Col[enso]. Hedycarya dentata. </i>... Kaiwiria, <i>Geolog. Surv. Panax simples. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),765</i>)

>  Kaiwhiria (<i>Panax simplex</i>). A small, dark-foliaged, 1-foliolate shrub-tree. <i>ibid.</i> 42 Kaiwhiria. Panax simplex, Forst. <i>(1869,TrNZi,1/iii/33</i>) [NOTES: (Buchanan Botany Otago)]

>  <b>BULLER</b> i have sometimes found its crop distended with the ripe pulpy seed of the tataramoa (<i>Rubus australis</i>), or with the berries of the kaiwiria (<i>Parsonia albiflora</i>) [?Parsonsia] [and kareao (<i>Rhipogonum scandens</i>)\}; and it is said to feed also on the leaves of the thistle and wild cabbage <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,154,{1888 2nd edn 1/3}. ed.</i>)

>  <b>HAY</b> The Kaiwhiria (<i>Hedycarya dentata</i>) is remarkable [for its handsome foliage]. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/198</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> `Kaiwhiria, a small evergreen tree, twenty to thirty feet high; the wood is finely marked and suitable for veneering.' <i>(1883,Handbook of New Zealand,129</i>)

>  [Native name] Kaiwhiria .. [Settlers' name] Honeysuckle .. [Family] Monimiaceae <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,121</i>) [NOTES: 1886 T.Kirk in 1902 Settler's Handbook of NZ 121]

>  <b>MORRiS</b> <i>Kaiwhiria, n</i>. Maori name for New Zealand tree [sic], <i>Hedycarya dentata</i>, Forst., N.O. <i>Monimaceae</i>. Porokaiwhiri is the fuller name of the tree. <i>(1898,Austral English,227</i>)

>  <b>TREGEAR</b> The sticks were generally made of<i> matai </i>or <i>kaiwhiria. </i> <i>(1904,Maori Race, The,67</i>)

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> <i> P[arsonsia] heterophylla, A.Cunn. </i>... A tall and slender branching climber, ... <i>Kaiku; Kaiwhiria. </i> <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,440</i>)

>  <b>GUTHRiE-SMiTH</b> The limestone range east of the lake [Tutira] at one time grew ... dense fern, high tutu, koromiko, and a considerable area of `whitey-wood' bush, kowhai, fuchsia, rama rama, ngaio, kaiwhiria, etc., etc., ... . <i>(1927,Birds of New Zealand,2</i>)

### kaka
 
 <i>v.</i> to make a noise like a kaka

>  . the egg-bound mob are merely kah-kah-ing on the kerbstone again. <i>(Sep 25, 1915,Hutt Valley independent,5(245)/</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Environment) [ORIGIN: Maori, attrib. uses]

>  ...a kaka parrot, presumably a young male wandering from Little Barrier island in spring, taking up residence in a macrocarpa at the foot of our garden for a fortnight. <i>(May 2002,Forest & Bird,26</i>)

>  <b>HEPBURN</b> The supper consisted of roast beef, boiled mutton, steak and kaka pies, fowls, tongues, plum puddings, custards, jellies, etc, port and sherry wines, brandy, Scotch Whisky in abundance. <i>(Jun 28, 1856,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1856 June 28 Hepburn Letter from Otago in Journal (1934) 161]

>  ... Few things are better appreciated by travellers than a caw-caw `pasty' <i>(Jul 28, 1866,Lake Wakatip Mail,2</i>) [NOTES: (Griffiths Collection)]

>  <b>BULLER</b> NESTOR MERiDiONALiS. (KAKA PARROT.) ... <i>Native names</i>. Kaka; [several varieties distinguished] <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,39</i>) [NOTES: {1888 2nd edn 1/150]

>  <b>BULLER</b> The Kaka is particularly abundant in the Urewera country, and during the short season the rata is in bloom the whole Maori population ... are out Kaka-hunting. <i>(1888,Birds of New Zealand,1/163</i>)

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> To the Maori, kaka-taking was keen sport, and to be successful demanded first-class mimicry, .. <i>(1905,Where the White Man Treads,20</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Environment) Nestor spp. <i>Nestor meridionalis </i> (Moncrieff) and <i>N. septentrionalis,</i>, a large bush parrot, dull green with brown colouring; North island kaka: brown parrot, bush parrot, coloured parrot, New Zealand parrot, tomahawk. [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  When the lady kakas saw that we'd used up all the snow they invited us in to afternoon tea. <i>(Sep 25, 1912,Otago Witness,16</i>)

>  The old chief in particular was profuse in his generosity, he went into the bush to obtain honey for us; he made his women prepare huge masses of potatoes and salt kaka. <i>(Jul 17, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),56</i>) [NOTES: Original quote from Charles Money, 1860, given in Simpson's <i>A Distant Feast - The Origins of New Zealand's Cuisine</i>.]

>  i pause to watch kaka stripping bark from a dead tree trunk as it looks for insect larvae. <i>(Jun 12, 2004,Dominion Post,E16</i>)

>  <b>YATE</b> Kaka - a bird of the parrot kind; much larger then any other New Zealand parrot. <i>ibid.</i> 162 ... on the image, placed at the nose of the canoe, is fixed a large wig of the feathers of the kaka, or New-Zealand parrot. <i>(1835,Account of NZ, An,54</i>)

Kukupa.], and wattle-birds [fn Glaucopis cinerea, Gmel. Kokako.]. Among the branches of the trees hopped the neat fantail flycatcher [[fn] Muscicapa flabellifera, Gml. New Zealand; ... <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,1/29</i>)
