[Nestor meridionalis]</i> or as it used to be called in parts of Otago, the tomahawk... <i>(1957,Mr Explorer Douglas,184</i>) [NOTES: 1892 Douglas West Coast Report in 1957 Pascoe Mr Explorer Douglas 184]

>  <b>BAUGHAN</b> Kaka (ka'h - kah') <i>(1905,Shingle-Short,72</i>)

>  <b>WHiTE</b> Our introduction to the `Ka Ka,' so called from its cry, or, rather, his introduction to us (for we eat him), was with mixed feelings. <i>(1905,My NZ Garden,68</i>)

>  KAKA. - A New Zealand parrot, the scarlet feathers from the under wing of which were prized by the Maoris for ornamentation, and especially in the manufacture of their feather mats or cloaks. <i>(1917,New Zealand At the Front,xiii</i>)

>  <i> kaka </i> n. <i> a large parrot. [Maori imit. fr. its cry] </i> <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,6</i>)

>  <b>GUTHRiE</b> [kawkaw] The liquid notes of tuis and moko-mokos rang out in unison, and kaw-kaws and [90] parakeets made splashes of colour as the sun caught their iridescent wings. <i>(1930,NZ Memories,89</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> Through the forests the outlaws and the native parrots, the red-plumed kakas, had swept in varying degrees of magnificence. <i>(1936,Passport to Hell,31</i>)

>  <b>AYSON</b> But not always did a kaka's screech end so fortunately: ... <i>(1937,Thomas,45</i>)

>  <b>HOWARD</b> [Stewart island birds include] the fern-bird <i>(Bowdleria fulva) </i>, the bell-bird <i>(Anthornis melanura) </i>, South island Robin <i>(Miro Albifrons) </i>, Tui (<i> Prosthemadera novae zealandiae </i>), Kingfisher <i>(Halcyon vagans )</i>, Yellow-fronted parakeet <i>(Cyanorhampus auriceps) </i>, Red-headed parrakeet <i>(Cyanorhampus novae zealandiae )</i>, Kaka <i>(Nestor meridionalis )</i>; and in the second class: Orange-wattled crow <i>(Glaucopis cinerea) </i>, Blue Heron <i>(Demiegretta sacra) </i>, Weka (<i> Ocydromus </i>) and Kiwi <i>(Apteryx). </i> <i>(1940,Rakiura,323</i>)

>  <i> ka[a]ka[a]</i>, a bird (Nestor meridionalis), brown parrot. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/143</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <i> kaka </i>: brown or green parrot. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,174</i>)

>  <b>SMiTH</b> [cawcaw] Prior to each election the parties hold what is called a Caucus. ... it seems reasonable to suggest that this word was originally spelt "Kakas', the sound made at a meeting of Kakas or native parrots. At this `caucus' the parties `prepare their platform.' <i>(1947,From N to Z,38</i>)

>  Kaka A New Zealand bird of the parrot family. <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),229</i>)

>  <i> kaka n -s </i> [Maori] : an olive brown New Zealand parrot (<i> Nestor meridionalis </i>) that is marked with gray on the crown and red on the face, neck, abdomen, rump, and underwing coverts and that talks and mimics well in captivity <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third international,1230</i>)

>  <i> kaka n </i>. the New Zealand brown parrot [Maori]. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),618</i>)

>  <b>DURRELL</b> <i>(1966,Two in the Bush,42</i>)

>  <b>NATUSCH</b> We have two races of kaka, the brownish Nestor <i> meridionalis septentrionalis </i>... of the North island and neighbouring islets, and the slightly larger <i> N. m. meridionalis </i> ... of the South and Stewart islands, a bird which, though it looks brown in action, has a greenish tinge. <i>(1967,Animals of NZ, The,289</i>)

>  <i> kaka... n </i>. the N.Z. brown parrot [Maori]. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),11</i>)

>  <i> kaka n.</i> a green New Zealand parrot with a long compressed bill. [C18: < Maori, ? imit. of its call] <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,613</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 409; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 270]]

>  <i> Kaka Nestor meridionalis </i> (GMELiN, 1788) ... OTHER NAMES: Brown parrot, bush parrot; ... <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,244</i>)

>  Bird enthusiasts are in for a treat with a good chance of seeing .. tui, bellbirds and kaka in the regenerating forests ... <i>(Nov 2006,Footnotes,26 / 7</i>)

>  i was hailed by the kaka (Nestor Meridionalis). <i>(Dec 17, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,8</i>)

>  <b>Angas</b> ...i obtained a kaka, or New Zealand parrot (nestor meridionalis). <i>(1847,Savage Life,1/274</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> These birds, the ka-kas, are slenderer in build and have smaller beaks than their relative, the sheep-killing kea of the eastern slopes. <i>(1911,Through South Westland,80</i>)

>  A few kiwis, blue ducks and hens were got on the flats.. and caw-caws were fairly plentiful. <i>(1936,Canterbury Mountaineer, The,V/37</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> nonsense; (literally) faeces [ORIGIN: Maori, not in Williams or Ryan in these senses; I've seen 'whare kaka' in bilingual signage with 'toilets' (TD)]

>  <b>Fiu</b> Everyone knew it was just kaka but it was fun and most boys laughed harder once they knew the truth ... <i>(2006,Purple Heart,143</i>)

>  <b>Fiu</b> Mrs Wharenui again interrupted 'That's just kaka, Desuma!' Kaka was a word my Maori mates used often; it was another word for 'shit'. <i>(2008,Purple Heart,174</i>)

>  "Then it all went brown ka-ka. Hopeless!" <i>(Mar 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),78</i>)

### kaka beak
 
 <i>n.</i> (Environment) a native flowering plant, renamed <i>Clianthus maximus </i> (formerly <i>Clianthus puniceus </i>). [also known as parrot's bill or parrot's beak and kaka's beak]

>  Kaka beak (clianthis puniceus)... Dangling from arching branches, its large pea-shaped red, pink or white flowers are most pleasing. <i>(Nov 11, 1993,Evening Post,14</i>)

>  Species most at risk in the wild include garden favourites like kakabeak[sic]. One species of kaka beak, <i> Clianthus puniceus </i>, is down to just one known wild plant, on an island in Kaipara Harbour. <i>(Mar 28, 2009,Dominion Post,A11</i>)

>  New Zealand has two species of Kaka beak. Previously known scientifically only as Clianthus puniceus, most Kaka beak have been recently renamed Clianthus maximus. <i>(Jan 29, 2005,Dominion Post,E13</i>)

>  With so few spectacular native bloomers here it's no surprise that our Kaka's beak, Clianthus puniceus, was a hit with Captain Cook's plant hunters and those who followed them, which may or may not have resulted in its having become rare in the wild. <i>(Aug 26, 2009,Dominion Post,B18</i>)

>  People travelling State Highway 35 over the winter of 2003 will have noticed a great kakabeak flowering season... The blooms have been welcomed by people who still remember when the colourful kakabeak was a common sight. <i>(Jul 2004,Conservation Action,7</i>)

>  <b>ViRTUE</b> She'd sat Guda down in the painting on a grass-covered paddock, surrounded by Red Kaka Beak shrubs in full flower. <i>(1991,Always the islands of Memory,113</i>)

>  Clumps of mountain daisies (Celmisia species) displayed silver leaves and white daisy flowers too. <i>(Aug 1991,NZ Gardener,64</i>)

>  The kaka beak (Clianthus puniceus), was stocky and massed with flowers in an open position. <i>(Aug 1991,NZ Gardener,64</i>)

>  Some have gorgeous flowers, such as the East Coast, North island, plant called Kaka King, or tree kaka beak (<i>Clianthus puniceus var maximus</i>) which has been saved from extinction only because nurseries have cultivated it. <i>(Nov 2, 2002,Dominion Post,F15</i>)

>  it is not unlike the bloom known as the "Ka Ka's beak." it.. is known to Victorians as the "desert pea" of Australia. <i>(Jan 15, 1885,Wairarapa Daily,2</i>)

>  Deer, goat, hares, pigs and rats eat kakabeak and can devastate an entire plant in one feeding visit .. Kakabeak is one of the first plants to grow on open sites like slips. These days it is so rare that it is unlikely to grow near a slip. <i>(Jan 11, 2007,NZ Herald,A7</i>)

>  The white kowhai, or kaka beak, has attracted the attention of an American visitor. <i>(Jan 22, 1924,Otago Daily Times,6</i>)

>  While several native plants - the kaka's beak for instance - are near extinct in the wild, they thrive in garden cultivation. <i>(Sep 1, 2001,Dominion,10</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a barnacle

>  <b>NATUSCH</b> <i>Mitella </i>... is one of the rugged stalked barnacles of exposed coasts;known as kaka-beak, it is edible. <i>(1967,Animals of NZ, The,116</i>)

### kaka bill
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [DNZE](../D#dnze))

>  <i>Kai.ka-billi</i>. (ka.i.k&adot.-b&ibreve.li.), <i>n</i>. [Maori <i>kaka</i> a kind of parrot + Eng. <i>bill.] (Bot.) </i>The glory pea of New Zealand<i> (Clianthus puniceus)</i>; - called also <i>parrot's bill kowhai. </i> <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third international,2025</i>)

### kakaha
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Astelia</i> spp. astelia, bush flax [ORIGIN: Maori, [Ma. <b>kakaha</b> mountain astelia (<i>astelia nervosa</i>)] cf. P.M. Ryan Dict. Modern Maori (1995) 78.] (See [bush flax](bri-byo.md#bush-flax))

>  Now looking safely back from the borderline, but still classified as nationally endangered, is Astelia chathamica, or kakaha, endemic to just two islands in the Chathams group, where it survives in areas that are safe from grazing. <i>(Oct 23, 2004,Dominion Post,E13</i>)

>  Kakaha or Moriori flax (<i>Astelia chathamica</i>) has also doubled in these areas ... <i>(Nov 2006,Footnotes,26 / 7</i>)

>  Other large species are A fragrans (kakaha) and A grandis (swamp astelia). <i>(Apr 7, 2007,Dominion Post indulgence,17</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Kakaha, <i>Hector. Astelia nervosa. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),763</i>)

>  <b>RiCHARDS</b> <i>ASTELiA .. nervosa</i> .. Bush Flax. KAKAHA (strong). <i>(1952,Chatham islands,21</i>)

>  [Ruahines] the large clusters of berries of the kakaha (astelia) glow bright red among the green. This leads us up to about the 3000 foot contour ... <i>(1978,Manawatu Tramping Club Jubilee,100</i>)

>  <b>WiLSON</b> <i>Astelia fragrans</i> BUSH FLAX .. Kakaha .. Big fresh green tussocky clumps up to about 1 m tall .. (= <i>A. nervosa</i> var. <i>sylvestris</i>). <i>(1982,Stewart island Plants,300</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Environment) a bird

>  <b>BULLER</b> SCELOGLAUX ALBiFACiES. (LAUGHiNG-OWL.) ... <i>Native names.</i> Whekau, Ruru-whekau, and Kakaha; `Laughing Jackass' of the colonists. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,21</i>) [NOTES: {1888 2nd edn 1/198}]

### kakahi
 
 <i>n.</i> a species of whale or large porpoise.

>  <b>LEE  & KENDALL</b> Kak|ahi; A certain fish. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,157</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> NZ freshwater mussel, <i>Diploden entulentus</i> (See [mussel](mob-mys.md#mussel),[freshwater](../F#freshwater))

>  Around Rotorua, Maori wet nurses used to feed motherless children or foundlings. if no wet nurses were available, the orphaned babies were fed fresh-water mussels - kakahi. <i>(Aug 16, 2004,Dominion Post,B5</i>)

>  The native freshwater mussels known to Maori as kakahi and to science as <i>Hyridella menziesii</i> (and several related species), are oval shellfish typically between 50 and 90 mm in length. They resemble a pipi in shape,though they possess a black horny covering - the periostracum - like a marine mussel. Kakahi once formed extensive beds in New Zealand lakes and rivers, living shallowly buried in sand or mud, like pipi, rather than attached to rocks in the manner of most marine mussels. <i>(Sep 2003,NZ Geographic,No. 65/16</i>)

>  <b>BRUNNER</b> There is a fresh-water mussel abundant in the [Lake] Roturoa,called the kaiehau [[ed. fn] Kakahi (<i>Diploden entulentus</i>).] <i>(1846,Great Journey,21,1952 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BEATTiE</b> Kakahi - this is given in among the salt-water shellfish, but in Otago this name is used exclusively for the fresh-water shellfish, the sea one being called the Kakihi (or in standard Maori Ngakihi). <i>(1945,Maori Place-Names of Canterbury,62</i>) [NOTES: c1880 South island Maori Lists `Wahi Mahika Kai' in 1945 Beattie Maori Place-names of Canterbury 62]

>  <b>BEATTiE</b> The newcomer's attention was never caught by the kakahi shellfish which is quite common in the Waikaka, but they noticed an occasional koura or freshwater crayfish. <i>(1947,Pioneer Recollections,6,1956 ed.</i>)

>  The freshwater mussel, the kakahi, although the least appetising of all the old-time food supplies in New Zealand lakes, was the most important in story, song and proverb amongst the Rotorua people. <i>(Aug 12, 1924,Otago Witness,6</i>)

### kakaho
 
 <i>n.</i> toetoe (Cortaderia species), or its reed-like stem. [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  Crush the flax roots and heat the juice in a paua shell - part of the potion is applied to the person's anus, a stalk of kakaho is then filled with the flax potion. The end of the stalk is pushed into the person's anus and the other end is blown so that the potion spurts into the rectum. <i>(Sep 8, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),38</i>)

>  <b>Grace</b> it was a modest house really, a house of its time. The walls were made of sawn planks lined on the inside with kakaho. <i>(2001,Dogside Story,137</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> [Word list] Kakaho/Toetoe a tall grass the stem used in place of reeds <i>(Aug 1843,Journal,51</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Arundo <i>conspicua</i>, Forst. ... Nat. name, `Kakaho', <i>Col. </i> <i>(1853,Flora NZ,i Flowering Plants 299</i>)

>  Kakaho, <i>Col[enso]. Arundo conspicua. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),765</i>)

>  ... stems of the Kareao (<i>Rhipogonum parviflorum</i>) and also Kakaho reeds (<i>Arundo conspicua</i>) ... were often used for the same purpose. <i>(1869,TrNZi,1/iii/34</i>) [NOTES: (Colenso Botany North isd)]

>  <b>WiLLiAMS</b> [if this is not a copying error it surely is a misprint for kakaho] ... the huts were always well constructed ..., the thatch of toe-toe being securely fastened ... with the wire-like stems of the ake or metrosideros scandens. The interior was lined with reeds of the toe-toe, [88] kakapo or arundo conspicua. <i>(1932,East Coast Historical Records,87</i>) [NOTES: a1876 W.L.Williams East Coast Historical Records (1932) 87-88]

>  <b>MORRiS</b> <i>Toe-toe</i> and <i>Toi-toi</i>, Maori name of several species of native grass of the genus <i>Arundo</i>, especially <i>Arundo conspicua</i>, A. Cunn. <i>Toe-toe</i> is the right spelling in Maori, given in Williams' `Maori Dictionary.' in English, however, the word is frequently spelt <i>toi-toi</i>. it is also called <i>Prince of Wales feather. </i> <i>(1898,Austral English,472</i>)

>  .. .. there shall be levied in respect of every building constructed wholly or in part of <i>raupo, nikau, toitoi, wiwi, kakaho</i>, straw or thatch of any description [ .. .. [pounds]20].' [1861, 1867 tohi, 1892, 1895] [a number of quotations] <i>(1843,Austral English</i>) [NOTES: 1843. `An Ordinance for imposing a tax on Raupo Houses, Session ii. No. xvii. of the former Legislative Council of New Zealand': [From A. Domett's collection of Ordinances, 1850.] `Section 2]

>  The Rangiatea Church is being renovated, i hear, and the School of Art has been asked to reconstruct the arapaki work. it is i believe all poutama, and kakaho work, the real thing. We have assembled a lot of kakaho at Rotorua, material much cheaper than mock kakaho, but a little more difficult to handle. <i>(Aug 3, 1931,Na To Hoa Aroha,2/120,1987 ed.</i>) [NOTES: Ngata]

>  <b>COWAN</b> And along the river bank the <i>kakaho</i>, the <i>toetoe</i> or pampas grass, once waved its plumes abundantly; ... <i>(1940,Sir Donald Maclean,146</i>)

>  <b>BROOKER et al.</b> <i>Cortaderia fulvida</i> (Buchan.) Zotov <i>Cortaderia splendens</i> Connor and allied species Maori names: <i>Toetoe, kakaho</i> ...The second has been known as <i>Arundo kakaho</i> Steud., <i>A. conspicua </i>Forst.f., and <i>Cortaderia toetoe</i> Zotov. <i>(1981,NZ Medicinal Plants,58</i>)

### kakahu
 
 <i>n.</i> Maori cloak [[Ma. <b>kakahu (-ria)</b> cloak] cf. P.M. Ryan Dict. Modern Maori (1995) 79.]

>  The caller expected the kakahu [cloaks] to be worth about $2000 each. The visitors were out of luck. A kakahu can't be completed in a month... "it takes nearly 12 months out of a woman's life." <i>ibid.</i> 14 Traditional kakahu are made by taking the fibre out of the harakeke [flax leaf] and washing, pounding and drying it in a process that takes months. The weaving in of the feathers then begins. <i>(Mar 10, 2000,Evening Post,13</i>)

>  Some cloaks (kakahu) can take more than a year to complete, says Ms Davis. <i>(Feb 27, 2004,Dominion Post,B8</i>)

>  The two were confined in a whare and were put to making kakahus. <i>(Oct 23, 1890,Otago Witness,27</i>)

>  ...One Man offer'd to sale an Haahow, that is a square piece of cloth such as they wear [fn5] kakahu or woven cloak <i>(Nov 9, 1769,Cook's Journals,1/195,1955 ed.</i>)

>  <b>PARKiNSON</b> A Vocabulary of the Language of New Zealand. [...] Egooree, <i>A dog</i>. Teyka,<i> Fish</i>. Hewhai[a-grave], <i>A skate</i>. Eraperape, <i>A fish called Chimaera.</i> Hepaooa, <i>Ear-shells</i>. Hekohooa[a-grave], <<i>Small ear-shells</i>. [...] Heanoohe[e1-grave], <i>Fern root</i>. [...] Hewai, <i>Water</i>. [...] Tochee, <i>A hatchet, or adze</i>. Hecacahoo, <i>A garment.</i> Opoonamoo, <i>A green ear-ring.</i> Potai, <i>A feather ornament on their head</i>. Emaho, <i>Tataow.</i> Hewaca, <i>A canoe.</i> [...] Orero, <i>To speak, or a speech.</i> [...] Ekeeno[e2-grave],<i> Bad. </i>-- <i>(1770,Journal,126</i>)

>  E'wakka .. A boat or Canoe./ Nooe .. Large./ Kak'ahoo .. Cloth. <i>(Feb 25, 1777,Cook's Journals,3/817,1967 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1777 Feb 25 Anderson Journal in Cook Journal (1967) 3/817]

>  <b>KiNG</b> The old Chief who appeared to be about seventy years of age, had not a visible feature, the whole of his face being covered with spiral lines; on his coming on board he embraced Tooke with great affection; after which Tooke introduced me to him, and after the ceremony of Ehonge (i.e., joining noses) he took off his how, how, [sic] or mantle, and put it on my shoulders. in return, i made him a present of a ha-a-how made of green baize decorated with broad arrows <i>(Nov 19, 1793,Journal,2/.547</i>) [NOTES: 1793 Nov 19 King's Journal in 1908 McNab Historical Records 2/547 [check i or ii: and text]]

>  <b>SAVAGE</b> Kakahou ... A mat, or clothing. <i>(1807,Some Account of NZ,75</i>)

>  <b>COLLiNS</b> Eka-ka-how, <i>Cloth wove from the flax. </i> <i>(1804,English Colony in NSW,561</i>)

>  <b>MARSDEN</b> About three o'clock in the morning (20th) i arose and walked about the camp surveying the different groups of natives; some of them put out their heads from under the top of their kakkahows,[sic?] which are like a beehive, and spoke to me. <i>(Dec 20, 1814,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1814 Dec 20 Marsden Journal in 1932 Elder Letters and Journals 89]

>  <b>ELDER</b> There we made a good fire, and i slept by the side of Whittohee (Whetoi), having for my bed some dry fern and his kakkahow (<i>kakahu</i>), and the canopy of heaven for my covering. <i>ibid.</i> 67 The women employ part of their time in making kakkahows (kakahu),<i> mats, moka </i>(muka), <i>etc., ... </i> <i>(Jun 17, 1814,Marsden's Lieutenants,62,1934 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1814 June 17 Kendall in Elder Marsden's Lieutenants (1934) 62]

>  <b>NiCHOLAS</b> ... Duaterra got the body upon deck, and pressing the knees close to the head, he tied it up in the <i>kackahow,</i> or outside mat of the deceased; ... <i>ibid.</i> 2/ 332 Raiment Ka[acute]kkahow <i>(1817,New Zealand (various authors/titles),1/121</i>)

>  <b>CRUiSE</b> [Tetoro] said he would give thirty of his finest mats for it, and tying a thread pulled from his Kaka-how or upper garment, round the trigger, said it was<i> tabooed. </i> <i>(1823,Journal,23,1957 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1823 (1820 Feb 16) Cruise Jrnl (1957) 23]

>  <b>SHEPHERD</b> The sailors here have a temporary dwelling by dragging their boats up ... and turned them keel uppermost laid a quantity dry fern ... rolls themselves up in a blanket or New Zealand cackakoo or Matt and sleeps upon the Fern. <i>(Mar 25, 1826,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1826 Mar 25 Shepherd Journal in 1940 Howard Rakiura 365]

>  The natives had evidently been confused, for their cockatoos and spears were scattered about on the beach, .... <i>(1826,Boultbee Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1826 Boultbee Journal in 1979 Begg 148]

>  Tarbuka told me, if i would leave my own way of clothing & dress in a cockatoo, i should be well liked, ... <i>(1827,Boultbee Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1827 Boultbee Journal in 1979 Begg 204]

>  <b>EARLE</b> A dozen men, of the largest and most athletic forms, their cakahoos (or mat-dresses) laid aside, and their huge limbs exposed to the red glow of the fire; <i>(1832,Narrative of a Nine Months Residence in NZ,71,1966 ed.</i>)

>  The natives had evidently been confused, for their cockahoos, and spears were scattered about on the beach, ... <i>ibid.</i> 86 Tarbuka told me, if i would leave my own way of clothing and dress in [87] a cokaho|o|, i should be well liked ... <i>(1835,Boultbee Journal,46,1986 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MARKHAM</b> Moyterra ... sent back two large Pigs, and a Cacahow [<i>kakahu</i>] mat, [fn below] in return to Col Arthur, and said he should report this to his People and Friends at the time he gave his Sharkatty or Harkatty [<i>hakari</i>], meaning a Feast, ... he covered himself with his Cacarhow or Native Cloak ... [1963 fn] The word `mat' is a marginal interpolation; below Markham gives the more accurate rendering, `cloak'. <i>ibid.</i> 40 Cacahows are now getting very scarce and a Ki tuck-er [<i>kaitaka</i>] or Mat Cacahow, [with] ornamented borders, is rarely to be seen in the present day. <i>(1834,New Zealand (various authors/titles),30,1963 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MAY</b> `Kakahoo' mats. <i>(1838,Journal of a Voyage to NZ,224</i>)

>  <b>WiLKES</b> The blanket is worn in the same manner as the native kakahu. <i>(1852,U.S. Exploring Expedition,1/301</i>) [NOTES: 1840 Mar in 1852 Wilkes U.S. Exploring Expedition 1/301]

>  <b>POLACK</b> The flax (<i>korari</i>,) forming the dresses is of two or [176] three kinds; the most coarse, called muka, is employed for working the common garment (<i>kakaou</i>[a1-acute] <i>maori</i>[a-acute].) This flax is also made use of for laying up ropes, lines, &c. The superior dress called Kaitaka[a2-a3-acute], is made from silken flax, (<i>hunga hunga</i>[as-acute].) <i>(1840,Manners & Customs of the NZers,1/175</i>)

>  <b>angas</b> ... he wore a very coarse flax cloak of the kind called <i>kakahu, ... </i> <i>ibid.</i> 1/291 [kokahu: phs merely a misprint cf1/262] ... he [Chief Te Kawaw] wore a <i>kokahu</i>, or coarse garment of strips of black and yellow flax. <i>(1847,Savage Life,1/262</i>)

>  All the Maoris to whom he showed the butterfly [Danais sp.] said they knew it, and the old Maoris say it is called `ka[macron]ka[pothook]hu[pothook]', and is in some years very plentiful [in Hawke Bay]. <i>(1874,TrNZi,6/184</i>) [NOTES: (Fereday)]

>  Two small mats, just commenced <i>(kakahu). </i> <i>(1897,TrNZi,29/174</i>)

>  These 100-hour courses help you learn new techniques in kowhaiwhai, raranga, whakairo, taniko and kakahu. <i>(Nov 17, 2006,Whakatane Beacon,13</i>)

>  She discovered kakahu (cloaks), traded or gifted to the Europeans, hardly worn and in very good condition. <i>(Aug 20, 2003,Dominion Post,A15</i>)

### kakahu-kura
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>TREGEAR</b> Feather cloaks (<i>kakahu-kura</i> or <i>huruhuru</i>) were woven of flax ... but in the process of weaving feathers were fastened in by the woof-thread. <i>(1904,Maori Race, The,235</i>)

### kakahu-roimata
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>ELDER</b> `August 15th. - When an husband dies the widow laments and lives in a small house called the aretaua <i>(Whare-taua)</i>, making mats to wrap the corpse in when taken up again. She must not wet her fingers with her spittle while making the mats, called watu-tangi-knitting - crying <i>(Whatu-tangi)</i>; the mat is called kakaho-wroimate, mat (of) tears, or bed-wet-with-tears-while-making <i>(Kakahu-roimata)</i>, a mat of sorrow; a widow of Rangeehoo (Rangihoua) made two for her departed husband and one for herself. <i>(Aug 15, 1822,Marsden's Lieutenants,254,1934 ed.</i>)

### kakapo
 
 <i>n.</i> [ORIGIN: Maori, attrib. uses]

>  ... the double pass and the black birch kakapo bush between the Clarence and the Awatere mentioned to me by `Kaikora' must be to the east of `Barefell's Pass' ... <i>(Mar 8, 1851,Lyttelton Times,7</i>)

>  <b>BARRiNGTON</b> Continued through the forest, alongside the kakapo range till we came to a swamp ... <i>(Mar 27, 1864,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1864 Mar 27 Barrington Diary in 1959 Taylor Early Travellers 404]

>  <b>1/iii/12</b> ... there can be little doubt that as colonization spreads into Kakapo country, this species like many others will rapidly disappear. <i>(1869,TrNZi</i>) [NOTES: (Buller Ornithology)]

>  Among our own party, Mr. McKay was an old experienced bushman and explorer in kakapo country ... At Martin's Bay i met ... an old settler ..., who spent much time ... collecting kakapo-skins. <i>(1891,TrNZi,23/115</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> ... in 1938, between Hodges Creek and the Leslie, and through surrounding country, kakapo tracks were very distinct, a foot wide and clearcut, through the tussock and passing clearly under the logs of occasional fallen trees. <i>(1981,Exiles of Asbestos Cottage, The,114</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Environment) <i>Strigops habroptilus</i>, a rare, dark green flightless bird, found now only in Fiordland; aka green bird, ground parrot, night parrot, owl parrot. [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  <i>Kakapo Strigops habroptilus</i> GRAY, 1845 ... OTHER NAMES: Tarapo, tarepo, owl parrot, ground parrot, night parrot. <i>(1845,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,242</i>)

>  MPs report back that their constituents are unenthusiastic about the Order of the Kakapo. <i>(Feb 3, 1996,Dominion,2</i>)

>  <b>SKEY</b> On pipis he lives - on cockles and eels,/ And lobsters so fine and so red;/ ... / And kakapos stewed for desert; [p.ix ERRATA] Page 17 - 6<i> for</i> lobsters <i>read</i> crayfish. Page 17 - 10 for <i>kakapos</i> read <i>katipos </i> <i>(1889,Pirate Chief and the Mummy's Complaint..., The,17</i>) [NOTES: (`The Hermit')]

>  The kakapo is one of our most unique birds <i>(Jun 11, 1896,Otago Witness,53</i>) [NOTES: (Morris)]

>  <b>MORRiS</b> <i>Kakapo, n.</i> Maori name for the Night-parrot, <i>Stringops habroptilus, </i> Gray. Called also <i>Owl-parrot</i>. See <i>Kaka</i>. The syllable <i>po </i> is Maori for <i>night</i>. Compare <i>Katipo</i>(q.v.). <i>(1989,Austral English,228</i>)

>  <b>GOULD</b> Stringops, <i>owl-parrot - ground-parrot of the colonists.' </i> <i>(1869,Birds of Australia</i>) [NOTES: (Supplement)]

>  <i>Stringops habroptilus</i>. - Kakapo. Both islands and Chatham islands. incapable of flight. Very rare. <i>(1886,TrNZi,18/112</i>) [NOTES: (Martin)]

>  <b>PARKER</b> <i>(1889,Catalogue NZ Exhibition,117</i>)

>  <i>ka[a]ka[a]po[o]</i>, a bird (Strigops habroptilus), ground-parrot; green parrot; night-parrot; once common in both islands, now confined to rugged south-west mountains of South island (po, night, whence night-parrot). in O.E.D., but no macrons. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/143</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <i>Strigops habroptilus</i> The Maori name means night-parrot; bushmen know it as ground-parrot or owl-parrot. ... the name owl-parrot is justified by the facial disk, the soft texture of its plumage, and the nocturnal habits, all of which are qualities found in the owl tribe. <i>(1948,NZ Forest inhabiting Birds</i>)

>  ~ A large green New Zealand flightless parrot that comes out at night. `Po' is the Maori word for `night'. <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),229</i>)

>  <i>kakapo n </i>-s [Maori] : a New Zealand parrot (<i>Strigops habroptilus</i>) with soft green and brown barred plumage that has well-developed wings but little power of flight, lives in holes or burrows in the ground, and has nocturnal habits - called also <i>owl parrot </i> <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third international,1231</i>)

>  <b>McLiNTOCK</b> Kakapo .. .. kakapo .. .. <i>Strigops habroptilus </i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-712</i>)

>  <b>DURRELL</b> <i>(1966,Two in the Bush,107</i>)

>  <i>~ n.</i> New Zealand green nocturnal parrot. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),618</i>)

>  <i>kakapo ... n</i>. N.Z. green nocturnal parrot. [Maori]. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),11</i>)

>  <i>214. Strigops habroptilus</i> Gray, 1845 <i>Kakapo </i> <i>(1970,Annotated Checklist of Birds of NZ,57,1980 ed.</i>)

>  <b>McLEOD</b> <i>Kakapo:</i> A ground parrot living in burrows, flightless and now perhaps extinct. <i>(1972,Mountain World,310</i>)

>  <i>kakapo n</i>. pl. <i>-pos.</i> a ground-living nocturnal parrot of New Zealand, resembling an owl. [C19: < Maori, lit.: night kaka] <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,613</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 409; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 270]]

>  <i>Kakapo Strigops habroptilus</i> GRAY, 1845 ... OTHER NAMES: Tarapo, tarepo, owl <i>parrot, ground parrot, night parrot. </i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,242</i>)

>  <b>BRUNNER</b> They [the Maori guides] returned ...with ten wekas, six kakas, three teal, and fourteen crows or kakapos. [fn] kokakos. <i>(1847,Great Journey,36</i>) [NOTES: ?Brunner's error for kokako]

>  <b>PFAFF</b> When Jemmy the Warrigal and i ... stopped at the Big Grey River, [we] saw a lot of men looking at a big green bird, a Cocyps, and they could not make out what sort of bird it was. At last a Geordie ... came up and settled the question for us by exclaiming, `Why, he is a hawk, kill the ----, kill the ----!' <i>(1914,Diggers' Story, The,112</i>) [NOTES: Cocyps prob. typo for Cocypo]

>  Twenty of the world population of 21 female kakapo laid eggs this year. <i>(May 2002,Forest & Bird,No. 304/9</i>)

>  The kakapo, for instance, lays one egg every second year. <i>(Mar 6, 1890,Otago Witness,35</i>)

>  in a small wine store you're usually served by the person who's tasted and bought every wine on the shelves. When was the last time you talked wine at the New World checkout? Wellington recently lost one of these kakapos of the wine world. <i>(Jul 21, 2004,Capital Times,13</i>)

>  At certain times the kakapo makes a great booming sound. <i>(Apr 1, 1924,Otago Witness,71</i>)

>  The Kakapo is somewhat slow And usually half asleep. <i>(1918,New Zealand At the Front,84</i>)

>  The Order of the Kakapo mightn't yet have the cachet of the Order of the Bath, but not all traditions are worth preserving in a society where Jack's usually as good as his master. <i>(Jan 2, 1996,Evening Post,4</i>)

>  it starts low down in the once-famously stapled stomach, and you're never sure whether it will result in one of his legendary, sulphuric observations or the rumbling laugh. Both sound like a kakapo booming for its mate. <i>(Sep 25, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),16</i>)

>  "There was a sheer drop on one side and i thought, 'Oh my God, i've just killed the last kakapo in New Zealand.'" <i>(Apr 6, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),64</i>)

>  ...they would be given a civic reception in Wellington and the Order of the Kakapo from Dame Sylvia if they turned up. <i>(Mar 7, 2002,Dominion Post,C8</i>)

>  A few years ago truly dry Riesling was as rare as kakapo in Queen Street. <i>(Jul 2001,Cuisine,149</i>)

>  Bishop tells the sad story of Richard Henry who in the 1890s single-handedly ferried hundreds of kakapo from the mainland to predator-free Resolution island in Dusky Sound only to find, six years later, that his efforts had been to no avail because stoats had swum to the island. <i>(Jul 18, 1992,Listener (New Zealand Listener),53</i>)

>  <b>von HAAST</b> The year that the Kie-Kie has three cobbs the Kakapo have three eggs and the year that the Kie-Kie has four cobbs the Kakapo has four eggs, but stranger still the year that the Kie-Kie has three cobbs and a little abortive one that never ripens, that very year the Kakapo has three eggs and one more she never brings out. <i>(1948,Life & Times of Sir Julius von Haast,550</i>) [NOTES: Quote from DOHERTY 1879]

>  i think you'll like my latest poems... They are "Dance of the Totara", "Kakapo, Kakapo! Hail to Thee Slow Parrot!", and the love sonnet "O, Glenys, Let Me Adjust Me Eyes to the Size of Your Creamy White Thighs." <i>(Aug 17, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),94</i>)

>  green-bird - ko'kabboo [kakapo] <i>(1835,Boultbee Journal,109,1986 ed.</i>) [NOTES: c1826-1827 list of Maori words in c1835 Boultbee Journal (1986) 109]

>  Te Warru amused us with accounts of the Kakapo a species of Kiwi Kiwi as large as a Turkey and of the Moia a species of Ostrich supposed to be extinct. <i>(Apr 20, 1841,Journal of Ensign Best,291,1966 ed.</i>)

>  <b>DiEFFENBACH</b> in former times the birds called kiwis (Apterix australes), and kakapos (Centropus?), formed part of the food of the natives, but now these birds have become nearly extinct in the northern island. <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/45</i>)

>  Another bird, called by the whalers the `green bird,' by the natives the kakapo, is abundant on the west coast. <i>(Jul 20, 1844,Nelson Examiner</i>) [NOTES: 1844 Monro Notes of a Journey (Nelson <i>Examiner</i> July 20 et seq.) in 1898 Hocken Contributions 261]

>  <b>HEAPHY</b> Up this stream, the bush natives used to go for the Kakapo, now nearly or quite extinct. This bird ... appears to have been a ground parrot. <i>(1846,Account of an Exploring Expedition</i>) [NOTES: 1846 Heaphy Exped. S-W Nelson in 1959 Taylor Early Travellers 199]

>  <b>BRUNNER</b> On the hills bounding the Grey River i caught four kakapos or green ground-parrots. <i>(1848,Great Journey,81,1952 ed.</i>)

>  ... a great addition has been made to our knowledge of the kiwi and ground parrot (kakapo) peculiar to New Zealand. <i>(Apr 12, 1851,Lyttelton Times,7</i>)

>  Although the Kakapo is said to be still found occasionally on some parts ... of the North island ..., the only place where we met with it ... was at the S.W. end of the Middle island. <i>(Feb 24, 1852,Proceedings Zoological Soiety of London,1/181</i>)

>  <b>HAAST</b> During the night we heard for the first time the cry of the kakapo, and of the large kiwi, ... i was much interested here on observing the tracks of numerous kakapos, or night parrots, ... <i>(1861,Report on Topographical Exploration of Nelson,57</i>)

>  <b>VON HAAST</b> [it was] the best hunting ground that i met with ..., literally alive with woodhens, and many kakapos to fill our larder, so that we could even spare some of our flour. <i>(1948,Julius Von Haast,287</i>)

>  <b>BUTLER</b> There is a green ground parrot too, called the kakapo, a night bird, and hardly ever found on the eastern side of the island. <i>(1863,First Year in Canterbury Settlement,ix/139</i>)

>  <b>ANGAS</b> The extraordinary <i>Stringops habroptilus</i>, or `kakapo' of the natives, is a large green nocturnal parrot ... now extinct ... excepting on the south-west coast of the Middle island, ... <i>(1866,Polynesia,63</i>)

>  [The Maori] ... said that formerly he had often been there to catch kakapos (green night parrots) in a black birch wood above the Awatere pass. <i>(Feb 21, 1851,NZ Government Gazette,31</i>) [NOTES: 1851 Jan Weld Letter in 1851 Feb 21 NZGG 31]

>  <b>HUTTON</b> 46. STRiNGOPS HABROPTiLUS. Gray. Ground Parrot. Kakapo. <i>(1871,Birds of New Zealand,18</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> STRiNGOPS HABROPTiLUS. (OWL PARROT.) ... <i>Native names</i>. Kakapo \{,Tarapo\} and Tarepo; `Ground-Parrot' of the colonists. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,26,{1888 2nd edn 1/177} ed.</i>)

>  <b>HAAST</b> [The Maori judged] its value not by its extent but by the great number of Wekas (Wood hens) and Kakapos (Ground parrots) which ... had here enjoyed an undisturbed existence ... <i>(1879,Geology of the Provinces of Canterbury & Westland,52</i>)

>  46. <i>Stringops habroptilus</i>, Gray. Ground parrot, Night parrot, Kakapo. - it is still abundant in many parts of Westland; notwithstanding the persecution that constantly pursues it for the sake of supplying the requirements of museums and collectors. <i>(Jul 1885,NZ Journal of Science,479</i>) [NOTES: (T.H. Potts]

### kakapo bowl
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Male kakapo use networks of tracks and bowls on display grounds during the breeding season... The same tracks and bowls are thought to remain in operation for hundreds, if not thousands, of years - as long as there are birds to use them. <i>(Mar 2002,NZ Geographic,No. 56/25</i>)

>  <b>MORRiS & SMiTH</b> Looking around the next day, more kakapo tracks and bowls were found. <i>(1988,Wild South,142</i>)

### kakapo garden
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Not until February 1974 was another kakapo discovered, By then it had been established that the birds favoured so-called kakapo gardens - areas where the beech forest had been broken up by an avalanche, creating conditions for faster-growing herbs, tussocks and berry plants to flourish. <i>(Mar 2002,NZ Geographic,No. 56/3</i>)

>  <b>HENRY</b> There are considerable areas in the bottoms of valleys and around landslips where berries are produced in great plenty, and such places are called "kakapo gardens." <i>(1903,Flightless Birds of NZ,21</i>)

>  <b>MORRiS & SMiTH</b> Don Merton was looking for areas which Richard Henry had once described as "kakapo gardens." <i>(1988,Wild South,136</i>)

### kakapo-cuddling
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  "Flax-bag terrorists" and "kakapo-cuddling" are funny but does anyone use them? <i>(Apr 10, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),47</i>)

>  No amount of kakapo-cuddling now is going to make up for that. <i>(May 14, 1995,Sunday Star Times,C2</i>)

### kakaporium
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Back in the mid-1980s, possums had been eradicated from Codfish, and even egg-stealing weka removed, and now, having been blitzed with 22 air-dropped tonnes of poison pellets, the island had been declared a predator-free "kakaporium" - the safest haven the birds would ever know. <i>(Mar 2002,NZ Geographic,No. 56/32</i>)

### kakaramu
 
 <i>n.</i> W.91 Kakaramu[u], kakarangu[u], n. <i>Coprosma robusta, ... = karamu[u].</i>

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Kakaramu, <i>Geolog. Surv. Coprosma lucida. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),765</i>)

>  Coprosma lucida Kakaramu <i>(1870,TrNZi,2/123</i>) [NOTES: (Armstrong Botany Canterbury)]

>  <b>LAiNG & BLACKWELL</b> <i>Coprosma robusta (The Robust Coprosma).</i> A stout shrub, 2 ft.- 12ft. in height... Maori name, <i>Kakaramu </i>or <i>Karamu. </i> <i>(1907,Plants of NZ,396,1940, p. 423 ed.</i>)

### kakarapiti
 
 <i>n.</i> bush-hawk, quail-hawk

>  <b>BULLER</b> HiERACiDEA \{HARPA\} NOVAE ZEALANDiAE. (QUAiL HAWK.) ... <i>Native names.</i> Karearea, Kaiaia, Kaeaea, Kakarapiti, Karewarewa, and Tawaka. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,1,{1888 2nd edn 1/213} ed.</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> HiERACiDEA BRUNNEA \{HARPA FEROX\}. (BUSH-HAWK.) ... <i>Native names.</i> Karearea, Kaiaia, Kaeaea, Kakarapiti, Karewarewa, and Tawaka; but sometimes distinguished as Karewarewa-tara. `Sparrow-Hawk' of the colonists. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,6,{1888 2nd edn 1/220} ed.</i>)

### kakariki
 
 <i>n.</i> a small green parrot, <i>Cyanoramphus auriceps</i> [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  Kakariki are a native New Zealand member of the parrot family. They come in three varieties: red-crowned, the most common but confined to offshore islands; yellow crowned, which had mainland populations but were rarer; and orange-fronted, which were very rare. <i>(Apr 29, 2004,Wellington Contact,35</i>)

>  Two kakariki then fluttered in. <i>(Dec 9, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),47</i>)

>  Their reward is seeing the return of native animals to the island, including kakariki, North island robin, tuatara, giant weta, and forest geckos. <i>(Nov 2006,Footnotes,26 / 1</i>)

>  i shot by the way two very beautiful Paroquets (Kakarekas) <i>(Apr 23, 1841,Journal of Ensign Best,292,1966 ed.</i>)

>  <b>2/192</b> <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,DiEFFENBACH</i>) [NOTES: 1842 Gray's Fauna in 1843 Dieffenbach Travels in NZ 2/192-193]

>  <b>DiEFFENBACH</b> it is also in these open and cultivated spots that the kakariki chiefly lives. This is a small [59] paroquet .. <i>ibid.</i> 2/192 <i>36. Platycercus Novae Seelandiae</i>. Wagl. .. Kakariki of the natives.<i> Dieffenbach.</i> Powaitere of the natives. <i>Yate</i> Po-e-tere [e1 acute, e2 e3 grave] of the natives. <i>Less.</i> Very common in the Chatham islands. -<i> Dieffenbach. </i> <i>ibid.</i> 2/365 Kakariki - a green parroquet, a green lizard. <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,1/58</i>)

>  <b>WiLLiAMS</b> The Powaitere [kakariki?], native pronunciation, is a peroquet [parakeet] of a beautiful bright coloured plumage with a little red under the wings and on top of the head and the natives say it can be taught to speak. <i>(1844,NZ Journal,113,1956 ed.</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFiELD</b> <i>Kakariki.</i>- There are two species of this little green parroquet, (<i>Platycercus Novae Zelandiae</i> and <i>Trichoglossus Aurifrons</i>) neither of them much larger than a canary bird. <i>(1848,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),163</i>)

>  <b>POWER</b> [spg kaka-riki] There are some pot-shooters, who are brutes enough to kill the gaily coloured little parrot, the `Kaka-riki', as he calls to the passer-by from the trees. <i>(1849,Sketches in NZ,72</i>)

>  <b>POWER</b> The little parroquet, `the kaka-riki', with green jacket and red vest, is too much of a dandy to make himself otherwise agreeable ... <i>(1853,Recollections (various authors/titles),345</i>)

>  <b>DRURY</b> BiRDS MET WiTH iN PELORUS .. New Zealand Crow - Kokako of the natives. Parrot - .. or kaka of the natives. Paroquets - two kinds .. called Kakariki by natives. <i>(1855,Sailing Directions,65</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> <i>(1855,Te ika a Maui,404</i>)

>  Platycercus pacificus. Kakariki. Parrakeet. Common. <i>(1869,TrNZi,1/160</i>) [NOTES: (Hutton Birds Great Barrier)]

>  PLATYCERCUS NOVAE ZELANDiAE, Sparrm. Kakariki. Parroquet .. PLATYCERCUS AURiCEPS, Kuhl. Kakariki. Parroquet. <i>(1870,TrNZi,2/64</i>) [NOTES: (Potts Birds)]

>  <b>HAY</b> The Kakariki (<i>Platycercus N.Z.</i>) comprehend several species of small parrot or parrakeet. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/219</i>)

>  <i>Kai.ka-rii.ki</i> (ka.i.k&adot.-r&emacr.i.ke.), <i>n.</i> [Maori. Cf. KAKAPO, in the Dictionary.] <i>(Zoo.l.) 1. </i>A small green parrakeet of New Zealand <i>(Platycercus Novae Zealandiae). </i> <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third international,2025</i>)

>  <i>ka[a]ka[a]riki</i>, a bird (Cyanoramphus novae zealandiae), red-headed parakeet (riki, little; whence little kaka). <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/143</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <i>kakariki</i>: parrakeet. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,174</i>)

>  <i>kakariki</i> or <i>kakawariki n </i>-s [Maori] 1 : either of two green parakeets of New Zealand : <i>a</i> : one (<i>Cyanorhamphus novae-zelandiae</i>) having a red crown<i> b</i> : one (<i>Cyanorhamphus auriceps</i>) having a yellow crown 2 : a small green New Zealand lizard of the genus <i>Lygosoma </i> <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third international,1231</i>)

>  <i> kakariki</i> .. n. small green parrot; green lizard [Maori]. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),11</i>)

>  <i>Red-crowned Parakeet Cyanoramphus novaezelandiae</i> (SPARRMAN, 1787) ... There are four subspecies of the red-crowned parakeet in New Zealand: the New Zealand red-crowned parakeet<i> Cyanoramphus novaezelandiae novaezelandiae</i>, the Kermadec parakeet <i>C.n. chathamensis</i>, and Reischek's parakeet <i>C.n. hochstetteri.</i> OTHER NAMES: <i>Kakariki, New Zealand parakeet, Antipodes red-crowned parakeet. </i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,248</i>)

>  Janeen notices flowers ripped apart by birds. She suspects the kakariki, who regularly dart through the forest like small green and red comets, are the culprits. <i>(Aug 2009,Forest & Bird,333/38</i>)

>  A kakariki chatters in the treetops of the 130-year-old regrowth. <i>(Oct 4, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),28</i>)

>  The threatened extinction of the green New Zealand parrot, the kakariki, is causing naturalists considerable concern. <i>(Nov 15, 1937,King Country Chronicle,4</i>)

>  Two weeks ago a native kakariki (red-crowned parakeet) was spotted by the Bush Trust chairman Bill Winstanley, the first sighting of the kakariki at Otari for many years. <i>(Apr 23, 2003,Capital Times,2</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a lizard

>  Who has seen the kakariki (death lizard) crawl down the open throats of the tribes? <i>(Apr 5, 1879,Auckland Weekly News,16</i>)

>  <b>DiEFFENBACH</b> ... a beautiful green lizard, called kakariki, is especially dreaded, as being a metamorphosed Atua. <i>ibid.</i> 2/365 Kakariki - a green parroquet, a green lizard <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/117</i>)

>  <b>DiEFFENBACH</b> <i>Naultinus elegans.</i> Gray, ... inhab. `Northern island, amongst decayed frees, and running about between the fern. Called <i>Kakariki.' </i> <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/203</i>) [NOTES: 1842 Gray's Fauna in 1843 Dieffenbach Travels in NZ 2/203]

>  <b>BULLER</b> Two sorts of lizards are to be met with, the <i>Tuatara</i> and the <i>Kakariki</i> - ... the latter eight inches long <i>(1878,Forty Years in NZ,498</i>)

>  The name kakarika [sic] (indicative of colour) is applied alike to the green lizard and to the green Parrakeet of our woods. <i>(1894,TrNZi,27/95fn</i>) [NOTES: [Morris]]

>  <b>MORRiS</b> The word <i>kakariki</i> means literally little parrot, <i>kaka</i>(q.v.). and <i>iki</i>(little), the <i>r</i> is intrusive. it is applied also to a green lizard. in Maori it becomes later an adjective, meaning `green.' <i>(1898,Austral English,228</i>)

>  <i>kakariki</i> ... 2 : a small green New Zealand lizard of the genus <i>Lygosoma </i> <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third international,1231</i>)

>  <i>kakariki n.</i> small green parrot; a green lizard [Maori]. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),618</i>)

>  Geckos may be various shades of brown or grey, but the North island green gecko, or kakariki, is the commonest in the Urewera. its Maori name comes from the chattering sound it makes, like that of its namesakes, the red and yellow-crowned parakeets. <i>(1983,Land of the Mist,76</i>)

### kakawai
 
 <i>n.</i> a bird (See [hakuwai](h-hea.md#hakuwai))

>  The most incredible is the strange tale of the mystery bird - named, because of its call, the kakawai. <i>(Oct 14, 1953,NZ Observer,16</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> fish, sic, not kahawai.

>  <b>BUiCK</b> it has been reported that [moas] caught the small fresh-water fish - the kokopu, the inanga, the kakawai, and the karohi (swamp spotties). <i>(1937,Moa-Hunters of NZ,236</i>)

### kakawariki
 
 <i>n.</i> a parakeet (See [kakariki](k-kea.md#kakariki))

>  <b>BULLER</b> PLATYCERCUS NOVAE ZEALANDiAE. (RED-FRONTED PARRAKEET.) ... <i>Native names.</i> Kakariki, Kakawariki, Powhaitere, Porere, and Torete ...[59] The Red-fronted Parrakeet is very generally dispersed over the whole country - but is more plentiful in the southern portion of the North island than in the far north, where the yellow-fronted species predominates. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,58,1888 2nd edn 1/137 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> PLATYCERCUS AURiCEPS. (YELLOW-FRONTED PARRAKEET.) ... <i>Native names.</i> Kakariki, Kakawariki, Powhaitere, Porere, and Torete ...[59] The Red-fronted Parrakeet is very generally dispersed over the whole country - but is more plentiful in the southern portion of the North island than in the far north, where the yellow-fronted species predominates. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,62,1888 2nd edn 1/142 ed.</i>)

>  <i>kakariki or kakawariki n </i>Maori] 1 : either of two green parakeets of New Zealand : a : one (<i>Cyanorhamphus novae-zelandiae</i> having a red crown b : one (<i>Cyanorhamphus auriceps</i>) having a yellow crown 2 : a small green New Zealand Lizard of the genus <i>Lygosoma </i> <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third international,1231</i>)

### kakeno
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [kekeno](kee-kiw.md#kekeno))

### kaki
 
 <i>n.</i> (Environment) black stilt, an endangered indigenous wading bird. [ORIGIN: Maori, Kaki are endangered due to introduced predators, widespread habitat loss, and hybridisation with poaka (pied stilt).]

>  in the far-off Mackenzie Country, the black stilt, or kaki, struggles to postpone its date with extinction... This kaki flew into a pylon and broke its wing, so now can't mate... The black stilt is not a striking bird, but it has its enthusiasts. <i>(Oct 2, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),32</i>)

>  From August to February, [the braided rivers of the eastern South island] are the only nesting grounds for kaki, the black stilt, which is now the world's rarest wading bird with only 40 adults and only four breeding pairs in the wild. <i>(Nov 1999,AA Directions,48</i>)

>  Jim joined with DOC to fence off a large wetland in the Ahuriri valley to keep cattle out, and assisted with black stilt/kaki releases. <i>(Jun 2007,Conservation Action,17</i>)

>  Kaki evolved in isolation in New Zealand for about one million years and are different from poaka [pied stilt] which arrived from Australia in the early 1800s. Kaki and poaka have different plumage and behavioural differences. <i>(Nov 2007,Open Space,71/14</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> HiMANTOPUS NOVAE ZEALANDiAE. (BLACK STiLT.) ... <i>Native name\{s\}</i>.- Kaki \{and Tuarahia; Torea- pango (of Arawa tribe)\}. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,205,{1888 2nd edn 2/24} ed.</i>)

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> BLACK STiLT <i>Himantopus novaezealandiae Maori name</i>: Kaki. <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,148</i>)

>  <i>184. Himantopus novaexealandiae</i> Gould, 1841 <i>Black Stilt (Kaki) </i> <i>(1970,Annotated Checklist of Birds of NZ,51,1980 ed.</i>)

>  <i>Black Stilt Himantopus novaezealandiae </i> GOULD, 1764 ... Now only 40 to 50 birds breed in inland river valleys of South Canterbury and North Otago. OTHER NAME: <i>Kaki. </i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,214</i>)

>  One of the world's rarest birds - the black stilt, or kaki - is making a determined fightback with help from the Department of Conservation's captive breeding facility in Twizel. <i>(Sep 16, 2001,Sunday Star Times,A14</i>)

### kakika
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Kakika, <i>Geolog. Surv. Senecio glastifolius. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),765</i>)

### kakino
 
 <i>adj.</i> bad, treacherous

>  <b>PARKiNSON</b> A Vocabulary of the Language of New Zealand. [...] Egooree, <i>A dog</i>. Teyka,<i> Fish</i>. Hewhai[a-grave], <i>A skate</i>. Eraperape, <i>A fish called Chimaera.</i> Hepaooa, <i>Ear-shells</i>. Hekohooa[a-grave], <<i>Small ear-shells</i>. [...] Heanoohe[e1-grave], <i>Fern root</i>. [...] Hewai, <i>Water</i>. [...] Tochee, <i>A hatchet, or adze</i>. Hecacahoo, <i>A garment.</i> Opoonamoo, <i>A green ear-ring.</i> Potai, <i>A feather ornament on their head</i>. Emaho, <i>Tataow.</i> Hewaca, <i>A canoe.</i> [...] Orero, <i>To speak, or a speech.</i> [...] Ekeeno[e2-grave],<i> Bad. </i> <i>(1770,Journal,126</i>)

>  <b>BAiLEY & ROTH</b> Kakino Georgy Grey, You have let us [sc Maoris] get away, <i>(1967,Shanties by the Way,37</i>) [NOTES: 1864 Oct 29 Charles Thatcher in 1967 Bailey and Roth Shanties By the Way 37]

>  <b>ALEXANDER & CURRiE</b> <i>Kakino:</i> treacherous. <i>(1906,Treasury of NZ Verse,290</i>)

>  <b>ALEXANDER & CURRiE</b> <i>Kakino:</i> treacherous. <i>(1906,Treasury of NZ Verse,290</i>)

>  <i>kakino</i>, kino, bad; ka, verbal particle here used to give emphasis: a mother might say ka kino! - bad boy! <i>(Jun 1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/151</i>)

 <i>n.</i> (See [kakahu](k-kea.md#kakahu))
