 <i>n.</i> (See [korari](kiw-kwi.md#korari))

### kale
 
 <i>n.</i> money, cash (cf kail @n)

>  Everybody ... must think every day of money, but although one may think `money' he may call it `gonce', `oof', `hoot', `stuff', `brass', `ooftish', `boodle', `cash', `gilt', `kale', or a dozen other ... things. <i>(Jul 11, 1921,Quick March,13</i>)

>  <b>COOZE</b> if you want to make kale... <i>(1946,Ten Bob Each Way,8</i>)

>  <b>DAViN</b> A man would get very meskeen too, losing his kale on Two-up. <i>(1947,For the Rest of Our Lives,147</i>)

>  <b>BAXTER</b> Tony would have the kale. But he did not like putting the bite on Tony. <i>(1985,Horse,43,1962 ed.</i>) [NOTES: Quick and the Dead]

>  i might give you a hand to knock off some of that kale. <i>(Mar 9, 1955,NZ Truth,4</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> cultivated NZ (distinct from Cabbage) (See [curly kale](../C#curly-kale))

>  <b>YATE</b> <i>(1835,New Zealand (various authors/titles),76</i>)

>  <b>FORSTER</b> A little brandy & water commonly called <i>grog</i> [by the seamen] or Spruce beer with a little brandy & sugar commonly called <i>kalibogas </i>is the liquor drank on these [`Marooning'] Expeditions. <i>(Jul 7, 1773,Resolution Journal of J.R. Forster, The,2/271</i>) [NOTES: ??? Polynesian (?Ma.) ?karipoka or Caribbean]

### kaloota
 
 <i>n.</i> behind

>  Some of these people i would like to give [a] good swift kick up the kaloota, particularly those little darlings who drink with their pinkies pointed in the air and think that it's smarter to be trendy than just plain sensible. <i>(Sep 6, 2003,Dominion Post,E7</i>)

### kaloss
 
 <i>adj.</i> finished

>  <b>PARTRiDGE</b> <i>Kaloss</i>. Finished: Australian and New Zealand troops in North Africa: 1940-2. Arabic word <i>(1949,Dictionary of Slang Addenda,1090</i>)

### kamahi
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Weinmannia</i> spp. often confused in popular terminology with beech (birch) species, esp. perhaps <i>towai</i> through similarity with <i>tawhai</i> (beech), hence popular misapplication of <i>kamahi</i> to <i>Nothofagus </i>spp.: some distinguish <i>towai</i> as <i>W. sylvicola</i> only; others apply <i>towai</i> to both spp. (so Williams) reserving <i>kamahi</i> (and <i>tawhero</i>) for W. racemosa only; a tall, spreading forest tree [ORIGIN: Maori] (See [red birch](ra-red.md#red-birch))

>  Karmahi, <i>Hector. Weinmannia silvicola</i> and <i>racemosa. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),765</i>)

>  The flat land and low spurs are covered with the common species of Pines and Birch, such as Rimu, ...Weinmannia (Karmahi), and Fagus (Tawai) ... <i>(1869,TrNZi,1/iii/4</i>) [NOTES: (Hector)]

>  <b>HARPER</b> There is also the rata tree, the prince of firewoods, ... miki-miki, kamahi, or so-called red-birch, white and black birches (which are really beeches), the mountain broom, ribbon wood, broad leaf, totara, and many others, ... and always available in the Ranges [sc. Alps]. <i>(1896,Pioneer Work in the Alps of NZ,35</i>)

>  24 Kamahi slab. <i>(1890,PWD Catalogue of Timbers,16</i>)

>  Kamai is called black birch in the Catlin River District and Southland, which name is given on account of a supposed resemblance to the `birches', or more correctly, `beeches', a number of which occur in that locality. i cannot understand how such an idea could have originated, for except in the ease of the bark of one there is not the slightest resemblance between the birches and kamai. Whatever be the reason, the misapplication of names is complete, for the birches are still commonly called kamai in Southland. <i>(1876,TrNZi,9/148</i>) [NOTES: Blair]

>  <b>STRACHAN</b> ...mountain birch or kaimai.... <i>(1892,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1892 Letter to Strachan in 1957 Pascoe Mr Explorer Douglas 175]

>  <b>LAiNG & BLACKWELL</b> <i>Tawhero & W. sylvicola; Kamahi W. racemosa</i>. Kamahi ... is particularly common in the forests of Westland where it is usually termed red birch (!), but most of the popular names there are misnomers. The foliage of the kamahi is much used particularly in Christchurch for decorative purposes, and sold under the name `red birch'. <i>(1940,Plants of NZ,197</i>)

>  (And) <i>Nothofagus fusca </i> <i>(1877,TrNZi,9/</i>)

>  (And) <i>Nothofagus Menziesii </i> <i>(1877,TrNZi,9/</i>)

>  The kamahi, veronica and fuchsia are also in bloom. <i>(Jan 8, 1924,Otago Witness,65</i>)

>  Racemes of small white flowers of the red birch - kamahi in part of the South island, tawhera in the North island, Weinmannia racemosa to botanists.. are reflected in magic mirrors while they drink the waters of the lake. <i>(Nov 23, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,17</i>)

>  The kamahi, veronica and fuchsia are also in bloom. <i>(Jan 8, 1924,Otago Witness,65</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> Dick.. had found.. a big old hollow-butted kamahi (a sort of red birch) with a fair-sized hole among its twisting roots. <i>(1965,Open Country,72</i>)

>  Kamahi, which can no longer be used for chipping because of an export ban, had a particularly nice grain that could make attractive furniture. <i>(Apr 16, 1993,North & South,15</i>)

>  The flat land and low spurs are covered with the common species of Pines and Birch, such as Rimu, ...Weinmannia (Karmahi), and Fagus (Tawai) ... <i>(1869,TrNZi,1/iii/4</i>) [NOTES: (Hector)]

>  Towai, or Karmai (<i>Weinmannia racemosa</i>). A beautiful large tree, especially when in flower. <i>(1869,TrNZi,1/iii/37</i>) [NOTES: (Buchanan Botany Otago)]

>  (And) [KARM <i>(1872,Catalogue of the Colonial and Vienna Exhibition</i>)

>  [KARMAi] <i>Weinmannia racemosa </i> <i>(1877,TrNZi,9/</i>)

>  <b>HAY</b> The Kamahi (<i>Weinmannia racemosa</i>) is a small tree. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/191</i>)

>  <b>HECTOR</b> <i>Weinmannia racemosa</i>, Forst. Towhai, Kamahi.--A large tree; trunk 2-4 feet in diameter, and 50 feet high. <i>(1883,Handbook of New Zealand,132</i>)

>  <b>FEATON</b> WEiNMANNiA RACEMOSA. (<i>Forst</i>.) ... The `TAWHERO' OR `KAMAi'. This handsome and useful tree is found in both islands; ... in Otago the tree is popularly known as the `Black Birch'. <i>ibid.</i> 177 Kamai [Maori name] .. Birch [Settlers' name] .. Weinmannia racemosa .. Tree <i>(1889,Art Album NZ Flora,137</i>)

>  The kahikatea stands next in abundance, and is closely approached by the kamai, while the miro, matai, totara, Hall's totara, are generally distributed; but the northern rata and pukatea scarcely occur south of Greymouth. <i>(1894,NZ Official Yearbook,330</i>) [NOTES: (T. Kirk)]

>  <i>(1876,TrNZi,9/148</i>) [NOTES: W. N. Blair]

>  Kamahi .. ... Wood hard, strong, and firm, liable to twist when exposed; ... grain prettily marked <i>(1890,PWD Catalogue of Timbers,10</i>)

>  <i>(1894,NZ Official Yearbook,330</i>) [NOTES: (T. Kirk)]

>  <b>MORRiS</b> <i>Karmai, n.</i> used by settlers in South island of New Zealand for <i>Towhai</i>(q.v.), a New Zealand tree, <i>Weinmannia racemosa</i>, Forst. N.O. <i>Saxifrageae</i>. Kamahi is the Maori, and <i>Karmai</i>, or <i>Kamai</i>, the corruption. <i>(1898,Austral English,242</i>)

>  The timbers of commercial value are totara, rimu, miro, matai, rata, and kamahi, in mixed bushes; but <i>Fagus fusca</i> and other beeches predominate on the high lands between the lakes and the fiords on the west coast. <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,30</i>)

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> <i> W[einmannia] racemosa, Linn. ... Towai; Kamahi. </i>Very closely allied to <i>W. Sylvicola</i> [Tawhero]. <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,139</i>)

>  in the rain-forest ...young kamahis are very rarely found germinating on the ground ... <i>(1925,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,8/96</i>)

>  <b>LAiNG & BLACKWELL</b> <i>(The Kamahi). A tree from 70 ft.- 90 ft. in height, with larger leaves and flowers than those of <i>W. sylvicola. </i> <i>(1940,Plants of NZ,197</i>)

>  <i>ka[a]mahi,</i> a tree (), with koromiko-like flowers; whence sometimes Christmas-tree because the upright tall flowers look like candles. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/151</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <b>LEVY</b> ... such species as lancewood, karamu, toro, ramarama, koromiko, kanono, kohuhu, kawakawa, hangehange, rangiora, five finger, patete, toru, tree daisies, broadleaf, kamahi, mahoe, tarata, karo, lacebark, wineberry, fuchsia, kowhai, with tree ferns, weki, and mamuku. Toetoe, phormium, cabbage tree, with swamp coprosma and hupiro may figure as a developmental phase on wet soils. <i>(1951,Grasslands of NZ,1970</i>)

>  kamahi The Maori mame of a tall New Zealand forest tree. <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),229</i>)

>  <i> kamahi n</i> -s [Maori] : a New Zealand tree () of the family Cunoniaceae that yields timber and firewood - called also <i>towai </i> <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third international,1231</i>)

>  <b>McLiNTOCK</b> Kamahi .. .. kamahi .. .. <i>Wienmannia racemosa </i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-712</i>)

>  <i>kamahi</i> See <i>towai. </i> <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),11</i>)

>  <i>kamahi</i> [s] L.f. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,41</i>)

>  <i>kamahi n.</i> tall NZ hardwood tree with dark timber. [M] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,409</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> Weinmannia silvicola

>  <b>FEATON</b> WEiNMANNiA SiLViCOLA (<i>Banks & Sol</i>.) ... The `TOWAi'.- ... The wood is similar in quality to its ally <i>W. racemosa,</i> is denser, and of finer grain; ... [136] This tree is sometimes called Tawhero, or Kamai, and is not unusually confounded with <i>W. racemosa,</i> the Tawhero pr Kamai proper. in the same manner the tawhero is confused with this species by the uninitiated. <i>(1889,Art Album NZ Flora,135</i>)</i>

### kamakama
 
 <i>adj.</i> talkative; high-spirited [Jim writes: Not in DNZE and not known to me.]

>  <b>GRACE</b> Rose is the hardcase one in the family, the kamakama one. <i>ibid.</i> 153 Rose made a good impression with her kamakama ways and Jane's two nuisance kids took a liking to her straight away. <i>(1972,Landfall,152</i>) [NOTES: also in 1972 Grace `A Way of Talking' in 1975 NZ Short Stories iii 185]

### kamana
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BEATTiE</b> Totokepio is the `little diver' of the settler to distinguish it from the `big diver' (the crested grebe). The Canterbury compiler spells it as given, but in Southland the name is Totokipio, and if taken in sections To-toki-pio it is easy to pronounce. The kamana or crested grebe is not mentioned. <i>(1945,Maori Place-Names of Canterbury,64</i>)

### kamate kamate
 
 <i>n.</i> the traditional All Black haka [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  .. calls by All Black captain Tana Umaga and senior players for something that better represented them had prompted the new haka, an alternative to the more traditional Kamate Kamate. <i>(Aug 29, 2005,NZ Herald</i>)

### kamokamo
 
 <i>n.</i> a stubby vegetable marrow, Cucurbita pepo. [ORIGIN: Maori, possibly derived from <i>cucumber</i>]

>  <b>iHiMAERA & LONG</b> Not like sugarbags at all, not rough nor having the earth smell of kumara and kamokamo. <i>(1982,into the World of Light,247</i>)

>  KAMOKAMO, excellent for picking. Ph 308 6240. <i>(Jan 18, 2006,Whakatane Beacon,14</i>)

>  <b>TUWHARE</b> She would make doughboys to go with the kamo-kamo. <i>(1972,Sapwood & Milk,15</i>)

>  <b>HAY</b> The juice was strained from the berries.. and the liquor fermented and bottled in the shell of a kuma-kuma. This was a gourd, resembling a vegetable marrow. <i>ibid.</i> 33 The Maoris grew.. a kind of a gourd they called "Cooma-Cooma". <i>(1915,Reminiscences of Earliest Canterbury,19</i>)

>  Spread an old newspaper on the floor and on it place a steaming pot of puha, kamo-kamo, riwai, brisket-on-the-bone and dumplings what we call: doughboys. <i>(Sep 7, 1985,Listener (New Zealand Listener),12</i>)

>  <b>EDWARDS</b> We need a big pot of boiled potatoes and kamokamo, some corn, and we'll have our lunch. <i>(1992,Mihipeka,29</i>)

>  The garden has produced well, despite a not so good growing season, with crops of kamo kamo, kumara, Maori potatoes, pumpkin, butternuts, watermelon, puha and even saffron. <i>(May 2005,Kokiri Paetae,16</i>)

>  Kamokamo was one of the foods brought to Aotearoa on the great migration. A bit like courgette, it's part of the Maori staple diet. <i>(2009,Mana,87/59</i>)

>  <b>WiLLiAMS</b> <i>Kamokamo, </i>n. <i>vegetable marrow. </i> <i>(1871,Dictionary of the Maori Language</i>) [NOTES: also 1892 edition, p. 49]

>  <b>WiLLiAMS</b> Fruit of the gourd (possibly from<i> cucumber</i> and restricted to imported gourds. <i>ibid.</i> 109 <i>(1917,Dictionary of the Maori Language,109</i>) [NOTES: 1957 ed. p. 92]

>  <b>COWAN</b> [the reference is to four surveyors captured by Hopeta in 1882] [Hopeta said] `We fed them with boiled potatoes with the skins on, kumi-kumi, and eels.' <i>Kamokamo</i> (i) n. <i>Fruit of the gourd. </i> Possibly from <i>cucumber</i>, and restricted to imported gourds.) [so 1957/66] <i>(1916,Bush Explorers</i>)

>  <b>GUTHRiE-SMiTH</b> kumi [=??? check] <i>(1928,Tutira,65</i>)

>  <b>ZiMMERMAN</b> For to season the large lump of home cured corned beef, she had picked a heap of the tender end-shoots of a squash vine and had boiled them as she would <i>puha </i>- and the result was <i>kamokamo,</i> and it was good. <i>(1946,Where the People Sing,75</i>)

>  <i>(1950,NZ Journal of Agriculture,73/2</i>) [NOTES: also (Feb 171/1, June 544/1)]

>  <b>HiLLiARD</b> `...We should put in some kamokamo too.' You mean kumi kumi' `No, i don't. i mean kamokamo. Stupid pakeha saying, come-ee, come-ee.'. <i>(1978,Glory and the Dream, The,17</i>)

>  <b>TUWHARE</b> Kamo-kamo: young marrow; squash <i>(1979,Sapwood & Milk,41</i>) [NOTES: see also p. 15]

>  it is called kamokamo, sometimes spelled kumukumu, less often kumakuma, cooma-cooma or kumi-kumi, and very often sloppily pronounced `cum-cum' for short.... By 1936 (at the very latest) the kamokamo was recognised as a commercial variety of squash, since it was listed as such in a book `Vegetable Growing in New Zealand' published that year. <i>(Feb 8, 1983,Evening Post,26</i>)

>  Then, i'd roll out my threadbare Whaariki/ - to help you remember to take your boots/ off - spread an old newspaper on the floor/ and on it place a steaming pot of puha,/ kamo-kamo, riwai, brisket-on-the-bone and/ dumplings what we call: doughboys. <i>(Sep 7, 1985,Listener (New Zealand Listener),12</i>) [NOTES: (Hone Tuwhare)]

>  i'm always relieved when i see a pile of kamokamo at the greengrocer's because this means that this small, sweet tender marrow, which is part of our culinary heritage, hasn't been squeezed out by zucchini. <i>(Mar 6, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),43</i>)

### kanae
 
 <i>n.</i> the mullet, <i>Mugil cephalus</i> (See [grey mullet](gra-gyp.md#grey-mullet))

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> Kan|ae, s. The mullet fish. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,158</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> Some deep banks lie off the east coast, on which the <i>kanai</i>, or mullet, <i>wapuka</i>, or cod-fish, and the <i>kahawai</i> [a1,a2-acute], or colourless salmon, abound. <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/322</i>)

>  <b>DiEFFENBACH</b> 40. <i>Mugil forsteri</i>. - ... Polack says that mullets are named by the natives `Kanai,' but we do not know whether this be the species he means or not. <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/211</i>) [NOTES: 1842 Gray's Fauna (Richardson's Fish) in 1843 Dieffenbach Travels in NZ 2/211]

>  <b>COLENSO</b> A large lake [Waikare] in which are quantities of Kanae (<i>Mugil-</i>) and Patiki (Pleuronectes-), neither of which are found in the Waikato. <i>(1844,Excursion in the Northern island of NZ</i>) [NOTES: 1844 Colenso Excurs Nthn. isd in 1959 Taylor Early Travellers 42]

>  <b>ANGAS</b> From the summit of Taupiri the fresh-water lake of Waikari is seen: it is remarkable for having a salt stream running through it, in which the sea-fish called <i>kani</i> are caught; <i>(1847,Savage Life,2/42</i>)

>  English Name Native Name Rock cod Whapuka Snapper Tamuri Black rock-fish Parore Mullet Kanae <i>(1849,NZ Journal,9/125</i>) [NOTES: (quoting Anglo Maori Warder Oct 5)]

>  English Name Native Name Rock cod Whapuka Snapper Tamuri Black rock-fish Parore Mullet Kanae <i>(1849,NZ Journal,9/125</i>) [NOTES: (quoting Anglo Maori Warder Oct 5)]

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> Near this place [Pawera near Wangaehu] there is a large lake ... it is filled with kanae and soles which is remarkable as they are salt water fish. <i>(Mar 2, 1852,Journal,7/278</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> Of the Fam. Mugiloideoe, mugil fosteri, <i>kanae, </i>this is a delicious fish, it frequents the mouths of rivers, but it is not usual to find it beyond the influence of the tide. <i>(1855,Te ika a Maui,624</i>)

>  Tu 25 Dec Mosquitoes rather worse than usual ... Magnificent breakfast of kanae & oysters. Talked read & listened. <i>(Dec 25, 1860,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/671,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (A.S. Atkinson)]

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> On the banks of the Waikato are two lakes ... Waikari and Wangape, both of considerable size; the latter is remarkable for having the Kanae, a sea-fish, in its waters. <i>(1868,Past and Present of NZ,196</i>)

>  <b>HECTOR</b> 57. KANAE. ... The Grey Mullet (<i>Mugil Perusii</i>) is a very familiar fish to residents in the northern ports of the colony, ... <i>(1872,Edible Fishes,113</i>)

>  <b>HAY</b> in the bays and tidal rivers are the mango or sharks - the most highly-prized food-fish - the tamure or schnapper, the whapuka or rock-cod, the kahawai or mackerel, the porahi or herring, the kanae or mullet, the patiki or sole, and many others. On the shores are oysters, mussels, cockles, mutton-fish, crabs, and other shell-fish in profuse abundance. in the fresh-water creeks are eels (tuna), lampreys (pipiharau), and whitebait (inanga). <i>ibid.</i> 231 We prefer the substantial schnapper, the goodly whapuka or kanae, or the luscious poraki. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/151</i>)

>  <b>SHERRiN</b> KANAE (<i>Mugil Perusii</i>). ... The grey-mullet is a very familiar fish to residents in the northern parts of the Colony, where it forms a staple article of food among the Natives at certain seasons, and is one of the commonest fish sold in Auckland. <i>(1886,Handbook Fishes of NZ,52</i>)

>  <i>Ponui Passage</i>. - Mullet, kanae, dog-fish, stingaree. <i>ibid.</i>11. <i>Manukau Heads</i>. -5. Mullet (kanae) <i>ibid.</i> 21. Mullet, or Kanae (<i>Mugil perusii</i>). <i>(1892,TrNZi,24/203</i>) [NOTES: (Thomson)]

>  <i>Ka-nai.e</i> (ka.-na.i.&amacr.;<i> colloq. </i>Ka.-n&imacr.i.), n. [Maori.] The New Zealand name for the mullet. See under MULLET. <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third international,2025</i>)

>  <b>MORRiS</b> <i>Kanae n.</i> [a2 dieresis] (trisyll.) Maori name for a fish of New Zealand, the Silver-Mullet, <i>Mugil perusiior argenteus. </i> <i>(1898,Austral English,229</i>)

>  The months of December, January, and February in each year are here prescribed a close season for the fish of the species of the mugil known as mullet or kanae.' <i>(Jan 10, 1888,Order in Council</i>) [NOTES: >1888. Order in Council, New Zealand, Jan. 10, `Regulations under the Fisheries Conservation Act':]

>  <b>TREGEAR</b> The sea-mullet (<i>kanae</i>: Mugil perusii) often ascends tidal rivers in great numbers. <i>(1904,Maori Race, The,188</i>)

>  MUGiL CEPHALUS Linnaeus. <i>Grey Mullet; Kanae</i>. Netted up to 7lb. weight around North Auckland Peninsula. ... Distribution: i have recorded this species as far south as D'Urville island. <i>(Jun 1921,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,4/120</i>) [NOTES: PHiLLiPPS]

>  <b>PHiLLiPPS</b> <i>Mugil cephalus</i> Linnaeus. GREY MULLET; KANAE. <i>(1927,Bibliography of NZ Fishes,30`,1971 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BEST</b> The following fish were caught with hook and line: araara, maomao, moki, mango, ngoiro, pakirikiri, kahawai, tamure, tarakihi, and warehou. The kanae, kehe, kopipiro, moho, nanu and other species were taken by net. <i>(1929,Fishing Methods,43</i>)

>  <b>BEATTiE</b> Aua - mullet; ... Said to be the same as the kanae of the North island. <i>(1945,Maori Place-Names of Canterbury,61</i>)

>  <b>POWELL</b> GREY MULLET (<i>Mugil cephalus</i> ), Kanae of the Maoris, is an excellent food fish, rich in fat and protein. <i>(1947,Native Animals of NZ,67</i>)

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> GREY MULLET (KANAE) <i>Mugil broussonetii </i>Cuvier and Valenciennes <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An identification Guide,213</i>)

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> GREY MULLET ... <i>Other names: Mugil cephalus;</i> mullet, river mullet; sea mullet, mangrove mullet (Australia); kanae (Maori). <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Fishes of NZ,221</i>)

>  <b>PARROT</b> THE GREY MULLET <i>Mugil broussonnetti (Cuv. & Val.)</i> ... Maori name: KANAE. <i>(1957,Sea Anglers' Fishes of NZ,38</i>)

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> GREY MULLET ... <i>Other names: Mugil cephalus</i>; mullet, river mullet; sea mullet, mangrove mullet (Australia); kanae (Maori). <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,221</i>)

>  <b>SORENSON</b> MULLET, GREY (a) Scientific name: <i>Mugil cephalus</i>. (b) Suggested commercial name[s]: Mullet. (c) Other common names: Grey Mullet; Kanae (Maori). (d) Principal localities where taken: From Bay of Plenty north to Mangonui and from Raglan north to Hokianga on the other coast. <i>(1970,Nomenclature of NZ Fish,38</i>)

>  <b>BURTON</b> Mullet are slender, bluish-grey and silver fish, ... New Zealand has two species : the grey mullet or<i> kanae, </i>and the yellow-eyed mullet or <i>awa</i>. <i>(1982,Two Hundred Years of NZ Food & Cookery,78</i>)

>  [species name] grey mullet .. [scientific name] <i>Mugil cephalus</i> .. [other name(s)] kanae, striped mullet <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)

>  <b>SCOTT</b> The harbour is celebrated for its fish. Among these are mullet or kanae, a jumping fish ... <i>(1987,Seven Lives on Salt River,18</i>)

>  <b>HOCHSTETTER</b> They call them [sc. belemnites] Rokekanae, excrements of the fish kanae which they thought was in the habit of leaping out of the water and leaving these stones behind on the shore. <i>(1867,New Zealand (Hochstetter),327</i>)

### kanainai
 
 <i>n.</i> kanakana ?kanae. =?

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> Also the <i>Kanainai</i>, of the same size, but with a much larger head, and long narrow body, very small eyes, which are indistinct, being covered with a skin, a large mouth, only pectoral fins, which are white tipped with black, and a small caudal one. <i>(1855,Te ika a Maui,628</i>)

### kanaka
 
 <i>n.</i> a native

>  <b>CAPTAiN JOY</b> 14 July: ... [sic] a Kanaka drunk. Flog him ... [sic] 4th mate sick. <i>(Jul 14, 1836,Log of Mary Mitchell</i>) [NOTES: 1836 July 14 Captain Joy Log of Mary Mitchell (Whaler) in 1974 Manson Story of a New Zealand Family 50 ALSO in 1913 McNab Old Whaling Days 450 .]

>  <b>ADAMS</b> ... and the Kanakas (the natives of the Sandwich islands) still call them brothers <i>(1853,Spring in the Canterbury Settlement, A,79</i>) [NOTES: -->1853 C.W.Adams Canterbury Settlement 79 (Griffiths Collection)]

>  <b>ADAMS</b> ... and the Kanakas (the natives of the Sandwich islands) still call them brothers <i>(1853,Spring in the Canterbury Settlement, A,79</i>) [NOTES: 1853 C.W.Adams Canterbury Settlement 79 (Griffiths Collection)]

>  We had one Maori and two Kanakas in the boat, and one Maori and one Negrow on shore with one Moriore and two Maori women to cook. <i>(Feb 11, 1868,Chudleigh Diary,215,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <i>kanaka,</i> the equivalent of the tangata in Newzealand, where kanaka is never used for a Maori. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/152</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

### kanakana
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [also kunakuna](kiw-kwi.md#kunakuna))

>  <b>HULME & MORRiSON</b> There is an abundance of fish here, eel and kanakana. <i>(1989,Homeplaces,9</i>)

>  <b>BEATTiE</b> Kanakana - the lamprey. <i>(1945,Maori Place-Names of Canterbury,63</i>) [NOTES: c1880 South island Maori Lists `Wahi Mahika Kai' in 1945 Beattie Maori Place-names of Canterbury 63]

>  <b>SHAW & FARRANT</b> Raupo and toe-toe flourished in the swamps, eels and waterfowl were to be had for the taking, pigs were plentiful in the hills and certain streams harboured the choice kana kana. <i>(1949,Taieri Plain,77</i>)

>  <b>AYLiNG</b> <i>LAMPREY</i> (Kanakana) <i>Geotria australis </i> <i>(1982,Collins Guide,39,1984 ed.</i>)

>  "Tuna [long-finned eels], inanga, koaro,kokopu [three types of galaxiid], kanakana [lamprey], paraki [smelt] and aua [yellow-eyed mullet] were all taken, but tuna was the most important species." <i>(May 2001,NZ Geographic,51/44</i>)

### kaneree
 
 <i>n.</i> Hooker's 1853 (203) misprint or transcription error for kauere. (See [kauere](k-kea.md#kauere))

### kanga
 
 <i>n.</i> curse

>  The cause of the trouble was a curse or 'kanga'. <i>(Oct 8, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,14</i>)

### kanga Mouri
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>MARKHAM</b> i have many times made a grand Meal in a Kanga Mouri or Native Village on Pippies [pipi], sitting round the Coppre Mouries, each helping themselves till it was done. <i>(1834,New Zealand (Heaphy),45</i>) [NOTES: 1963 edition. ?Kainga Maori]

### kanga pirau
 
 <i>n.</i> fermented corn [ORIGIN: Maori, first word also spelt kaanga; second word also piro, kopiro, kopirau; also kanga wai kopiro]

>  These multipurpose cobs can be eaten as sweetcorn when young, or ground into meal, but the more traditional use was making kaanga piro - fermented corn. .. i've never tried kaanga piro, but it is reputedly sweet-tasting, in spite of an unholy aroma. <i>(Nov 5, 2005,Dominion Post,indulgence 23</i>)

>  Add to that list a vegetable called kanga wai kopiro (fermented corn traditionally made by leaving it in running water for a couple of months. <i>(Jul 31, 2010,Press, The,17</i>)

>  <b>WiLLiAMS</b> They brought however a basket of stinking corn which had been soaking in water for some weeks. [ed fn] <i>Kanga pirau.</i> This is still a Maori method of preparing corn. <i>(Nov 7, 1833,Early Journals of Henry Williams, The,341,1961 ed.</i>)

>  i found the owner of the shawl, the bag of bullets & the kaanga pirau - a young fellow named Te Horo. <i>(Feb 20, 1867,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,2/288</i>) [NOTES: (A.S. Atkinson)]

>  <b>MEREDiTH</b> i sampled a new Maori dish on this trip - <i>kanga kopirau</i> (rotten corn). it has a bouquet just about as potent as the dead whale i mentioned in my last. <i>(1875,Adventuring in Maoriland in the Seventies,146,1935 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BEAGLEHOLE</b> A few Maori families are able to grow ... enough sweet corn to make the special food called kanga pirau - fermented corn. <i>(1946,Some Modern Maoris,97</i>)

>  <b>HiLLiARD</b> He told her he was putting down some corn to go rotten; but she knew he would never use a stagnant drain to prepare such a table-delicacy as kanga pirau. [fn] <i>Kanga pirau</i> - corn left for months in running water to rot; highly-prized [Maori] food. <i>(1960,Maori Girl,16</i>)

>  Perhaps.. the 14th century originators of this pious fraud did not use sausage skin.. but stripped a blood-filled vein from the saint's leg, say, and piously washed it in a mountain stream, like kaanga pirau. <i>(Dec 1992,NZ Sceptic,8</i>)

>  Aunts would come from their family land in Coromandel bearing gifts such as strings of dried mussels, bags of dried kumara and jars of kaanga pirau, the so-called stinking corn. <i>(Jun 21, 2003,Dominion Post,F6</i>)

>  Kaanga Kopiro - Maize steeped in water until the odour makes it a delicious item of diet. Much sought after by gourmands. <i>(Apr 18, 1917,Christchurch Girls' High School,ii/16 79</i>)

>  With no means of grinding or cooking maize, Maori placed it in a flax kit and left it in running water until putrid. "This was known as kaanga pirau. it has a faecal odour, but if this is ignored, a pleasant piquant taste, rather like cheese". <i>(Jun 17, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),61</i>)

### kanga pungarehu
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>STEVENS</b> if it's the Maori corn, kanga pungarehu is really pretty. <i>(1991,NZ Women,538</i>)

### kanga wai
 
 <i>n.</i> = kanga pirau [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  <b>MALCOLM</b> Kangawai, generally called 'rotten corn' is not rotten at all, only the smell is unpleasant while the corn itself tastes delicious. <i>(1982,Where it all Began,93</i>)

>  <b>BAKER</b> There was kanga wai, teroi and bowls of raw fish, kina roe and marinated kutai for the old folk familiar with such delicacies. <i>(2000,Kokopu Dreams,62</i>)

>  <b>EDWARDS</b> Also kanga wai. This corn was put into clean sacks, laid into a stream for at least three months or more, till the kernels are fermented and soft to the touch. it stinks. <i>(1992,Mihipeka,39</i>)

>  in Henare Everitt's finely judged story, "Kanga Wai", the main character hates the eponymous rotten corn, a Maori delicacy. <i>(Apr 29, 1995,Listener (New Zealand Listener),57</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

### kangaroo
 
 <i>n.</i> a kangaroo dog

 <i>n.</i>

### kangaroo apple
 
 <i>n.</i> ?poroporo.

>  ... growth of the <i>Solanum laciniatum </i>... when the bush is cleared away ... evergreen shrub ... berries smooth and oval and hang in clusters - acrid when green but eatable when yellow; it is the real Kangaroo apple, and is used as common food among the Tasmanians. <i>(1852,Australian & NZ Gazette,36/60</i>) [NOTES: (Otago)]

### kangaroo dog
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  ... our kangaroo dog, timid as he was, partook of his share of the action, running in amongst , and biting the naked bodies of the natives; ... <i>(1835,Boultbee Journal,45,1986 ed.</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFiELD</b> Many kinds of dogs have been introduced by the Colonists; including <i>Bull-dogs</i>, by the whalers, <i>Kangaroo-dogs</i>, (a mixture of greyhound and mastiff) from New South Wales, and various crosses between these and the natives' dogs, which go under the general name of <i>pig-dogs</i>, and combine courage with speed, being used chiefly to hunt the wild hog, and taught to hold him, when caught, by ear or jowl, until the hunter comes up. <i>(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,166</i>)

>  i bought a kangaroo dog from the mate of the <i>Bee. </i> <i>(Sep 9, 1849,Torlesse Papers,102,1958 ed.</i>)

>  FOR SALE, An Excellent KANGAROO DOG. <i>(Jul 12, 1851,Lyttelton Times,1</i>)

>  <b>MACKiE</b> A kangaroo dog will stalk up to the table which from his great height he is able to overlook ... <i>(May 16, 1853,Traveller under Concern,109,1973 ed.</i>)

>  <b>TRiPP</b> The wild dogs have been among the sheep again and killed two ... Mrs Teschemaker cannot refuse lending, if not selling, the kangarro [sic] dog they have. Abner says we shall want two good fast dogs to kill the wild dog. <i>(Sep 13, 1856,Letter</i>) [NOTES: [13 Sep 1856] Tripp Letter in 1967 Harper Kettle on Fuchsia 28]

>  Sent Walter to Hamilton for Kangaroo dog - <i>(Jan 27, 1866,Puketoi Station Diary</i>)

>  <b>ELWELL</b> The sheep were feeding in sight, and my kangaroo dog was chained to his hut. <i>(1878,Boy Colonists,200</i>)

>  <i>kangaroo-dog n.</i> kind of greyhound used for hunting. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),11</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

### kangaroo hound
 
 <i>n.</i>

These big dogs of a boarhound type were imported from Australia to Otago in the 'fifties to aid in the extinction of the real wild dogs then so common in the backblocks. <i>(Sep 1864,Puketoi Station Diary</i>) [NOTES: c1864 Sep Puketoi Station Diary in 1947 Beattie Early Runholding 81]

>  in September 1864 the diggers' dogs were such a menace to the flocks that Murisons sent over to Hamilton's run for the loan of a `kangaroo hound.' These big dogs of a boarhound type were imported from Australia to Otago in the 'fifties to aid in the extinction of the real wild dogs then so common in the backblocks. <i>(Sep 1864,Puketoi Station Diary</i>) [NOTES: c1864 Sep Puketoi Station Diary in 1947 Beattie Early Runholding 81]

### kanohi-mowhiti
 
 <i>n.</i> the silver-eye

>  Probably to the genus Zosterops ... belongs a small migratory bird, called by the natives `Kanohi-mowhiti'. it is properly a South island species ... it crossed over [to the North island], for the first time in the memory of the native inhabitants, in the winter of 1856. <i>(1869,TrNZi,1/iii/9</i>) [NOTES: (Buller Ornithology)]

>  <b>BULLER</b> ZOSTEROPS CAERULESCENS. (THE SiLVER-EYE.) ... Native names. Tau-hou, Whiorangi, Hiraka, Motingitingi, Kanohi-mowhiti, Karu-patene, Karu-ringi, Karu-hiriwha, Poporohe, and iringatau. <i>ibid.</i> 81 <i>(1888,Birds of New Zealand,1/77</i>) [NOTES: The natives distinguish the bird as Tau-hou ..., or Kanohi-mowhiti (which may be interpreted as spectacle-eye or ring-eye). it is also called Poporoke and iringatu, names suggested by its accidental or periodical occurrence.]

### kanono
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Set off up the gravel road past shrubby rata and kanono, veering left up to where the track departs from the road just a few minutes in... <i>(Jan 3, 2004,Dominion Post,C2</i>)

>  Here, you'll see the largest coprosma, kanono - with its broad green marbled leaves - a smaller, more slender version called karamu and a shrubby, pale green job which when crushed and held to the nose, will tell you in pungent terms how it earned the Latin moniker Coprosma foetidissma and it's common one of stinkweed. <i>(Dec 27, 2005,Dominion Post,C2</i>)

>  <b>LEVY</b> ... such species as lancewood, karamu, toro, ramarama, koromiko, kanono, kohuhu, kawakawa, hangehange, rangiora, five finger, patete, toru, tree daisies, broadleaf, kamahi, mahoe, tarata, karo, lacebark, wineberry, fuchsia, kowhai, with tree ferns, weki, and mamuku. Toetoe, phormium, cabbage tree, with swamp coprosma and hupiro may figure as a developmental phase on wet soils. <i>(1951,Grasslands of NZ,87,1970 ed.</i>)

>  <b>PHiLLiPPS</b> Joined leaves of the kanono or manono, <i>Coprosma australia</i> [sic], were wrapped around the bodies of eels which were being prepared for the oven. <i>(1966,Maori Life and Custom,28</i>)

>  <b>MOORE & iRWiN</b> <i>COPROSMA AUSTRALiS</i>. [formerly <i>C. grandiflora</i>] Kanono, the largest-leaved New Zealand <i>Coprosma</i>, is a small tree of lowland forests of the North island and northern South island. <i>(1978,Oxford Book of NZ Plants,122</i>)

>  <b>WiLSON</b> <i>Coprosma grandifolia</i> KANONO ... Shrub or small tree. ... (=<i>C. australis</i>). <i>(1982,Stewart island Plants,108</i>)

>  <b>DAWSON</b> Other common shrubs are the thin-leaved hangehange <i>(Genistoma rupestre)</i>, kanono <i>(Coprosma grandifolia)</i> and pate <i>(Schefflera digitata)</i> with its large palmately compound leaves. <i>(1988,Forest Vines to Snow Tussocks</i>)

### kanui te utu
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  <b>THATCHER</b> Though with Whitaker and Lundon he's having a dispute, / Old Bobby chuckles to himself and says kanui te utu; <i>(1869,Wit and Humour: Local Songs...,9</i>) [NOTES: 1869 Thatcher Sights at Grahamstown in Local Songs 9]

### kanuka
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Leptospermum ericoides<\i>, now <i>Kunzea ericoides</i> tree manuka, white manuka (See [manuka](m-mar.md#manuka))

>  "if wallabies remain, the last generation of big native trees will eventually die childless," says Weaver. That would just leave the kanuka and "Governor Grey's Big Weed" - the pines centred around DOC's land at Mansion House Bay. <i>(Apr 13, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),31</i>)

>  Our manuka and kanuka were also planted in Hawaii about 70 years ago, and now threaten to overgrow native Hawaiian vegetation. <i>(Nov 3, 2003,Dominion Post,B5</i>)

>  Frequently, manuka is confused with its close relative, Kanuka (Kunzea ericoides). <i>(Aug 1991,NZ Gardener,10</i>)

>  Larger-sized timber is obtained from the manuka's close relative, the kanuka or "white" tea-tree. <i>(1945,Korero (AEWS Background Bulletin),3(2) : 13</i>)

>  There's little native bush left on the property - one giant rata has been kept. Jim says that more of the original kanuka and manuka were ripped out during the early development phases in the 1970s... it was originally covered in kanuka. in those days it was a weed but with well developed pasture it doesn't get a chance to spread. <i>(Jun 2002,Elements,17/8</i>)

>  The larger tea-tree is also a manuka, but the Maoris sometimes distinguish it as kanuka. <i>(Jan 12, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,15</i>)

>  if the leaves run softly through your fingers, you're stroking a kanuka; if they rasp, it's a manuka... <i>(Dec 26, 2005,Dominion Post,B2</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Leptospermum <i>ericoides</i>, A.Rich. ... Nat. name, `Rawiri', Northern island, <i>Cunn.; `Manouea' [?=manuka], Middle island, D'Urville.</i> A scarcely less common plant than [L. scoparium], attaining a greater size (20 feet) ...] A variety of large leptospermum (now Kunzea spp.) [Maori kanuka[a1-macron]; <i>(1853,Flora NZ,i Flowering Plants 70</i>)

>  <i>Kaanuka,</i> n.<i> leptospermum ericoides;</i> a tree. <i>(1871,Dictionary of the NZ Language,44</i>)

>  [Native name] Kanuku .. [Settlers' name] Tea-tree .. [Family] Myrtaceae <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,121</i>) [NOTES: 1886 T.Kirk in 1902 Settler's Handbook of NZ 121]

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> <i>L[eptospermum] ericioides, A.Rich.</i> ... Abundant from North Cape to the Bluff, ... <i>Kanuka; Maru.</i> ... Easily distinguished from [manuka] by its greater size, ... wood durable; much used for piles, house-blocks, posts and rails, &c. <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,161</i>)

>  <b>LAiNG & BLACKWELL</b> <i>Leptospermum ericoides (The Tree Manuka).</i>.. Maori name <i>Manuka-rauriki</i>, but now very often called the <i>kanuka</i>. Both islands. <i>(1940,Plants of NZ,289</i>)

>  <b>LEVY</b> The development of the kauri to dominance was preceded by scrub and low-forest phases, the chief species being mingimingi, <i>Gaultheria</i>, manoao, kumarahou, tauhinu, <i>Lycopodiumm, umbrella fern, manuka, kanuka, tree ferns, tanekaha, toro, and rewarewa.</i> <i>(1951,Grasslands of NZ,3,1970 ed.</i>)

>  ... cords of Kanuka (L. ericoides) firewood were cut under permit. <i>(1953,NZ Forest Services Report,267</i>) [NOTES: 1953 NZ Forest Services Report from Esk Forest (TS) 267]

>  The insect is found associated with both red manuka (<i>Leptospermum scoparium</i>) and white manuka or kanuka (<i>Leptospermum ericoides</i>). <i>(Oct 1959,Tararua Tramper,7</i>)

>  <b>MASTERS</b> Jack twigged a sturdy kanaka [check spg] down the track a bit. He made a dive for it. <i>(1960,Back-Country Tales,160</i>)

>  <i>kanuka n </i>-s [Maori] : a shrubby New Zealand tree (<i>Leptospermum ericoides</i>) with strong resistant wood that is used for making fences - compare MANUKA <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third international,1232</i>)

>  <b>SEVERiNSEN</b> [Napier dist.] Half a dozen wild sheep appeared quite close, grazing in dense kanuka. <i>(1970,Hunt the Far Mountain,70</i>)

>  Myrtaceae <i>L[eptospermum] eroicides</i> kanuka <i>(1975,Tane,21/8</i>)

>  Myrtaceae <i>L[eptospermum] eroicides</i> kanuka <i>(1975,Tane,21/8</i>) [NOTES: (Wright Vegetation Mt Maungatawhiri)]

>  <b>T AYLOR</b> <i>Kanuka (Leptospermum ericoides) </i>Native shrub resembling manuka ... it is common throughout New Zealand, more abundant relative to manuka than formerly because it has resisted manuka blight. >  <b>DAWSON</b> Where the previous forest cover is completely removed ..., the first coloniser is bracken fern <i>(Pteridium esculentum)</i> [107] followed in order by two small-leaved light demanding species - the shrub manuka (<i>Leptospermum ericoides</i>) and the small tree kanuka (<i>Kunzea ericoides</i>). <i>(1988,Forest Vines to Snow Tussocks,106</i>)

>  <b>KENDRiCK SMiTHYMAN</b> [Notice on exhibit] CATAMARAN One of the more popular methods of getting logs out of the bush it was usually associated with the skidded road, a small section of which is shown here, and hauled by a team of bullocks consisting of 12 to 16 animals. This particular `Cat' is made of white ti-tree runners. The skidded road is also of white ti-tree or `Kanuka'. Constructed by Mr Reg Poyner, Matakohe <i>(1988,Personal Communication</i>) [NOTES: ? Museum, Northland]

>  Acccording to P M E Williams in his book <i>Te Rongoa Maori</i>, "A small industry has started on the East Coast to extract the oil of these species [manuka and kanuka] which is said to be effective against some diseases where antibiotics have proved unsuccessful. <i>(Sep 8, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),38</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [Ma.] tall scrub [spp. Leptospermum and Kunzea , second growth establishing plants that can cover expansive areas of farm land.]

### kao
 
 <i>n.</i> sun-dried kumara

>  The Maoris there are preparing kao, which is a form of dehydrated kumera, for the use of the Maoris of the district who are serving overseas. <i>(Jun 14, 1943,NZEF Times,ii/103 : 11</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> Kao, or dried kumara, was a favourite food of the Maori when on a journey. <i>(1930,Maori Yesterday and To-day, The,189</i>)

>  After he had been with her for some time, her father asked him what the food of his own land was. Aokehu replied fish, kumara and kao. <i>(1936,Journal of Polynesian Studies,45/1</i>)

>  ... the roots of the Tikoraha <i>(Cordyline stricta)</i>, were tied separately for baking ... for their Kao, or prepared sweet potatoes, they used the leaves of the Parataniwha <i>(Elatostemma rugosum); ... </i> <i>(1869,TrNZi,1/iii/36</i>) [NOTES: (Colenso Botany North isd)]

>  <b>BURTON</b> [Title] Kao (sweetmeats) Kao were usually made in the leisure hours after the kumara had been gathered in autumn, ... To prepare kao, select some elongated kumara and with a sharpened manuka stick or knife scrape out the dirt from the eyes. ... Wash the kumara and wrap each in puriri leaves. Place in a slow hangi for 24 hours, then dry in the sun for as long as two weeks <i>(1982,Two Hundred Years of NZ Food & Cookery,4</i>)



 
 <i>exclam.</i> no [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  <b>Trish Fong</b> 'Mo-ko.' He coughs. 'i want to tell you something.' 'Lay still, Papa. Tell me later.' 'Kao. Now.' <i>(2007,Huia 7: Contemp. Maori Fiction,26</i>)

### kapa haka
 
 <i>n.</i> [ORIGIN: Maori, John Thompson tells me that the Maori Studies Unit had a Kapa Haka group about 1990.]

>  He has succeeded in stalling funding in the national ballet and the symphony orchestra until there is some state backing for the country's champion Maori kapa haka team, which would enable the team to represent New Zealand abroad. <i>(Mar 8, 1998,Sunday Star Times,Money 7</i>)

>  Waihirere won the first national kapa haka festival in 1972. <i>(Apr 1998,Mana,No. 21 : 29</i>)

>  Back in 1860 when Matutaera Potatau Tawhiao became the second Maori King, he named a small settlement near Meremere, Te Pou o Mangatawhiri - after the river. Sixty years later, his mokopuna, Princess Te Puea started a kapa haka group at Turangawaewae marae. She later toured with the group to raise funds to restore the marae, and for the building of Mahinarangi. <i>(Aug 28, 2008,Scoop,1</i>)

>  And so to the saga of the Auckland kapa haka group, which includes the daughters of Henare and Tuku Morgan. <i>(Sep 4, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),13</i>)

>  So what if your Kapa Haka group is short on members? <i>(Sep 4, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),93</i>)

>  The first half is given over entirely, in fact, to a foot-stomping, tongue-poking, butt-baring, poi-swinging performance by Te Matarae i Orehu, the national kapa haka champions out of Rotorua. <i>(Jun 30, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),52</i>)

>  The exhibition of the cream of Maori art, including weaving, carving, ta moko and kapa haka performances, runs for 10 days. <i>(Aug 4, 2005,NZ Herald,A10</i>)

>  [Hdg] Kapa haka challenge. Kapa haka (dance and performance) students from Porirua School perform waiata (songs) with guitar music. <i>(Nov 21, 2000,Evening Post,21</i>)

>  No Creative New Zealand money is being applied for to finance the bed's transport and installation, and no kapa haka party will be required to accompany the bed to Venice. <i>(Jun 15, 2006,NZ Herald,A15</i>) [NOTES: Attributive use.]



 
 <i>n.</i> a Maori cultural performance or performance group [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  They took such a shine to the welcome, their hosts, and the kapa haka that they were reluctant to head back to their flash hotel. <i>(Jun 1998,Cuisine,No. 22: 18</i>)

>  Aotearoa is the home of the Maori, and kapa haka is the cutting edge of the renaissance of a positive Maori identity, and the nothing part, is that's how much recognition this Auckland Regional Amenities Funding Bill has given to kapa haka. <i>(Aug 28, 2008,Scoop,1</i>)

### kapa Maori/kopa Maori
 
 <i>n.</i> var. of copper Maori [also other variant spellings below]

>  <b>POLACK</b> The only method of cooking formerly in use [by the New Zealanders] was by the <i>a.pa. </i>or<i> angi maori</i>, the native oven, already described. <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/399</i>) [NOTES: in error]

>  <b>MEREDiTH</b> These natural utensils are called <i>kaipa maoris</i>, generally pronounced `copper maori' <i>(1935,Adventuring in Maoriland in the Seventies,37</i>) [NOTES: 1870s (East Coast)]

>  <b>MEREDiTH</b> They [sc Maoris in the Wairarapa] had cooked some pork, maize, and <i>kumaras</i> in a <i>kapa-maori. </i> <i>(1935,Adventuring in Maoriland in the Seventies,89</i>) [NOTES: c1875 Meredith Adventuring in Maoriland (1935) 89]

>  <b>HAY</b> ... the earth-oven, the kopa or hangi. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/153</i>)

>  ... the kapas continued in use until superseded by the kohua, or iron pot. <i>(1894,Journal of the Polynesian Society,4/224</i>) [NOTES: (Rutland `Traces of Human Occupation in the Pelorus District')]

>  <b>W.A.Q.</b> The stray embers from a `kopa maori' or `hangi.' <i>(1904,Story of Hawera, The,7</i>)

>  <b>WASHBOURN</b> For a long time after the arrival of the pakeha the Maoris did their cooking in a `koppa Maori.' <i>(1933,Reminiscences of Early Days,47</i>) [NOTES: (Nelson)]

>  <b>KENWAY</b> ... kopa maori (pronounced copper mowry) ... [reference c1885] <i>(1928,Pioneering in Poverty Bay,151</i>)

>  <b>REiSCHEK</b> The Maori women prepared pork and kumaras in a kapa-Maori, or cooking-pit, which tasted famous after the fatigue of my tramp. <i>(1930,Yesterdays in Maoriland,178,1933 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MATTHEWS</b> And so the first sermon was preached in Kaitaia, with the kapa Maoris heating to cook the preacher! <i>(1940,Matthews of Kaitaia,20</i>)

### Kapa o Pango
 
 <i>n.</i> the 2005 All Blacks' alternative haka [ORIGIN: Maori, [Ma. <b>kapa</b> copper, penny, team, procession, panel of people] cf. P.M. Ryan Dict. Modern Maori (1995) 82.[Ma. <b>o</b> of, belonging to] 159.]

>  <B>WHAT THE HAKA SAYS</B>..KAPA O PANGO, AUE Hi! All Blacks! PONGA RA! Silver fern!... <i>(Aug 29, 2005,NZ Herald,A3</i>) [NOTES: [Ma. <b>kapa</b> copper, penny, team, procession, panel of people] cf. P.M. Ryan Dict. Modern Maori (1995) 82. [Ma. <b>o</b> of, belonging to] 159. [Ma. <b>pango</b> black] 170.]

>  The All Blacks used to have a haka - Te Rauparaha's haka - but they devised another, Kapa o Pango, and did it during the Springbok test at Dunedin this year. <i>(Oct 29, 2005,Listener (New Zealand Listener),55</i>)

>  For a start, there's the fact <i>Kapo O Pango</i> already is a celebration of the multicultural All Blacks/New Zealand. Anyone with an inkling of understanding of Pacific island culture will see that it is a blend of haka (New Zealand), cibi (Fiji), kailao (Tonga) and siva tau (Samoa). Possibly the sad truth behind all this carry-on is that those in the so-called home countries simply don't like seeing the natives stamping their feet and standing strong. <i>(Nov 27, 2005,Sunday Star Times,B11</i>) [NOTES: Kapo here typo or copying error?]

>  The All Blacks performed their Kapa o Pango haka for just the second time to loud booing from the Twickenham crowd. <i>(Nov 21, 2005,Dominion Post,D2</i>)

>  Saturday's performance of Kapa O Pango (Team in Black)was the culmination of more than a year's develpoment ... <i>(Aug 29, 2005,NZ Herald,1</i>)

>  Wallabies coach John Connolly claimed the Kapa o Pango haka was going to incite murderous intent in otherwise innocent children around the globe. <i>(Jul 30, 2006,Sunday Star Times,B1</i>)

>  " 'Kapa O Pango" talks about the silver fern, the blackness of the jersey and living your time as an All Black.' <i>(Aug 28, 2005,NZ Herald,B2</i>)

>  <i>Kapa O Pango</i> was unveiled last August, instead of the traditional <i>Ka Mate Ka Mate</i>, before the All Blacks played the Springboks at Carisbrook. <i>(Jun 8, 2006,NZ Herald,A1</i>)

>  The All Blacks have been asked to mothball their new <i>Kapa O Pango</i> haka and its controversial throat slitting gesture. <i>(Jun 8, 2006,NZ Herald,A1</i>)

### kapai
 
 <i>adj.</i> fine, OK [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  <b>ROSiER-JONES</b> Maude turns to Tui. `How are you?' `Kapai. Ke te pai.' Tui presses noses with the old lady, then Maude. ... `Whenever i'm out here in the wop-wops with youse jokers, the hori comes out in me. Kapai and hongi..' <i>(1985,Cast Two Shadows,88</i>)

>  <b>McKiLLOP</b> We all reached the ships unmolested, after an [132] absence of five weeks, during which time we had become quite colonial - an expression very common both in New South Wales and New Zealand, and means that we had learned how to sleep in the bush with a blanket round us, ... build a ware [e-acute] or hut, ... and say Cawpie the maori, `very good the native!' - ' <i>(1849,Reminiscences,131</i>)

>  <b>McLEAN</b> My own thoughts is that it is a very `kapai' one. <i>(1849,Papers,Vii-28</i>)

>  <b>WOODHOUSE</b> When a forward young [Maori] woman approached very near,/ And she dropped a remark that she meant me to hear,/ <i>it<i> was <i>`Tisho!'</i> its meaning is, `Steady! Go slow!'/ But i don't its was orthography happen to know ,/ i replied she was <i>`Kapai'</i> (excessively good),/ <i>(1950,Farm & Station Verse,16</i>) [NOTES: 1889 Williams Colonial Couplets 12 New Chum's Letter Home in 1950 Woodhouse Farm and Station Verse 16]

>  (Leading Article) [Morris] The Maori word which passed most familiarly into the speech of Europeans was `kapai,' `this is good.' <i>(Feb 14, 1896,NZ Herald</i>)

>  <b>MORRiS</b> <i>Kapai, adj.</i> Maori word for <i>good</i>, used by the English in the North island of New Zealand: e.g. `That is a kapai pipe.' `i have a kapai gun.' [1896] <i>(1898,Austral English,241</i>)

>  <b>REEVES</b> [NZ Speech] Very few words have been adopted from the vigorous and expressive Maori. The convenient `mana', ....."whare', ... `taihoa' ... and `ka pai' `tis good' are exceptions. <i>(1898,Long White Cloud, The,403</i>)

>  <b>ALEXANDER & CURRiE</b> <i>Kapai</i>: a word of approbation. <i>(1906,Treasury of NZ Verse,291</i>)

>  As far as defensive work [in football] was concerned, one can only say that it was `kapai'. <i>(May 19, 1906,NZ Truth,48-2-7</i>)

>  Also his two sisters-in-law are peerless, and have money ... <i>Ka pai</i> arrangement <i>(Oct 10, 1911,Triad, The,19</i>)

>  KAPAi [heading on a leading article] <i>(Sep 21, 1912,Hutt Valley independent,1</i>)

>  <i>kapai a</i>. good. [Maori] <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,6</i>)

>  <b>PARTRiDGE</b> <i>Kapai</i>. Good; agreeable; (mostly North island) New Zealand: mid-C. 19-20. <i>The New Zealand Herald</i>, Feb. 14, 1896. Borrowed direct from Maori, <i>kapai</i> = this is good. Morris. <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang,449</i>)

>  Slang words of Maori origin were once common, but most of them are dead and dying [and include] ... `kai,' `kapai,' `hoot' (utu), ... <i>(Apr 2, 1938,Press, The,18</i>) [NOTES: McNab NZ Slang]

>  <b>MYERS</b> Uncle Alec says your pastry is ka-pai. You won his heart with that cherry pie. <i>(1941,Valiant Love,33</i>)

>  <b>BAKER</b> <i>...kapai</i>, for good, or as an exclamation of pleasure. <i>(1941,NZ Slang,56</i>)

>  <b>BENNETT</b> <i>Kapai</i>, good, fine and <i>tena koe</i> ... are less common on New Zealand lips than formerly. <i>(1943,English as it is spoken in NZ,93</i>)

>  <b>SOLJAK</b> New Zealand colloquialisms which are of Maori origin include: ... <i>kapai: </i>good <i>(1946,New Zealand (various authors/titles),116</i>)

>  kapai The Maori word for `good' in common use among the white population in New Zealand. <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),229</i>)

>  <i>kapai adj</i> [Maori] <i>New Zeal </i>: GOOD <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third international,1232</i>)

>  <b>McCALLUM</b> it's a kapai morning, the sun is shining, the birds are singing.... <i>ibid </i> 98 [glossary] <i>kapai</i> good. <i>(1963,Half-Gallon Jar,34</i>)

>  [spg error Kaipai?] [Aviemore Vistors' Book] KAiPAi see Fantabulous <i>(Oct 15, 1966,Dominion,3/1</i>)

>  <b>TURNER</b> <i>Tena koe</i>, a greeting, is less common in English than it was ... [170] as are <i>kapai</i> `good' and <i>ka nui te pai</i> `very good' as exclamations. <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,169</i>)

>  <i>kapai adj.</i> Maori word for good. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),618</i>)

>  <i>ka pai (Phr.)</i> Maori for it is good [Maori]... <i>kapai ... adj.</i> Maori word for good. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),11</i>)

>  My brothers' slang and `funny' sayings delighted me. ... i remember [126] such words as `batty', `hoot' and `spondulicks' and many that ... were corruptions of Maori words. i spell them here as they sounded to me then: <i>ehoa, harka, kupai, kure, munga munga typo, markuta, pooku, tangy. </i> <i>(1988,Through the Looking Glass,125</i>) [NOTES: (Meikle)]



 
 <i>adj.</i> (in food names)

>  <i>KAPAi MUTTON STEAKS </i>... butter ... grated horseradish ... cayenne pepper ... Worcester sauce ... juice of half a lemon ... spread over the steaks ... dip in egg and breadcrumbs. Fry carefully. <i>(1913,Australasian Cookery Book, The,117</i>)

>  <b>BURTON</b> [Title] Kapai salad (...) Very typical of the old school of New Zealand salad making, this one, so no wonder that it has been dubbed kapai, or `OK'. <i>(1982,Two Hundred Years of NZ Food & Cookery,127</i>)



 
 <i>adv.</i> well, fine [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  The pudding turned out kapai until we came to cut it. <i>(1860,Taranaki Punch,6/2</i>)



 
 <i>exclam.</i> that's good! well done! [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  <b>LUSH</b> At which he grinned again, and ... said,<i> `Kapai-Kapai</i>. it is good - it is good.' <i>(Aug 8, 1851,Auckland Journals,84,1971 ed.</i>)

>  <b>GUTHRiE</b> [She] accepted them with pleasure crying, `Kapai, Kapai'. <i>(1930,NZ Memories,49</i>)

>  <b>HiLLiARD</b> Afterwards they lolled back on the beds, the tray piled high with dirty dishes... `Kapai!' shouted Henry. <i>(1960,Maori Girl,153</i>)

>  <i>ka pai NZ </i>exclamation of pleasure or approval. [M] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,409</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  To which he [the chief] replied `Capai! capai! - Good! good!' apparently much satisfied. <i>(1841,GBPP,(No.311) 109</i>) [NOTES: 1840 June 28 Thomas Bunbury's Report in GBPP House of Commons 1841 (No.311) 109]

>  <b>ROCHFORT</b> [The chief] deliberately turned the ornamented portion of his person [sc. his tattooed behind] round for her examination, at the same time shouting <i>Kaupi!! Kaupi!!!</i> (good!! good!!!). <i>(1853,Adventures of a Surveyor,18</i>)

>  <b>ASKEW</b> if he was disposed to give all they asked, `Kaupie' (very good) was the response <i>(1857,Voyage to Australia & NZ,348</i>)

>  <b>HUNT</b> ... they threw up their arms and uttered the words ,us; `Kapai , kapai , kapai !' - (Good, good, good.) <i>(1866,Chatham islands,11</i>) [NOTES: 1860 July in 1866 Hunt Chatham islands 11]

>  <b>KEENE</b> <i>Kapai!</i>: Good! <i>(1963,O Te Raki,191</i>)

>  <b>BRATHWAiTE</b> KA PAi it is good! <i>(1967,Evil Day,379</i>)

>  <b>FiNLAYSON</b> ka pai `that's good'. <i>(1973,Brown Man's Burden,140</i>)

>  <b>HULME</b> `Ka pai, but you let me get tea on say, Friday though?' [a Maori speaks] <i>ibid.</i> 465 60 Ka pai = good, great, thanks mate etc. <i>(1983,Bone People, The,110</i>)

>  <b>DAViS`</b> `Ka pai, Curly. We got enough. The flow of mutton ceased and we started stacking. <i>(1964,Watersiders,32</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i> good, excellent; well

>  <b>BAUME</b> You're looking kapai, Ngaire. <i>(1933,Half Caste,55</i>)

>  <b>NiCHOLAS</b> Good Ka-pi <i>ibid.,</i> 2/346 [Sentences] i know the language of England well Kamatou aou eta koraro no England kapi <i>ibid.</i> 2/347 it is good to read the language of New Zealand Kapi ta karakea a koraro no New Zealand <i>(1817,New Zealand (various authors/titles),2/332</i>)

>  (Letter from Port Nick) Little George can talk Maori very well, and so can the other children ... [Maories] say `car-pe the porkei', which means `very good the white man'. <i>(1841,Journal of Civilisation 'NZ',316</i>)

>  <b>BURNS</b> Good Ka-pi <i>(1844,NZ Chief,22</i>)

>  Another chief comes and stays with us ... He and i go and get <i>rappoo</i>, that is the native name for firewood; he takes a tune on the trump; different natives can play, they say is is <i>capi</i>, that is, good. <i>(Jul 20, 1848,Letters from Otago,16,1978 ed.</i>)

>  <b>POWER</b> Unluckily [the officer's] knowledge of Maori was on a par with his swimming; so, when the Maories pointed out the hole, and told him it was "Kapai' (good), he thought it good for a man who could <i>not swim</i>, and in he jumped without hesitation. <i>(1849,Sketches in NZ,163</i>)

>  (The Maori's Choice) `My friend' .../ You like hapuku drying;/ Kapai the smelt, the duck, the eel,/ And shark when putrifying./ <i>(1866,Canterbury Rhymes,85</i>)

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> ... two highly-educated half-caste women, ... both married to honest European gentlemen; whose children and grandchildren understand of Maori `kapai' and no more; ... <i>(1905,Where the White Man Treads,235</i>)

>  <b>SHADBOLT</b> `More music,' Tom insisted. `<i>Kapai te</i> music.' <i>(1959,New Zealanders, The,147,1986 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (Play the Fife Lowly)]



 
 <i>adj.</i> good [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  [Hdg] Maori eatery gets 'ka pai' from jobless. <i>(Sep 8, 2003,Dominion Post,A3</i>)

>  [Hdg] Ka pai kai <i>(Sep 18, 2003,Dominion Post,D5</i>)

>  Give them a listen - the music is always fine. in fact, ka pai. <i>(Aug 7, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),64</i>)

>  [Hdg] Tiki fetches ka pai price <i>(Jul 28, 2002,Sunday Star Times,A13</i>)

>  [Hdg] Does unanimity mean everything is kapai? <i>(Jun 27, 2002,Evening Post,6</i>)

>  <b>SCOTT</b> The spree was at the Pahi pub/ ... Te kai, kapai, waipiro plenty,/ Advice to you who bottle empty/ When full of ink, to walk unable,/ Don't hesitate but try the stable. <i>(1987,Seven Lives on Salt River,55</i>) [NOTES: 1912 poem in 1987 Scott Seven Lives on Salt River 55]

### kapara
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  it consisted of `light wood', or what is locally known as kapara - that is, the wood is permeated with resin, which gives it an amber-like appearance on a smooth-cut surface, while on splitting the wood looks very little different from the ordinary kauri wood....! <i>(1929,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,11/54</i>) [NOTES: (Hansson)]

>  <b>KEENE</b> <i>kapara</i>: gummy heart of <i>rimu. </i> <i>(1963,O Te Raki,191</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a cobbler, the last sheep in a pen to be shorn. [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  a cobbler - a kapara. <i>(2001,Questionnaire Respondents (DB)</i>)

>  <b>MEREDiTH</b> When the word is given to start, the shearers go into the "catching-pen' and feel over the sheep, select one, carry it out on to the shearing-floor, and commence shearing. Naturally, the easiest-shorn sheep -- `bare-bellies' and `bare points' -- are selected first. it is amusing to watch the tactics of some of the shearers to dodge the Kapara (Cobbler) the last sheep in the pen, which, presumably, is the worst shearing sheep of the lot. <i>(1875,Adventuring in Maoriland in the Seventies,143,1935 ed.</i>)

 <i>n.</i> az hand-net

### kapeta
 
 <i>n.</i> dogfish

>  SPiNY DOGFiSH. <i>Squalus fernandinus,</i> Molina. The spiny dogfish <i>(Squalus fernandinus)</i> is one of those species of shark which has attained importance as an economic species. ... it is generally called oke-oke, or kapeta, by [Maori]; the term pioke is applied by some tribes to this species, while to other tribes pioke is any small species of shark. <i>(1929,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,11/223</i>) [NOTES: (Phillipps)]

### kapia
 
 <i>n.</i> kauri gum [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> Kap|ia, s. A hard resinous substance from a tree; gum. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,159</i>)

>  <b>POLACK2/265</b> Te Kapia no te Kauri or Kouri-gum <i>(1840,Manners & Customs of the NZers</i>)

>  Very much interested in a large stratum of lignite to be seen under the sandcliffs [at Moiatoa], kapia still in ... Watched the karoros carrying up the pouas & letting them drop to break the shells... Saw my first korora (dead) <i>(Jan 30, 1861,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/685,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (A.S. Atkinson)]

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Kapia, <i>Geolog. Surv.</i> Gum dug up of <i>Dammaris australis. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),765</i>)

>  ... the black pigment for [tattooing was] obtained from the soot of ... Kapia, or Kauri resin, dugout of the earth; and also from the ashes of the curious vegeto-caterpillar fungus, the Hawhato <i>(Cordiceps Robertsii)</i>, which was sometimes mixed with the black juice of the Mahoe berry ... <i>ibid.</i> 49 The Kapia, or Kauri Gum, is (as its colonial name shows) a Gum,or rather a Resin, from the Kauri Pine ... <i>(1868,TrNZi,1/37</i>) [NOTES: (Colenso Botany North isd)]

>  <b>HAY</b> Kauri-gum - or Kapia, as the Maoris call it - ... is another peculiar product of this northern extremity of New Zealand. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/14</i>)

>  in the larger of the semi-subterranean huts are fires burning, fed with <i>manuka</i> branches and heaps of <i>kapia</i>, or kauri-gum. <i>(1922,NZ Wars, The,1/55</i>)

### kapu ti
 
 <i>phr.</i> Maorified form of "cuppa tea"

>  <b>J C YALDWYN</b> i would like to draw your attention to the Maori term used for a long time in the Museum of New Zealand Te Papa Tongarewa and in the National Museum of New Zealand before Te Papa was set up, for a tea break - kapu ti - i am told it means in Maori just what it sounds, i.e. it is not a joke, but i think it is just a delight and no wonder it has been used here freely and without complaint from our extensive bicultural staff... The word "hinetama" is the formal name for one of the smaller meeting rooms in the main Te Papa waterfront complex. <i>(Sep 30, 2004,Letter</i>)

>  A closing blessing will take place in the gallery on Monday 20th September at 8.30am, followed by kapu ti in Hinetitama. <i>(Sep 17, 2004,Panui,No. 871/1</i>)

### kapuka
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [broadleaf](bri-byo.md#broadleaf))

>  <b>Crowe</b> As a garden tree or shrub, kapuka offers attractive foliage and a tolerance of persistent winds and salt spray. <i>(2009,Which Native Tree?,50</i>)

>  <b>MONRO</b> Perhaps the commonest tree [near Waikouaiti] is one that i was not aware of having seen before. it grows to a considerable size, but in a very twisted, irregular shape. its leaf is of a smooth, glossy, green colour, about the size of the leaf of a Portugal laurel, but more heart-shaped. The native name of the tree is kapuka. it is commonly called broad-leaf by the white men there. The kohai is also a very common tree in the woods, and grows to a large size. <i>(1844,Notes of a Journey</i>) [NOTES: (Griffiths Collection)1844 Monro Notes of a Journey (Nelson Examiner July 20 et seq.) in 1898 Hocken Contributions 242]

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Kapook, <i>Hector. Griselinia lucida. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),765</i>)

>  <i>Podocarpus ferruginea</i> and <i>Griselinia littoralis</i>, the miro and the kapuka, are plentiful, but the matai <i>(Podocarpus spicata) </i>is extremely rare. <i>(1884,TrNZi,17/216</i>) [NOTES: (Kirk)]

>  [Native name] Kapuka .. [Settlers' name] Broadleaf .. [Family] Corneae <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,122</i>) [NOTES: 1886 T.Kirk in 1902 Settler's Handbook of NZ 122]

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> <i>g[riselinia] littoralis, raoul. Kapuka; Papaumu; Broad-leaf. </i>Timber strong, close-grained and durable; ... <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,239</i>)

>  <i>kapuka n</i> -s [Maori] : a rather small New Zealand broadleaf evergreen tree (<i>Griselinia littoralis</i>) sometimes cultivated in warm regions as an ornamental <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third international,1232</i>)
