>  <b>KiNG</b> The sea also brought them snapper, mullet, parore and flounder; whitebait in season; and oysters, pipi and karahu (periwinkles).. But you had to cook karahu properly. You washed the mud off, put them in cold water on the fire, and the almost let them boil. Just before they boil you see a white piece coming out from the inside. <i>(1983,Whina,38</i>)

### karaka
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>karaka</i> is the chief modern name among New Zealanders; others are obsolesc. Among the more precise and knowedgeable people it is n1. pronounced [k'rak a] but the old anglicisations ['kraeka] [ka'raeka] are still very common, especially in country districts and in the bush. Early spgs (<i>cracker, kraka</i>) point to these pronunciations; even as accurate an observer as E. J. Wakefield would spell it `kraka' (perhaps [krak a]), although in his `Adventures in NZ' it is always written <i>karaka.</i> cf <i>cracker, caraceer;</i> a spreading tree common in coastal areas and producing orange fruit, the kernels of which are poisonous unless soaked and cooked [Maori]; cowtree, New Zealand laurel (kopi, Chatham island).

>  Karaka (<i>Corynocarpus laevigatus</i>) is usually considered a native but research indicates it is originally from Rapanui in the Easter islands. in the Chatham islands it is called Kopi. <i>(Oct 2000,Growing Today,40</i>)

>  Karakas might be grown more generally in Southern gardens <i>(Mar 30, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,15</i>)

>  <b>HAY</b> The Koraka [sic] (<i>Corynocarpus laevigata</i>) was brought to New Zealand by the Maori. ... The fruit is edible; the kernel contains `korakine' [sic], a narcotic poison. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/193</i>) [NOTES: [misspelling: cf Hooker??]]

>  [Native name] Karaka .. [Settlers' name] Karaka .. [Family] Anacardiaceae [Native name] Karaka .. [Settlers' name] Native lilac .. [Family] Saxifrageae <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,121</i>) [NOTES: 1886 T.Kirk in 1902 Settler's Handbook of NZ 121-122]

>  <b>FEATON</b> CORYNOCARPUS LAEViGATA (<i>Forst.</i>) ... The `KARAKA'. <i>ibid.</i> 177 Karaka [Maori name] .. Karaka [Maori name] .. Corynocarpus loevigata .. Tree <i>(1889,Art Album NZ Flora,100</i>)

>  Karaka .. Corynocarpus laevigata .. Wood white, close grained, ... <i>(1890,PWD Catalogue of Timbers,10</i>)

>  <b>MORRiS</b> <i>Karaka, n.</i> Maori name for a tree, <i>Corynocarpus laevigata, </i> Forst. N.O. <i>Anacardiaceae</i>; also called <i>Cow-tree</i>(q.v.), forty feet high, with orange-coloured berries, two to three inches long. <i>(1898,Austral English,241</i>)

>  <b>BAUGHAN</b> <i>Karaka (kah-rakk-ah):</i> The New Zealand laurel. <i>(1908,Shingle-Short,65</i>)

>  <b>COCKAYNE`</b> Some English names are corruptions of Maori ones, as biddy-biddy for piripiri, cracker for karaka, maple for mapou. <i>(1910,NZ Plants,153</i>)

>  <b>SHAND</b> [Chatham isd] inihina - Hinahina or Mahoe, in Maori - was considered the best wood for the rubber [in firemaking]; but Karamu, Karaka, Ake, Rautini, and Kokopere (Maori, Kawakawa) were used as the <i>Kahunaki</i>, or grooved piece of wood. <i>(1911,Moriori People of the Chatham islands, The,8</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> Here and there a little watercourse ... arboured over by a matted roofing of <i>kotukutuku,</i> ... and <i>mahoe</i> or whitewood and glossy-green <i>karaka. </i> <i>(1926,Travel in NZ,2/32</i>)

>  ..."cracker' (also falling into disuse) for `karaka' <i>(1926,TrNZi,670</i>) [NOTES: (Andersen Pop Names)]

>  <b>LAiNG & BLACKWELL</b> <i>Corynocarpus laevigata (The Karaka). </i> <i>(1940,Plants of NZ,245</i>)

>  <i>karaka</i>, a tree (Dysoxylum spectabile), large glossy leaves and large orange berry with poisonous kernel: introduced by Maori from Sunday island [sic] because of food value, the poison being soaked out. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/143</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <i>karaka:</i> tall tree with orange-coloured fruit. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,174</i>)

>  <b>PARTRiDGE</b> <i>Cracker</i>. 12. Karaka: New Zealand: since ca, 1860; by 1926 ob. B., 1941. By `Hobson-Jobson'. <i>(1949,Dictionary of Slang Addenda,1023</i>)

>  <b>HUNT</b> Our chief source of revenue was to gather in the bush fungus, which usually grew upon the dead and partly decayed stems of karaka and mahoe trees, dry it in the open air, and sell it to the Chinese merchants ... <i>(1951,Confessions,20</i>)

>  karaka A New Zealand coastal tree with glossy leaves and large orange- coloured fruit. The Maoris used the outer pulp as food; the inner part is poisonous. <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),229</i>)

>  <i>karaka n</i> -s [Maori] : a New Zealand tree (<i>Corynocarpus laevigata</i>) having orange-colored fruit with edible pulp and poisonous seeds that when cooked and dried are also edible and form an important article of native diet <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third international,1233</i>)

>  <b>KEENE</b> <i>karaka:</i> New Zealand laurel tree; grows to 30 to 50 ft. in height; has large, orange, fleshy berry 1 in. to 2 in. long. ' <i>(1963,O Te Raki,191</i>)

>  <b>McLiNTOCK</b> Karaka .. .. karaka .. .. <i>Corynocarpus laevigatus </i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-712</i>)

>  <i>karaka n.</i> the New Zealand cowtree, with bright orange-coloured berries like damsons <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),618</i>)

>  <i>karaka ... n.</i> N.Z. cowtree, with orange berries like damsons. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),11</i>)

>  <b>BAXTER</b> karaka <i>tree with orange-yellow berries </i> <i>(1982,Selected Poems,188</i>)

>  <b>BURTON</b> A handsome tree with dark green, glossy leaves, the karaka (<i>Corynocarpus</i> laevigatus) yields a bountiful crop of oval-shaped berries, ... . The outer flesh is tasty but the bulk of the food value is in the inside kernel. <i>(1982,Two Hundred Years of NZ Food & Cookery,6</i>)

>  <i>karaka n</i>. NZ tree with orange berries and poisonous seeds. [M] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,409</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  ... the kraka, a fruit of oblong shape <i>(Apr 17, 1837,Sydney Herald</i>) [NOTES: 1837 Wakefield and Ward British Colonization 324 [quoting Sydney Herald 17 April 1837]]

>  <b>POLACK</b> A long lane was formed on an extensive level plain, fifty feet in width, by two high walls, formed by perhaps upwards of ten thousand baskets of potatoes, kumeras, indian corn (<i>Holcus indicus</i>), tarro (<i>arum</i>), water melons, turnips preserved, (<i>kou</i>) steamed kernels of the native fruit, (<i>karaka</i>[as-acute]) baked roots of the ti palm tree, (<i>korou</i>) a quantity of dried cod, eels and other fish, also of shell-fish, bundles of fern-root, onions, peaches, grapes, &c. <i>(1840,Manners & Customs of the NZers,1/91</i>)

>  Some foods, such as bracken roots, are known to be carcinogenic, and wrongly prepared karaka fruit can poison. <i>(Jul 23, 2004,Dominion Post,A4</i>)

>  ...two [trees] that bear a sort of plumb of the size of prunes, the one yellow [[fn a] These are call'd Karraka by the natives] and the other black [[fn b] call'd Mai-tao by the indians]. <i>(Feb 25, 1777,Cook's Journals,3/804,1967 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1777 Feb 25 Anderson Journal in Cook's Journals (1967) 3/804]

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> Kar|aka, s. A certain fruit-tree. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,159</i>)

>  <b>WiLLiAMS</b> The spot we chose was on the bank of a river, amongst beautiful shrubs and <i>karakas</i>, a tree very like the laurel; the ground was covered with grass. <i>(Jan 7, 1832,Journal,214,1961 ed.</i>)

>  <b>WiLLiAMS</b> At daybreak we landed in a quiet bay, where the boys immediately commenced to explore the neighbourhood for<i>Karakas, ... </i> <i>(Feb 13, 1833,Early Journals of Henry Williams, The,282,1961 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MARKHAM</b> ... some of their Woods stink very much in burning for instance the Caraceer or Cracker [<i>karaka</i>] as we call it, ... <i>(1834,New Zealand (various authors/titles),47,1963 ed.</i>)

>  <b>DOMETT</b> The thick karaka's [a2-acute] varnished green./ <i>ibid./</i> 1/302 Karaka ..... O. Anacardiaceae; G. Corynocarpus; S.C. laevigata. <i>(1883,Ranolf and Amohia,1/157</i>)

>  <b>BAKER</b> ... also falling into disuse. <i>(1941,NZ Slang,45</i>)

>  <b>McDONNELL</b> The <i>Courie Croeka</i> and <i>Pou te Cowa,</i> i think, are the finest trees in the island - the first for the beauty of its trunk and top (independent of its value as a wood), the second, for the unrivalled beauty of its leaf, which is a species of laurel, and the third, for the splendour of its flower, which grows in such thick clusters and is of so rich a color, that looking at it from a short distance, one would imagine a sumptuous scarlet pall had been thrown over the top of the tree. The <i>Courie</i> does not grow further South than 38 degrees, ... <i>ibid.</i> 45 `Their caraceer or cracker as we call it.' <i>ibid.</i> 47 ... some of their Woods stink very much in burning for instance the Caraceer or Cracker [karaka] as we call it, ... <i>(1834,Extracts From a Journal,34,1979 ed.</i>)

>  ...kraka, tawa, tutu <i>(1837,British Colonization of NZ,324</i>) [NOTES: (Sydney Herald)]

>  <b>POLACK</b> <i>Karaka</i> [a2,a3-acute] is the general name for fruit. The principal is called the <i>karaka</i> [a1,a2-acute] <i>maori</i>, ornative fruit, which grows in clusters about the size and form of the Spanish olive, of a bright yellow, when ripe. ... The wood of the karaka [a1,a2-acute] is very handsome; ... <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/286</i>)

>  <b>TAYL,OR</b> The full moon is shining upon us from amongst the dark foliage of the karaka our laurel tree in New Zealand ... <i>(Apr 30, 1839,Journal,2/122</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFiELD</b> a fine grove of krakas <i>(Mar 1640,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1840 March (1839) E.J. Wakefields M.S. Diary]

>  <b>WAKEFiELD</b> ...grove of kraka trees <i>(1840,NZ Journal,24/298</i>) [NOTES: (E.J. Wakefield Journal)]

>  <b>POLACK</b> Karaka is generic name for fruit. <i>(1840,Manners & Customs of the NZers,2/263</i>)

>  <b>BiDWiLL</b> We travelled some miles along the plain to seek a good place for encampment, but did not meet with any wood till nine at night, and then could only get a few stunted Karoaka or Karooka bushes [fn <i>Corynocarpus laevigatus?</i>] to make a fire with. <i>(1841,Rambles in NZ,55 & fn</i>)

>  <b>HEAPHY</b> The leaf of the `Kraka' laurel is [the cattle's] favourite food, ... <i>(1842,Narrative of a Residence in  Various Parts of NZ,33</i>)

>  <b>DiEFFENBACH</b> Near the beach appear shrublike veronicas [fn Veronica ligustrifolia.], myrtles [fn Myrtus bullata.], fuchsias, solanum [fn Solanum laciniatum.], the karaka tree [fn Corynocarpus laevigata.], tutu [fn Coriaria sarmentosa.], and flax [fn Phormium tenax.]. <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,1/26</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> ... the pa which is enclosed with Karaka trees <i>(Jun 8, 1843,Journal,27</i>) [NOTES: 1843 June 8 Taylor Journal 1842-1843 [TS:ATB] 27]

>  <b>WAKEFiELD</b> The karaka tree much resembles the laurel in its growth and foliage. it bears bright orange-coloured berries about the size and shape of damsons, growing in bunches. The fruit is sickly and dry; but the kernnel forms an important article of native food. <i>(1845,Adventure in NZ,1/233</i>)

>  <b>POWER</b> The berries of the Karaka and Kahikatoa trees are articles of food, and though, in an extensive tract, these trees may not average more than two or three to an acre, yet they give value to the whole forest. <i>(1849,Sketches in NZ,134</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Corynocarpus <i>laevigata</i>, Forst. ... Nat. name, `Koraha'. [check this: it's obviously my mistranscription, or an original misprint or mishearing of KARAKA] <i>(1853,Flora NZ,i Flowering Plants 48</i>)

>  <b>SWAiNSON</b> These belts and clumps consist of a rich variety of wood; the graceful tree-fern, and the deep-green, glittering-leaved karaka, clustering, in unusual profusion, around the tall stems of the statelier forest trees. <i>(1853,Auckland,38</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Karaka,<i> Col[enso]. Corynocarpus laevigata. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),765</i>)

>  <b>BAiNES</b> ... on either side [of the harbour] much of the land was covered with a growth of manuka (tea-tree), rewa-rewa, towai, karaka, and other smaller kinds of timber .. <i>(1874,Edward Crewe,144</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a <i>Coprosma</i> spp [Cook's turban shell (West Coast)] (See [Cook's turban shell](con-cyp.md#cooks-turban-shell))

>  <b>PEARSON</b> [Gloss] <i>cracker</i>: Common West Coast bushman's name for a larger-leaved species of <i>Coprosma.</i> (Probably from Maori <i>karaka</i>, a very different tree whose leaves are vaguely similar. <i>(1963,Coal Flat,420</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> fruit of the karaka, the kernels of which were eaten by the Maoris as food, after treatment to remove their poison (karakine)

>  kernel of the <i>krakaberry </i> <i>(1841,NZ Journal,2/45/242</i>) [NOTES: (E.J. Wakefield Journ.)]

>  <b>HEAPHY</b> The <i>Towa</i> and <i>Kraka</i> berries also yield an useful oil ... <i>ibid.</i> 94 ... canoes ... loaded ... with ... water-melons, and baskets full of the kernel of the krakaberry ... <i>(1842,Narrative of a Residence in  Various Parts of NZ,43</i>)

>  (New Plym) My eldest girl got up a high tree to get me what i called cracker berries. <i>(1852,Australian & NZ Gazette,407</i>)

>  <b>EARP</b> The<i> karaka</i> berry is one of the chief articles of food amongst the natives, who never destroy the tree itself. <i>(1853,New Zealand (various authors/titles),130</i>) [NOTES: 1853 Allom letter in 1853 Earp NZ 130]

>  One of the earliest of these traditions ... is ... that Maruroa and Kauanga brought the karaka-berry from Hawaiki ... <i>(1904,TrNZi,36/11</i>)

>  <i>karakine</i>, the poisonous substance of the karaka berry. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/143</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <b>HiLLiARD</b> She would go to the creek ... to look for koura under the stones or fish for karawakas with a worm tied to a stalk of paspalum; or to the bush to gather karaka-berries ... <i>(1974,Maori Woman,177</i>)

### karaka berry
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  One of the most popular berries eaten by Maori was the karaka berry, which has a fleshy exterior. <i>(Jul 24, 2002,Capital Times,25</i>)

>  Your interesting note about the Waiotahi family of boys who lived on Karaka berries, and all became dissenting parsons, is not believed. <i>(Jun 7, 1913,NZ Observer,24</i>)

### karaka date
 
 <i>n.</i> = karaka berry

>  <b>DOMETT</b> Hard violet drupes of the great <i>laurel-tree/</i> And gold karaka-dates - <i>(1883,Ranolf and Amohia,2/16</i>)

### karaka Maori
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>POLACK</b> <i>Karaka</i> [a's-acute] <i>Maori, (laurus karaka</i> [a-acute],) grows to the height of forty feet, with handsome frontated leaves. <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),2/399</i>)

### karaka nut
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SHAND</b> For variety they had Fernroot (<i>Eruhe</i>)[e2-grave] and Karaka nuts (of which latter, in good seasons, they preserved very large quantities) ... <i>(1911,Moriori People of the Chatham islands, The,5</i>)

>  The most relied upon as food to be preserved for winter use was the karaka nut. <i>(1928,BP Bishop Memoirs,9(5):359</i>) [NOTES: 1928 Baucke Manuscript in Skinner and Baucke The Morioris (Bishop Museum Memoirs Vol. iX No. 5 pp. 343-382) 359]

>  <b>BROOKER et al.</b> Karakin, the poisonous principle of the karaka nut, has been found by Carter to contain several nitro groups ... [ref. 1951 J.Sci. Fd.Agric.2/54-55] <i>(1981,NZ Medicinal Plants,13</i>)

### karaka tree
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  "Even the karaka trees i see from my window stick up like wonderful paper pop-ups," she says. <i>(Jul 13, 2005,Northern Courier,11</i>)

>  Conservationists spend a lot of time cutting down our karaka trees in Hawaii. it seems that someone flew a plane over four of the islands in 1929 dropping New Zealand karaka fruit on the bush - to what end, nobody knows. <i>(Nov 3, 2003,Dominion Post,B5</i>)

>  <b>ANGAS</b> The tropical-looking dragon-trees (dracaena), called <i>ti</i> by the natives, and the <i>karaka</i>-tree, grow up the steep sides of the hills ... <i>(1847,Savage Life,1/240</i>)

>  <b>MOSER</b> Small potatoes, termed `kotero', and the berries of the karaka tree, (<i>corynocarpus laevigata</i>), are both ... considered great delicacies. <i>(1863,Mahoe Leaves,22</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> This was situated amid a cluster of the <i>karaka</i> [a2,a3-acute] fruit-trees, whose poracious appearance produced a pleasing effect. <i>(1939,New Zealand  (Polack),1/92</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> The Waitapu of a native village is generally selected amid a cluster of trees, among which the <i>karaka</i>[a3-acute] (a laurus) fruit-tree, is generally found; of course the fruit is prohibited from being eaten. <i>(1840,Manners & Customs of the NZers,1/120</i>)

>  The wide placed river and beautiful groups of karaka trees ... We dined in the karaka grove. <i>(Feb 8, 1854,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/143,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (A.S. Atkinson)]

>  Edith and Harry Hodges, of Christchurch, have been married 13 years, and there was a time when domestic felicity flourished like the green karaka tree, and the crimson blossom of the pohutukawa symbolised the affection that subsisted between them. <i>(Jun 10, 1911,NZ Truth,311/6/3</i>)

>  <b>MANSFiELD</b> Against the karakas. Then the karaka-trees would be hidden. <i>(1922,Stories,488,1984 Alpers ed.</i>)

### karakahia
 
 <i>n.</i> a white-eyed duck

>  <i>Myroca australis</i>. - Karakahia, White-winged (white-eyed?) Duck. Both islands; Australia. <i>(1886,TrNZi,18/117</i>) [NOTES: (Martin)]

>  <i>White-eyed Duck Aythya australis australis</i> (EYTON, 1838) ... OTHER NAMES: <i>Karakahia, hard head, Australian white-eyed duck. ... Eastern and south western Australia. Vagrant to ... New Zealand. </i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,151</i>)

### karaki
 
 <i>n.</i> a gannet

>  <b>KEENE</b> <i>karaki</i>: gannet. <i>(1963,O Te Raki,191</i>)

### karakia
 
 <i>v.</i> [The application of the word to public worship is, of course, modern.': the noun is recorded in Maori contexts only, e.g. 1874 Johnstone, Maori 193 note - incantations - In their use of this word the Maoris had no idea of prayer, and it is a mistake to attach that meaning to karakia. The verb is recorded in English form, and is mainly confined to in ???19th century use.]

>  <b>ELDER</b> Six days men were allowed to work, and every seventh day was appointed as a day of rest from labour, and to <i>keakea atua</i>; for this was the only term which we could then make use of in order to convey to their ideas our worshipping the Supreme Being. <i>(1934,Marsden's Lieutenants,59</i>) [NOTES: 1814 Sep 6 Kendall in Elder Marsden's Lieutenants (1934) 59]

>  <b>WiLLiAMS</b> if they should be in a situation that they cannot land, everyone immediately ceases talkg. [sic], and they commence `Karakia ` their incantations. <i>(Jan 6, 1832,Journal,213,1961 ed.</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFiELD</b> ... payment for the boys who went to Karakia ... <i>(Mar 27, 1840,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1840 March 27 (1839) E. J. Wakefield Ms Diary]

>  <b>WiLLiAMS</b> a poor dying native is . . . drawn into the open air, to [be] karakiaed or prayed for by the priest.... <i>(1844,NZ Journal,96,1956 ed.</i>)

>  <b>CARLETON</b> The natives said he had karakia'd us - a term they apply to our religious worship. <i>(1874,Life of Henry Williams</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> When the people gathered at the <i>maara,</i> the cultivation plot, the priest <i>karakia'</i>d the field, chanting his incantations to Maru and the gods of the<i> kumara. </i> <i>(1930,Maori Yesterday and To-day, The,184</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> That was the only tree that wasn't <i>karakiaed. </i> <i>(1948,Taina,26</i>)

>  Huka himself.. was in the great pa, having gone there to karakia (use some solemn form of words), and to hang the poor man's heart in their sacred place, in order to avoid any dangers to himself - a superstitious custom. <i>(1999,James Preece Missionary Papers,23</i>) [NOTES: Notes From <i>The Missionary Register</i> 1838]



 
 <i>n.</i> . [ORIGIN: Maori, prayer-chant, incantation, religious service (Ryan 84)]

>  Prof Dunmore's own faculty has been recently prone to a Chicago cult that holds quite irrational views about mysterious unseen hands that guide the world's markets. They might need some pretty strong karakia. <i>(Feb 18, 2000,Evening Post,6</i>)

>  We have not been able to obtain a description of his karakia or modes of worship. <i>(Apr 16, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,34</i>)

>  William Colenso, writing in <i>The Transactions of the New Zealand institute</i> last century, cited in full a karakia relating to kumara planting that he felt was "among the most beautiful and becoming of the rites of natural religion". <i>(Nov 26, 1988,Listener (New Zealand Listener),36</i>)

>  Aunt Biddy, the marae's kuia, gets up and begins to weave her thin karakia into the mist. <i>(Jul 3, 2004,Dominion Post,B5</i>)

>  This involved withdrawal from normal activities, relieving of karakia and bathing in sacred waters. <i>(Dec 6, 1986,Listener (New Zealand Listener),86</i>)

>  it would not surprise us if Te Kooti.. were to on the first celebration of the Hauhau karakia, turn the tables. <i>(May 17, 1879,Auckland Weekly News,8</i>)

>  Mum told me to do the karakia. i don't know why, but i always hated doing the karakia. <i>(Oct 6, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),93</i>)

>  <b>NiCHOLAS</b> [The New Zealander] replied, that `it [tapu] was no gammon at all; New Zealand man,' said he, `say that Mr. Marsden's <i>crackee crackee</i> (preaching) of a Sunday, is all <i>gammon.</i>' `No, no,' i rejoined, `that is not <i>gammon</i>, that is <i>miti,</i>' (good.) `Well then,' retorted the tenacious reasoner, `if your <i>crackee crackee</i> is no gammon, our taboo taboo is no gammon;' ... <i>(1817,New Zealand (various authors/titles),1/274</i>)

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> Karak|ia, ... s. A religious ceremony; a calling, as upon God; a prayer. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,159</i>)

>  <b>WiLLiAMS</b> The party assembled naked, ... the priest an old greybearded man ... stood up in front with outstretched arms, ... and repeated over his <i>karakia</i>, or prayer, to Tu, the God of War. <i>(Mar 14, 1833,Early Journals of Henry Williams, The,294,1961 ed.</i>)

>  <b>DiEFFENBACH</b> E' Waiata is a song of a joyful nature; E' Haka, one accompanied by gestures or mimics; E' Karakia is a prayer or an incantation, used on certain occasions, - ... <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/57</i>)

>  <b>SELWYN</b> When his `karakia' (charm) was completed, the suspected food was ordered to be burnt. <i>(1845,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1845 Selwyn Letter in 1847 NZ Part iV (The Church in the Colonies Xii) 10]

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> in the afternoon we had a long converstion with Heuheu, his acquaintance with their ancient traditions, waiatas, karakias and the number of them is surprising. <i>(Nov 15, 1845,Journal,3/172</i>)

>  <b>SHORTLAND</b> While the priest chants a `karakia' or incantation in measure, the `taua' keep time by leaping together, and by other uniform motions of the body. <i>(1851,Southern Districts of NZ,68fn</i>)

>  <b>MEADE</b> He therefore announced ... that there must first be a great <i>karakia</i>, or ceremony of worship, to induce the great <i>Atua </i>(spirit) to inspire them rightly as to what was to be done with the pakeha. <i>(Jan 27, 1865,Ride Through Disturbed Districts</i>) [NOTES: 1865 Jan 27 Meade Ride through Disturbed Districts in 1959 Taylor Early Travellers 491]

>  <b>MANiNG</b> He [the tohunga] came on, mumbling ... a perfectly unintelligible <i>karakia</i> or incantation. <i>(1863,Old New Zealand,(viii) 142</i>)

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> His `karakia' (incantations) were invocations to his gods to preserve him from the Unknown; of placation, of propitiation. <i>ibid.</i> 260 After a senseless jumble of karakia-cum-humbug, he [the tohunga] sat down cross-legged in front of the sufferer ... <i>(1905,Where the White Man Treads,38</i>)

>  <b>BAUGHAN</b> <i>Karakia (kah-rah-kee-ah):</i> An incantation. <i>(1908,Shingle-Short,46</i>)

>  the first and most urgent remedy in a Maori sickness is the appropriate `karakia.' <i>(Jul 1910,Kai Tiaki,104</i>)

>  <b>BABBAGE</b> At the meeting ... there was to be a grand performance of the Pai Marire Karakia (incantation) in the hope of drawing over the Poverty Bay natives, hitherto well affected to the Mission. <i>(1937,Hauhauism,58</i>)

>  <i>karakia</i>, incantation, spell, charm; thus a church is a whare-karakia as well as a whare-tapu. <i>(Jun 1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/152</i>) [NOTES: (J.C.Andersen)]

>  <i>karakia:</i> spell, incantation <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,174</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> Tane insisted upon reciting the <i>karakias </i> to propitiate the <i>atuas</i> of the forest ... <i>ibid.</i> 156 `Oh, he say <i>karakia </i>- save my life.' <i>(1948,Taina,26</i>)

>  <b>SiNCLAiR</b> The higher class of<i> tohunga</i> had many functions. They could recite the <i>karakia</i> (prayers, incantations, spells) with greater efficacy than the layman. <i>ibid.</i> 319 [Maori glossary] <i>karakia</i> charm, spell, incantation <i>(1959,History of New Zealand,23</i>)

>  <b>KEENE</b> <i>karakia</i>: charm, spell or incantation. <i>(1963,O Te Raki,191</i>)

>  Here he [a tohunga] said a karakia (incantation) which was so powerful that it unlocked the gate. <i>(1970,NZ Mysteries,102</i>)

>  <b>MASON</b> HiNGAWARU ... But i remember my grandfather. Spoke not a word of English, never, till he died. Full of waiata and karakia; taught me the whakapapa right back to Hoturoa and the Tainui. <i>(1974,Hand on the Rail, The,18,1981 ed.</i>)

>  393 Karakia = prayers, sacred chants <i>(1983,Bone People, The,469</i>)

>  Sometimes a tree was selected years before it was felled, and the bark removed on one side to assist later shaping. Karakia (prayers) accompanied every phase of making waka to ensure a harmonious relationship with Tane, god of forests. <i>(Nov 22, 1988,Dominion,10</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> prayer; worship [[Ma. <b>karakia</b> prayer-chant] cf. P.M. Ryan Dict. Modern Maori (1995) 84.]

>  [The man], who had been guarding the gate to the track for most of the day, said the karakia was said at the request of [her] family. <i>(Sep 20, 2003,Dominion Post,A3</i>)

>  <b>WiLLiAMS</b> [? first Christian use in Jrnl] in the evening we held <i>karakia</i>, and delivered our message in the name of the Lord. <i>(Mar 31, 1833,Early Journals of Henry Williams, The,303,1961 ed.</i>)

>  <b>WiLLiAMS</b> [=preaching] ... we were pressed by the people to stay over the night, in order that they might hear more about our karakia (preaching). <i>(Dec 4, 1832,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1832 Dec 4 W.Williams's Journal in 1940 Matthews Matthews of Kaitaia 33]

>  <b>ANGAS</b> ... the whole party sat down to their evening meal, until the bell struck for <i>karakia</i>, or prayers. <i>ibid.</i> 2/13 At sunset, the natives went through their <i>karakia</i>, or worship, which is performed by the Christians every night and morning. <i>(1847,Savage Life,1/237</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> . [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  <b>AGENDA</B> Karakia, Mihi, Chairman's Report, Minutes, Treasurer Report, Forestry, Land Development, housing ... <i>(Aug 17, 2005,Whakatane Beacon,11</i>) [NOTES: commas are additional, the items were in a vertical list in the newspaper.]

>  She did not want to contribute any skills or knowledge to the front-row role such as whaikorero or karakia, or display institutional leadership. <i>(Oct 31, 2005,Dominion Post,B6</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> [?service ?prayers] This morning the natives rang the bell for karakia before five but i told them to wait until i had dressed. <i>(Nov 13, 1845,Journal,3/169</i>)

>  <b>GRACE</b> i said that i would travel very cautiously, that i did not intend to ask them to come to our 'karakia' (religion); but that i did think it was my duty to go and see them. <i>(Nov 29, 1866,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1866 Nov 29 T.S.Grace Journal in Grace Pioneer Missionary (1928) 151]

>  Karakia - 'prayer'. This word refers to both the church service, and the prayer or special chant performed at the opening and close of meetings <i>(1985,Towards Taha Maori,19</i>)

>  <b>Hui-a-iwi</b> *Karakia/Mihi .. * Confirm Minutes of the Hui-a-iwi held at Te Kuri-a-Tuatai Marae on 28 May 2006 <i>(Jul 1, 2006,Dominion Post,C16</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  <b>HUNT</b> The morrow was the Sabbath. He would then attend the <i>karakia</i>, or church, and during the morning service i determined to search his <i>whare. </i> <i>(1866,Chatham islands,50</i>)

### karakin
 
 <i>n.</i> a poison contained in the seed kernels of the karaka tree. [Spg <i>korakine</i> may be from Hooker??; also spg karakine]

>  The [karaka] fruit attracts the native pigeon which must digest the flesh and pass the poisonous kernel whole, before it kills. The poison is known to science as karakin. According to <i>The Poisonous Plants of New Zealand</i> by H.E.Connor, the effect of karakin on humans is to cause 'paralysis preceded by violent spasms and convulsions'. <i>(May 2003,Forest & Bird,No. 308/29</i>)

>  There are a lot of trees, with most beautiful names:.. karaka - the flesh of the heavy orange fruit is edible but the seed kernels contain a deadly poison, karakin, that causes convulsions and deformity. Maori steamed and washed the kernels to remove the poison. <i>(Apr 28, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),40</i>)

>  The author [W. Skey] announced that he had succeeded in isolating the bitter principle of the Karaka berry, and had found it to be a crystallizable resin, analogous to the active poisons picrotoxine and digitaline. Mr. Skey proposed to name the new resin `Karakine'. <i>(1872,TrNZi,4/363</i>)

>  <b>HAY</b> The Koraka [sic] (<i>Corynocarpus laevigata</i>) was brought to New Zealand by the Maori. ... The fruit is edible; the kernel contains `korakine' [sic], a narcotic poison. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/193</i>)

>  <i>karakine</i>, the poisonous substance of the karaka berry. <i>ibid.</i> 148 <i>karakin</i> is an English word derived from a Maori word, karaka: had the Maori given the name it would no doubt have been karakino (kino, bad). <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/143</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <b>BROOKER et al.</b> Karakin, the poisonous principle of the karaka nut, has been found by Carter to contain several nitro groups ... [ref. 1951 J.Sci. Fd.Agric.2/54-55] <i>(1981,NZ Medicinal Plants,13</i>)

### Karamea Granite
 
 <i>n.</i> Geol. definition

>  Thus there is a general similarity in colour and symbol for the Waingaro Schist and Devil River Vocanics (hd and hw), the Karamea Granite (ko) anad the Onehau Granite (so). <i>(Jun 1972,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,32/27</i>)

### karamu
 
 <i>n.</i> a species of <i>Coprosma</i>

>  <b>Crowe</b> Karamu belongs to the same family as coffee, a fact that inspired experiments in the late 1870s to find out whether the roasted and ground seeds might make a worthwhile substitute for that popular drink. <i>(2009,Which Native Forest Plant?,52</i>)

>  Here, you'll see the largest coprosma, kanono - with its broad green marbled leaves - a smaller, more slender version called karamu and a shrubby, pale green job which when crushed and held to the nose, will tell you in pungent terms how it earned the Latin moniker Coprosma foetidissma and it's common one of stinkweed. <i>(Dec 27, 2005,Dominion Post,C2</i>)

>  The cold, high bluffs of Northland's Waim&#257; Forest are home to rare woody plants found nowhere else, including karam&#363; (<i>Coprosma waima</i>), a tree daisy (<i>Olearia crebra</i>) and a very recently discovered tree - Turoa onamata (<i>Ackama nubicola</i>). <i>(Jul 2004,Conservation Action,42</i>)

>  <b>HOCHSTETTER</b> The slope.. was overgrown with bushes of the New Zealand coffee-shrub - karamu of the natives (<i>Coprosma lucida</i>.. ) - full of red berries. <i>(1867,New Zealand (Hochstetter),337</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> a NZ wheat cultivar

>  Otane was one of a series of highly successful wheat cultivars Dr McEwan, always thinking a decade ahead, bred while working for the DSiR. Others.. were Karamu (1979), Rongotea and Oroua (both 1979). They were grown from Southland to Waikato, as was Endeavour (1994). He was best known for his work in wheat but he also worked in oats and barley, naming one, a black feed oat, Finlay, after an early All Black, Jack Finlay. <i>(Feb 12, 2004,Dominion Post,B9</i>)

### karamuka
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <i>Kai.ra-mu</i> (ka.i.ra.-mu; <i>colloq</i>. k&abreve.ri.&adot.-mu),<i> n</i>. [Maori.] <i>(Bot.)</i> Any one of several species of rubiaceous trees of the genus <i>Coprosma. C. arborea </i>is called <i>tree karamu. [Local, N. Z.] See COPROSMA. </i> <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third international,2025</i>)

### karamuramu
 
 <i>n.</i> Hector's spg prob. represents kaaramuramu, poss. a mistake or Otago dial. or var. form of karamu.

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Luma-luma, <i>Hector. Coprosma foetidissima. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),766</i>)

### karanga
 
 <i>n.</i> a traditional cry of welcome onto a marae, chanted by women [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  <b>STEAD</b> What he's fairly sure of is the karanga - the cry of the kuia - and the slow impressive dignified progress towards the meeting house of Donovan O'Dwyer, the decorated officer back from war, come to report to the Maori family how one of their brave sons, who was one of his men, had met death in battle. <i>(1999,Talking about O'Dwyer,43</i>)

>  <b>BAKER</b> A karanga or someone else's tears would bring on the waterworks every time. <i>(2000,Kokopu Dreams,46</i>)

>  The karanga (call) is cried out first from the women of the tangata whenua (host people of the marae), signalling the start of the welcome. <i>(Dec 19, 1999,NZ Catholic,7</i>)

>  There was no karanga - so no women were participating in the formal ceremony at all... <i>(Feb 4, 2006,NZ Herald,22,Canvas ed.</i>)

>  Nor did she contribute to the roles of the second row, such as supporting the korero, lead waiata, lead or support karanga. <i>(Oct 31, 2005,Dominion Post,B6</i>)

>  Wairaka marae invites Ngati Awa whanui to participate in the wananga: Kaupapa - Whakorero and Karanga. <i>(Aug 31, 2005,Whakatane Beacon,15</i>)

>  "i'm still not happy for the women to give up their role of karanga and i wouldn't want to see the men take up the role. it's to do with the women's role in childbirth and bearing the child into this world." <i>(Nov 18, 2005,NZ Herald,A6</i>)

>  <i>Karanga a te Kuia</i> is primarily concerned with the art of the karanga, that part of Maori protocol that involves the older women, the kuia, calling people onto the marae. <i>(Feb 22, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),53</i>)

>  <b>OWEN & PERKiNS</b> At a tangi, of course, the body would be lying in state on the porch of the meetinghouse, and as we advanced there would be the karanga, calling to us to come and weep for the dead. <i>(1986,Speaking for Ourselves,109</i>)

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> it was part of his [sc. the Maori's] festal ceremonies to chant a `karanga' (call of welcome), when he brought food to set before the stranger guests. <i>(1928,Where the White Man Treads,256</i>)

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> Now it was part of [Maori] festal ceremonies to chant a `Karanga' (call of welcome), when he brought food to set before the stranger guests. <i>(1905,Where the White Man Treads,253</i>)

>  All of a sudden i was jolted .. the karanga, the shrill haeremai, haeremai coming down the marae .. We all moved up the marae, we did our tangi, paying our silent respects for the old man Te Kaho. <i>(Jul 20, 1974,Listener (New Zealand Listener),10/4</i>)

>  Five Maori women lifted their voices in unison with the rising sun and intoned the karanga, a ritual call of welcome. <i>(Sep 24, 1984,Time (NZ edition),50</i>)

>  i can imagine a chorus .. singing a sort of murmured karanga to this bi-cultural - <i>Landmark</i>, it would probably be called. <i>(Sep 1985,Landfall,302</i>)

>  WANANGA .. kaupapa - Whaikorero and Karanga ... <i>(Aug 19, 2005,Whakatane Beacon,41</i>)

>  Suddenly a cry went up, echoing like a karanga across the hills. <i>(May 23, 2004,Sunday Star Times,B11</i>)

>  When the Prince of Morocco arrives to seek Portia's hand in marriage, he is given a ceremonial Maori welcome with a conch shell, and karanga, counterpointed by Moroccan trumpets and song. <i>(Feb 9, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),64</i>)

>  She [Tungia baker] .. was a great exponent of the "karanga", the women's call .. Pearl Sidwell, described her as "a woman of words, an educator who has left behind a lasting legacy in her work in karanga". <i>(Jul 28, 2005,Dominion Post,A10</i>)

>  We went on a marae trip in intermediate school and my feet felt rooted to the spot when a karanga rang out in the wind and tears pricked my eyes as if i had been pinched deep inside. <i>(2007,Huia 7: Contemp. Maori Fiction,62</i>)

>  Mrs Harata Solomon, of the Takapuwahia Marae in Titahi Bay, performs the karanga (call) at the start of the PPTA conference in Wellington today. <i>(Aug 26, 1986,Evening Post,34</i>)

>  Karanga: a call of welcome, or a call of reply to the welcome. <i>(Sep 17, 1986,PSA Journal,3</i>) [NOTES: 1986 Sep 17 PSA Journal Conference Supplement 3]

>  <b>iHiMAERA</b> To one side, a special group of elders was sitting, ready to give their speeches of welcome once the karanga of the women had been completed. <i>(1986,Matriarch, The,28</i>)

>  in addition to four speeches there was a karanga, powhiri, two waiata, three hymns. <i>(Feb 19, 1986,Wanganui Chronicle,6/2</i>)

>  <b>BURCHFiELD</b> <i>karanga</i> /|kara a/. N.Z. [Maori]. A call of welcome. <i>(1988,Lexicographical & Linguistic Studies,194</i>)

>  <b>Lashlie</b> This latter step [waiting in a corridor] was necessary because the performance began with a karanga and the audience being called into the theatre space by the performers. <i>(2003,Journey to Prison, The,62</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> [[Ma. <b>karanga (-tia)</b> call, shout, related person] cf. P.M. Ryan Dict. Modern Maori (1995) 84.]

>  <b>HULME</b> Waina shivers. Her voice rises in a beautiful quaver, [95] controlled keening, as she karangas in return. <i>(1986,Te Kaihau,94</i>)

 <i>n.</i> a shellfish

### karangu
 
 <i>n.</i> karamu

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> Kar|angu; Name of a certain tree. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,160</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> There are many other woods of much service to Europeans, differing in quality, among others: the <i>30 Warangai - 31 Pata -32 - Niho - 33 Tangio, - 34 Maihoi - 35 Nene [e's-acute] - 36 Taraiti - 37 Kohihihu 38 Waihupuku - 39 Kaikamoko - 40 Pukapuka - 41 Karanghu - 42 Utuhutu - 43 Angiani - 44 Akkas </i> of various kinds. 45 <i>Parekireki, &c.</i> Of all these woods there are abundant quantities. <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),2/400</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Coprosma<i> lucida</i>, Forst. ... Nat. names, `Karamu' and `Karangu', <i>Colenso.</i> <i>ibid.</i> 105 Also for <i>C. foetidissima </i> <i>(1853,Flora NZ,i Flowering Plants 104</i>)

>  <b>LiNDSAY</b> [<i>Coprosma lucida</i>, Forst.] The `Broad-leaf' or `Orange-leaf' tree of the settler; terms, however, which are probably not only applied to other species of the genus <i>Coprosma, (e.g. C. robusta, <i>Raoul,) but to species of genera belonging to very different families - and especially to <i> </i>Griselinia lucida, (N.O. Corneae,) whose foliage closely resembles that of <i>Coprosma lucida ... C. lucida</i> is also known to the Otago natives as `Kalamou' (Hector); and to the North island Maoris as `Karamu', [fn] `Kakaramu,' or `Karangu,' [fn] (Colenso.) [fn] Also applied to <i>C. foetidissima. </i> <i>(1868,Contributions to NZ Botany,71</i>)

>  Karangu. Coprosma foetidissima, Forst. <i>(1869,TrNZi,1/iii/43</i>) [NOTES: (Buchanan Botany Otago)]

### karapapa
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  i ate both white and red snowberries, the red maire tawake fruit, the tiny mingimingi berries, the sweet-smelling and sweet-tasting karapapa fruit, plenty of fern fiddleheads and ever ripe blackberries in some of the old clearings. <i>(Apr 23, 2004,Sunday (Sunday Star Times Magazine),15</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

### karea

### kareao
 
 <i>n.</i> supplejack (See [supplejack](spa-sys.md#supplejack))

>  Bright orange berries of the karaka and karamu trees and the vivid red kareao berries (supplejack) hang in bright clusters and litter the floor of the forest in a carpet of colour. <i>(Nov 19, 2005,Dominion Post,E15</i>)

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> K|areau; A species of shrub. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,160</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> i had a narrow escape of being strangled a kareao (souple jack) got round my neck <i>(Jan 10, 1840,Journal,2/179</i>)

>  Creepers ..., as the calawars, rendering the forest impenetrable. <i>(1840,NZ Journal,18-232</i>) [NOTES: W.D.B. Mantell]

>  <b>WAKEFiELD</b> karewau ... supplejack <i>(Oct 7, 1840,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1839]

>  <b>DiEFFENBACH</b> This is the supplejack of the Europeans, kareao of the natives. <i>ibid.</i> 2/366 Kareao - a wild vine (<i>Smilax</i>) <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,1/425</i>)

>  (And) <i>Rhipogonum scandens</i> [KAREWAN ?misprint for KAREWAU] <i>(Jul 29, 1844,Report of Select Committee on NZ</i>) [NOTES: 1844 Jul 29 House of Commons Report from the Select Committee on NZ]

>  <b>WAKEFiELD</b> . . . the walls [of Te Awaiti whaling station] were generally constructed of wattled supplejack called karreau filled with clay. <i>(1845,Adventure in NZ,44</i>) [NOTES: 1845 EJW Ad.NZ 44-5 quoted in 1967 Drummond At Home in NZ 36]

>  <b>McL;EAN</b> currawau <i>(1847,Papers,iV-82</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> There is scarcely a bare stem to be seen, even the tallest are embraced by a variety of twining plants, parasites and creepers, amongst which the <i>kia-kia</i>, decked at every joint with bunches of iris-shaped leaves, and the <i>mangamanga,</i> a creeping fern, are the most conspicuous; the <i>kareo,</i> also, forming a network of cane-like stems, hangs its bunches of glossy leaves pendant from every bough, and many others of equal beauty join to form a verdant arch, midway between the spreading branches of the noble trees to which they cling. <i>(1959,Early Travellers,121</i>) [NOTES: 1847 Johnson Notes from a Journal in 1959 Taylor Early Travellers 121]

>  <b>WAKEFiELD</b> The woods are almost impenetrably interwoven with lianes, or supple-jacks, (<i>Ripogonum parviflorum</i>,) called Kareau by the natives, which give great trouble to the explorer. <i>(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,144</i>)

>  FOREST BiVOUAC. ... Strengthened by the usual New Zealand food of roasted potatoes and `damper', we set off ... but soon found ourselves bewildered ... in the surveyor's lines which crossed one another in all directions in the wood. We then resorted to the usual expedient of torches made of `karehao' (supple Jack) or `katoa'; ... it was [agreed] ... that we should light a fire and bivouac for the night. <i>(1851,New Zealand (various authors/titles),Part V (Church in the Colonies XX) 76</i>) [NOTES: 1848 Aug 30 Selwyn in 1851 NZ Part V (Church in the Colonies XX) 76]

>  <b>EARP</b> in the [alluvial bush land], which is wooded chiefly with <i>pukateas, kaikateas, </i> and <i>rata</i> trees, and thick underbrush of <i>konine</i> (fuchsia) or <i>roe-roe, kaua kaua</i> (or pepper), &c., entwined with kuriwau or supple-jack, .. <i>(1852,Handbook for Emigrants,230</i>) [NOTES: 1852 Wellington Gazette in Earp Hand-Book for intending Emigrants 230]

>  <b>DRURY</b> TREES [MET WiTH iN PELORUS]. Ti - Ti palm or Ti bush, common everywhere. Supplejacks - Karea of natives, ... <i>(1855,Sailing Directions,67</i>)

>  <b>VON MUELLER</b> (And) "`Hartwhan' of the natives; 'Supplejack' of the New Zealand colonist.' ;Rhipogonum scandens [HARTWHAN ?a spg of variant -->karawau] <i>(1864,Vegetation of the Chatham islands,54</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Kareao, <i>Col[enso]. Rhipogonum scandens</i>. <i>ibid.</i> 766 Kuri-wao, <i>Hector. Rhipogonum scandens. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),765</i>)

>  ... the curious red fruit of the Titoki, or Titongi, <i>(Alectryon excelsum)</i>; the fruit of the Tutu-papa <i>(Coriaria thymifolia)</i>; of the New Zealand Bramble, Tataramoa, <i>(Rubus australis)</i>; of two of the New Zealand Myrtles, the Ramarama <i>(Myrtus bullata)</i>, and the Rohutu<i> (M. pedunculata);</i> of several species of <i>Coprosma,</i> - particularly the Karamu <i>(C. lucida)</i>, and <i>C. robusta),</i> of the Papaauma <i>(C. grandifolia)</i>, and of the two littoral species, Taupata <i>(C. retusa)</i>, and Tataraheke <i>(C. acerosa)</i>; of the Koropuku <i> (Gaultheria depressa); </i>... and of the Kareao, or Pirita, <i>(Rhipogonum parviflorum). </i> <i>ibid.</i> 1/iii/34 (Colenso Botany North isd) ... stems of the Kareao <i>(Rhipogonum parviflorum)</i> and also Kakaho reeds <i>(Arundo conspicua)</i> ... were often used for the same purpose. <i>(1869,TrNZi,31</i>) [NOTES: (Colenso Botany North isd)]

>  Kareao. Rhipogonum scandens, Forst. <i>(1869,TrNZi,1/iii/50</i>) [NOTES: (Buchanan Botany Otago)]

>  <b>HAY</b> The Kareao ..., well-known to the settlers under the detested name of `supple-jack'. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/201</i>)

>  <b>DOMETT</b> Supplejack (<i>Kareao</i>) .. .. O. Liliaceae; G. Rhipogonum; S. R. scandens. <i>(1883,Ranolf and Amohia,1/302</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> i have sometimes found its crop distended with the ripe pulpy seed of the tataramoa (<i>Rubus australis</i>), or with the berries of the kaiwiria (<i>Parsonia albiflora</i>) [?Parsonsia] and kareao (<i>Rhipogonum scandens</i>); and it is said to feed also on the leaves of the thistle and wild cabbage. <i>ibid.</i> 1/12 Down in the bed of the ravine, and hiding the babbling brook, the stunted overhanging trees were for the most part clothed in a luxuriant mantle of kohia, kareao, and other epiphytic plants. <i>(1888,Birds of New Zealand,1/3</i>) [NOTES: [copy without second square bracks]]

>  All hands at bush fencing and all very tired. Karawas [supplejacks] as thick as a cloud, every tree hung together like wax. <i>(Jun 25, 1888,Chudleigh Diary,362,1950 ed.</i>)

>  The open land is covered ... in sheltered places [with] karioi <i>(Rhipogonum scandens),</i> wharangi <i>(Melicope ternata) ... </i> <i>(1889,TrNZi,21/35</i>)

>  <b>MORRiS</b> <i>Kareau or Kareao, n.</i> Maori name for <i>Supplejack</i>(q.v.). <i>(1898,Austral English,242</i>)

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> <i>R[hipogonum] scandens, Forst. ... Supplejack; Kareao; Pitita. </i>... an extract from the root has been employed in the place of sarsaparilla. <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,703</i>)

>  <b>LANG & BLACKWELL</b> The native name [of supplejack] is <i>Kare-ao</i>, which is plausibly interpreted to mean<i> a twisting rope. </i> <i>(1907,Plants of NZ,90,1940 p. 93 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BAUGHAN</b> <i>Kareao (karry-ow)</i>: The supple-jack; a liana. <i>(1908,Shingle-Short,189</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> Here the trees are totara, manuka, and a kind of cypress, or arbor vitae, no kareao; ... The akepiri ... is here abundant. <i>(1916,Bush Explorers</i>) [NOTES: 1916 Cowan Bush Explorers `More Reminiscences' (TS:VUW)]

>  <b>GUTHRiE</b> Two native boys were ... working on the pattern of a whaler's or maori house, [and] first made a framework of <i>kareau</i> or supplejack ..<i>ibid.</i>165 ... Hays' piece of reserved bush, where ... golden kowhai, scarlet kareau berries, white clematis, ... flourished undisturbed. <i>(1930,NZ Memories,47</i>)

>  <b>BEATTiE</b> To make a log fence you drove in two stakes here and there opposite each other and a few inches apart, and filled up with sticks lying flat and lashed with karewao vine. <i>(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,98</i>)

>  <i>kareao</i>, a vine (Rhipogonum scandens), supplejack; tough thin black vine. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/143</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <b>LYON</b> ... from the bush [near Buller River] plaited two cables of the karewa, or as we call it, supplejack vine. <i>(1952,Faring South,156</i>)

>  <b>WiLSON</b> <i>Rhipogonum scandens</i> SUPPLEJACK ... Kareao ... Karewao <i>(1982,Stewart island Plants,44</i>)

>  <b>BELiCH</b> <i>kareao</i> supplejack, a climbing plant <i>(1986,NZ Wars, The,337</i>)

>  <b>RiCHARDS</b> The sharp end of a kareao (supplejack) sprang back and pierced the corner of my eye ... <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,87</i>)

### karearea
 
 <i>n.</i> the indigenous falcon, of which there are three forms: the bush, the eastern, and southern falcons. [ORIGIN: Maori] (See [bush hawk](bri-byo.md#bush-hawk))

>  The New Zealand falcon, or karearea, is known to be a fearless and aggressive hunter. <i>(Jul 12, 2005,Dominion Post,A8</i>)

>  WHEN Lesley Barlow heard a loud noise from the upper floor of her Hillcrest home she had no idea a native New Zealand falcon, or Karearea, had just crashed into her house. <i>(Mar 15, 2005,Whakatane Beacon,3</i>)

>  if the karearea, or bush-hawk, screamed in rainy weather, it was an accurate sign of a change [sc. in the weather]. <i>(Sep 28, 1938,Auckland Weekly News,11</i>)

>  <b>DiEFFENBACH</b> 2. <i>Falco brunnea.</i> Gould. ... Kauaua of the natives. <i>Yate, Polack, Dieffenbach. </i> Kari-area of the natives of Queen Charlotte's Sound. <i>Forst. </i> <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/186</i>) [NOTES: 1842 Gray's Fauna in 1843 Dieffenbach Travels in NZ 2/186]

>  The Kaiaia [Karearea] is the Sparrow-Hawk quite as troublesome and more important than the Common Hawk. <i>(1844,NZ Journal,113,1956 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1844 J.B.Williams NZ Journal (1956) 113]

>  <b>SHORTLAND</b> KAREAREA, a hawk, believed by the natives to be `tuakana' to the mole. <i>(1851,Southern Districts of NZ,308</i>)

>  KAREAREA, a hawk, believed by the natives to be `tuakana' to the mole. <i>(1867,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,2/234,1960 ed.</i>)

>  Owing to the misapplication of the native names, Kahu and karearea, in Mr. Gray's first list [in Dieffenbach], writers in this country have inevitably fallen into the error of considering our large brown hawk the <i>Falco harpe</i> and our `Sparrow-hawk' the <i>Falco brunnea</i>, of that author. <i>(1869,TrNZi,1/iii/6</i>) [NOTES: (Buller Ornithology)]

>  FALCO NOVAE ZELANDiAE, Gml. Ka rewa rewa-tara. Quail-hawk. in New Zealand, ... the head of the family [Raptores] must be fairly assigned to this bird, which is commonly known by the name of the Quail or Sparrow-hawk ... <i>(1870,TrNZi,2/51</i>) [NOTES: (Potts Birds)]

>  <b>BULLER</b> HiERACiDEA \{HARPA\} NOVAE ZEALANDiAE. (QUAiL HAWK.) ... <i>Native names.</i> Karearea, Kaiaia, Kaeaea, Kakarapiti, Karewarewa, and Tawaka. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,1,{1888 2nd edn 1/213} ed.</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> HiERACiDEA BRUNNEA \{HARPA FEROX\}. (BUSH-HAWK.) ... <i>Native names</i>. Karearea, Kaiaia, Kaeaea, Kakarapiti, Karewarewa, and Tawaka; but sometimes distinguished as Karewarewa-tara. `Sparrow-Hawk' of the colonists. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,6,{1888 2nd edn 1/220} ed.</i>)

>  <i>karearea</i>: bush hawk. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,174</i>)

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> NEW ZEALAND FALCON <i>Falco novaeseelandiae Other names</i>: Bush Hawk, Sparrow Hawk, Quail Hawk, Karearea. <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,99</i>)

>  <i>120. Falco novaeseelandiae </i>Gmelin, 1788 <i>New Zealand Falcon (Karearea) </i> <i>(1970,Annotated Checklist of Birds of NZ,39,1980 ed.</i>)

>  <i>New Zealand Falcon Falco novaeseelandiae </i>GMELiN, 1788 ...[155] OTHER NAMES: <i>Karearea, sparrowhawk, bush hawk, quail hawk, eastern falcon, southern falcon, bush falcon. ... Bush falcon: in the North island confined to the main ranges, ... Eastern falcon: The main ranges of the South island ... Southern Falcon: Fiordland, the Auckland islands, and Stewart island. </i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,154</i>)

### kareau
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [karaeo](../K#karaeo))

### kareko
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HULME & MORRiSON</b> Kareko (also known, in the north. as parengo) is a kind of laver. <i>(1989,Homeplaces,60</i>)

### karengo
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  Lightly poached smoked eel served over a creamy Kawkawa risotto .. and a karengo seaweed salsa. <i>(Jul 2005,Capital Times,13-19 July / 13</i>)

>  The Karori restaurant, which offered a Born to Be Wild Salad (blue borage honey-glazed smoked eel and watercress, lime pepper salad with karengo crostini) matched with Monteith's Black, and Pheasantly Phruity Purple Haze (pheasant on crushed urenika potatoes with port and blackcurrent sauce, wild mushrooms and parsnip crisps) matched with Monteith's Celtic Red. <i>(Jul 21, 2004,Capital Times,13</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Porphyra columbina</i>, a common edible red seaweed [Maori], parengo (s. SI var. kareko). [ORIGIN: Maori, [Ma. <b>karengo</b> smooth, edible seaweed (<i>Porphyra columbina</i>)] cf. P.M. Ryan Dict. Modern Maori (1995) 86.]

>  Maori have always valued seaweed, especially karengo, which is regarded as taonga by Ngai Tahu. <i>(Jan 2005,Cuisine,216</i>)

>  <b>KOHERE</b> We get sea-foods: crayfish, paua, pupu, limpet and karengo the edible seaweed and sea-eggs. <i>(1951,Autobiography of a Maori,127</i>)

>  <b>CROWE</b> During the Second World War, when considerable quantities of karengo were sent to the Maori fighting in the Middle East, it was reported to be more thirst-quenching than chewing gum on desert marches. <i>(1997,Field Guide to Native Edible Plants of NZ,148</i>)

>  What many Pakehas may not realise, however, is that we have a close relation of nori growing along our coastline, commonly known by its Maori name, karengo .. The most commonly used species for Japanese nori is Porphyra tenera, whereas our karengo is Porphyra columbina .. But to be honest, i've tried karengo fresh, dried, cooked with orange juice, diluted with kumara, disguised with parmesan - and, still, i couldn't honestly say i adore its flavour, which, to me, has rather too strong an iodine edge to be delicious. <i>(Oct 12, 2002,Dominion Post,F7</i>)

>  More commonly known as karango, the seaweed [sc. <i>Porphyra columbina</i>] was rated by 19th century naturalist and missionary William Colenso as New Zealand's 12th-most important wild food plant. <i>(Sep 13, 1999,Dominion,6</i>)

>  There's no pretty-pretty garnish here, just the matt-black effect of granulated <i>karengo</i> or kelp sprinkled over the surface of the meat. Very minimalist - and very Ralph Hotere. <i>(Jan 2001,Cuisine,134</i>)

>  it has transformed the lettuce-like fronds of the mild-flavoured karengo - a close relative of the Japanese nori - into soft flakes that are perfect to use as a garnish of seasoning. <i>(Sep 2005,Air New Zealand inflight magazine,12</i>)

>  Air dry your karengo in the sun and use it dried. .. Add korengo to omelettes or scrambled eggs. <i>(Feb 5, 2010,Dominion Post,A15</i>)

>  The seaweed the Maori called karengo, known to the Japanese as nori and used to wrap sushi, has so many ways to reproduce. <i>(Jul 25, 2001,City Voice,4</i>)

>  There is the reef which is the seasonal gathering ground of the edible seaweed, karengo, a favourite article of diet of the Maori. <i>(Mar 2, 1938,Auckland Weekly News,42</i>)

>  .. dried karengo is now commercially available (and is being used brilliantly by Canterbury baker Rachel Scott, whose karengo bread is to be a permanent ficture on icon's menu), while fresh piko piko fern fronds, which taste a little like asparagus, are creating a sensation now that they are being gathered from the wild and promoted to the hospitality industry ... <i>(Jun 21, 2003,Dominion Post,F6</i>)

>  <b>EDWARDS</b> The tables themselves are loaded: hangi food, kaimoana, trout, tuna, titi, puha, karengo. <i>(1992,Mihipeka,21</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Karengo, <i>Col[enso]. Laminaria</i> sp.s <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),765</i>)

>  A few also of the sea-weeds were eaten; such as, the Karengo, (A tidal species of <i>Laminaria</i> found plentifully from the East Cape to Cape Turnagain), the Rehia, the Rimurapa (<i>D'Urvillea utilis</i>, and some others ... ) <i>(1869,TrNZi,1/iii/32</i>) [NOTES: (Colenso Botany North isd)]

>  <i>Laminaria sp. (Karengo.) </i>This seaweed, when fermented with the juice of the poisonous <i>tutu</i>, is used as an aperient. <i>(1905,TrNZi,37/120</i>)

>  <b>BEATTiE</b> Notable omissions from this list [of food sources compiled by Maori hands c1880] are the kareko (edible sea-weed), tio-kohatu (rock oysters), tio-pati (oysters), and various kinds of shellfish. <i>(1945,Maori Place-Names of Canterbury,62</i>)

>  <b>MARTiN</b> The most characteristic red seaweed of this [lower tidal] zone is the PURPLE LAVER (<i>Porphyra columbina</i>) or KARENGO of the Maoris. <i>(1929,Flora NZ,23,1961 ed.</i>)

>  <b>McLiNTOCK</b> Upper intertidal levels are occupied by species such as <i>Porphyra columbina</i>, the karengo ... <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/214</i>)

>  <b>FULLER</b> EDiBLE SEAWEED (PARENGO or KARENGO) Parengo (<i>Porphyra columbina</i>) is found at intertidal levels and was used by the Maori as a sort of relish .. Karengo looks rather like lettuce ... <i>(1978,Maori Food and Cookery,37</i>)

>  <b>BROOKER et al.</b> <i>Porphyra columbina </i>Mont. Maori name: <i>Karengo </i> Botanical notes: Hooker and Goldie listed karengo as <i>Laminaria</i> sp. but in the latest edition of Williams' Maori Dictionary the name is applied to a red seaweed, <i>Porphyra columbina. </i> <i>(1981,NZ Medicinal Plants,22</i>)

>  <b>BURTON</b> Karengo or parengo (<i>Porphyra columbina</i>) is a species of seaweed common on the East Coast of the North island and was well known to the Maori for its nutritive qualities. <i>(1982,Two Hundred Years of NZ Food & Cookery,9</i>)

>  in New Zealand karengo - porphyra columbina - was eaten with great relish by Maoris. A greenish-purple seaweed containing among other elements, vitamin C, it is found on most New Zealand coasts. <i>(Feb 22, 1985,Dominion,9</i>)

>  The kaimoana or seafood is all available from the nearest and hopefully clean coastline. Karengo (seaweed) is gathered off the rocks in August or September. Maoris used to cook it in the hangi but it can be steamed and dried or pickled in a jar with a few mussels. <i>(Jan 4, 1985,Evening Post,9</i>)

>  Karengo, a traditional Maori food, is New Zealand's only commonly occurring edible seaweed ... <i>(Nov 18, 1986,Dominion,1</i>)

>  This is the genus Porphyra, varieties of which are found all over the world. in Wales it is known as laver (P. laciniata) in Japan as nori (P. tenera) while here in New Zealand we call it karengo (P. columbina.). Karengo looks like greenish purple sheets of cellophane with frilly edges, and is to be found clinging to rocks at low tide. in shape, it resembles green sea lettuce (Ulva lactuca) which is also edible. <i>(Sep 15, 1987,Press, The,16</i>)

### karengorengo
 
 <i>n.</i> a freshwater fish

>  <b>SHERRiN</b> There are two other small fresh-water fish, the parikou and the karengorengo, but little information is at hand concerning their form or habits. <i>(1886,Handbook Fishes of NZ,143</i>)

### karerarera
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>STARK</b> KARERARERA (Water Fern) <i>Azolla rubra </i> <i>(1979,Maori Herbal Remedies,33</i>)

>  <b>BROOKER et al.</b> <i>Azolla filiculoides </i>Lam. var. <i>rubra</i> (R.Br.) Diels Common name: <i>Water fern </i>Maori name: <i>Karerarera </i>... `Karerarera' has been identified as `<i>Azolla rubra</i>' by Williams bu L. Smith comments that the plants used appear to be filamentous green algae. <i>(1981,NZ Medicinal Plants,26</i>)

### karetao
 
 <i>n.</i> a puppet [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  But the carvings are not poupou (columns). They are puppets, or karetao, representing the MPs holding each of the seven Maori seats in the last parliament. ... Some of the karetao carry two sets of strings, metal linking them back to their parties and wider democratic system, and flax signifying the link back to their Maori constituents. <i>(Nov 23, 2005,NZ Herald,B5</i>)

### karetau
 
 <i>n.</i> Maori action figures

>  Maori had action figures, called karetau. They had songs with them. Maori culture rocks - they had action figures before anyone. <i>(Aug 6, 2003,Capital Times,11</i>)

### karetu
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Hierochloe antarctica</i>, a sweet-scented grass

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> Kar|etu, s. A plant so called. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,160</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> [Word list] Karetu a sweet scented grass <i>(Aug 1843,Journal,51</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Karetu, <i>Col[enso]. Hierochloe redolens. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),765</i>)

>  The daisy like flowers of the Roniu <i>(Brachycome odorata,) </i>and the flowering tops of the sweet-scented grass Karetu<i> (Hierchloe redolens)</i>, were worn around the neck ... <i>(1869,TrNZi,1/iii/37</i>) [NOTES: (Colenso Botany North isd)]

>  <i>Hierochloe redolens,</i> Br., `Karetu.' Common in wet places and swamps, of rather stout habit but sweet and succulent ... <i>(1870,TrNZi,2/102</i>) [NOTES: (Kirk Grasses)]

>  <i>Hierochloe redolens,</i> , Br. - Karetu, swamp grass; abundant in swamps and wet places; eaten by stock, but far too coarse for general cultivation. November and December. <i>(1872,TrNZi,4/301</i>) [NOTES: (Report on Grasses)]

>  <b>HAY</b> (Report on Grasses) <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/199</i>)

>  <b>TREGEAR</b> The sweet-scented grass <i>karetu</i> (Hierochloe redolens) was spread in the sleeping houses of chiefs, as in Germany the same plant is strewed on festival days before the doors of churches. <i>(1904,Maori Race, The,14</i>)

>  First she must obtain a handful of the fragrant grass called <i>karetu (Hierochloe redolens)</i>, and insert therein a portion of <i>paraheka</i> or <i>tatea</i> (semen and perputial secretion). <i>(1905,TrNZi,37/97</i>)

>  <i>karetu</i>: scented grass. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,174</i>)

>  <b>BUCK</b> <i>(1949,Coming of the Maori, The,289 + 175</i>)

>  <b>MARTiN</b> Moist soils both on coastal cliffs and in subalpine valleys are the usual station of the KARETU or SCENTED HOLY GRASS (<i>Hierochloe antarctica</i>) and in subalpine areas only of the smaller <i>H. fraseri </i>... ALPiNE HOLY GRASS. <i>(1929,Flora NZ,132,1961 ed.</i>)

>  <i>karetu [s] Hierochloe redolens</i> (Sol. ex Vahl) R. & S. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,41</i>)

>  <b>STARK</b> KARETU (Holy Grass) <i>Hierochloe redolens,</i> Holy Grass is a relatively common New Zealand growth previously known as <i>Hierochloe antarctica. </i> <i>(1979,Maori Herbal Remedies,33</i>)

>  <b>WiLSON</b> <i>Hierochloe</i> [=holy grass] <i>redolens </i>[=sweet scented] HOLY GRASS ... Kaaretu <i>(1982,Stewart island Plants,410</i>)

>  Karetu grass, or flax saturated witrh scented oils like raukawa, taramea gum, kopuru (fragrant moss) and tarata (lemonwood), were used as grass belts and head bands by Maori. Karetu was important in aiding conception, courtship and Maori ceremonies. <i>(Jan 15, 2003,Capital Times,6</i>)

### karewa
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [karaeo](../K#karaeo))

### karewarewa
 
 <i>n.</i> = karearea [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> As we went we heard a karewarewa (the sparrow hawk) screaming, the natives immediately exclaimed, tomorrow will be a rainy day repeating the old saying Ka tangi te karewarewa ki waenga o te rangi pai ka ua apopo [etc.] <i>(Apr 18, 1847,Journal,5/33</i>)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> Of the <i>Falcon</i> family, there are two species in New Zealand; one, called Kahu, is about the size of a pigeon, and the other, named Karewarewa, is a sparrow-hawk: <i>(1859,Story of New Zealand, The,1/23</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> HiERACiDEA \{HARPA\} NOVAE ZEALANDiAE. (QUAiL HAWK.) ... <i>Native names.</i> Karearea, Kaiaia, Kaeaea, Kakarapiti, Karewarewa, and Tawaka. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,1,{1888 2nd edn 1/213} ed.</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> HiERACiDEA BRUNNEA \{HARPA FEROX\}. (BUSH-HAWK.) ...<i>Native names.</i> Karearea, Kaiaia, Kaeaea, Kakarapiti, Karewarewa, and Tawaka; but sometimes distinguished as Karewarewa-tara. `Sparrow-Hawk' of the colonists. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,6,{1888 2nd edn 1/220} ed.</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> HiERACiDEA BRUNNEA \{HARPA FEROX\}. (BUSH-HAWK.) ... <i>Native names.</i> Karearea, Kaiaia, Kaeaea, Kakarapiti, Karewarewa, and Tawaka; but sometimes distinguished as Karewarewa-tara. `Sparrow-Hawk' of the colonists. . <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,6,{1888 2nd edn 1/220} ed.</i>)

 <i>n.</i> (See [kareao](k-kea.md#kareao))

### kariba weed
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Salvinia molesta</i>

>  Potentially more serious is the kariba weed (<i>Salvinia molesta</i>), an agressive weed of hybrid origin that clogs many tropical waterways, but one which has been largely eradicated in New Zealand by a combination of careful management and temperate climate. <i>(Jan 2001,NZ Geographic,No. 49/80</i>)

### karikiti kiraki
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  Some of O'Leary's jokes are bilingual, such as the splendid piece of onomatopeia in which the rattle of a railway carriage is described as "karikiti kiraki". <i>(Jun 16, 2002,Sunday Star Times,C7</i>)

### Karitane
 
 <i>n.</i> (humorous) fig. & transf. senses from colour of babies' faeces [ca 1940's (G.L. Gabites) Used esp. by servicemen for scrambled eggs made from egg-powder.]

>  <b>HAYWARD</b> At Parliament our offices have been repainted, and deep pile carpet of an unfortunate `Karitane' gold has been laid. <i>(1981,Diary of the Kirk Years,112</i>) [NOTES: 1973 Feb 13 Hayward Diary of the Kirk Years (1981)]



 
 <i>n.</i> 2 Bat. NZA Tank Brigade (from their yellow shoulder-patches) [ca 1941 (G.L. Gabites)]



 
 <i>n.</i> a) the name given to Plunket Society hospitals and family support units. b) a nurse trained by the Plunket Society for the care of babies and preschoolers. 1. Short form of Karitane nurse, named from the Otago bay and Truby King's house there.

>  i went round to various houses as a Karitane... it was something special to be a Karitane and we honoured the name, but oh, it was hard work. <i>(1990,Matriarchs,142</i>)

>  The distinction between a. Plunket nurse and a Karitane is an important one. Plunket nurses must be registered nurses before they embark on the 17-week Plunket training course and they often have additional midwifery or nursing qualifications. Karitanes have basic child-care training with no nursing qualification required. <i>(Nov 1992,North & South,108</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> Erik was mightily puzzled by the boy's strange conduct. Was he training to be a Karitane or what? <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,167</i>)

>  <i>Karitane n. N.Z.</i> a nurse for babies; nanny. [ < former child-care hospital at <i>Karitane</i>, New Zealand] <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,614</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 409; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 270]]

>  <i>Karitane, </i>a Plunket nurse, trained member of the Plunket Society, the Royal New Zealand Society for the Health of Women and Children, founded by Sir Truby King in 1907 at his Karitane home near Dunedin. (He called his society after the then Governor General.) <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A</i>)

>  <b>HALL</b> Raelene had this Karitane who went straight to town, bought a few knick knacks for her glory box. <i>(1980,Prisoner of Mother England,21</i>)

>  it is estimated that the number of Plunket nurses will have been cut by 70... Some of their duties will be taken over.. by less qualified staff such as karitanes. <i>(Nov 7, 1992,Evening Post,4</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> Describing or pertaining to Karitane nurses or nursing, or the Plunket Society, from the noun, and its further use for the nursing headquarters of the Plunket Society: hence used in many gen. and special combinations: - foods, put out by the Society ("Karitane' is the brand-name for Plunket Society products), - nurse,-hospital.

>  <b>iRELAND</b> it took a year of the punishing rigours of Karitane nursing to encourage me to learn the first essential for survival. <i>(1998,Under the Bridge & Over the Moon,12</i>)

>  When i visited the Karitane home there were twenty-three small inmates. <i>(Jul 28, 1938,NZ Observer,7</i>)

>  [Hdg] Days of Karitane hijinks. More than 300 former Karitane nurses from around the world swapped tales of babies, youthful hijinks and "board-stiff" uniforms at a reunion in Wellington at the weekend. Karitane Hospital, founded in Wellington in 1927 by Sir Truby King, had about 2000 graduatres pass through its halls until its demise in 1979. <i>(Oct 28, 2003,Dominion Post</i>)

>  On a terrace below the Karitane Home, there is a small but well-equipped laboratory. <i>ibid.</i> 13 When the war began, Karitane Products had poor stocks of imported oil. <i>(1944,Korero (AEWS Background Bulletin),2(22) : 12</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> You give 'em the Karitane treatment, Bob? <i>(1962,One Foot at the Pole,156</i>)

>  <b>SCRYMGEOUR</b> Always a leader in the van of social welfare, the Dominion had given much thought and practical expression in the methods of `Karitane' and `Plunkett' care of babies <i>(1960,Memories of Maoriland,153</i>)

>  <b>BAXTER</b> The misunderstanding between them ... stretched right back to the cotton wool and enemas and foreskin-clipping doctors of a Karitane nursing home. <i>(1985,Horse,7,C1962 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (Ghost in Trousers)]

>  Plunket and Karitane were household words which had earned respect and recognition beyond these shores in countries where infant and child were subjects of study and concern. <i>(Nov 5, 1964,Evening Post,10</i>)

>  <b>McLAGLAN</b> Other delicate babies followed them and so his beach cottage at Karitane became the first Karitane baby hospital. All the Plunket Society's baby hospitals are called `Karitane' hospitals and Plunket nurses are trained in them. <i>(1965,Stethoscope & Saddlebags,78</i>)

>  <b>BOYD</b> in a year the Society established itself on a firm footing with 72 subscribers but it faced three major problems: finance, finding a suitable nurse, giving her a Karitane training and retaining her services, and objections from doctors. <i>(1984,City of the Plains,217</i>)

>  <b>McGiLL</b> We passed pretty little Karitane Bay, where Truby King's first plunket house survives; it could use some solid historic protection.] <i>(1985,G'day Country,172</i>)

### Karitane Hospital
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Mothers came into the Karitane Hospital if they were having trouble nursing their children, or if the babies were not well. <i>(1990,Matriarchs,140</i>)

### Karitane nurse
 
 <i>n.</i> a nurse trained at the Karitane Hospital esp. in the care of babies and nursing mothers.

>  i saw a bunch of young kickers on my rounds, in charge of a cheerful Karitane nurse. <i>(Jul 28, 1938,NZ Observer,7</i>)

>  There is also the Karitane nurse, who is qualified in much the same way [sc. as the Plunket nurse]. <i>(Jun 6, 1925,Evening Post,13</i>)

>  Nurses trained only in baby management are called Karitane Nurses, so called because the first children so cared for came under [Dr. King's] personal supervision at his home in Karitane. <i>(Feb 16, 1938,Auckland Weekly News,11</i>)

>  <b>GASKELL</b> Margaret had started training as a Karitane nurse ... <i>(1978,All Part of the Game,121</i>) [NOTES: 1947 Gaskell `Holiday' in 1978 All Part of the Game 121]

>  <b>MACFARLANE</b> A Karitane nurse in the shape of a man in dingy blue dungarees or a cow girl in jodhpurs takes charge of [the calf] immediately [it is born] ... <i>(1954,Te Raka,36</i>)

>  <b>MACNiCOL</b> None of the children ... ever saw a Karitane nurse or was weighed after leaving the nursing home. <i>(1965,Skippers Road,45</i>)

>  These were the days when a Karitane nurse could be hired for three guineas a week. Evelyn hired one. <i>(Jul 27, 1987,Dominion,9</i>)

>  When they [mother and baby] came home it was to the tender care of one of Auckland's best-loved Karitane nurses, Jenny Wrenn. <i>(1988,Through the Looking Glass,32</i>) [NOTES: (Tessa Duder)]

>  Here girls.. learn how to qualify as baby nurses in private homes, with the certificate of a Karitane nurse. <i>(Apr 1, 1924,Otago Witness,61</i>)

### Karitane nursing
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Karitane nursing in England was a different experience from working here. <i>(1990,Matriarchs,142</i>)

### Karitane yellow
 
 <i>n.</i> a colour noun, from the supposed colour of baby's excreta [ca 1945]

>  Dunne has made a habit of betting on the right side when all is turning to karitane yellow around. <i>(Oct 23, 2005,Sunday Star Times,C13</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> Green Band, Red Band, Gold Top, Double Brown, Nut Brown, ... all the colours of purge. Karitane yellow. What was he babysitting for on Friday night ...? <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,173</i>)

>  <b>McGiLL</b> <i>Karitane yellow</i> hideous yellow paint associated with Plunket hospitals. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,65</i>)

### Karitane-ing
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Once back in London i did a certain amount of Karitane-ing. <i>(1990,Matriarchs,144</i>)

### karito
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Karito, <i>Lindsay. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),765</i>)

### kark it
 
 <i>v.</i> to die (See [also cark](c-chi.md#cark))

>  if the sap doesn't rise in the city of love, we reckon, you might as well kark it. <i>(Dec 27, 1998,Sunday Star Times,A2</i>)

>  Mehrts is firing up a kick and the sodding telly karks it. <i>(Oct 2, 1999,Evening Post,7</i>)

>  "On day two i lay on my bed and thought i'd karked it." <i>(Oct 27, 2002,Sunday Star Times,A13</i>)

>  if i left her [sc. a dog] at home she would probably kark it. <i>(Feb 5, 1994,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  "Excellent. Can i come too?" "Sure. Wanna kark it, or just be a tetraplegic? <i>(Jan 8, 1998,Dominion,6</i>)

>  After all this,. i didn't want to cark it in Carcassonne. <i>(Mar 6, 2001,Dominion,23</i>)

>  important potato newsflash! You will not cark it if you eat green spuds. <i>(Sep 23, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),48</i>)

>  Rhett came up trumps after his namby- pamby wife Ann.. obligingly carked it with yellow fever. <i>(Feb 18, 1995,Dominion,23</i>)

 <i>n.</i> <i>Weinmannia racemosa</i>
