 <i>n.</i> (See [kamahi](k-kea.md#kamahi))

### karo
 
 <i>n.</i> a mainly coastal shrub or small tree, with sticky seed capsules [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  The most obvious [native plant] is karo, which is pretty serious in the Salamanca Slope bush remnant above the rose garden... <i>(Sep 2004,Branch Out,No. 14/8</i>)

>  Some native plants, such as karo, have been introduced to encourage nectar-eating birds. <i>(Dec 10, 2001,Dominion,17</i>)

>  Picked up a pretty shrub `Te Karo' from which the Maories extract an oil which they highly esteem. [ed fn] <i>Pittosporum crassifolium</i> and <i>P. Cornifolium</i> which give a black dye. <i>(Apr 10, 1842,Journal of Ensign Best,365</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Pittosporum <i>tenuifolium, </i>Banks et Sol. ... common as far south as Akaroa, ... Nat. names, `Mapauriki', <i>Cunn.</i>; `Kohuhu', <i>Col.</i>; `Karo' of Middle island, <i>Lyall. </i> <i>ibid.</i> 23 Pittosporum <i>cornifolium,</i> A.Cunn. ... HAB. Northern island; ... Nat. name, `Karo', <i>Colenso</i>; `Piri-piri', <i>Auct? </i> <i>(1853,Flora NZ,i Flowering Plants 21</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Karo, <i>Col[enso]. Pittosporum cornifolium </i>and <i>crassifolium. </i> ... Karo, Middle island, <i>Lyall. Pittosporum tenuifolium. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),765</i>)

>  Of our shrubs and smaller timber trees, several are of strikingly beautiful growth ... <i>(e.g.)</i> the Houhere, <i>Hoheria populnea </i>...; the Horopito <i>Drimys axillaris;</i> the Manuka-rau-riki, <i>Leptospermum ericoides; </i> the Kohuhu, <i>Pittosporum tenuifolium</i>; the Kowhai, especially the small-leaved mountain variety, <i>Sophora tetraptera</i> var.: <i>grandiflora</i>; the Koromiko-taranga, <i>Veronica,</i> several species; the Mairehau, <i>Phebalium nudum</i>; the Toro, <i>Persoonia</i> Toro; the Pukapuka, <i>Brachyglottis repanda</i>; the Northern Maire, <i>Santalum Cunninghami</i>i; the Tawari, <i>ixerba brexioides</i>; the Tipau, </i>Myrsine Urvillei</i> and <i>M. salicina</i>; the Tangeao, <i>Tetranthera calicaris</i>; the Ramarama, <i>Myrtus bullata</i>; the Ti, <i>Cordyline australis</i>; the Kahikomako, <i>Pennantia corymbosa</i>; the Pate, <i>Schefflera digitata</i>; the Horoeka, <i>Panax crassifolia: </i>and, on the sea-coast, ... the Karo, <i>Pittosporum crassifolium; </i>and the truly evergreen Ngaio, <i>Myoporum laetum ... </i> <i>(1869,TrNZi,1/iii/5</i>) [NOTES: (Colenso Botany North isd)]

>  Pittosporum tenuifolium Karo <i>(1870,TrNZi,2/122</i>) [NOTES: (Armstrong Botany Canterbury)]

>  [Native name] Karo .. [Settlers' name] Karo .. [Family] Pittosporeae <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,121</i>) [NOTES: 1886 T.Kirk in 1902 Settler's Handbook of NZ 121]

>  <b>FEATON</b> PiTTOSPORUM CRASSiFOLiUM (<i>Banks & Sol</i>.) ... The `KARO'.- This species is common on the coastline throughout the Northern island ... in common with some of the other Pittosporads, it is known to the settlers as one of the `Turpentine' trees; which are probably so called on account of the gluten, in which the seeds in the capsule are immersed, emitting a peculiar odour, when expressed. <i>ibid.</i> 35 PiTTOSPORUM UMBELLATUM (<i>Banks & Sol.</i>) ... This beautiful species is indigenous to the Northern island only, and is common about the Bay of islands and neighbourhood. To the natives living in that locality it is known as the `Karo'. <i>ibid.</i> 177 Karo [Maori name] .. Turpentine tree [Settlers' name] .. Pittosporum crassifolium .. Tree <i>(1889,Art Album NZ Flora,34</i>)

>  The trees growing with the pohutukawa are karo, ... and oho<i> (Panax lessoni). </i> <i>(1889,TrNZi,21/34</i>)

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> Other common coastal trees in the north are the karo <i>(Pittosporum crassifolium)</i> ... and the whau ... <i>(1910,NZ Plants,79</i>)

>  <b>LEVY</b> ... such species as lancewood, karamu, toro, ramarama, koromiko, kanono, kohuhu, kawakawa, hangehange, rangiora, five finger, patete, toru, tree daisies, broadleaf, kamahi, mahoe, tarata, karo, lacebark, wineberry, fuchsia, kowhai, with tree ferns, weki, and mamuku. Toetoe, phormium, cabbage tree, with swamp coprosma and hupiro may figure as a developmental phase on wet soils. <i>(1951,Grasslands of NZ,87,1970 ed.</i>)

>  <i>karo n</i> -s [Maori] : either of two New Zealand plants of the genus <i>Pittosporum:</i> a : a shrub or small tree (<i>P. crassifolium</i>) with fastigiate branches b : an epiphyte (<i>P. cornifolium</i>) with whorled leaves <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third international,1233</i>)

>  <i>karo n.</i> NZ shrub or small tree with 5-petalled brown flowers. [M] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,409</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  251 Lithograph of karo foliage. <i>(1890,PWD Catalogue of Timbers,20</i>)

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> (And) <i>tosporum Fairchildii </i> <i>(1919,NZ Plants & Their Story,2ND ed.</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> The word in this sense appears only in Torlesse's writings from Cant., the second 1849 form if genuine represents an uncertain hearing of Maori [r]. The plant denoted would seem to be rather the BUSH LAWYERT than MATAGOURI, and if so may represent a hearing of a *<i>karamoa</i> form of <i>taramoa</i> `lawyer' parallel with<i> karamea / taramea</i> `spear grass'. If it denotes <i>tumatakuru,</i> it perhaps represents an idiosyncratic hearing of the final- <i>kuru. </i> [[?Is this the sense in 1949 Buck Coming of the maori 396 check.]]

>  Fine soil, thick growth of grass and karo. <i>(Mar 26, 1849,Torlesse Papers,70,1958 ed.</i>)

>  Thick growth of kalo and talamea near the bush which was ... a black birch forest with an occasional red pine. [ed. fn] Matagouri or Wild irishman. <i>(Apr 14, 1849,Torlesse Papers,74,1958 ed.</i>)

>  (Torlesse) There are two plants [in Canterbury], the karo (prickly thorn) and taramea (spear grass), whch are well worthy of their names, but are almost exclusively confined to the hilly regions. There generally grows near these a plant called the timuka, from the leaf of which the natives make mats and leggings, which are impervious to the sharp points of the former. <i>(Jul 28, 1851,Lyttelton Times,7</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> as a hidden play on Maori meaning poss. not known to readers slipped into a list, ??was this a slang term for `woman' at the time, ??or is there another connection or sense.

>  Warriors, Wahines, Kuri, Poaka, Tamariki, Kumeras, Karos, interpreters (1st, 2nd and 3rd grade), Poets Laureate, Poi Dancers, Guides, &c. on application to the Registrar. ...WAiPiRO - - An acursed drink ... TAiHOA - - The Police have the matter in hand POAKA - -a pig PUKEKO A bokakker out of season KORERO A long talk UTU - - The spike HAPU - - A Tribe KUMERA - - A sweet Spud <i>(1913,Auckland University Carnival Book,27</i>)

### karohi
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BUiCK</b> it has been reported that [moas] caught the small fresh-water fish - the kokopu, the inanga, the kakawai, and the karohi (swamp spotties). <i>(1937,Moa-Hunters of NZ,236</i>)

### Karori tractor
 
 <i>n.</i> a 4WD vehicle [cf. Remuera tractor, Fendalton tractor]

>  if you are looking for a Karori tractor, then look elsewhere, but if you want a great offroad 4wd then this is the 4x4 for you. <i>(Jan 19, 2009,internet - see Notes,1</i>) [NOTES: www.trademe.co.nz/nz/auction-84978994.htm]

>  [Hdg] Akatarawas trek true test of 'Karori tractors'. You've bought a four-wheel-drive but its standard outings are trips to the supermarket and dropping the kids off. <i>(Feb 10, 2004,Hutt News,6</i>) [NOTES: cf. Remuera tractor, Fendalton tractor]

>  if it's going to cost more to drive, the Government should increase the levy on 4WDs or Karori tractors. After all, they chew up more of the road surface than an ordinary car ... <i>(Mar 16, 2009,Dominion Post,B4</i>)

### karoro
 
 <i>n.</i> the black-backed gull,; a large common NZ seagull with black on back and above wings; the black-backed gull. [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  The karoro (larus dominicanus) so termed from the black and white plumage of those gulls. <i>(Nov 12, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,8</i>)

>  The dissimilarity in appearance of immature gulls and their elders prompted Maori to give fully grown birds one name - karoro - and juveniles another - ngoiro. <i>(May 2005,NZ Geographic,No 73 / 47</i>)

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> K|aroro, s. Name of a certain bird. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,160</i>)

>  <b>SHORTLAND</b> [probably for <i>karoro</i>, rather than <i>kororaa</i> blue penguin] One day ... we were much delighted at the ... ingenuity displayed by a sea-bird, a species of tern, called a `korora' by the natives, of which there were large flocks always hovering over the water, or crowded together on the sand-banks. <i>(1851,Southern Districts of NZ,12</i>)

>  Went on to Kapoai ... had dinner - well supplied with pipis but alas no tupakihi ... saw another noteworthy sight ...[sic] a young karoro just out of the nest, running along in a curious human way all by himself. <i>ibid.</i> 1861 Jan 30 1/685 Very much interested in a large stratum of lignite to be seen under the sandcliffs [at Moiatoa], kapia still in ... Watched the karoros carrying up the pouas & letting them drop to break the shells... Saw my first korora (dead) <i>(Jan 21, 1861,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/680,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (A.S. Atkinson)]

>  <b>HAAST</b> Another ... sea bird belonging to the gulls is the karoro, which skims rapidly over the surf ... <i>(1861,Report on Topographical Exploration of Nelson,140</i>)

>  <b>WEKEY</b> Among gulls, albatrosses, and other sea birds ... the <i>toria</i>, or oyster eater, and the <i>korora</i>, also living on a species of shell-fish, called <i>pipi</i>, is met with on the sea shore. <i>(1862,Otago As it is,66</i>)

>  <b>HUTTON</b> 108. LARUS DOMiNiCANUS. Licht. Black-backed Gull. Karoro. <i>(1871,Birds of New Zealand,40</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> LARUS DOMiNiCANUS. (SOUTHERN BLACK-BACKED GULL.) ... <i>Native names.</i> Karoro; the young bird distinguished as Ngoiro, \{Koiro, and Punua\}. [271] This fine Gull, which ranges over the whole Southern Hemisphere, is extremely plentiful. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,270,{1888 2nd edn 2/47} ed.</i>)

>  <b>MORRiS</b> <i>Karoro, n</i>. Maori name for a Black-backed Gull, <i>Larus dominicanus</i>, Licht. [1888 Buller] <i>(1898,Austral English,242</i>)

>  ... the Karoro, or Black-backed Gull, in the wild and dreary solitudes of the Ocean Beach. ... named Larus Dominicanus (Dominican Gull) from his likeness to the mendicant brothers of that order ... <i>(Mar 1904,NZ illustrated Magazine,482</i>)

>  <b>TREGEAR</b> The large sea-gull (<i>karoro</i>: Larus dominicanus) and another gull, the Oyster-catcher (<i>torea</i>: Haemotopus sp.), were domesticated, but merely as pets ... <i>(1904,Maori Race, The,180</i>)

>  <i>karoro</i>, sea-bird (Larus dominicanus), black-backed seagull. <i>(Jun 1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/152</i>) [NOTES: (J.C.Andersen)]

>  <i>karoro</i>: gull. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,174</i>)

>  <b>POWELL</b> BLACK-BACKED GULL (<i>Larus dominicanus</i>), Karoro of the Maoris, is easily distinguished from either the Red or the Black-billed gulls by its much larger size, <i>(1947,Native Animals of NZ,80</i>)

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> SOUTHERN BLACK-BACKED GULL <i>Larus dominicanus Maori name:</i> Karoro, Ngoiro (imm). <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,154</i>)

>  <i>karoro n.</i> New Zealand black-blacked gull [Maori]. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),618</i>)

>  <i>karoro ... n.</i> N.Z. black-blacked gull [Maori]. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),11</i>)

>  193. <i>Larus dominicanus</i> Lichtenstein, 1823 <i>Southern Black-backed Gull (Karoro [Dominican Gull]) </i>Circumpolar ... <i>(1970,Annotated Checklist of Birds of NZ,53,1980 ed.</i>)

>  Of birds. One of them is eyeing the bait, head of the soldier, and is following. ... it's dead. meat, hooked, Krazy Karoro. <i>(1985,Landfall,186/450</i>) [NOTES: Grace (Kahawai)]

>  <i>Dominican Gull Larus dominicanus </i>LiCHTENSTEiN, 1823 ...The New Zealand population has increased this century. ... OTHER NAMES: <i>Karoro, southern black-backed gull, kelp gull. </i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,221</i>)

>  [Names used for Air NZ fleet aircraft include:] ZK-NAO `KARORO' (Black-Backed Gull) <i>(Jun 1988,Pacific Way,98</i>)

>  <b>WiLLiAMS</b> The Kauau [Kawau] or common diver, the Raruhiruhi, the first name is generally used, the Black Shagg or diver and the latter to the smaller diver, with white brest [sic]; ... [Glossary, 118 kawau black shag (<i>phalacrocorax varius</i>)] <i>(1844,NZ Journal,115,1956 ed.</i>)

>  <i> Pied Cormorant Phalacrocorax varius</i> (GMELiN, 1789) ... OTHER NAMES: <i>Karuhiruhi, pied shag, yellow-faced cormorant. ... Common around mainland Australia .</i>.. <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,116</i>)

### kataha
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <i>... large numbers of aua or kataha </i>(Agonostoma forsteri); <i>about 60 lb. ... weight of pilchard or mohimohi (Clupea sagax),</i> two varieties; ... hundreds of young red-cod, rarii <i>(Lotella bacchus)</i>, and what i believe are young rock-cod, or kokopu or rawaru (Percis colias). The red-cod were from 3 in. to 4 in. in length, and the rawaru, or, as the natives here call them, `toitoi' or `panepane', from 2 in. to 6 in. long. <i>(1903,TrNZi,35/319</i>) [NOTES: Mair Fish in Piako River]

### kataitai
 
 <i>n.</i> pihoihoi, ground lark

>  <b>DiEFFENBACH</b> 35. Alanda Novae Seelandiae. Gmel., ... Kogoo aroure of the natives of Queen Charlotte's Sound. <i>Forst</i>. Kataitai of the natives of Cook's Straits. <i>Dieffenbach</i>. A `Ground Lark' is given under the name of Pihoihoi, by Mr Yate; Piohiohi, by Mr Polack; Pi-o-oie, by M. Lesson, which may prove to be the above species. <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/192</i>)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> The <i>Fringillidoe,</i> or Finch family, has two species; one of them, the Kataitai or ground-lark, is the most numerous bird in the country. it rarely sings unless its nest is approached, when it rises in the air a short distance and sings, evidently for the purpose of drawing off the danger. <i>(1859,Story of New Zealand, The,1/25</i>)

### katakata
 
 <i>n.</i> mirth, laughs [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  The Morningside Boys are back for more katakata (or laughs) as the second series of Bro'town hits the screens and an annual hits the bookshops. <i>(Oct 2005,Tearaway,10</i>)

### katatai
 
 <i>n.</i> banded rail.

>  <b>YATE</b> <i>Katati</i> - This bird answers nearest to the godwit, of any i am acquainted with. <i>(1835,New Zealand (various authors/titles),69</i>)

>  <b>DiEFFENBACH</b> 56. <i>Rallus assimilis</i>. The pectoral band on the breast, and rufous colour of the cheeks and on the sides of the neck, are much less prominent than on the Australian specimens, ... Konini of the natives of Cook's Strait. Dieffenbach. Katatai of the natives. <i>Yate</i> and <i>Miss Stone. </i> <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/1`97</i>) [NOTES: 1842 Gray's Fauna in 1843 Dieffenbach Travels in NZ 2/197]

### Kath
 
 <i>n.</i> Kathleen Mavourneen n1 [slang]

>  <b>PARTRiDGE</b> <i>Kath (or K)</i>. An indefinitely long term of imprisonment: New Zealand c. (- 1914). -2. Hence, `the duration' (q.v.): New Zealand military: 1915-18.[O.J. T. Alpers <i>Cheerful Yesterdays</i>, 1928.] <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang,449</i>) [NOTES: slang]

>  <b>BAKER</b> [Underworld slang includes] ... <i>kath</i> a prison sentence of indefinite length ... [FN.] The Australian underworld has <i>Kathleen Mavourneen ... </i> <i>(1941,NZ Slang,53</i>)

>  <b>PARTRiDGE</b> An indeterminate prison-sentence: Australia and New Zealand: since ca. 1910. in, e.g., Partridge, 1937; Baker, 1941 (N.Z.) and 1942 (Australia). Ex the famous old song, Kathleen Mavour- neen (`it may be for years and it may be for ever') Cf. the next two entries. [Katleen maroon/mavourneen] <i>(1949,Dictionary of the Underworld,378,1961 ed.</i>)

>  <b>WALL</b> A rare example of real ... wit in the criminal class is `Kathleen Mavourneen' or `kath' for an indeterminate sentence. This dates from about 1910 and originated either in New Zealand or in Australia. i remember well the delight with which it was told to me by the late Mr Justice O.T.J. Alpers who had come upon it in the course of his practice as a barrister. This was about 1910. Another fancy term for this tantalising sentence, `Cat and Mouse', is also clever and witty but less poetical. <i>(1958,Queen's English, The,127</i>)

>  <b>McGiLL</b> <i>kath</i> indefinite term of imprisonment; originally the duration of WWi; from <i>Kathleen Mavourneen</i>, early-C19 song by one Miss Crawford, with words: `it may be for years and it may be forever.' NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,65</i>)

### Kathleen Maroon
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [Kathleen Mavourneen](k-kea.md#kathleen-mavourneen))

### Kathleen Mavourneen
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> an old term for an indeterminate prison sentence, (esp. under the `habitual criminal' legislation).

>  New Zealand has now introduced `habitual criminals' legislation..... The criminal classes, with caustic wit, christened the obnoxious measure the `Kathleen Mavourneen' Act, the allusion being to a familiar line in that most pathetic and charming ballad- `it may be for years, and it may be for ever'. [Phs refers to the NSW act which may have been named thus before the NZ act was passed] <i>(1907,Red Funnel,iV/21</i>)

>  Amy has now finished her set sentence, and has been transferred to New Plymouth, there to undergo detention as a `Kathleen Mavourneen.' in the Dunedin jug she has been of exemplary conduct, and it is prognosticated that she won't have to stay long at New Plymouth. <i>(Dec 10, 1910,NZ Truth,285/4/6</i>)

>  <b>ALPERS</b> it is the indeterminate duration of this sentence [reformative detention for habitual criminals] that crimals so dread......i had never heard this name for the indeterminate sentence, which is now well established in prison slang, `Kath', videlicet, `Kathleen Mavourneen because: `it may be for years, and it may be for ever'. <i>(1928,Cheerful Yesterdays,274</i>)

>  <b>BAKER</b> [Underworld slang includes] ... <i>kath</i> a prison sentence of indefinite length ... [FN.] The Australian underworld has <i>Kathleen Mavourneen ... </i> <i>(1941,NZ Slang,53</i>)

>  <b>PARTRiDGE</b> A three-years prison-sentence: an indefinite term of imprisonment: Australian: since ca. 1920. in, e.g., Baker, 1942 (the former sense), and Baker, 1945 (the latter). A corruption of Kathleen Mavourneen. <i>(1949,Dictionary of the Underworld,378,1961 ed.</i>)

>  <b>WALL</b> A rare example of real ... wit in the criminal class is `Kathleen Mavourneen' or `kath' for an indeterminate sentence. This dates from about 1910 and originated either in New Zealand or in Australia. i remember well the delight with which it was told to me by the late Mr Justice O.T.J. Alpers who had come upon it in the course of his practice as a barrister. This was about 1910. Another fancy term for this tantalising sentence, `Cat and Mouse', is also clever and witty but less poetical. <i>(1958,Queen's English, The,127</i>)

>  <b>McLiNTOCK</b> Examples of slang and colloquial expressions of local origin are ... <i>Kathleen Mavourneen,</i> a witty name for an indefinite [prison] sentence, ... <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,2/680</i>) [NOTES: Wall NZ Speech]

>  <b>MacKENZiE</b> in 1953 i made a study ... of New Zealand's habitual criminals. These 520 people ... had received the accolade of New Zealand criminality ... in prison language they had been given the `collar', the `key', the Kathleen Mavourneen sentence - `it may be for years and it may be for ever'. <i>ibid.</i> 97 <i>Kathleen Mavourneen</i> indeterminate sentence <i>(1980,While We Have Prisons,9</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>PARTRiDGE</b> (or, by corruption, Mavoureen). A confirmed criminal, habitual malefactor: Australian: since ca. 1920. Baker, 1942. For origin, see Kath. <i>(1949,Dictionary of the Underworld,378</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i> a standard (witty) term for anything which will extend into the undefined or distant future; delay; a laggard.

>  <b>PARTRiDGE</b> <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang</i>) [NOTES: D.S. p? 2. `the duration': 1915-1918, NZ Military]

>  A Kathleen Mavourneen loan is one that may be for years and it may be for ever. <i>(Aug 12, 1905,NZ Truth,9-1-3</i>)

>  <b>SCANLAN</b> i hate these Kathleen Mavourneen [??] engagements ... it may be for years and [??] it may be forever. <i>(1934,Winds of Heaven,298</i>)

>  <b>LEE</b> `i'll pay you soon. `Kathleen Mavourneen', the publican started to sing, `it may be for years or it maybe for ever.' <i>(1944,Shining with the Shiner,31</i>)

>  [in answer to a question on when the Government intended to proceed with new works] Ho. W. Sullivan said `There will be no Kathleen Mavourneen as far as we are concerned' in an election address on 22 November 1949. <i>(May 1952,Standard, The,31</i>) [NOTES: 1949 Nov 22 W. Sullivan at Taumaranui in 1952 May? The Standard 31]

>  <b>LOCKE</b> Not that she cared about appearances. She earned extra money when she could and in her third year had a `Kathleen Mavourneen' loan arranged through the registrar. `Kathleen Mavourneen'? The tag had currency [ca 1933 at Auckland University College] from an irish song popular at community sings, with the punch line `it may be for years and it may be for ever.' Such a loan was interest-free, but Kath wasn't the sort to leave it for ever. She paid it back three years after graduating. it must have come from a private source - another of Rocke O'Shea's little dodges for keeping students keeping on. <i>(1981,Student At The Gates,53</i>)

### Kathleen Mavourneen Stakes
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  We now and again,. . .,hear a great deal about the reforming influences of present-day gaol systems, which fondly aim at effecting complete reformation in the evil doer by keeping him gaoled for life as an habitual criminal. incidentally, a candidate for the `Kathleen Mavourneen Stakes,' in the person of one Charles William Nicholson appeared ... in Wellington's Magistrate's Court on Saturday and Monday last. <i>(May 21, 1910,NZ Truth,256/8/4</i>)

>  [Headline] Kathleen Mavourneen Stakes [sub] Sheehan a Sure Starter ... Sheehan did not exhibit any intention of worrying, and when sentenced to twelve months and labelled as an habitual criminal, which at his age (63) means life, he passed down the trap feeling apparently relieved. <i>(Jun 4, 1910,NZ Truth,258/7/5</i>)

>  Accused is not an infant in crime; he has been convicted 19 times during the 24 years he has been on the planet, and he sailed so close to the Kathleen Mavourneen Stakes that his Honor had to consider whether he would be justified in sending him up for `it may be for ever.' <i>(Jun 11, 1910,NZ Truth,259/7/5</i>)

### katipo
 
 <i>n.</i> a poisonous spider found in some coastal regions. [ORIGIN: Maori, 1870 [see Morris]; also often as katipo spider]

>  <b>iHiMAERA & LONG</b> i am the black hole, the den where katipo are busy spinning deadhavens <i>(1982,into the World of Light,263</i>)

>  katipo venom is potentially lethal to humans, earning the diminuative creatures its Maori name, which means "night stinger". <i>(May 2005,NZ Geographic,73:12</i>)

>  <b>REiSCHEK</b> Under the grass of these east coast dunes i found the little poisonous black spider known as the katipo. <i>(1930,Yesterdays in Maoriland,110,1933 ed.</i>)

>  <b>ANDERSEN</b> <i>katipo,</i> a spider (Latodectrus katipo), the only poisonous insect in New Zealand, though some think the name another. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/143</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <i>katipo:</i> venomous spider. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,174</i>)

>  The Maori name of a small poisonous spider of Australia and New Zealand. in New Zealand it lives on sea-beaches; in Australia more widely. <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),229</i>)

>  <i>katipo n</i> -s [Maori] : a small venomous spider (<i>Latrodectus hasselti</i> or<i> L. scelio</i>) of eastern Asia, Australia, and New Zealand related to the American black widow and commonly black with a red stripe on the abdomen <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third international,1234</i>)

>  <i>katipo n.</i> Australian and New Zealand venomous red-backed spider [Maori]. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),618</i>)

>  <b>McLiNTOCK</b> Katipo .. .. katipo .. .. <i>Latrodectus katipo </i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-712</i>)

>  <i>katipo ... n.</i> Australian and N.Z. venomous red-backed spider [Maori]. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),11</i>)

>  <i>katipo n. </i>small red-backed poisonous NZ spider. [M] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,409</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 270]]

>  <b>BAXTER</b> NARAH its still not safe. if there's a katipo under a stone, its better not to lift it. <i>(1982,Collected Plays,39</i>) [NOTES: Wide Open Cage, 1968]

>  No, most likely it's come round to frame the pistol that quivered in the crack of the doorway, zooming in on the katipo slowly creeping the length of its barrel. <i>(Jan 29, 1994,Listener (New Zealand Listener),50</i>)

>  <b>REES</b> Of arachnidae, two, called by the natives katipo, are poisonous; the bite of one (a small black spider, with a red spot on the back,) being very particularly dreaded. <i>(1851,GB Parliamentary Papers (irish Univ. series),1779/28</i>)

>  <b>LORETZ</b> The redbacks are every bit as poisonous as katipos and much more plentiful. <i>(1976,Moments of Life,42</i>)

>  A bite by either a katipo, red-back or black widow would not require a record-shattering attempt in the direction of your local hospital. <i>(Dec 4, 1989,Listener (New Zealand Listener),19</i>)

>  The female katipo is New Zealand's only venomous native animal... "Katipo were here when New Zealand first drifted away from Gondwana." <i>(Jul 2, 2002,Dominion,7</i>)

>  <b>HALL</b> "Watch out for the katipos". <i>(1977,Glide Time,15</i>) [NOTES: 1984 edition]

>  Early Maori who named the spider 'night stinger' were loath to sleep in dunes at night and even now, tales of the dreaded katipo arouse anxiety amongst driftwood collectors or those contemplating a frolic in the dunes... There are two recognised species of katipo; <i>Latrodectus katipo</i> and <i>L. atritus</i>. Both are peculiar to New Zealand and can be distinguished by the coloration [sic] of adult females. Adult female <i>L. katipo</i> are primarily velvet black with the exception of a bright red dorsal stripe and a distinctive red, hourglass-shaped marking on the underside of the abdomen... in contrast, adult female <i>L. atritus</i> although almost identical in colouration and size, lack the dorsal stripe, and the red hourglass on the underside of their abdomen is less distinct. <i>(Nov 2002,Forest & Bird,305/23</i>)

>  <b>Gee</b> She taught us to shake our shoes before putting them on in case a katipo spider had crawled into the toe. <i>(2005,Blindsight,48</i>)

>  it might be good news for people who sunbathe in the sandhills but scientists say the drop-off in katipo spiders could spell disaster for one of New Zealand's native icons... "The katipo is endemic to New Zealand. it is only found here. it is an icon like the kiwi and the tuatara." <i>(Aug 6, 2000,Sunday Star Times,A7</i>)

>  Mrs. Bettis.. was bitten by a poisonous spider, the Katipo, last week, and suffered excruciating agony in consequence. <i>(Oct 11, 1879,Auckland Weekly News,8</i>)

>  Mrs. Bettis.. was bitten by a poisonous spider, the Katipo, last week, and suffered excruciating agony in consequence. <i>(Oct 11, 1879,Auckland Weekly News,8</i>)

>  Several low, sandy flats were passed.. where that venomous spider, the katipo, lurks. <i>(Nov 5, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,7</i>)

>  <b>RENeE</b> People who enjoy the challenge of sleeping on rocks, who positively welcome visits from weta and katipo spiders, who set their watch alarm so they won't miss a note of the dawn chorus - they're the ones who jump up and down with ecstasy when they spy an old kerosene lantern in a secondhand shop. <i>(1998,Yin & Tonic,108</i>)

>  We procured Specimens of the Katipo a Spider held in great by the Mauries. <i>(Apr 10, 1841,Journal of Ensign Best,286,1966 ed.</i>)

>  <b>DiEFFENBACH</b> Katipo - a black spider on the seashore, regarded as poisonous <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/366</i>)

>  <b>ANGAS</b> The <i>katipo,</i> a small, black, and very venomous spider, is found upon the beach on the west coast; ... <i>(1847,Savage Life,2/68</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFiELD</b> ... there is always found [expecially among driftwood on the sea-shore] a<i> Spider</i>, which the natives call <i>katipo</i>, and report to [171] possess the venomous qualities of the <i>tarantula;</i> but of this no positive evidence has yet been obtained. <i>(1848,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),170</i>)

>  <i>On the KATiPO a poisonous Spider of New Zealand.</i> By F.W. Wright. <i>(1870,TrNZi,2/81</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> ... nor is there any venemous insect but the <i>Katipo ... </i> <i>(1878,Forty Years in NZ,488</i>)

>  <b>SKEY</b> On pipis he lives - on cockles and eels,/ And lobsters so fine and so red;/ ... / And kakapos stewed for desert; [p.ix ERRATA] ... 10 for <i>kakapos</i> read <i>katipos </i> <i>(1889,Pirate Chief and the Mummy's Complaint..., The,17</i>) [NOTES: (`The Hermit')]

>  <b>MORRiS</b> <i>Katipo, n.</i> a small venomous spider of New Zealand and Australia. The name is Maori. The scientific name is<i> Latrodectus scelio, </i>Thorel. in New Zealand, it is generally found on the beach under old driftwood; but in Australia it is found widely scattered over the Continent, and always frequents dark sheltered spots. The derivation may be from <i>Kakati</i>, verb, to sting, and <i>po, </i>night. Compare <i>Kakapo</i>. it is a dark-coloured spider, with a bright red or yellowish stripe. <i>(1898,Austral English,242</i>)

>  <b>HOCHSTETTER</b> <i>(1867,New Zealand (Hochstetter),440</i>) [NOTES: also Buller 1870]

>  <b>WHiTE</b> We have one small venomous Spider, the `Katipo,' but as it is not in the least aggressive, and is branded on the back with a red stripe, it is easily avoided, ... <i>(1905,My NZ Garden,75</i>)

>  <b>LYSNAR</b> There are hundreds of kinds of spiders ... but the katipo is the only one whose bite is very poisonous and dangerous. <i>(1924,New Zealand (various authors/titles),104</i>)

>  <i> ~ n</i>. a small venomous spider. black in colour with a red stripe. [Maori] <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,6</i>)

### katipo spider
 
 <i>n.</i> [attrib.]

>  Katipo spiders live on or near beaches, in grasses and under driftwood. <i>(Dec 4, 1989,Listener (New Zealand Listener),18</i>)

>  No one has died from a katipo bite for more than a century... Life is hard for a katipo spider. <i>(Jul 2, 2002,Dominion,7</i>)

>  <b>ANGAS</b> `Katipo' spider <i>(1850,Savage Life,269</i>)

>  The common locust and grasshopper were seen, and the poisonous katipo spider is also to be found, <i>(1884,TrNZi,17/419</i>) [NOTES: (Gold-Smith)]

>  <b>HYDE</b> This was about the time when she was bitten by a katipo spider. The katipo, a tiny black spider with a red spot on his back, is almost the only poisonous thing in the country, though the boys called the rose and indigo blobs of jelly washed in with the tides at Lyall Bay [Wellington] `stingarees', and said that once a little girl was stung to death by them. <i>(1938,Godwits Fly, The,4,1970 ed.</i>)

>  When a composite company of Mounted Rifles was in camp near Wanganui recently, sections of the men had a competition in collecting katipo spiders. <i>(May 27, 1938,Evening Post,8</i>)

### katiu
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [kahu](k-kea.md#kahu))

### kato fat
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  in the house, big fire of dead tete-a-weka wood going. Two witch's pots (small edition of a whaler's try-pot) and a camp oven swinging above the fire. Titis sizzling in their own kato fat (fat from insides). Piro fat, the fine fat from the intestines, is more precious. <i>(Apr 20, 1952,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1952 Apr 20 in 1982 Sansom in the Grip of an island 154]

### katoa
 
 <i>n.</i> kahikatoa, manuka.

>  <b>M'DONNELL</b> A great variety of hard-wood grows at New Zealand, admirably adapted for the timbering of any sized ships; among them is the boride[e-acute], rattar[as-acute], taraide[ase-acute], mai[a-acute], totara[a1-acute], koi katoa[a2-acute], toa[a-acute] toa[a-acute], tani raha[as-acute], to wai[a-acute], reiva[a-acute] reiva[a-acute], tana[as-acute], and many others. <i>(1834,Journal,19,1979 ed.</i>)

>  <b>TAYL;OR</b> <i>(1848,Leaf from Natural History</i>)

>  FOREST BiVOUAC. ... Strengthened by the usual New Zealand food of roasted potatoes and `damper', we set off ... but soon found ourselves bewildered ... in the surveyor's lines which crossed one another in all directions in the wood. We then resorted to the usual expedient of torches made of `karehao' (supple Jack) or `katoa'; ... it was [agreed] ... that we should light a fire and bivouac for the night. <i>(1851,New Zealand (various authors/titles),Part V (Church in the Colonies XX) 76</i>) [NOTES: 1848 Aug 30 Selwyn in 1851 NZ Part V (Church in the Colonies XX) 76]



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The lowest banks ... generally covered with gadoa trees. <i>(1841,NZ Journal,2/29/51</i>) [NOTES: (Dieffenbach)]

>  <b>DiEFFENBACH</b> (Andersen) ... the kahikatoa, also called gadoa, or manuko, a philadelphus with a very hard and brown wood. <i>(Jul 29, 1844,Report of Select Committee on NZ,611</i>) [NOTES: 1844 Dieffenbach Report in House of Commons Report from a Select Committee on NZ 611]

### katoitoi
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [also katuhituhi](k-kea.md#katuhituhi))

>  <b>DiEFFENBACH</b> 25. <i>Miro longpipes. Less.</i> ... [191] Gha toitoi of the natives of Dusky Bay. <i>Forst.</i> Miro miro of the natives. <i>Garnot.</i> [possibly <i>Miro australis</i>, the robin (= katoitoi), rather than <i>miromiro</i>, Ni Tit <i>Petroica</i>] <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/190</i>) [NOTES: 1842 Gray's Fauna in 1843 Dieffenbach Travels in NZ 2/190-191]

### katote
 
 <i>n.</i> a fern

>  Smaller and finer, C. smithii, or Katote, is the only Cyathea that retains a skirt of old complete fronds below the crown. <i>(Nov 30, 2002,Dominion Post,F15</i>)

>  We are crashing about in the katote ferns somewhere inside the eastern boundary of 300,000ha of contiguous Urewera forest. <i>(Oct 4, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),28</i>)

>  The best way to distinguish katote, the soft tree fern, is tactile; the horizontal fronds are very soft to the touch. <i>(Jan 3, 2004,Dominion Post,C2</i>)

>  The most common species are the poka (<i>Dicksonia squarrosa</i>) and the katote (<i>Hemitelia smithii</i>); the stem and fronds of the latter are sometimes used as food for cows. The mamaku (<i>Cyathea medullaris</i>) is local, and has not been observed south of Halfmoon Bay. <i>(1884,TrNZi,17/217</i>) [NOTES: (Kirk)]

>  The kaponga or silver fern is almost as lovely. A peculiarity of another kind, the katote, is that its fronds ... all incline sharply upwards ... Different again is the more common fern-tree, the tuakura (dicksonia squarrosa) ... <i>(Mar 10, 1920,Quick March,38</i>)

### katuhituhi
 
 <i>n.</i> South island robin, bush robin (See [also katoitoi](k-kea.md#katoitoi))

>  <b>BRUNNER</b> ... i have taken [wekas] with my hands only by shaking a <i>katuhituhi</i>, or robin, before them. They ... will fly at a bunch of feathers, or a red rag. <i>(Mar 14, 1848,Journal of an Expedition</i>) [NOTES: 1848 Mar 14 Brunner Expedition Middle isld in 1959 Taylor Early Travs. 308]

### katydid
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Melampsalta</i>

>  <b>McLiNTOCK</b> Katydid .. .. kihikihi kai, etc. .. .. <i>Melampsalta </i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-712</i>)

>  <b>LOCKSLEY</b> Rather more pleasing to look at is the common green grassshopper ...: The katydid, which is diurnal ... The Maori, who used to eat the plump ground-burrowing larvae, call it `kikiki-pounamu', the `soft-voiced green one'. <i>(1980,House Above the Sea,141</i>)

### kauaua
 
 <i>n.</i> a sparrtow-hawk

>  <b>YATE</b> <i>Kauaua</i> - A sparrow-hawk, nothing differing from the sparrow-hawks of England. <i>(1835,New Zealand (various authors/titles),60</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> The<i> kauaua</i> [a1,a2-macrons] is similar to our sparrow-hawks. <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/300</i>)

>  <b>DiEFFENBACH</b> <i>Falco brunnea.</i> Gould. ... Kauaua of the natives. <i>Yate, Polack, Dieffenbach. </i> Kari-area of the natives of Queen Charlotte's Sound. <iK>t. </i> <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/186</i>) [NOTES: 1842 Gray's Fauna in 1843 Dieffenbach Travels in NZ 2/186]

>  <b>DiEFFENBACH</b> <i>Falco brunnea.</i> Gould. ... Kauaua of the natives. <i>Yate, Polack, Dieffenbach. </i> Kari-area of the natives of Queen Charlotte's Sound. <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/186</i>) [NOTES: 1842 Gray's Fauna in 1843 Dieffenbach Travels in NZ 2/186]

### kauere
 
 <i>n.</i> [ORIGIN: Maori, Spg kaneree is prob. (if not a misprint) merely a misreading of n for Colenso's u.]

>  Vitex <i>littoralis</i>, Cunn. ... Nat. names, `Puriri' and Kaneree', south of the Thames, <i>Colenso. </i> <i>(1853,Flora NZ,i Flowering Plants 203</i>)

>  Kauere. <i>Vitex littoralis.</i> KAWAKA ...Kawaka, <i>Col[enso]. Libocedrus Doniana.] </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),765</i>)

>  <i>V[itex] lucens, T.Kirk. ... Puriri; Kauere.</i> ... A well-known tree, producing the most valuable hardwood in the colony, ... <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,565</i>)

### kauhoa
 
 <i>n.</i> litter

>  <b>MATTHEWS</b> At the landing Martha was disappointed at not meeting a party with horses and a <i>kauhoa </i>(litter) for her father. `However, the natives we had with us soon formed two kowshores,' she remarked, .. <i>(1940,Matthews of Kaitaia,98</i>) [NOTES: 1837 Martha Marsden in Eric Ramsden Marsden and the Missions in 1940 Matthews Matthews of Kaitaia 98]

>  <b>KEENE</b> <i>kauhoa</i>: litter <i>(1963,O Te Raki,191</i>)

### kaumatua
 
 <i>n.</i> a Maori elder; an elderly Maori (or occasionally Pakeha) [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  <b>iHiMAERA & LONG</b> The kaumatua shuffles, bone-fingered hand grasping Joe's forearm. <i>(1982,into the World of Light,264</i>)

>  He says the group wants to transform the hall into a wharekai (eating house) and build a wharenui (meeting house) and kaumatua units. <i>(May 27, 2004,Wellington Contact,4</i>)

>  <b>KEENE</b> <i>kaumatua</i> : leader of fishing party; adult or elder of a village. <i>(1963,O Te Raki,191</i>)

>  <b>MASON</b> HAMiORA, <i>sternly</i>: irapeta: the decision of the kaumatua is not to be questioned. <i>(1969,Awatea,64,1978 ed.</i>)

>  A kaumatua (elder), Mr Ralph Love, who made submissions on behalf of the council ... to the local authorities for land for a Maori cemetery, ... <i>(Feb 20, 1981,Evening Post,2/5</i>)

>  <b>HULME</b> 324 Kaumatua = an elder/elders <i>(1983,Bone People, The,468</i>)

>  `it has been four years since you first set foot on this marae. ... Ngati Tupoho kaumatua (elder) Hori Hipango told Prince Edward in Wanganui making a short visit to renew old friendships. <i>(Jul 16, 1986,Dominion,13</i>)

>  <i>kaumatua n.</i> a Maori elder or head of the family. [M] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,409</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  <b>HOWE</b> Kaumatua - `elders' - as such, retainers of wisdom and the passers on of knowledge, therefore deserving of respect and care. <i>(1985,Towards Taha Maori,20</i>)

>  The discouraging thing, as John Scott, a local kaumatua puts it, is not that things have changed in Wairoa. <i>(Jun 13, 1987,Listener (New Zealand Listener),17</i>)

>  Whichever kaumatua Harrison consulted before engaging the team, Reac's moves have displeased Dick Whitau ... <i>(Apr 9, 1988,Listener (New Zealand Listener),18</i>)

>  <b>MACRAE</b> The kaumatua, the old man, hit his daughter because she had embezzled money <i>he</i> wanted to embezzle. <i>(1988,Awful Childhoods,142</i>)

>  Another kaumatua, Terry Putaka, said he had seen marae-based justice operate in Taranaki in the late 1950s. <i>(Jul 20, 1989,Dominion,3</i>)

>  The kaumatua, or senior chief, headed the procession. <i>(1900,Auckland Weekly News,Xmas No. : 12</i>)

>  [His] sentences are fluent and perfectly shaped, as befits a kaumatua, albeit one of Cornish origin. <i>(Nov 11, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),59</i>)

>  He's going to be a good kaumatua (wise man) when he gets older; he's already got that limp - give him a walking stick and he'll be away. <i>(Oct 1, 1999,Evening Post,24</i>)

>  You can never be quite sure where you are with Hone Tuwhare. He's an off-the-wall kaumatua, the wise old man with a gleam in his eye and a slingshot in his pocket. <i>(Apr 27, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),20</i>)

>  But then in his speech, i realised he was pressurising the kaumatua to allow Helen Clark to speak. <i>(Jun 6, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),35</i>)

>  We learn later that it's a one-bedroom kaumatua flat. <i>(Aug 6, 1994,Listener (New Zealand Listener),26</i>)

>  Why were these kaumatua always right? These old men who sat hunched on the paepae in rumpled grey suits looking like someone had just shaken them out of mothballs for the occasion. <i>(2005,Huia 6: Contemp. Maori Fiction,18</i>)

>  Why were these kaumatua always right? These old men who sat hunched on the paepae in rumpled grey suits looking like someone had just shaken them out of mothballs for the occasion. <i>(2005,Huia 6: Contemp. Maori Fiction,18</i>)

>  The lampooning of a dodgy rent-a-kaumatua and a Pakeha romance novelist by the whanau is particularly hilarious. <i>(Jul 7, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),61</i>)

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> Kau m|atua; A grown-up person. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,161</i>)

>  <b>WiLLiAMS</b> We assembled ... for English service, where we mustered twelve <i>kaumatuas</i> besides children. <i>(Feb 22, 1835,Early Journals of Henry Williams, The,414,1961 ed.</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> He said they were like a widow feeble having lost their kaumatua, (his father) ... <i>(Jun 4, 1846,Journal,iV/34</i>)

>  Komene & others ... recommended them to turn back as they would not see the kaumatuas if they went on ... <i>(Mar 7, 1863,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,2/25,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (A.S. Atkinson)]

>  ... many a deliberation of the <i>Kaumatuas</i> or elders took place over the prepared model, ere the shape was finally settled. <i>(1879,TrNZi,11/72</i>)

>  Most of the <i>kaumatuas</i> (or elders) have passed away. <i>(1911,TrNZi,43/598</i>)

>  When a party goes out eel-fishing at night, the old man of the band, the kaumatua or tohunga, divests himself of his clothes and goes first to the river-bank. <i>(May 10, 1920,Quick March,38</i>)

>  The trouble is that there are too many babus of the Rangi Marumaru, Henare Pumipi type & no kaumatuas to steady them. <i>ibid.</i> 1930 Sept 23 p. 2/60 (Buck) i made a speech of welcome to George, occupying the dual position of a Maori Kaumatua and a Pakeha interpreter. <i>(Jan 5, 1929,Na To Hoa Aroha,1/168,1986 ed.</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> .. - the old man of the party, the <i>kaumatua</i> or <i>tohunga,</i> divests himself of his clothes and goes first to the river-bank. <i>(1930,Maori, The,18</i>)

>  He was as much at home with the kaumatua as with the younger generation. <i>(Oct 2, 1933,Na To Hoa Aroha,3/111</i>) [NOTES: (Ngata)]

>  <b>LAMBERT</b> ... nightly the <i>kaumatuas</i>, or learned men of the tribe, make the flesh of little children creep with tales of dread <i>taniwhas ... </i> <i>(1936,Pioneering Reminiscences of Old Wairoa,58</i>)

>  <b>ZiMMERMAN</b> Kingi Tahiwi, the <i>kaumatua</i>, the old leader, was one of the wisest and most adroit handlers of young people that i have ever seen, ... <i>(1946,Where the People Sing,165</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> A number of the kaumatua spoke, but the drift of their remarks showed that they aquiesced in Tane's proposal. <i>(1948,Taina,18</i>)

>  <b>HiLLiARD</b> The kaumatuas sit on apple-boxes, in their dungarees, braces over their shirts, boots covered with dust. <i>(1960,Maori Girl,36</i>)

>  The old men, the wiseacres, the kaumatuas who had been through it all half a century ago ... moved away. <i>(1977,Listener Short Stories,58</i>) [NOTES: 1961 Hilliard in 1977 N.Z. Listener Short Stories 58]

>  <b>HiLLiARD</b> There's the old <i>kaumatuas</i> talking away to me in Maori and i don't know half of what they're saying. <i>ibid.</i> 192 SOME MAORi WORDS ... <i>kaumatua</i> respected old man <i>(1963,Piece of Land, A,12</i>)

### kaumatua flat
 
 <i>n.</i> a Maori granny flat [[Ma. Williams 106 <b>Kaumatua</b> 1. n. Adult. 2. Old man or woman.]]

>  Turning left, you pass the entrance to the whare hui (meeting house) then, tucked in behind the punga fence enclosing the marae, six kaumatua flats with neat flower gardens out front. <i>(Dec 2004,North & South,63</i>)

>  But i was watching an old woman from the kaumatua flats crossing the road the other day and it was nerve-racking. <i>(Jun 28, 2006,Whakatane Beacon,4</i>)

 <i>n.</i> [[Ma. Williams 106 <b>Kaumatua</b> 1. n. Adult. 2. Old man or woman.]]

### kaupapa
 
 <i>n.</i> a canoe [proper name.]

>  <b>ANGAS</b> Our canoe, which was hewn out of a solid tree, was of the kind used for river conveyance, called <i>kaupapa</i>; it is quite simple without ... the ornamental carving ... that adorn the war canoe and the gaily decorated <i>waka</i> of the harbours on the coast. <i>(1847,Savage Life,2/18</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> strategy, theme, platform [ORIGIN: Maori, [Ma. <b>kaupapa</b> strategy, theme, level floor, fleet of ships] cf. P.M. Ryan Dict. Modern Maori (1995) 89.]

>  The kaupapa (guiding principle) of Port Nicholson claimants: 'Together we arrived. Together we survived. Together we live. Together we go forward. Together we will succeed.' <i>(May 20, 2003,Dominion Post,B5</i>)

>  it's not part of our kaupapa to do that. <i>(May 14, 2002,Radio NZ / National Radio</i>) [NOTES: Rangiora Waste Recycling Manager talking to Geoff Robinson (Morning to Noon)]

>  But who were they, and what exactly was their shtick, their mission statement, their kaupapa? <i>(Apr 8, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),30</i>)

>  There are far better ways to deal with this kaupapa. <i>(Oct 20, 2006,Whakatane Beacon,2</i>)

>  ...one of his functions was "to ensure that the kaupapa of the film continues to come from a Maori direction and a Maori perspective." <i>(Feb 1, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),23</i>)

>  <b>WANANGA .. kaupapa -</b> Whaikorero and Karanga... <i>(Aug 19, 2005,Whakatane Beacon,41</i>)

>  it puts us in a very good position, particularly because part of our kaupapa was to get theatre out to people. <i>(Aug 29, 1998,Listener (New Zealand Listener),38</i>)

>  "The kaupapa of Gecko," says Sanderson.. "is to bring professional theatre to towns that don't normally see it." <i>(Aug 11, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),51</i>)

>  Radio isn't just about what you hear, it's about what you do in the community, a larger kaupapa. <i>(Aug 29, 1992,Listener (New Zealand Listener),44</i>)

>  Any book, any book, needs a firm kaupapa. For this reason, its reason for being is set out in the first chapter. <i>(May 6, 1995,Listener (New Zealand Listener),51</i>)

>  i believe that the kaupapa of this bill is totally correct. <i>(1994,NZ Parliamentary Debates,533/13663</i>)

>  The kaupapa of the party is entirely consistant with the values of our pacific relations. <i>(Jun 9, 2006,NZ Herald,A12</i>)

 <i>adj.</i> based on Maori principles [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  The project is a 10-bed kaupapa Maori rehabilitation unit aimed at helping people who have a mental illness and who have committed a crime. <i>(Sep 24, 2005,Listener (New Zealand Listener),32</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

### kauri
 
 <i>n.</i> in early usage applied (?erron.) to other timber trees

>  <b>McDONNELL</b> Seals are numerous in the season, and the [Stewart] island abounds in flax and splendid resorts of `Cawdie'. [ed fn] in common with other early commentators on the South, McDonnell uses `kawdie' (kauri) loosely for any large forest tree, it seems. <i>(Mar 10, 1834,Extracts From a Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1834 Mar 10 Thos.McDonnell Observations on New Zealand in 1940 Howard Rakiura 366 White pine on Stewart island [McD has used courie, cawdi etc for kauri at Hokianga pp.18-19 and 34.], ?white pine, kahikatea.]

>  <b>WALTON</b> One species, called the Koudi pine, has been found very serviceable, from its strength and straightness, as spars for ships.... Another species [apart from the Koudi pine], called the cowdie by the natives, the <i>dammara excelsa </i>of botanists, is no less remarkable for its strength and useful properties. <i>(1839,Twelve Months' Residence in NZ,15</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> = kauri gum [in occas. early usage]

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> Kaudi, s. A species of fir-tree; resin; <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,161</i>)

>  i arrived here [at Kaitaia] on the <i>Fly</i> ... i now send her back with about 3/4 tons of Kowrie and four bales wool. ... all the Kowrie traders about here are mostly out of goods ... i think the Kowrie sent is very good and clean ... The natives are going now to dig for me on some land you formerly purchased. <i>(Dec 20, 1845,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1845 Dec 20 Clarke to Mair in 1972 Reed Gumdiggers 105]



 
 <i>n.</i> the kauri = the backblocks, the boohai

>  <b>BELL</b> Now he lived in the kauri, and a place called Hare-Kare/ And he owned a big red Maori horse, used to call him Harry. <i>(1974,Ballads of a Racegoer,9</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (attrib. uses) [kauri appears in some obvious loose combinations: kauri-bush, -forest, -spar, -timber, -trade, -tree, -wood; and in special combinations: -bug, -fern, -grass, -gum, -land.]

>  <b>MARSDEN</b> On the <i>Dromedary's</i> return from Shokee Hangha to the Bay of islands, Captain Skinner purposed to take in some spars of the kikatea (<i>kahikatea</i>)(dagger sign) and afterwards to examine the River Thames or some other part of New Zealand for timber, as he was anxious if possible to get kowree (<i>kauri</i>) spars, as that wood was deemed of a much superior quality to the kikatea. <i>(1820,Letters & Journals,87,1932 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MARKHAM</b> He used Gum tree from Sydney for Keels, Coudy planks, Hoc-car [aka] Timber and Kicaitore Knees, sometimes Pouriedie Keels. <i>(1834,New Zealand (various authors/titles),34,1963 ed.</i>)

>  <b>YATE</b> Kauri (<i>Dammaris Australis</i>, or <i>Pinus Kauri</i>). This tree is of the genus Pine; ... it will scar[c]ely be believed by an English timber-merchant, that i have measured a Kauri-tree whose circumference was forty feet ... the gum oozing out of it, when the bark was wounded, ... <i>(1835,New Zealand (various authors/titles),36</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> ... the almost impervious forest of Pamaki [a's-acute], which was densely studded with splendid kouri-trees ... <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/109</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> ... poor sterile pipe clay land ... with great abundance of the cowry resin evidently proving these plains [Wangaroa] were once cowry forests ... wherever the same soil prevails the cowry gum is seen, ... <i>(Nov 5, 1839,Journal,2/162</i>)

>  <b>BiDWiLL</b> it will be seen, from what i have said, that people have been greatly deceived in England with regard to the supply of cowry spars, &c., to be derived from New Zealand : ... <i>(1841,Rambles in NZ,28,1952 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HEAPHY</b> The Kauri trade has been for a long time a source of great profit, ... <i>(1842,New Zealand (Heaphy),41</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> (And) [KAUDi-TREE] <i>Agathis australis </i> <i>(1864,Handbook NZ Flora</i>)

>  <b>BRETT</b> 1798 .... probably mark [s] the commencement of the export <i>Kauri</i> trade. <i>(1890,Early History of NZ,115</i>)

>  404 Kauri feather veneer. <i>(1890,PWD Catalogue of Timbers,24</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [timber ref as opp to tree]

>  Half the "party" walls in the flimsy buildings that do duty as shops or dwellings are no more fire resistant than painted kauri. <i>(Jul 12, 1913,NZ Observer,3</i>)

>  <b>McCAULEY</b> 'Most likely kauri,' he was saying. 'Absolutely solid.' <i>(1990,Bad Music,100</i>)

>  <b>J.C. PATTESON</b> .. the kauri is a darkish grained wood. <i>(Jul 12, 1855,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1855 July 12 J.C. PATTESON letter in 1874 Yonge Life of John Coleridge Patteson 2/216 .]

>  Kauri .. Agathis Australis .. The best known timber in New Zealand; colour yellowish-white to brown; timber straight in grain, ... <i>(1890,PWD Catalogue of Timbers,10</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a massive cone-bearing timber tree of the northern North island, prized for its straight grained timber. b) a similar tree of the Pacific islands [Maori]; also once called New Zealand pine. [kauri(-)pine was often used by early writers: occas. kauri fir, and occas. New Zealand Yellow Pine, Mountain Pine) Kauri is now, and has been for long, the common usage; king pine, New Zealand pine.]

>  <b>MACKAY</b> Only a few of these huge kauris have survived. <i>(1991,Working the Kauri,2</i>)

>  <b>NiCHOLAS</b> Of those trees which were generally to be met with, the pine was by far the most beautiful, and in height exceeded all the others beyond any degree of comparison. We observed two species of it, but the mountain-pine, called by the natives <i>cowrie</i>, which we found growing upon the sides and summits of the hills, and rising to an enormous size, was the most remarkable. <i>ibid.</i> 1/233. Mountain pine, called by natives cowrie <i>ibid.</i> 1/304-305 ... we looked about in search of resin, which is brought down by the streams from various parts of the interior. ... [305] ... The pine, called by the natives <i>cowree</i> abounds with resin; and i believe it is the only tree of that species in the island that has any. <i>ibid.</i> 2/244 Pines of different descriptions, and which are utterly unknown to Europeans, are here to be met with ...; and those species which bear the uncouth names of the cowrie, the totarra, the towha, and the zarida, afford such a supply of valuable timber as the profusion of some ages to come will not be able to exhaust. <i>(1817,New Zealand (various authors/titles),1/233</i>)

>  <b>CRUiSE</b> <i>(1823,Journal of Ten Months' Residence in NZ,145</i>) [NOTES: also 1852 Mundy, 1860 Bennett, 1867 Hochstetter, 1874 Ed Crewe, 1875, 1883, 1886, 1889, 1890]]

>  <b>BAUGHAN</b> <i>Kauri</i> and <i>Totara, Rimu,</i> and <i>Matai</i> and <i>Maire</i>,/ Red-as-blood <i>Rata</i>, and bright-as-blood <i>Pohutukawa,/ Manuka</i> dark-ey'd, Convolvulus star-ey'd - / The glittering of you that morning! [fn2 attached to the italicised names] 2. <i>Kauri</i>, etc. (<i>Kow-ree</i>, To[acute]-ta-ra, Ree[e acute]-moo, <i>Mah[a acute]-ti</i>, Mi-reh, Rah[a acute]-tah, Po-hoo-too-kah[a acute]-wah, <i>Mah[a acute]-noo-kah</i>): New Zealand Bush trees. <i>(1908,Shingle-Short,54</i>)

>  The chief indigenous trees of commercial value are: Kauri (<i>Agathis Australis</i>), Kahikatea or white pine (<i>Podocarpus dacrydioides</i>) ... <i>(1936,Agricultural Organization in NZ,578</i>)

>  <i>kauri</i>, a tree (Dammara australis), a noble tree, the first to attract shipping in Newzealand, the navy seeking it for masts and spars; gum widely but wrongly known as cowdie gum. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/144</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <i>kauri</i>: large pine tree. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,174</i>)

>  <b>HUNT</b> it (New Zealand) has the largest tree in the world in the Kauri ... and the smallest pine in the world, the Pygmy Pine (<i>Dacrydium laxifolium</i>), of sub-alpine areas, the height of which is normally about twelve inches ... <i>(1951,Confessions,136</i>)

>  ~ A large timber-tree, a pine, of northern New Zealand, one of the largest trees in the world; other kinds of kauri, or <i>Agathis</i>, grow in Australia and on some Pacific islands. <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),289</i>)

>  <b>SiNCLAiR</b> The trade in kauri, a pine which rises sixty and sometimes eighty feet before the first branch, was also worth several thousands of pounds a year to Sydney firms. <i>ibid.</i> 302 [Maori glossary] <i>kauri</i> Large pine tree (<i>Agathis australis</i>) <i>(1959,History of New Zealand,39</i>)

>  <b>SHADBOLT</b> i grew to awareness rejecting as alien our scrubby backblocks farm, the thick bush of tall kauri, rimu and kahikatea ..., the mangroved tidal creek, ... the cabbage trees in the playground ... <i>(1959,New Zealanders, The,10,1986 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (The Woman's Story)]

>  <i>kauri</i> also <i>kaurie</i> or <i>kaury</i> or <i>kawrie</i> or <i>kawry or <i>cowrie</i> or <i>kaori, n</i>, pl <i>kauris</i> also <i>kauries</i> [Maori <i>kawri</i>] or <i>kauri pine</i> : a tree of the genus <i>Agathis</i>; esp : a tall timber tree (<i>A. australis</i>) of New Zealand having fine white straight-grained wood 2 : the wood of the kauri tree 3 or <i>kauri resin</i> or <i>kauri gum</i> or <i>kauri copal</i> : a light-colored to brown copal from the kauri tree found usu. as a fossil in the ground but also collected by tapping living trees and used chiefly in making varnishes and linoleum. <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third international,1234</i>)

>  <b>KEENE</b> <i>kauri</i>: valuable timber tree used for building of furniture and houses. <i>(1963,O Te Raki,191</i>)

>  <b>McLiNTOCK</b> The vocabulary of New Zealand English has been influenced by ... the adoption of Maori words and phrases. ... Examples of the native words now in general use are the names of birds, fish, plants, and trees: <i>kea, tui, hapuku, tarakihi, koromiko, raupo</i>, and <i>kauri.</i> <i>ibid.</i> 3/705-12; -->Kauri .. .. kauri .. .. <i>Agathis australis </i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,2/679</i>) [NOTES: Wall NZ Speech]

>  <i> kauri n.</i> New Zealand coniferous pine tree yielding valuable softwood; kauri gum, the resin used for making varnish. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),618</i>)

>  <i>kauri ... n</i>. N.Z. coniferous pine tree, yielding valuable soft-wood; <i>kauri gum,</i> the resin used in making varnish. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),11</i>)

>  <i>kauri n.</i> pl. -<i>ris.</i> a New Zealand coniferous tree with oval leaves and round cones, cultivated for wood and resin. [C19: < Maori] <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,614</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 409; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 270]]

>  <b>BELiCH</b> <i>kauri</i> a large conifer tree <i>(1986,NZ Wars, The,337</i>)

>  Gum climbers and bleeders scaled kauri using a weighted line and rope, and later, taking advantage of kauri's soft outer layer of bark and sapwood, hand spikes, climbing hooks, and boots equipped with toe spikes. <i>(Apr 1991,NZ Geographic,32</i>)

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> Kaudi, s. A species of fir-tree; resin; <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,161</i>)

>  <b>McCRAE</b> [We ] came to a very fine forest of Koude fit for our purpose - ... conveying it when felled to the water. <i>(1820,Journal,21,1928 ed.</i>)

>  <b>McNAB</b> Q. Are the trees of the cowdee very tall and strait? [sic] <i>(1908,Historical Records,1/535</i>) [NOTES: 1821 May Evidence of Ensign McCrae before Commissioner Bigge in 1908 McNab Historical Records 1/535]

>  <b>CRUiSE</b> The one [tree fit for masts] is called by the natives Kaikaterre, the other <i>Cowry</i> or Cowdy. ... Some of the Cowry trees ... rose one hundred feet without a single branch. <i>(1823,Journal,20,1829 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1823 (1829 Feb) Cruise Jrnl (1957) 20]

>  <b>CRAiK</b> .. there are two kinds of trees known in New Zealand [for masts] ...; the one ... is called by the natives <i>kaikaterre</i>, the other<i> cowry</i> or <i>cowdy. </i> <i>(1830,New Zealanders, The,176</i>)

>  <b>MARKHAM</b> The Coudie or Courie [<i>kauri</i>], as it is called, the New Zealand Pine, growing 80 or 90 feet without a branch .... <i>(1834,New Zealand (various authors/titles),33,1963 ed.</i>)

>  <b>WiLLiAMS</b> Went with the Sawyers up to Waikino; felled one <i>Kauri</i> and one <i>Tanakaha; ... </i> <i>(Jan 28, 1834,Early Journals of Henry Williams, The,359,1961 ed.</i>)

>  <b>McDONNELL</b> Her spars, one and all, were of New [19] Zealand pine (cawdie); they were faultless. <i>ibid.</i> 34 The <i>Courie Croeka</i> [karaka] and <i>Pou te Cowa,</i> i think, are the finest trees in the island - the first for the beauty of its trunk and top (independent of its value as a wood), the second, for the unrivalled beauty of its leaf, which is a species of laurel, and the third, for the splendour of its flower, which grows in such thick clusters and is of so rich a color, that looking at it from a short distance, one would imagine a sumptuous scarlet pall had been thrown over the top of the tree. The <i>Courie</i> does not grow further South than 38 degrees, ... <i>(1834,Extracts From a Journal,18,1979 ed.</i>)

>  There is little or no <i>couru</i> (pine) on the immediate banks of the Hokianga itself, ... [possibly a misprint or misinterpretation in original for the <i>courie</i> of p34] ... there are some splendid <i>courie</i> forests up the river, ... <i>(Aug 9, 1834,NZ independent,32,1979 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1834 Aug 9 NZ (independent) (1979) 32]

>  <b>YATE</b> Kauri (<i>Dammaris Australis</i>, or <i>Pinus Kauri</i>). This tree is of the genus Pine; ... it will scar[c]ely be believed by an English timber-merchant, that i have measured a Kauri-tree whose circumference was forty feet ... the gum oozing out of it, when the bark was wounded, ... <i>ibid.</i> 37 As a shrub ... the kauri is not very graceful <i>(1835,New Zealand (various authors/titles),36</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> There is scarcely a more splendid sight to behold than a New Zealand forest. in the one we now entered, we did not perceive any kouri, or yellow pine-trees; but the totara [a's-acute], or red pine, grew in vast abundance. ... The rito, and innumerable other palm-trees, were in great quantity. <i>ibid.</i> 2/387 The tree which has hitherto attracted most attention has been the <i>Kauri or yellow pine</i> (pinus Australis) g. coniferae; ... <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/105</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> ... the most superior [canoes] are to be found at Wairoa, (Hawkes' Bay) the tribes at that place employ the <i>totarra</i> or red pine, the <i>kouri</i> or white pine being rarely found beyond the Thames (Horake[a-e-acute]). <i>(1840,Manners & Customs of the NZers,1/227</i>)

>  <b>JAMESON</b> kaori timber <i>ibid.</i> 328 Kaori, (erroneously pronounced Cowrie by Europeans) <i>(1841,New Zealand, S. Aust. and NSW,(256,257)</i>)

>  <b>BiDWiLL</b> The Cowrie or pine of the country <i>(Dammara australis)</i> is abundant here, although it is almost the southern limit of its growth. <i>(1841,Rambles in NZ,28,1952 ed.</i>)

>  [kowri] <i>(1841,NZ Journal,2/31/81</i>) [NOTES: G. Bennet FLS]

>  <b>HOOKER</b> The Kawdi, Cowri, Kaudi, Kowri, or Cowdi Pine of New Zealand, as it is indifferently spelt or mis-spelt and pronounced, is too well-known a tree to require a detailed description for its identification; ... Dammara<i> australis</i>, Lamb. ... Nat. name, `Kauri'; `Wai Kauri' of the fresh gum, `Kapia' of that dug up. <i>(1853,Flora NZ,i Flowering Plants 231</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Kaudi. <i>Dammara australis.</i> Kauri, Col[enso]. <i>Dammara australis. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),765</i>)

>  <b>CAMPBELL</b> <i>(1881,Poenamo</i>)

>  <b>CAMPBELL</b> ... Waiheki had many a stately kouri [fn. <i>Pine-tree</i>. growing on it. <i>(1881,Poenamo,Bk2/3/73</i>)

>  <b>DOMETT</b> King-pine (Kauri) ..... O. Coniferae; G. Dammara; S. D. Australis. `The famous Kauri pine; some near two hundred feet high and forty in girth.' <i>(1883,Ranolf and Amohia,2/336</i>)

>  <b>KiRK</b> <i>(1889,Forest Flora,143</i>)

>  <b>MORRiS</b> <i>Cowdie</i> an early variant of <i>Kauri</i> (q.v.), with other spellings. <i>ibid.</i> 243 <i>Kauri,</i> or <i>Cowry</i>, or <i>Kauri-Pine, n.</i> Maori name for the tree <i>Agathis australis</i>, Sal. (formerly <i>Dammarae</i>A.), N.O. <i>Coniferae</i>. Variously spelt, and earlier often called <i>Cowdie</i>. in `Lee's New Zealand Vocabulary,' 1820, the spelling <i>Kaudi</i> appears. Although this tree is usually called by the generic name of <i>Dammara</i>(see quotation, 1832), it is properly referred to the genus <i>Agathis </i>an earlier name already given to it by Salisbury. There is a Queensland Kauri (<i>Dammara robusta</i>, F. v. M.). See <i>Pine. </i> <i>(1898,Austral English,103</i>)

>  <b>CRUiSE</b> <i>(1823,Journal of Ten Months' Residence in NZ,145</i>) [NOTES: also 1852 Mundy, 1860 Bennett, 1867 Hochstetter, 1874 Ed Crewe, 1875, 1883, 1886, 1889, 1890]]</i>

### kauri bug
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Polyzosteria novaezelandiae.</i>

>  <b>HAY</b> ... that malignant thing we call a <i>Kauri-bug ... </i>[FN]. species of <i>Blatta</i> or cockroach, ... called Polyzosteria novaezelandiae ... [152] it is oval in shape, .... up to the size of a crown-piece. it is flat, black, hard and shiny, and resembles a cross between a black-beetle and the woodlouse or slater. it stinks. That is all it does, but it is enough. <.i>ibid.</i> 2/228 i spoke of the mosquito ..., of the sand-fly ..., and of the kauri-bug <i>(Polyzosteria N.Z.). </i> <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,1/151</i>)

>  <b>BARLOW</b> The Kauri bug (called by the Maoris the [184] Kekereru), with its power of emitting a terrible ... smell when alarmed, has been ... fully dealt with ... <i>ibid.</i> The Kauri bug (Kekereru) ... has been so often and fully dealt with by writers. <i>(1888,Kaipara and Waitemata Echo,182</i>)

>  <b>BARLOW</b> The Kauri bug (called by the Maoris the [184] Kekereru), with its power of emitting a terrible ... smell when alarmed, has been ... fully dealt with ... <i>ibid.</i> The Kauri bug (Kekereru) ... has been so often and fully dealt with by writers. <i>(1888,Kaipara and Waitemata Echo,182</i>)

>  ...often incorrectly called the `black beetle,' and ... `Maori bug' ... it has been said that the term [Maori bug, q.v.] is a corruption of the original and inoffensive European name of `kauri bug' <i>(1960,NZ Junior Encyclopaedia,1/351</i>) [NOTES: (Miller)]

### kauri bush
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HAY</b> The Whangapoua kauri bush extends over some 30,000 acres. <i>ibid.</i> 2/185 The heavy bush, in these northern districts, is divisible into two kinds. There is the kauri bush and the mixed bush. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/9</i>)

>  The tufts of the toe-kiwi - the `cutting-grass' of the bushmen - which are a feature of the kauri bush, are set in a blaze; and as the flames flare up ... the masses of the puwharawhara (Asteliads) which encumber the branches are ignited ... <i>(1897,TrNZi,29/493</i>) [NOTES: (Walsh)]

### kauri forest
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HALKETT & SALE</b> The kauri forest is really a multitude of "mini-forests" or stands of trees, occurring like varicoloured tiles in a mosaic floor. <i>(1986,World of the Kauri, The,29</i>)

>  <b>MARKHAM</b> As the Coudie Forests is on the tops of the Hills all the way up and down the River. <i>(1834,New Zealand (various authors/titles),41,1963 ed.</i>)

>  <b>DiEFFENBACH</b> ... having many times seen kauri-forest burning, not fired for the purpose of clearing the land, but in order to get a dozen or two of logs; ... <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,1/228</i>)

>  <b>HECTOR</b> ... having many times seen kauri-forest burning, not fired for the purpose of clearing the land, but in order to get a dozen or two of logs; ... <i>(1883,Handbook of New Zealand,122</i>)

>  <b>WHiTE</b> They exude that valuable and wonderful product `Kauri gum,' which is found in the Kauri forests near Auckland, ... <i>(1905,My NZ Garden,95</i>)

>  <b>LAiNG & BLACKWELL</b> A kauri forest is a wonderful sight, with the clean, erect stems rising like grey columns to a height of from 80 to 100 feet - sometimes to 60 or 70 feet without throwing out a branch. <i>(1907,Plants of NZ,62,1940, P. 61 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HUNT</b> The low rolling clay hills had ... been the site of a vast kauri forest, and, being so remote, was still virgin country as far as gum diggers were concerned. ... Armed with a long spear of rod iron, we `diggers' tested these hillocks for gum. <i>(1951,Confessions,40</i>)

>  <b>MOORE & iRWiN</b> <i>PHEBALiUM NUDUM</i>, mairehau. <i>A small shrubby tree typical of dry ridges in kauri forest and not extending further south. No bush scent is sweeter than that of the dul reddish leaves. </i> <i>(1978,Oxford Book of NZ Plants,90</i>)

 <i>n.</i> [There are two species of the short-tailed bat. The greater short-tailed bat (Mystacina robusta) .. The endangered lesser short-tailed bat (M tuberculata) .. is divided into three sub-species ...]

 <i>n.</i> <i>Astelia trinervia</i>, a grasslike plant of the lily family, associated with kauri trees; bush-flax.
