>  <b>SALE</b> We find [toropapa] in a kauri grove, ... beneath the tufted neinei, or spiderwood, amid the smooth kauri grass, ... <i>(1982,Four Seasons,33</i>)

### kauri grub
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>REiSCHEK</b> On August 12 i went to the swamp to look for larks (<i>katata</i>), and also found the kauri grub (<i>Helix busby</i>), only to be seen in the kauri area. <i>ibid.</i> 70 i remained in this forest till the 20th, finding many kauri grubs; while under the rata roots i also found the Aweta, or plant-caterpillar, which to change into a chrysalis creeps underground, and in many cases instead of becoming a moth becomes - a mushroom! <i>(1930,Yesterdays in Maoriland,63</i>)

### kauri gum
 
 <i>n.</i> hardened, usu. fossilised, resin from kauri trees; often abbrev. gum and occas. and unidiomatically, called kauri resin [also gum [OED 1851.] The resin, often fossilised, from the kauri tree] (See [also gum](gra-gyp.md#gum))

>  <b>ADAMS</b> Kauri gum is still to be found in forest areas, or below the surface in cleared forest lands now under cultivation. <i>(1973,Kauri,26</i>)

>  <b>HALKETT & SALE</b> Kauri gum was used in fires by settlers, and by soldiers to heat their rations during early campaigns. <i>(1986,World of the Kauri, The,60</i>)

>  <b>MACKAY</b> Some bushmen had spent time on the gumfields, and many of them collected kauri gum on their days off. <i>(1991,Working the Kauri,4</i>)

>  Large quantities of kauri gum were also collected on Hauturu. <i>(Jan 2002,NZ Geographic,No. 55/20</i>)

>  Stanton had to boil his billy of tea with a few lumps of kauri gum. <i>(1900,Auckland Weekly News,Xmas No. / 27</i>)

>  Older boys at the Primary Scvhool took the fresh, white dribbles of kauri gum from the bark of trees. This was heated in hot water and moulded into small round balls for use as chewing gum. Unfortunately, it had a very slight oily flavour reminiscent of turpentine but this disappeared after the gum had been chewed for several hours. Brothers persuaded their younger siblings to chew the fresh gum and then repossessed it when the unwanted flavour had disappeared. <i>(Dec 2005,NZ Memories,No. 51/59</i>)

>  THE ROYALTY SYSTEM iN THE KAURi-GUM iNDUSTRY. [heading] <i>(1923,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,6/94</i>) [NOTES: (Firth)]

>  in speaking of Mercury Bay i had forgot to mention that the mangrove trees found there produce a resinous rosin, something trees found there produce a resinous substance very much like rosin, something of this kind i am told is found in both the East and West indias. [fn 3 1955] ... The `resinous substance' was kauri gum ... washed down the river ..., or caught in the mangroves on the way.] <i>(Nov 16, 1769,Cook's Journals,1/204,1955 ed.</i>)

>  <b>NiCHOLAS</b> ... we looked about in search of resin, which is brought down by the streams from various parts of the interior. ... [305] ... The pine, called by the natives cowree abounds with resin; and i believe it is the only tree of that species in the island that has any. <i>(1817,New Zealand (various authors/titles),1/304</i>)

>  <b>McNAB</b> The cowry ... yields abundance of rosin. <i>(1908,Historical Records,1`/691</i>) [NOTES: 1829 Mar 2 Atkins's Narrative in 1908 McNab Historical Records 1/691]

>  <b>MARKHAM</b> Quote ... one of the Boys set fire to the dry Fern ... and we saw lumps of Clear Coudy Gum which is always to be found about the roots of the Coudy. <i>(1834,New Zealand (various authors/titles),63,1963 ed.</i>)

>  <b>DARWiN</b> it is said, that by digging in the barest spots, lumps of the kind of resin which flows from the kauri pine are frequently found. [441, Dec 24] ... a quantity of resin oozes from the bark [of the kauri], which is sold at a penny a pound to the Americans, but its use was then unknown. <i>(Dec 23, 1835,Voyage of the Beagle,408</i>) [NOTES: (Everyman edn) (Chap. 18) 408]

>  Sailed from Bay of islands, Feb 8 1837 .... with 45 bags of cowdie gun <i>(Feb 8, 1837,Sydney Colonist</i>) [NOTES: 1837 Sydney Colonist Feb 8 (in 1937 McKenzie Gael Fares Forth 182)]

>  [kauri] gum: [FN] `Mr Darwin states that the resin is collected and sold at a penny a pound to the Americans, but its use is kept secret". <i>(1839,Narrative of a Voyage,20</i>) [NOTES: 1839 Narrative Surveying Voyages of Adventure and Beagle 20]

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> [???the tree or resin] ... poor sterile pipe clay land ... with great abundance of the cowry resin evidently proving these plains [Wangaroa] were once cowry forests ... wherever the same soil prevails the cowry gum is seen, ... <i>(Nov 5, 1839,Journal,2/162</i>)

>  <b>WiLKES</b> ... the quantity of Kaurie-gum embedded in the soil ... <i>(1852,U.S. Exploring Expedition,1/317</i>) [NOTES: 1840 Mar in 1852 Wilkes U.S. Exploring Expedition 1/317]

>  <b>SARAH MATHEW</b> On the shore i picked up a large mass of the Kaudi gum; ... <i>(Apr 12, 1840,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1840 Apr 12 SARAH MATHEW Journal in 1940 Founding of NZ 109]

>  <b>POLACK</b> Te Kapia no te Kauri or Kouri-gum <i>(1840,Manners & Customs of the NZers,2/265</i>)

>  <b>HEAPHY</b> fn i may mention that i have seen ... some varnish said to have been made from the Kauri gum; it appeared to be of very good quality. <i>(1842,New Zealand (various authors/titles),44</i>)

>  <b>FiTZROY</b> ... the sudden demand for Kauri Gum [in 1844-45] <i>(1846,Remarks on NZ,42</i>)

>  <b>ANGAS</b> At the foot of these [<i>cowdie</i>] trees are to be found masses of gum, which exudes from their trunks; large quantities of this substance are also to be met with beneath the ground ... B: The <i>cowdie</i> gum is a clear resin, having a very [167] strong aromatic flavour. it is chewed by the northern natives; ... <i>(1847,Savage Life,2/166</i>)

>  <b>SWAiNSON</b> [Exports from Auckland in 1852 include] Kauri spars . . . . . 5,850 0 0 . . Kauri gum . . . . . 805 0 0 . . . Gold dust, sand, and quartz 114 0 0 <i>(1853,Auckland,40</i>)

>  <b>ASKEW</b> The cargo consisted of ... wool, flax, sperm-oil, and kaurie gum. <i>(1857,Voyage to Australia & NZ,362</i>)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> ... pieces of Kauri gum have been dug out of the earth in the Middle island. <i>(1859,Story of New Zealand, The,1/18</i>)

>  <b>HOCHSTETTER</b> `in the year 1859 the amount of timber exportation from the province of Auckland was [Pounds] 34,376; that of kauri-gum exported [Pounds] 20,776.' [1874, 1893] <i>(1867,New Zealand (various authors/titles),140</i>)

>  The Kapia, or Kouri gum, is (as its colonial name shows) a Gum <i>(1868,TrNZi,1/49</i>)

>  <b>THATCHER</b> Where Maories are swarming, in vessels they come,/ And for powder and shot they exchange kauri gum,/ <i>(1869,Wit and Humour: Local Songs...,11</i>) [NOTES: (Beautiful Auckland)]

>  The Kapia, or Kauri Gum, is (as its colonial name shows) a Gum,or rather a Resin, from the Kauri Pine ... <i>(1869,TrNZi,1/iii/49</i>) [NOTES: (Colenso Botany North isd)]

>  <b>WARD</b> A resin exudes from [the Kauri] and is exported from Auckland under the name of Kauri Gum <i>(1872,Life Among the Maories of NZ,48</i>)

>  <b>HAY</b> Kauri-gum - or Kapia, as the Maoris call it - ... is another peculiar product of this northern extremity of New Zealand. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/14</i>)

>  Although a true resin, this is usually miscalled kauri-gum. <i>(1894,NZ Official Yearbook,338</i>) [NOTES: T.W.KiRK]

>  <b>MORRiS</b> <i>Kauri-gum, n.</i> the resin which exudes from the <i>Kauri</i>(q.v.), used in making varnish. <i>(1898,Austral English,244</i>)

>  <b>WHiTE</b> They exude that valuable and wonderul product `Kauri gum,' which is found in the Kauri forests near Auckland, ... <i>(1905,My NZ Garden,95</i>)

>  <b>THOMPSON</b> Among some foreign scum/ i am now upon a gumfield pokin' round for kauri gum!/ <i>(1909,Ballads about Business,94</i>)

>  The motive of the crime was apparently robbery, as it was known that the murdered man had some 40 [pounds] in his possession, being the proceeds of a recent sale of Kauri gum. <i>(Apr 29, 1911,NZ Truth,305/5/1</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> in the larger of the semi-subterranean huts are fires burning, fed with <i>manuka</i> branches and heaps of <i>kapia</i>, or kauri-gum. <i>(1922,NZ Wars, The,1/55</i>)

>  <i>kauri, kauri-pine n.</i> a coniferous tree of New Zealand ... - <i>kau'ri gum n.</i> a resinous gum dug up on the sites of ancient kauri forests. [Maori] <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,6</i>)

>  Most of this region, however, was once forested, and the search for the resin of the former trees (`Kauri gum') has given livelihood to a diminishing number of men. <i>(1936,Agricultural Organization in NZ,49</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> (gloss.) kauri gum: gum deposits from the enormous kauri of Northland, formerly collected by mdiggers and exported for use in the paint and varnish industry. <i>(1949,i Saw in My Dream,273,1974 ed. Rhodes ed.</i>)

>  ~ See <i>gum</i>. [14 edn: "Kauri gum', in New Zealand, is the resin given out by the kauri tree and used in making varnish.] <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),229</i>)

>  <i>kauri gum n.</i> the fossil resin of the kauri tree. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,614</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 410; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 270]]



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) natural resinous sap of the kauri tree, collected in rural Northland.

>  <b>KEENE</b> Kauri gum is the solidified resinous sap of the kauri tree, found only in northern New Zealand. Such 'bush' gum [that collected in cavities in high branches] was usually clean .. Another type of gum was 'hill' gum, found on low-lying hills and flats. The gum most prevalent in the far North was 'swamp gum' which was located by the 'hook' method. <i>(1977,To the Northward,123</i>)

>  <b>HAY</b> Kauri-gum - or kapia as the Maoris call it .. is another peculiar product of this northern extremity of New Zealand. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,ii 14</i>)

>  <b>KEENE</b> This is the country of black gum .. black gum is harder to grade and harder to judge than white gum ... [bush gum; hill gum; swamp gum; black gum; white gum] <i>(1977,To the Northward,124</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>POLACK</b> ... a large fire was made of wood of the Kauri gum, [sic] whose bituminous nature threw a vivid blaze around, .. <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),2/236</i>)

>  <b>ESCOTT</b> On the other side there's a bit of virgin bush. Kauri gums grow there a hundred and thirty feet. Howard's got a pair of doors made out of a slice of kauri gum: [editorial note explains the confusion] <i>(1936,Show Down,16,1973 ed.</i>)

>  <b>ESCOTT</b> On the other side there's a bit of virgin bush. Kauri gums grow there a hundred and thirty feet. Howard's got a pair of doors made out of a slice of kauri gum: [editorial note explains the confusion] <i>(1936,Show Down,16,1973 ed.</i>)

### kauri gum oil
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The distillation of kauri gum oil has been resuscitated after the lapse of years... Over a decade ago the distillation of peat for kauri gum oil was started at Mangawhero, in the Dargaville district. <i>(Aug 23, 1916,Press, The,2</i>)

>  <b>KENDRiCK SMiTHYMAN</b> An associated thing for <i>swamp gum</i> and <i>swamp kauri</i> is an `oil', which i refer to as `kauri gum oil' and which i must have seen talked about in print ... This is the oil which figures on northern maps extraordinarily, for instance, the L&S (1943) Provisional sheet N27 at map ref 601521, `Old Oil Well'. ... This oil was (can i say?) a seep of sap (pre-solid kauri gum?) from buried kauri; as far as i know it was used in mixing paint. <i>(1988,Personal Communication</i>)

 <i>n.</i> (See [gumdigger](gra-gyp.md#gumdigger))

### Kauri Joe
 
 <i>n.</i> (a person's (nick)name)

>  <b>HALKETT & SALE</b> The trade in kauri spars between New Zealand and Australia grew rapidly. <i>(1986,World of the Kauri, The,46</i>)

>  And there's old Kauri Joe, a Maori, who mainly made his name / By kicking fallen policemen. <i>(May 2, 1917,Chronicles NZEF,ii/17 ; 113</i>)

### kauri land
 
 <i>n.</i> land on which grow, or have grown, kauri trees; gumland.

>  <b>BiDWiLL</b> in most of what he called `Cowrie land' the trees are often a quarter of a mile asunder. <i>ibid.</i> 28 in most of what is here called `Cowrie land' the trees are often a quarter of a mile asunder, and rarely closer than a hundred yards : ... <i>(1841,Rambles in NZ,5</i>)

>  <b>DiEFFENBACH</b> it is utterly impossible ever to make good the damage done [by clearing forests] ..., as the kauri-land is so exhausted that scarcely anything will grow on it but fern and manuka. <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,1/228</i>)

>  <b>DiEFFENBACH</b> it is utterly impossible ever to make good the damage done [by clearing forests] ..., as the kauri-land is so exhausted that scarcely anything will grow on it but fern and manuka. <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,1/228</i>)

### kauri line
 
 <i>n.</i> latitude 38 degrees N: Tauranga to Kawhia

### kauri pine
 
 <i>n.</i> [see kauri]

>  ... the stately Kauri Pine (<i>Dammara australis</i>) uprearing its lofty head ... <i>(1869,TrNZi,1/iii/4</i>) [NOTES: (Colenso Botany North isd)]

>  The Kauri-pine is the finest tree in the country, and it attains a height from 120 to 160 feet. <i>(Jan 18, 1881,Waikato Times,3</i>)

>  <b>LANG</b> <i>(1839,New Zealand (various authors/titles)</i>) [NOTES: Koudi-pine]

>  <b>WiLKES</b> The gum ... is still produced by the Kaurie-pine, which is the finest of the timber trees of New Zealand. <i>(1852,U.S. Exploring Expedition,1/317</i>) [NOTES: 1840 Mar in 1852 Wilkes U.S. Exploring Expedition 1/317]

>  <b>BRODiE</b> <i>(1845,New Zealand (various authors/titles)</i>) [NOTES: 1845 Brodie Remarks on NZ ?p.(And)]

>  <b>ANGAS</b> Up a small ravine in the dense forest, we came to a saw-pit, close to which were growing some magnificent <i>cowdie</i> pines; ... <i>(1847,Savage Life,2/166</i>)

>  <b>HOCHSTETTER</b> The Kauri-pine is justly styled the Queen of the New Zealand forest. <i>(1867,New Zealand (various authors/titles),140</i>)

>  <b>TiNNE</b> ... there is not a single kauri pine now standing on the gum-fields; ... <i>(1873,Wonderland of the Antipodes,54</i>)

>  <b>LASLETT</b> The Kaurie or Cowdie-Pine (Dammara Australis) <i>(1875,Timber and Trees,295</i>)

>  <b>HECTOR</b> <i>(1883,Handbook of New Zealand,122</i>)

>  <b>LADY MARTiN</b> The first part of the road was bare and dreary, but as we drew near to the Waimate, we passed through an old forest of stately Kauri pines. <i>(1884,Our Maoris,30</i>)

>  <b>KiRK</b> The pine occurs only in the North island and north of Mercury Bay, and grows best near the sea on wet clay land. <i> ibid.</i> 143. The Native name `Kauri' is the only common name in general use. When the timber was first introduced into Britain it was termed `cowrie' or `kowdie-pine'; but the name speedily fell into disuse, although it still appears as the common name in some horticultural works. *`Cowrie-pine and Kowdie-pine were the names by which the kauri was first introduced to Britain.' --KEF, p.143. <i>(1889,Forest Flora</i>)

>  <b>LAiNG & BLACKWELL</b> <i>Agathis australis [(The Kauri)]</i>. The Kauri Pine is one of the most magnificent timber trees known... <i>(1907,Plants of NZ,62,1940 p. 41 ed.</i>)

>  <i>kauri, kauri-pine n.</i> a coniferous tree of New Zealand ... - <i>kau'ri gum n</i>. a resinous gum dug up on the sites of ancient kauri forests. [Maori] <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,6</i>)

>  <b>SOLJAK</b> The towering [11] kauri pine, which is found as far north as the Fiji islands, grows only in the warmer parts of New Zealand ... <i>(1946,New Zealand (various authors/titles),10</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> Few of the Kauri pine spars were procured by these vessels, their lading consisting of the [389] inferior white pine, called Kahikatea [a2,e-acute]; this injured the name of yellow pine, ... <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),2/388</i>)

>  ON SALE, 50 CORDS of MANUKA FiREWOOD, and a quantity of well-seasoned Kauri Pine Timber and Shingles, just imported. <i>(Feb 15, 1851,Lyttelton Times,1</i>)

>  Commencing with the New Zealand plants, the first we see here, in importance next to the most valuable of all - the Kauri pine - is the Totara (<i>Podocarpus totara<i/>). <i>(May 27, 1871,Otago Witness,5</i>)

Kapia, or Kauri resin, dugout of the earth; and also from the ashes of the curious vegeto-caterpillar fungus, the Hawhato (<i>Cordiceps Robertsii</i>), which was sometimes mixed with the black juice of the Mahoe berry ... <i>(1869,TrNZi,1/iii/37</i>) [NOTES: (Colenso Botany North isd)]

### kauri silk
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>R EED</b> ``Kauri Silk', generally in the form of plaited strands representing the long tresses of blonde hair often seen on young girls of the period, appeared during this time. ... `When a plait was made, often up to five feet long in finished form, it was tied with a ribbon and bow, top and bottom, and coiled in a suitable box, and offered for sale, or entered in any suitable exhibition competitions. ... To produce a nice specimen it is essential to have a good piece of gum. it would require to weigh about two pounds, [26] so that it could be firmly gripped, and should be pale and clear, and free from ruptures and defects. This seems to be difficult to acquire in these days. The lump must be scraped and cleaned and dried. ... `With an old metal frypan or an old metal plate on the hot range, moved about to get just the right temperature, the lump of gum was pressed on to the plate until the surface melted, and was then slowly drawn out to arm's length or further, and as this was done, the melted surface miraculously came away in these beautiful drawn fibres, which were placed on the nearby table. The operation was completed [sic: ? repeated] until the required three bundles of strands were produced. These were plaited with a standard three-plait. The conditions of the old-time warm and humid kitchen were very necessary for the success of this process, as the gum fibres when cold are very fragile. <i>(1972,Gumdiggers, The,25</i>) [NOTES: 1972 K.R.Mosheim in Reed Gumdiggers 25-26]

### kauri snail
 
 <i>n.</i> PUPURANGi or KAURi SNAiL (<i>Paryphanta busbyi</i>) (See [pupurangi](pop-pyo.md#pupurangi))

>  The reserves are home to geckos, kauri snails, fern birds and rare native orchids. <i>(Jul 2005,Conservation Action,26</i>)

>  He knows where to find the special treasures of this forest - big kauri snails, slimy and shiny in the spotlight's beam; cave wetas hanging from long, extravagantly articulated legs upside down on the undersides of logs; eels, koura and kokopu in the tiny creeks. <i>(Apr 2002,AA Directions,No. 51 (Autumn) : 17</i>)

>  Threatened species identified include kukupa, tomtit, kauri snail and Hochstetter's frog. <i>(Nov 2005,Open Space,5</i>)

>  i didn't know there was a species of native earthworm at Huia that grows one metre long .. or that meat-eating kauri snails suck up worms like spaghetti. <i>(Jul 2006,Air New Zealand inflight magazine,33</i>)

>  Similar.. interest attaches to the genus Paryphanta, of which the kauri snail is one of the best known. <i>(May 4, 1938,Auckland Weekly News,60</i>)

>  <b>POWELL</b> PUPURANGi or KAURi SNAiL (<i>Paryphanta busbyi</i>). A North Auckland representative of a group of large carnivorous snails found only in New Zealand. The shell is a flattened spiral of dark greenish colour, about 2 1/2 to 3 inches in diameter. <i>(1947,Native Animals of NZ,32</i>)

>  <i>(Sep 1950,NZ Journal of Agriculture,285/2</i>)

>  <b>J E PATTERSON</b> <i>(1855,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1855 J.E. Patterson Letter in 1873 C.M. Yonge Life (1875) 1/119]

>  <b>McLiNTOCK</b> SNAiL, KAURi or PUPURANGi (<i>Paryphanta busbyi</i>). This is a North Auckland representative of a group of large carnivorous snails found only in New Zealand, ..<i>ibid.</i> 3/705-12Kauri snail .. .. pupurangi .. .. <i>Paryphanta busbyi </i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/267</i>)

### kauru
 
 <i>n.</i> steamed ti-roots [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  <b>SHORTLAND</b> Just as we were leaving the place Te Rehe brought us a basket of `kauru', or baked root of the `ti' for which Waiateruati is celebrated. This root is in shape like a carrot, but from two to three feet long, and requires a deep rich soil for its growth. <i>(1851,Southern Districts of NZ,234</i>)

>  <b>SHORTLAND</b> KAURU, the root of the Ti or Whanake after it has been baked in the native oven. <i>(1851,Southern Districts of NZ,308</i>)

>  <b>BEATTiE</b> Kauru - the edible part of the cabbage tree. <i>(1945,Maori Place-Names of Canterbury,64</i>) [NOTES: c1880 South island Maori Lists `Wahi Mahika Kai' in 1945 Beattie Maori Place-names of Canterbury 64]

>  Small bundle of the vascular part of the cabbage tree stem or root<i> (kauru). </i> <i>(1897,TrNZi,29/175</i>)

>  <b>BEATTiE</b> i have also eaten some native foods. i thought the kauru prepared from the cabbage tree was nice, but i did not like fernroot. <i>(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,15</i>)

>  <b>STEVENSON</b> Other writers have stated that this food was known as kauru, and that it was allowed to remain in the ovens for at least 12 hours; ... <i>(1947,Maori and Pakeha in North Otago,80</i>)

>  <b>MiKAERE</b> Away in the western distance rose the foothills of the Southern Alps where the ti (cabbage) trees grew in abundance. The carrot-shaped roots and the young stems of the cabbage tree were the [29] source of the delicacy known as kauru ... The kauru was gathered just before the trees flowered, when the plants were at their sweetest. Roots and stems were steamed in large ovens to crystallise their sugar content. The cooked kauru was then pulled apart, dipped in the water and chewed. <i>(1988,Te Maiharoa,28</i>)

### kauta
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>MARKHAM</b> ... we found we could cook much better in the House than in cook house (Couter) [<i>kauta</i>] ... <i>(1834,New Zealand (various authors/titles),57,1963 ed.</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> <i>Koutas</i>, or cooking-houses, are merely small sheds to protect the <i>artistes</i> from the inclemency of the weather. <i>(1840,Manners & Customs of the NZers,1/214</i>)

>  <b>DiEFFENBACH</b> The native oven, <i>hangi</i> or <i>kohua</i>, made in the well-known manner with heated stones, is situated either in the open air or in a house (te-kauta) constructed of logs at a small distance from each other. <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/43</i>)

>  <b>BURTON</b> On a permanent marae, hangis were dug out of the floor of a cookhouse (<i>kauta</i>), which was usually a simple lean-to shelter but might be a sturdily constructed house (<i>whare umu</i>) with shuttered windows and outlets for smoke. <i>(1982,Two Hundred Years of NZ Food & Cookery,3</i>)

>  The extraordinary life of Dame Whina Cooper, who was born on the earthen floor of a kauta* and eighty years later awoke the conscience of a country. [fn] Kauta - a cooking house made from nikau and manuka <i>(1986,Speaking for Ourselves,39</i>) [NOTES: (Whina Cooper)]

### kava
 
 <i>n.</i> the root of the plant <i>Piper methysticum</i>, used, especially in Fiji but also in other Pacific islands, as the basis of a drink used for ceremonial occasions.

>  But kava is now being promoted in Europe, the US, Australia and New Zealand as a remarkable herbal medicine. There are hundreds of thousands of websites carrying information about kava or kawa kawa... <i>ibid.</i> 30 in kava.com, a demonstration store, three bottled versions of kava are on offer. Two of these, Kava Kazi and Kava Sutra(!), are fizzy. <i>(Sep 21, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),28</i>)

### kawa
 
 <i>n.</i> (Environment) (native pepper-tree)

>  The kawa, or native pepper-tree, Macropiper excelsum,.. is almost identical with the common kawa or kava of the South Sea islands. <i>(Mar 25, 1924,Otago Witness,6</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> the protocol or etiquette accepted on a marae [ORIGIN: Maori, [Ma. <b>kawa</b> protocol] cf. P.M. Ryan Dict. Modern Maori (1995) 91.]

>  "The Maori tikanga and kawa are also important." <i>(Apr 11, 2006,Whakatane Beacon,12</i>)

>  These are our kaitiaki, our guardians .. But the day we took up the harpoon we broke the kawa, the contract. it's our turn now to be the kaitiaki, the guardians of the future. <i>(May 10, 1997,Listener (New Zealand Listener),26</i>)

>  .. tikanga and kawa of Ngati Awa, and local environmental issues would be an advantage as would fluency or an interest in te reo Maori. <i>(Dec 9, 2005,Whakatane Beacon,54</i>)

>  We would need to make sure [use of] it doesn't trample on any of the kawa [custom]. <i>(Sep 21, 1999,Evening Post,5</i>)

>  First, other kawa has changed to meet modern requirements. <i>(Feb 6, 2000,Sunday Star Times,C4</i>)

>  Where would we Maori be without people like Josie Bullock and the media to point out our ignorance of our own tikanga and kawa? <i>(Oct 26, 2005,NZ Herald,A14</i>)

>  The Certificate in Te Pouhono is a marae-based programme designed to revitalise te reo, kawa, ahuatanga Maori and tikanga. <i>(Dec 2, 2005,Whakatane Beacon,10</i>)

>  .. all marae are facing the challenge of finding the next generation to maintain the tikanga and kawa. <i>(May 2007,Whakatane Beacon,8,Pu Kaea supplement ed.</i>)

>  We are totally abusing our tikanga and kawa by allowing these sorts of things to happen. <i>(Oct 21, 2005,Whakatane Beacon,2</i>)

>  Te Tai Tokerau MP Dover Samuels said while the kawa of the local people should be respected on the marae, powhiri at Government agencies were different. <i>(May 26, 2006,NZ Herald,A1</i>)

>  <b>OLiVER</b> it was a meeting of many separate mournings, held together by respect and affection for the .. figure in the open coffin, and by the capacity of Maori kawa to unify and dignify even such disparate elements. <i>(1983,James K. Baxter,155</i>)

>  <b>HOWE</b> Kawa - often confused in meaning with, or <i>used as a substitute for</i>, nga <i>tikanga o te marae</i> (q.v.) which refers to the <i>customs</i> or protocol accepted on a marae. These may differ from marae to marae. (The term comes from `pare kawakawa', the wreaths of leaves worn at a tangi). Williams dictionary gives 9 meanings for kawa, most of which seem to relate to the practice of removing a tapu by a special ceremony. <i>(1985,Towards Taha Maori,20</i>)

>  in A Block residents [in Paremoremo Prison, mainly Maori] analysed, discussed, settled differences, produced their own kawa and democratically decided on standards. <i>(May 12, 1985,NZ Times,5</i>)

>  <b>HULME</b> But i do know one thing Maki would have been good at .. - kawa. Marae protocol. The old people's way of doing things right. <i>(1986,Te Kaihau,114</i>)

>  The newspaper advertisement says the person must be acceptable to iwi (people, tribe) of the Porirua to Otaki area, preferably .. well-versed in local kawa (etiquette, protocol). <i>(Dec 15, 1986,Evening Post,17</i>)

>  Speaking steadily, calmly, remorselessly, making in his throat from time to time that small cough which is part of kawa, he [a Maori acitivist] appeals to their sense of pride, their mana. <i>(Feb 28, 1987,Listener (New Zealand Listener),11</i>)

>  Judge Durie transformed the Tribunal ... henceforth it met on the marae of the claimants, and observed their kawa in its hearings ... <i>(Apr 1987,NZ Journal of History,176</i>) [NOTES: Sorrenson]



 
 <i>n.</i> (?error for) kawe, swag strap

>  The balance pole.. was abandoned in favour of the kawa, which corresponds to the bushman's swag straps and to the soldier's knapsack. <i>(Jun 17, 1924,Otago Witness,4</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a New Zealand-bred cultivar of poplar. [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  Poplars like kawa are very appropriate to plant if they want to produce logs for a log fire. <i>(Aug 31, 2001,Dominion,Supp 26</i>)

### kawaka

>  <b>POLACK</b> The <i>Kawaka</i> grows to the height of thirty feet. <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),2/397</i>)

>  <b>DiEFFENBACH</b> i have not seen the fructification of the kawaka, or Dacrydium plumosum, ... <i>ibid.</i> 2/366 Kawaka - pine-tree (<i>Dacrydium plumosum</i>) <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,1/323</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Thuja <i>Doniana</i>, Hook. ... [232] Nat. names `Moko piko'.<i>Bidwill</i>; `Kawaka', Cunn. <i>(1853,Flora NZ,i Flowering Plants 231-232</i>)

>  ... the graceful fern-plumaged Kawaka, <i>([Libocedrus Doniana, Hook: f.]),</i> the handsome celery-leaved Tanekaha, <i>(Phyllocladus trichomanoides,)</i> ... the ornate Tawhai, <i>(Fagus Menziesii,)</i> and ... the ... Tawhai-rau-nui, <i>(Fagus fusca,). </i> <i>(1869,TrNZi,1/iii/4</i>) [NOTES: (Colenso Botany North isd)]

>  Kawaka. Libocedrus Doniana, Endl. <i>(1869,TrNZi,1/iii/48</i>) [NOTES: (Buchanan Botany Otago)]

>  ... the kawaka <i>Libocedrus Doniana</i>) or arbor vitae, recognised at a distance by its bark hanging in broad ribbon-like flakes ... showing how apposite was its old trivial name `plumosum' ... <i>(1872,TrNZi,4/249</i>) [NOTES: (Kirk Brit. and NZ Floras)]

>  <b>HAAST</b> .. the principal vegetation consisted of kahikatea, totara, and manuka, mixed with ... kawaka (Libocedrus donianus), ... <i>(1879,Geology of the Provinces of Canterbury & Westland,90</i>)

>  <b>HAY</b> The Kawaka (<i>Libocedrus Doniana</i>) is a grand tree of the largest class. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/194</i>)

>  <b>HECTOR</b> <i>Libocedrus doniana</i>, Endl. Kawaka, Cypress, Cedar.--This handsome tree attains a height of 60-100 feet, and a diameter of 3-5 feet. Wood reddish, fine-grained and heavy. <i>(1883,Handbook of New Zealand,122</i>)

>  [Native name] Kawaka .. [Settlers' name] Cedar .. [Family] Coniferae <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,120</i>) [NOTES: 1886 T.Kirk in 1902 Settler's Handbook of NZ 120]

>  <b>KNOX</b> Then there is the Matai, which is much like the English yew, and is used for making furniture; the Miro, which has a beautiful red fruit on which pigeons grow fat; and the Kawaka, which has a remarkable leaf and a durable wood. <i>(1888,Boy Travellers,201</i>)

>  Kawaka (cedar) .. Librocedrus Doniana .. Timber dark red colour, straight and even in grain, <i>(1890,PWD Catalogue of Timbers,10</i>)

>  The timber on the western side consists of red- and white-pine, matai (or black-pine), totara, kawhaka (or cedar), rata, and occasional silver-pine, besides black- and red-birch <i>(Fagus fusca). </i> <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,19</i>)

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> <i> L[ibocedrus] Doniana, Endl.</i> [647] ... NORTH iSLAND: <i>Kawaka</i>; New Zealand <i>Arbor-vitae. </i> <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,646</i>)

>  <b>LAiNG & BLACKWELL</b> <i> (Libocedrus [plumosa] (The Kawaka) </i>... Native name <i>Kawaka</i> or <i>Ngawaka</i>, often termed <i>New Zealand Arbor Vitae</i> by the settlers. [earlier, L. Doniana] <i>(1907,Plants of NZ,66,1940 ed.</i>)

>  <i>kawaka</i>, a tree (Libocedrus doniana). <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/149</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <b>WiLLiAMS</b> <i>Kawaka</i> (i), n. <i>Libocedrus plumosa, N.Z. cedar. =kaikawaka.</i> <i>(1957,Dictionary of the Maori Language,110</i>)

>  <i>kawaka n</i> -s [Maori] : a New Zealand timber tree <i>Libocedrus plumosa</i>) <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third international,1234</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Libocedrus Bidwillii.</i>

>  (And) <i>Libocedrus Bidwillii </i> <i>(1872,Catalogue of the Colonial and Vienna Exhibition</i>)

### kawaka fern
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Apteropteris malingii.</i> [JD ??]

>  <b>MARTiN</b> ... the peculiar brownish KAWAKA FERN (<i>Apteropteris malingii</i>) which is usually the only filmy to be found on the bark of the native cedar or kawaka, where alone it grows in most forests, ... <i>(1961,Flora NZ,100</i>)

### kawakawa
 
 <i>n.</i> greenstone [[Ma. <b>kawakawa</b> pepper tree <i>(Micropiper excelsum)</i>, dark greenstone, opaque, fern <i>(blechnum fluviatile)</i>]

>  Unlike gold, pounamu comes in a great range of colours and patterns... Kahurangi was highly translucent, flawless and rare, inanga pearly white to grey-green, kawakawa the dark-green stone that is commonly used in today's jewellery. <i>(Mar 2003,NZ Geographic,62/8</i>)

>  Kawakawa is the most common variety of pounamu. it comes in many shades - from strong rich green to dark green - and often has small dark flecks, called inclusions, which add to its character. Variations of kawakawa include the olive-gree raukaraka and totoweka pounamu, which has reddish spots and streaks. <i>(Oct 23, 2009,Press, The,4</i>)

>  <b>FiRTH</b> Among the Maori, considerations of aesthetic interest helped quite materially ... to decide the relative worth of objects made of the <i>pounamu, </i>the nephrite so much prized for pendants, <i>mere</i>, and adzes. Most prized was the <i>kahurangi</i> stone, bright green, translucent, hard and clear; the<i> inanga</i> variety perhaps comes next, with its white milky-veined appearance, [389] followed by the <i>kawakawa</i>, dark olive-green, dull and opaque. ... <i>Tangiwai</i> (`tear drop'), <i>bowenite</i>, a softer stone of a clear pale green, with the appearance of drops of water in the texture, was less esteemed, though more beautiful in the European eyes, whilst <i>kahotea</i>, green, spotted or streaked with black, was an inferior type, and was used for small chisels, and for objects of subordinate interest. <i>(1929,Primitive Economics of NZ Maori,388</i>)

>  <b>FiRTH</b> Among the Maori, considerations of aesthetic interest helped quite materially ... to decide the relative worth of objects made of the <i>pounamu, </i>the nephrite so much prized for pendants, <i>mere</i>, and adzes. Most prized was the <i>kahurangi</i> stone, bright green, translucent, hard and clear; the<i> inanga</i> variety perhaps comes next, with its white milky-veined appearance, [389] followed by the <i>kawakawa</i>, dark olive-green, dull and opaque. ... <i>Tangiwai</i> (`tear drop'), <i>bowenite</i>, a softer stone of a clear pale green, with the appearance of drops of water in the texture, was less esteemed, though more beautiful in the European eyes, whilst <i>kahotea</i>, green, spotted or streaked with black, was an inferior type, and was used for small chisels, and for objects of subordinate interest. <i>(1929,Primitive Economics of NZ Maori,388</i>)

>  <b>JOSEPH</b> `The jade, the pounamu ... it's a beautiful stone ... Look,' he said, holding up a long hooked ear-drop of pearly translucence, `that's tau tau and the stone is called inanga. And this, the pekapeka - they say the shape comes from the bat. This is for the ear, too - the mako, like a shark's tooth ... And these,' - he ran his fingers along a row of ... nephrite adzes - `toki. See this one' - he held the heavy red-mottled adze in his palm - `that's the red kind, totoweka, woodhen's blood. The deep green, that's kawakawa.' <i>(1962,Pound of Saffron, A,113</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a fish

>  Port Nicholson abounds with fish the principal are the Kawa Kawa, Snapper, Harbouker, Barracouta ... plenty of Cockles, Muscles [sic] and the fish which inhabits Venus's Ear. [fn 1966] This name proves elusive. <i>(1840,Journal of Ensign Best,230,1966 ed.</i>)

>  <i>2 kawakawa n</i>, pl<i> kawakawa</i> [Hawaiian] <i>Hawaii</i> : LiTTLE TUNA] <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third international,1234</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> pepper tree [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  My friend plays music on a kawa kawa leaf and says when he does this the tuis come and sit on him. <i>(Mar 8, 2000,Capital Times,5</i>)

>  .. TaaKawa beer has a point of difference .. the leaves of the kawakawa, a spicy, astringent plant that has been employed by Maori for generations as an internal and external remedy .. Simon says kawakawa, like its Fijian cousin kava, also offers a subtle analgesic effect, "a bit prickly on your lips". <i>(Aug 7, 2004,Dominion Post,E9</i>)

>  As part of the meal Akaroa salmon is smoked over kawakawa leaves. Kawakawa is often used to make tea. <i>(Jun 25, 2003,Capital Times,18</i>)

>  Kawakawa leaves can be chewed as a remedy for toothache or made into a green tea very good for digestive or kidney complaints. <i>(Feb 8, 2003,Dominion Post,F15</i>)

>  Kawakawa has ceremonial as well as medicinal importance to Maori; among its uses, the leaves are a symbol of mourning, often carried at funerals. <i>(Feb 8, 2003,Dominion Post,F15</i>)

>  Kawakawa is the easiest to spot; its heart-shaped leaves are usually full of holes left by caterpillars. it's a valuable plant to Maori, who use it to treat everything from cuts to rheumatism. <i>(Dec 27, 2003,Dominion Post,C4</i>)

>  Lightly poached smoked eel served over a creamy Kawakawa risotto .. and a karengo seaweed salsa. <i>(Jul 2005,Capital Times,13-19 july / 13</i>)

>  More recently .. He launched tea made from kawakawa (native pepper) and horopito, - both labelled as 'organically grown in nuclear-free New Zealand'. <i>(Apr 1991,NZ Geographic,116</i>)

>  [heading] KAi TiME. Maori chef Charles Royal gathers herbs and plants from the bush including pikopiko heads, kawakawa shoots, horopito and piripiri to make dry rubs, relishes and pickles. <i>(2005,AA Directions,Spring 2005, p54</i>)

>  <b>GEORGE</b> Gangs were working the silver pine stands for posts, strainers and telephone poles or splitting kawakawa battens. <i>(1990,Ohakune,204</i>)

>  The <i>kawakawa</i> or pepper tree (<i>piper excelsum</i>) is a useful medicine tree. <i>(Jun 1936,NZ Railways Magazine,29</i>)

>  The toothless smile, the heavily moko chin and lips, the deeply wrinkled face framed by the black shawl that she habitually wore, often topped off with a mourning wreath of kawakawa leaves, were all a bit frightening to the little ones. <i>(Dec 2003,NZ Memories,No. 45/25</i>)

>  .. but the toasted paraoa added an element of sweetness along side the fiery kawakawa. <i>(Aug 2005,Capital Times,17-23 Aug / 20</i>)

>  in the bush on the island they eat .. kawa-kawa berries. <i>(Aug 17, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,17</i>)

>  At one stage, Mr Thornley toyed with the idea of calling his menu "restorative", since, like the horopito used for the pork, the kawakawa tea used to smoke his Akaroa salmon is primarily used as a medicine by Maori, as a liver cleanser and a blood thinning agent. <i>(Jun 21, 2003,Dominion Post,F6</i>)

>  The banks by the track are massed with kawakawa, Macropiper excelsa, their heart-shaped leaves reduced to green "lace": by resident caterpillars .. "Kawakawa contains toxins in its leaves .. When it's chewed or browsed that increases the amount of toxins it produces. The caterpillar comes along - there's only one species of caterpillar that can feed on kawakawa - it chews a hole, gets a bellyache, falls off on a little strand of silk." <i>(Aug 10, 2002,Dominion Post,F14</i>)

>  <b>HAWES</b> He took kawakawa leaves for the pain and they worked pretty well. <i>(2000,Dream of Nikau Jam, The,48</i>)

>  After an hour or so, Wiapo collected kawakawa and herbs to make tea, which we shared while sitting on the steps of the administration block. <i>(May 15, 2010,Listener (New Zealand Listener),60</i>)

>  Flavours include tonic water with horopito (made by blending two separate horopito extractions to produce floral bitters with an aromatic note), bitter lemon with horopito (a blend of real lime juice and horopito essences) and Kiwi cola with kawakawa, a smooth and fragrant drink - great for mixing. <i>(Sep 26, 2010,Sunday  Star Times: Escape</i>)

>  Garlands of green leaves, preferably of the <i>kawakawa (piper excelsum)</i> were prepared and donned .. <i>(1900,Auckland Weekly News,Xmas No. : 12</i>)

>  Pretty as a ferny juvenile, the kawaka or Libocedrus plumosa also makes a good canopy tree as an adult with its naked trunk reaching 20 metre. <i>(Sep 15, 2001,Dominion,10,Weekend Magazine ed.</i>)

>  The kawakawa tree that grows in coastal areas has a variety of medicinal uses .. Kawakawa leaves made into tea is said to be good for kidney troubles. <i>(Sep 8, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),38</i>)

### kawakawa moth
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Cleora scriptaria</i> [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  The holes are the work of the Kawakawa moth, Cleora scriptaria. The plant is known to be either toxic or unattractive to other bugs, but the kawakawa moth appears to have evolved defences to the toxins and prefers the plant to all others. <i>(Jul 2, 2005,Dominion Post,E17</i>)

### kawanatanga
 
 <i>n.</i> [1845 S. Selwyn in 1960 Drummond 121]

>  The Principle of Government The Kawanatanga Principle The Government has the right to govern and to make laws. <i>(1989,Principles for Crown Action,7</i>)

>  But Maori also conceded "kawanatanga" or "governorship" in article 1... Just as "rangatiratanga" is an important power, so is "kawanatanga"... Whereas some Maori need to pull back from some of the stands that have been taken, the Government needs to recognise that it has not lived up to its full responsibility for kawanatanga. <i>(Sep 4, 2003,Dominion Post,B5</i>)

>  The 1870-72 Urewera expeditions were driven by a vengeful Colonial Government (also known as the Kawanatanga), desperate to track down and destroy Te Kooti Arikirangi Te Turuki and his Ringatu guerilla force then regrouping within the boundaries of the Tuhoe tribe's forested rohe. <i>(Oct 4, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),29</i>)

>  The Maori version of the Treaty ceded kawanatanga (governorship of New Zealand) to the Queen. <i>(Mar 29, 1997,Listener (New Zealand Listener),7</i>)

>  <b>SEWELL</b> As to the former, is it true they surrendered to the Queen the `Kawanatanga' - the governorship - or sovereignty; but they did not undestand that they thereby surrendered the right of self-government over their internal affairs, a right which we never claimed or exercised, and could not in fact exercise. <i>(1864,NZ Native Rebellion,9</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> The description of the Pai-marire, or Hauhau religion ... contains much that has not previously been recorded. For this ... my thanks go forth to my old warrior friends, both Kawanatanga and Hauhau. <i>(1922,NZ Wars, The,2/v,1983 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BELiCH</b> <i>kawanatanga</i> Governor's sphere, Governor's authority <i>(1986,NZ Wars, The,337</i>)

>  The New Zealand Maori Council has explained the concept of Rangatiratanga as meaning more than `possession': `it means the wise administration of all the assets possessed by a group for that group's benefit: in a word, trusteeship' (<i>Te Wahanga Tuatahi</i>, 1983, p 5) in the Crown's view, the First and Second Articles of the Treaty are both strong statements which necessarily qualify one another. <i>Kawanatanga</i> is subject to a promise to protect <i>Rangatiratanga. Rangatiratanga</i> is subject to an acknowledgment of <i>Kawanatanga</i>. The working out in practice of the balance between the two Articles must depend upon a case by case consideration. ... it is the <i>right</i> of all citizens, Maori, Pakeha, and other New Zealanders, that there be effective Government or <i>Kawanatanga</i> in such matters. <i>(1989,Principles for Crown Action,11</i>)

### kawau
 
 <i>n.</i> a shag (esp. the black shag)

>  c1835 Boultbee Journal (1986) 109 shag - ka'h [koau, kawau] <i>(1835,Boultbee Journal,109,1986 ed.</i>) [NOTES: c1826-1827 list of Maori words in c1835 Boultbee Journal (1986) 109]

>  <b>BULLER</b> Two species [of Cormorant] inhabit these shores; one, with entirely black plumage, which the natives call Kawau - the other with white foreneck, breast, and belly ... called by them Karuhiruhi; this last is most common. <i>(1888,Birds of New Zealand,152</i>) [NOTES: 1841 Colenso in 1888 Buller Birds of NZ 152]

>  <b>DiEFFENBACH</b> 82. <i>Graucalus varius</i>. - ... M.Lesson mentions a species of this genus under the native name of Ka-oua-ko. `All the species of cormorants are called Kauwau by the natives.' <i> Dieffenbach. </i> <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/201</i>) [NOTES: 1842 Gray's Fauna in 1843 Dieffenbach Travels in NZ 2/201 82.]

>  <b>DiEFFENBACH</b> ... the cormorants themselves have something solemn in their aspect, and are called by the New Zealanders kauwau, or preachers. <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,1/77</i>)

>  The Kauau [Kawau] or common diver, the Raruhiruhi, the first name is generally used, the Black Shagg or diver and the latter to the smaller diver, with white brest [sic]; ... [Glossary, 118 kawau black shag (<i>phalacrocorax varius</i>)] <i>(1844,NZ Journal,115,1956 ed.</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFiELD</b> There are six or seven varieties of the Cormorant or <i>Shag, (graucalus,) </i>called<i> kauwau </i>by the natives. <i>(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,164</i>)

>  <b>DRURY</b> BiRDS MET WiTH iN PELORUS ... Cormorants - several kinds ... The Phalacrocorax Punctatus, or Cristatus (Spotted Shag) is said to be common in Pelorus ... all kinds are called Kauwau by the natives. <i>(1855,Sailing Directions,66</i>)

>  Graculus varius. Kawau. Shag. Abundant. <i>(1869,TrNZi,1/161</i>) [NOTES: (Hutton Birds Great Barrier)]

>  <b>HUTTON</b> 147. GRACULUS CARBO. L. Cormorant. Black Shag. Kawau. <i>G. carboides</i>. GRAY. <i>Phalacrocorax Novae Hollandiae.</i> BULLER. <i>(1871,Birds of New Zealand,50</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> PHALACROCORAX NOVAE HOLLANDiAE. (BLACK SHAG.) ... <i>Native name.</i> Kawau. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,325,{1888 2nd edn 2/145} ed.</i>)

>  <b>HAY</b> The Kawau (<i>Phalacrocorax, sp.</i>) is one of the commonest birds. ... They build in trees, in large `shaggeries' ... <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/222</i>)

>  <b>MORRiS</b> <i>Kawau, n.|</i> Maori name for a Shag <i>Phalacrocorax novae-hollandiae</i>, Steph. <i>(1898,Austral English,245</i>)

>  <i>kawau</i>, a water-bird (Phalacrocorax spp.), cormorant; shag: the various species distinguished by the Maori with a suffix; but shag is the common name used by the less observant Pakeha. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/152</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  kawau: shag. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,174</i>)

>  <b>RiCHARDS</b> <i>Phalacrocorax ... carbo </i>... Black Shag or Cormorant. KAWAU <i>(1952,Chatham islands,89</i>)

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> BLACK SHAG <i>Phalacrocorax carbo Maori name</i>: Kawau. <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,63</i>)

>  <i>kawau n. </i>New Zealand shag [Maori] <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),618</i>)

### kea
 
 <i>v.</i> to screech like a kea; to make a kea sound.

>  <b>GREEN</b> ... pitching on a boulder, near my head, he `kea'd' at me with renewed vigour. [216] ... ere i could catch him he recovered ... and, flying to a boulder about 200 yards away, gave forth a volley of `keas' which surpassed anything i had ever heard before. <i>(1883,High Alps of NZ,215</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a dark green South island parrot, orange-red under its wings [Maori]; mountain parrot, mount cook parrot, occas. kea parrot.

>  <b>HAAST</b> What gave still greater interest ... was the presence of large green alpine parrots (<i>Nestor notabilis</i>), the Kea of the Natives, which visited constantly the small groves of beech trees near our camp. <i>(1948,Julius Von Haast,215</i>) [NOTES: 1862 Von Haast in 1948 Von Haast Julius Von Haast 215]

>  <b>VON HAAST</b> ... large green alpine parrots (Nestor notabilis), the kea of the natives. <i>(1879,Geology of Westland,36</i>) [NOTES: 1862 Von Haast Exploration of the Head Waters of Waitaki in 1879 Geology of Westland 36]

>  Polly is a kea, or mountain parrot. <i>(Sep 8, 1938,NZ Observer,10</i>)

>  The favourite morsel of the unhappy mutton which attracts the kea most strongly is the fat on the kidneys, and having located the spot on the living animal, he deliberately cuts his way to it. <i>(Jan 23, 1890,Otago Daily Times,43</i>)

>  Kea are another marketable species. Overseas, zoo-surplus kea fetch several thousand dollars when sold, quite legally, on the open market. <i>(Mar 24, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),30</i>)

>  We began to notice the keas soaring like eagles far overhead, and to hear their common cry "Kiiaa! Kiiaa!" <i>(Aug 18, 1899,Auckland Weekly News,Suppl. 5</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> These birds, the ka-kas, are slenderer in build and have smaller beaks than their relative, the sheep-killing kea of the eastern slopes. <i>(1911,Through South Westland,80</i>)

>  <b>GREEN</b> While engaged in making our cache, two keas or Mt Cook parrots came wheeling round us screaming loudly. <i>(1883,High Alps of NZ,17</i>) [NOTES: 1976 edition]

>  i was very much surprised to read in your issue of the 28th September an article stating, on the authority of Mr James Drummond, F.L.S., that there is no authentic evidence to prove that the kea kills sheep. <i>(Oct 16, 1908,Evening Post,4</i>)

>  <b>HUTTON</b> 52. NESTOR NOTABiLiS. Gould. Mountain Parrot. Kea. <i>(1871,Birds of New Zealand,20</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> NESTOR NOTABiLiS. (KEA PARROT.) ... <i>Native names</i>. `Kea' of the Maoris; \{Kea and Keha;\} `Mountain-Parrot' of the colonists. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,52,{1888 2nd edn 1/166} ed.</i>)

>  <b>HAAST</b> What gave still greater interest ... was ... a number of large green alpine parrots <i>(Nestor notabilis)</i> the Kea of the Natives, ... <i>ibid.</i> 117 A number of Keas (the green mountain parrot) ... broke the stillness of nature with their plaintive notes. <i>(1879,Geology of the Provinces of Canterbury & Westland,36</i>)

>  ..... the species known as the `Kea' by the Maoris, the `Mountain Parrot' of the colonists ... <i>(Feb 1880,Zoologist,57</i>) [NOTES: (Morris)]

>  <i>Nestor notabilis</i>, Gould. Mountain or Green parrot, Sheep-killer, Kea, Kia, Keha, Keo, Kaieo. - By all these names is this troublesome bird known by the highland shepherds and sheep musterers in alpine districts from Lakes Wanaka and Hawea to the river Rangitata. <i>(Jul 1885,NZ Journal of Science,481</i>) [NOTES: (T.H. Potts)]

>  <b>KNOX</b> When the sheep-farmers first established their stations among the mountains there were flocks of the <i>kea</i>, or green parrot, living in the glens and feeding entirely on fruit and leaves. <i>(1888,Boy Travellers,276</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> N. notabilis, Gould, Kea-parrot, Mountain - parrot of the colonists. <i>(1888,Birds of New Zealand,1/166</i>)

>  <b>MORRiS</b> <i>Kea, n.</i> a parrot of New Zealand, <i>Nestor notabilis</i>, Gould. For its habits see quotations. <i>(1898,Austral English,245</i>)

>  <b>HAAST</b> `What gave still greater interest to the spot was the presence of a number of large green alpine parrots (Nestor notabilis), <i>the kea of the natives, which visited continually the small grove of beech-trees near our camp.' </i>`On the 4th of November last the distinguished surgeon, Mr John Wood, F.R.S, exhibited before the Pathological Society of London the colon of a sheep, in which the operation known as Colotomy had been performed by a Parrot...the species known as the `kea' by the Maoris, - the `Mountain Parrot' of the colonists, <i>Nestor natabilis</i> of Gould. Only five species...are known, one of which (<i>Nestor productus</i>) has lately become extinct; they only occur in New Zealand and Norfolk island. They were formerly classed among the <i>Trichoglossina</i> or brush-tongued parrots ... more nearly allied to true <i>Psittaci</i> ... its ordinary food consists of berries and insects; but since its Alpine haunts have been reached by the tide of civilization, it has acquired a taste for raw flesh, to obtain which it even attacks living animals.' <i>(1879,Geology of Westland,36</i>) [NOTES: >1862. J. Von Haast, `Exploration of Head Waters of Waitaki, 1862,' - in `Geology of Westland' (published 1879), p. 36]

>  <b>PASCOE</b> The Kea Kea. [Nestor notabilis] ... unlike the Kakas, who have a dozen different cries, the Kea has just one, weird Key-a-Key-a, hence its name. <i>(1957,Mr Explorer Douglas,253</i>) [NOTES: c1899 Douglas in 1957 Pascoe Explorer Douglas 253, 254 (Birds of South Westland)]

>  <i>(Feb 1880,Zoologist,57</i>)

>  <i>kea n.</i> the sheep-killing parrot. [Maori] <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,6</i>)

>  <i>Kea</i>.- As often as not shepherds pronounce this word `keaw'. i am told by the greatest living authority on native birds that no recognised naturalist any longer denies that keas kill sheep. My own experience of them is that only a small percentage (perhaps 5 or 10 per cent.) are actual killers, but they are all potential killers, as they will all eat meat. <i>(Oct 17, 1933,Press, The</i>) [NOTES: Acland]

>  `Kea in O.E.D. ... [should be spelt] <i>kia,</i> a bird (Nestor notabilis), mountain-parrot: Williams in <i>Maori Dictionary</i>, says probably so named from its screech. it is so; but the usual spelling, kea, is not the sound of the screech, which is kia. i have often heard it in the mountains; a long-drawn kiaaaaa or vibrato lia-a-a-a: close at hand the cry is tender as the mewing of a kitten. The error has come through words like fear, tear, hear, but the Maori spelling of those words would be fia, tia, hia, ignoring the "r" as a non-Scot would. [This does not overcome the fact that kea [kea] is the accepted Maori spelling and pronuciation <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/152</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <i>kea</i>: mountain parrot. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,174</i>)

>  kea A mountain parrot of New Zealand; some keas kill sheep and a price is put upon the kea's head. [the last clause replaced in 14 edn by the kea is a cheeky and inquisitive bird and can do much damage to property with its strong beak.] <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),229</i>)

>  <i>kea n.</i> large red and green New Zealand parrot reputed to feed on kidney fat of sheep [Maori]. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),618</i>)

>  The vocabulary of New Zealand English has been influenced by ... the adoption of Maori words and phrases. ... Examples of the native words now in general use are the names of birds, fish, plants, and trees: <i>kea, tui, hapuku, tarakihi, koromiko, raupo</i>, and</i> kauri. </i> <i>ibid.</i> 3/705-712 kea .. .. <i>Nestor notabilis </i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,2/679</i>) [NOTES: (Wall NZ Speech)]

>  <i>kea ... n.</i> large red and green N.Z. parrot reputed to feed on kidney fat of sheep [Maori] <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),11</i>)

>  <i>217. Nestor notabilis</i> Gould, 1856 <i>Kea </i> <i>(1970,Annotated Checklist of Birds of NZ,50,1980 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BAXTER</b> kea <i>mountain parrot </i> <i>(1982,Selected Poems,188</i>)

>  <i>kea n.</i> a large New Zealand parrot with brownish-green plumage. [C19: < Maori, imit. of its call] <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,614</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 410; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 270]]

>  <i>Kea Nestor notabilis</i> GOULD, 1856 <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,245</i>)

>  He was puzzled why any kea would be found so far down-country [near Hokonui] <i>(Jul 29, 1986,Southland Times,19</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> (of a kea) to attack a sheep

>  ...i never saw a single sheep which had been `kea'd' [on the Wilberforce]. <i>(1952,High Country Journey,97</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> the cry of a kea

>  <b>GREEN</b> ... pitching on a boulder, near my head, he `kea'd' at me with renewed vigour. [216] ... ere i could catch him he recovered ... and, flying to a boulder about 200 yards away, gave forth a volley of `keas' which surpassed anything i had ever heard before. <i>(1883,High Alps of NZ,215</i>)

>  <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/152</i>) [NOTES: [see above]]

### Kea
 
 <i>n.</i> a junior Scout member; a group of junior scouts. [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  [Hdg] Resurgence in Cub and Kea numbers in Crofton and Ngaio areas .. Ngaio Scout Group also runs a Kea Group for six to eight years olds which is also growing in popularity. "We've got 15 Keas in the Waikowhai Keas Group at the moment and three are about to move onto Cubs." <i>(May 5, 1999,Norwester,4</i>)

>  The Keas were at the park for an hour, putting paper and other items into their plastic bags. <i>(Nov 21, 2000,independent Herald (Wellington),10</i>)

>  Keas, Cubs and Scouts are also held at the centre. <i>(Mar 2008,Northland News,2</i>)

### kea beak
 
 <i>n.</i> (Environment) the token beak from a slaughtered kea, used to collect money from Council, in the attempt to reduce numbers of high-country killer keas.

>  Would the Council urge the [Tourist] Department to take adequate measures for the destruction of keas on their reserves, otherwise their recent increased 'kea beak' subsidy will be partly nullified; or, failing that, would they advise me if one has the right to proceed on to the Government ground in order to destroy birds gazetted as noxious. it was said that the tourist guides made money out of the birds by selling them to tourists at 10s each. <i>(Aug 13, 1909,Grey River Argus,4</i>)

>  [Farmers] decided to petition the Government to increase the bonus on kea-beaks. <i>(1907,TrNZi,39/76</i>)

>  Kea beaks are purchased by the Mackenzie County Council but not by the Waitaki County Council. <i>(Jan 12, 1923,Dominion,2</i>)

### kea country
 
 <i>n.</i> (Environment) a high country area where kea are located. [ORIGIN: blend]

>  A kea-hunter, who spends a great deal of his time on sheep stations in the kea country amongst the sheep-killers of Canterbury, and who has caught hundreds of them, relates an incident to show that the evil name they have been given is not undeserved. <i>(Aug 21, 1909,Otago Daily Times,3</i>)

>  if anyone, who has lived in the kea country and believes the bird to be innocent, please send name and address to me, enclosing your reasons for believing the kea to be innocent. <i>(Mar 29, 1906,Grey River Argus,2</i>)

>  Mr Barnett's district is in the kea country, and he has something to say of this notorious sheep-killer. <i>(Jul 25, 1908,Evening Post,13</i>)

>  We observe <i> imprimis </i> that Mr WHiTE does not live in the kea country, but probably 400 miles from it. He quitted the desolate mountains of the South island many years ago. <i>(Dec 26, 1895,Otago Witness,3</i>)

>  .. it seems to me that we are sure to get nearer to the truth by taking the experiences of men who have spent most of their lives in kea country, than that of men who judge the birds mostly from caged or preserved specimens. <i>(Oct 19, 1908,Evening Post,4</i>)

### kea crossing
 
 <i>n.</i> a patrolled school crossing point

>  Kea crossings provide children with a safe place to cross the road. They're installed around schools so school patrols can control traffic and safely guide children across the street. <i>(Aug 5, 2005,internet - see Notes</i>) [NOTES: http://www.landtransport.govt.nz/factsheets/26.html]

>  Mr Wilson was pleased to be told that a kea crossing would be installed as requested and that it would happen before the end of the year. <i>(Jul 29, 2005,Whakatane Beacon,3</i>)

>  Kea crossings are commonly installed around schools so patrols can control traffic and safely guide children across the street. <i>(Dec 27, 2005,Whakatane Beacon,6</i>)

>  it was safety to the fore on Donald Bruce Road yesterday as Waiheke's second kea crossing got its first test run .. Kea crossings, already in operation at Waiheke Primary School on Seaview Road, use flags and swing out arms to provide a safe crossing for children. <i>(Jul 5, 2007,Waiheke Weekly, The,4</i>)

>  The purpose of this report is to inform the Committee of the Land Transport and Safety Authority (LTSA) extension of the trial of kea crossings and request that the Committe grant approval to five additional schools in Christchurch City to operate kea crossings. <i>(Feb 1999,internet - see Notes,1</i>) [NOTES: archive.ccc.govt.nz/Council/agendas/1999/February/CityServices/KeaCrossings-ExtensionofTrialRR4511]

### kea flea
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [bat flea](../B#bat-flea))

>  <b>NATUSCH</b> The kea flea (<i>Parapsyllus nestoris</i>), another `very nice new flea' (<i>P. lynnae</i>, host unknown) ... are ... new species recorded by F.G.A.M. Smit ... <i>(1967,Animals of NZ, The,160</i>)

### kea gun
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [mustering] a folding gun used to shoot sheep-destroying kea.

>  <b>BiLLiNG</b> ... nailed tramping boots, plimsolls, binoculars, a 12-gauge shotgun and a .410 kea gun - a locally designed hand-gun made from a Spanish .410 shotgun breech and hammer, with a flintlock style pistol grip and a sixteen-inch barrel. it was designed for high-country shepherds who wanted to shoot from the saddle the ... keas ... . <i>(1969,Alpha Trip,122</i>)

>  <b>McLEOD</b> Some of the men carried `kea guns' - usually a folding 410-bore shotgun with a single barrel which would fit in a haversack. <i>(1970,Glorious Morning,75</i>) [NOTES: [reference? late 1920s]]

>  <b>NEWTON</b> .. he could set a tussock alight every few hundred yards and also periodically give a shot from his kea gun. <i>(1972,Sheep Thief,88</i>)

>  <b>KERR</b> Kea guns make a hideous amount of noise ... <i>(2000,High Times in the High Country,126</i>)

>  Kea guns comprised a small shot-gun cartridge in a basic pistol with a long barrel. <i>(Jan 13, 2010,Dominion Post,A10</i>)

### kea hunter
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a person employed on a high country station to eradicate keas.

>  <b>BULLER</b> So destructive .. have they become on some of the sheep-runs that the aid of Parliament has been invoked to abate the nuisance by offering a subsidy to Kea-hunters. <i>(1888,Birds of New Zealand,i 169</i>)

>  Old Jack Scott, the kea hunter, was still scouring the hill for beaks and came back one day saying he had seen a hundred sheep on Powers Country far out towards the Bruce Saddle. <i>(1974,Kingdom in the Hills,139</i>)

>  <b>MCLEOD</b> Old Jack pricked up his ears - was he not the official station kea-hunter? <i>(1974,Kingdom in the Hills,100</i>)

>  A kea-hunter, who spends a great deal of his time on sheep stations in the kea country amongst the sheep-killers of Canterbury, and who has caught hundreds of them, relates an incident to show that the evil name they have been given is not undeserved. <i>(Aug 21, 1909,Otago Daily Times,3</i>)

### kea hunting
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the eradication of keas in high country pastoral areas.

>  <b>MCLEOD</b> Still a mountain man at heart, he took up kea hunting for a living and went from station to station offering his services wherever he heard that sheep were being killed. <i>(1974,Kingdom in the Hills,98</i>)

>  The way in which Mr Potts describes kea-hunting deserves to be quoted at length. He says:- "Kea-hunting is an arduous and uncertain employment..." <i>(Jul 17, 1883,Star, The,3</i>)

>  Mr Marriner relates his own experience when he went out in to the mountains kea hunting and he describes the rugged country in which the notorious sheep killer lives. <i>(Dec 23, 1908,Colonist, The,1</i>)

>  He adds that kea hunting is so unattractive that offers of 35s a week, with free tent, stores and ammunition, in addition to 5s a head, have received no answers. <i>(Jun 27, 1918,Grey River Argus,4</i>)

### kea killing
 
 <i>n.</i> the killing of sheep by kea

>  <b>DiCK</b> They evidently struck a terrible session of kea killing, which caused them to sell out smartly to the first interested buyer, W. Croucher, a South Canterbury farmer from Hook. <i>(1964,High Country Family,22</i>)

### kea louse
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [kea flea](k-kea.md#kea-flea))

>  The stories we had been told of kea lice seemed only too true. <i>(1978,Manawatu Tramping Club Jubilee,79</i>)

### kea pistol
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The two men.. were armed with a sawnof shotgun and what appeared to be a kea pistol - a smallbore shotgun with a long barrel. <i>(Sep 29, 1992,Dominion,3</i>)

### kea stew
 
 <i>n.</i> (Environment) a tramper's meal made of the indigenous kea or mountain parrot [ORIGIN: blend]

>  <b>PASCOE</b> They dug out a platform for the tent, lined the floor with rocks as a sleeping-bench or Maori bunk, and got stuck into another kea stew. <i>(1958,Great Days in NZ Mountaineering,156</i>)

>  Add to this that all tourists are not accustomed to camping out under canvas at such a high altitude, or even to cooking a chop, boiling tea in a "billy", or making a mess of "kea stew" ... <i>(Apr 7, 1885,Timaru Herald,3</i>)

>  in those days kea stew was a luxury. Now the keas are preserved to amuse the tourists, and Clarke, with wonderful celerity, will produce a four-course dinner that is warranted to satisfy even a Mount Cook appetite. <i>(Jan 27, 1906,Evening Post,9</i>)

### kea'd
 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of high country sheep] attacked by keas.

>  <b>NEWTON</b> .. although the birds were far more plentiful back on the latter place the only kea'd sheep i saw were in the vicinity of Red Hills. <i>(1973,Big Country,197,1977 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MCKENZiE</b> For the owner all that is left is the skin, and then its monetary value is lessened due to rips and tears - and if badly 'kead' - a gaping hole. <i>(1951,Wakatipu Pioneers,98</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> The only kea'd sheep i saw were in the vicinity of the Red Hills .. the next morning we found six kea'd sheep among them. <i>(1973,Big Country of the South island,197</i>)

>  <b>ASPiNALL</b> Since that night there has never been a reporting of a kea'd sheep in Jack Hall's basin. [kead] <i>(1993,Farming Under Aspiring,135</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> What few kea'd sheep we did find were further up the Esk on the Spurs Block .. we found no more kea'd sheep after that. <i>(1966,Boss's Story, The,108</i>)

### kea-infested
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  in most of the kea-infested country the annual damage is ... well under 5 per cent. <i>(1907,TrNZi,39/293</i>)

### kea-shooter
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  So serious was the trouble that ... men were engaged as `kea-shooters' ... <i>(1907,TrNZi,39/73</i>)

### Keating
 
 <i>v.</i> to dust with Keatings flea powder

>  <b>GUTHRiE-SMiTH</b> By noon ... we had scalded the floors ... burnt kapoc mattresses second or third hand probably when purchased ... Keatinged the bunks, ... <i>(1936,NZ Naturalist,58</i>)
